\title{
Finite Coherent Projection in Modal Triplet Theory\\[0.35em]
\large A Common Architecture for Duality, Gauge, Gravity, Quantization, Entanglement, and Measurement
}

\author{Peter Nero}

\date{April 2026}

\begin{document}

\maketitle

% ============================================================
% Abstract
% ============================================================

\begin{abstract}
Many of the central structures of modern physics appear in distinct formalisms: Dirac
delta localization, spectral wave propagation, finite measurement records, gauge
redundancy, scattering amplitudes, quantized spectra, entangled correlations, and
geometric curvature. Modal Triplet Theory (MTT) suggests that these are not unrelated
phenomena. They are downstream projection shadows of finite coherent admissibility.

The central analytic object is the admissible coherent projection operator
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P,
\end{equation}
where \(A\) is a sectoral stabilization or admissibility operator, \(P\) is the coherent-sector
projector, \(\chi(A)\) is an admissible spectral window, and \(\tau\) is the damping-selected
proper-time/heat-time scale. In a local representation, the kernel
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)=\langle x|\Badm|y\rangle
\end{equation}
is a finite source, response, or detector-amplitude kernel whose sharp limit recovers the
Dirac delta. In a spectral representation, the same object is a weighted coherent modal
structure,
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_{n\in\coh}
  \chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}
  \phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y),
\end{equation}
whose retained phases generate interference.

This paper proposes that the same finite projection architecture also organizes scattering,
gauge, electromagnetism, gravity, quantization, superposition, entanglement, and measurement.
In flat spectral charts, \(e^{-\tau A}\) produces Gaussian high-mode damping and finite
propagator corrections. In gauge sectors, \(P\) must be quotient-compatible, so finite filtering
acts only on gauge-admissible physical content or through gauge-covariant operators. In
gravity, the corresponding projector must be diffeomorphism-compatible, because geometry
itself is the object whose local representatives must glue globally. In quantization, admissible
spectra arise from Circle phase closure, Lens quotient consistency, and Nil survivor
selection. In entanglement, joint coherent sectors need not factor into independent local
sectors. In measurement, finite effects and survivor-basin stabilization replace primitive
collapse by context-dependent projection and selection.

The result is a structural unification theorem: local deltas, spectral waves, finite records,
gauge representatives, geometric gluing, quantized survivor spectra, and entangled joint
coherences are distinct shadows of one finite coherent projection architecture. The claim is
not that all numerical sector data are already derived. Rather, the claim is that MTT identifies
the common operator-theoretic mechanism by which these structures arise and specifies the
sector-by-sector execution tasks needed for empirical closure.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents

% ============================================================
\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:introduction}
% ============================================================

Wave--particle duality is usually presented as a foundational quantum puzzle. In one
experimental context a system appears localized, producing discrete detector records. In
another context the same system produces interference, phase coherence, and wave-like
propagation. The standard lesson is that neither classical particles nor classical waves are
adequate primitives.

This paper develops a sharper formulation of that lesson in Modal Triplet Theory (MTT).
The central claim is not that a quantum object is sometimes a particle and sometimes a wave.
The claim is that both particle-like localization and wave-like interference are downstream
projection shadows of one finite coherent admissibility structure.

The analytic object is
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
  \label{eq:intro-Badm}
\end{equation}
In earlier drafts this object was sometimes called a projection operator. We will use more
precise language here. The map \(P\) is the projector. The full operator \(\Badm\) is generally
not idempotent and therefore will be called the finite coherent admissibility operator, or
equivalently the admissible coherent filter.

The local kernel of \(\Badm\),
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)=\langle x|\Badm|y\rangle,
\end{equation}
gives a finite coherent response kernel. In the sharp limit this kernel becomes a Dirac delta:
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)\to\delta(x-y).
\end{equation}
This is the particle shadow.

The spectral representation of the same operator,
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y),
  \qquad
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n},
  \label{eq:intro-spectral-kernel}
\end{equation}
gives retained coherent modes, phases, and interference. This is the wave shadow.

Thus the first guiding thesis is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{particle and wave are local and spectral shadows of the same finite coherent kernel.}
  }
  \label{eq:intro-wave-particle-thesis}
\end{equation}

The rest of the paper shows that this duality is not isolated. Measurement records,
scattering form factors, gauge quotienting, electromagnetic holonomy, gravitational gluing,
quantized survivor labels, superposition, and entanglement all fit into the same finite
coherent admissibility architecture.

\subsection{From abstract filter to geometric realization}
\label{subsec:intro-geometric-realization}

The operator \(\Badm\) can be stated abstractly on a Hilbert space. However, the physically
important realization used in this paper is geometric. In the fixed-point realization of MTT,
the total space is modeled as a fibered ten-dimensional structure
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6,
  \label{eq:intro-M10}
\end{equation}
or, more generally, as an internal fiber product over a four-dimensional base.

Here \(Y^4\) is the observed four-dimensional Lorentzian base, while \(X^6\) is a compact
Riemannian internal space. The internal sector carries bundle directions
\begin{equation}
  B_n\to Y^4,
  \qquad
  n=1,2,3,
\end{equation}
with compact fibers and nonnegative fiber Laplacians
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{B_n(y)}\ge0.
\end{equation}
The default internal admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}(y)
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3
  \kappa_n\Delta_{B_n(y)},
  \qquad
  \kappa_n>0.
  \label{eq:intro-Aint}
\end{equation}
This operator is positive and elliptic along the internal fibers.

The coherent projector is the joint fiber-harmonic projector
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}(y)
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}(y)\Pi_{B_2}(y)\Pi_{B_3}(y),
  \label{eq:intro-Pcoh}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  \Pi_{B_n}(y):L^2(B_n|_y)\to\ker\Delta_{B_n(y)}
\end{equation}
is the fiberwise harmonic projector. Thus
\begin{equation}
  \Ran P_{\rm coh}(y)
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3\ker\Delta_{B_n(y)}.
  \label{eq:intro-joint-harmonic}
\end{equation}

In this realization, the finite coherent admissibility operator becomes
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
  \label{eq:intro-FP-Badm}
\end{equation}

Thus the abstract operator \eqref{eq:intro-Badm} has a concrete geometric meaning:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Badm
  =
  \text{finite coherent filtering over a compact internal fiber geometry}.
  }
\end{equation}

The coherent sector is therefore not a vague retained subspace. In the fixed-point realization,
it is the joint fiber-harmonic sector.

\subsection{Why the Dirac delta is still central}
\label{subsec:intro-delta-central}

The geometric formulation does not weaken the wave--particle result. It strengthens it.

In ordinary point-particle descriptions one often begins with a Dirac delta source,
\begin{equation}
  J(x)=q\delta(x-x_0).
\end{equation}
MTT reverses the explanatory order. The finite coherent kernel is the native object; the delta
is the singular sharp limit:
\begin{equation}
  \delta(x-y)
  =
  \lim_{\rm sharp}\Kadm(x,y).
\end{equation}

In the FP realization, this finite kernel is not arbitrary smearing. It is generated by the
positive internal admissibility operator \(A_{\rm int}\), the joint harmonic projector
\(P_{\rm coh}\), the spectral window \(\chi(A_{\rm int})\), and the finite damping scale
\(\tau\). Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Dirac localization is the zero-width local shadow of finite coherent fiber support.}
  }
  \label{eq:intro-delta-shadow}
\end{equation}

This is the first major physical shadow of finite coherent admissibility.

\subsection{Why the wave shadow is equally central}
\label{subsec:intro-wave-central}

The same finite kernel also has a spectral expansion. If
\begin{equation}
  A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n,
\end{equation}
then, in the commuting spectral case,
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
\end{equation}
The retained modes carry phase, recurrence, interference, and coherent transport.

In the FP realization, the eigenvalues \(\lambda_n\) are tied to internal fiber spectra or their
sectoral analogues. The zero modes define the coherent low-energy sector, while nonzero
internal modes are damped by
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{wave behavior is the spectral phase-coherent shadow of the same finite kernel.}
  }
  \label{eq:intro-wave-shadow}
\end{equation}

The central duality may therefore be written as
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent admissibility}
  \quad\longrightarrow\quad
  \begin{cases}
    \text{Dirac delta shadow},\\
    \text{spectral wave shadow}.
  \end{cases}
  }
  \label{eq:intro-duality-map}
\end{equation}

This is the physical entry point of the paper.

\subsection{The Lorentzian admissibility principle}
\label{subsec:intro-lorentzian-principle}

A major source of confusion in finite-kernel theories is the temptation to write a Lorentzian
damping factor of the form
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}.
\end{equation}
MTT does not take this as its fundamental Lorentzian construction.

The d'Alembertian \(\Box\) is not a positive elliptic operator. A naive exponential of
\(\Box\) can introduce acausal behavior, ghost-like pathologies, or uncontrolled analytic
continuation problems. The MTT admissibility principle is therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A\neq\Box
  \quad
  \text{as the fundamental Lorentzian damping operator.}
  }
  \label{eq:intro-A-not-box}
\end{equation}

Instead, \(A\) must be positive in the relevant admissibility sector. In the FP realization,
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int}
\end{equation}
is a positive fiber elliptic operator. In Cauchy-slice formulations, \(A\) may be a positive
spatial or Hamiltonian operator acting on a fixed time-slab or on constrained physical data.
In gauge and gravitational sectors, \(A\) must act only after quotienting or in a
constraint-compatible way.

Thus the second guiding thesis is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT damping is positive fiber/spatial/Cauchy-slice damping, not naive Lorentzian }
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}\text{ damping.}
  }
  \label{eq:intro-positive-damping}
\end{equation}

This is not a technical footnote. It is part of the physical architecture.

\subsection{Dimensional reduction and the KK-style reading}
\label{subsec:intro-KK-reading}

The FP realization has an honest Kaluza--Klein-style backbone. Compact internal fiber
spectra produce zero modes and massive internal excitations. If
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_n=\mu_n^2\phi_n,
\end{equation}
then the admissibility filter assigns internal weight
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_n^2}.
\end{equation}

After dimensional reduction, a schematic four-dimensional propagator has the form
\begin{equation}
  G_{4D}(p)
  =
  \sum_{n\ge0}
  \frac{Z_n\ee^{-\tau\mu_n^2}}
  {p^2-m_n^2+\ii\epsilon},
  \label{eq:intro-4D-propagator}
\end{equation}
where \(Z_n\ge0\) in ghost-free positive-norm sectors.

The zero mode satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
\end{equation}
so
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
Therefore ordinary low-energy four-dimensional propagation is recovered at the zero-mode
level. Finite coherent corrections enter through nonzero internal modes, threshold effects,
loop corrections, detector profiles, or sector-specific form factors.

This point is important:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the default FP/MTT damping acts on internal fiber momentum, not on the physical
  Lorentzian four-momentum of a zero mode.}
  }
  \label{eq:intro-fiber-not-base}
\end{equation}

Thus MTT is not a random nonlocal modification of four-dimensional quantum field theory.
It is a projection-and-filter architecture over a fibered geometric structure.

The connection to Kaluza--Klein theory is therefore not a weakness. It is the geometric
backbone that makes the framework concrete. The distinction is that MTT does not stop at
the existence of extra modes. It organizes finite kernels, measurement records, damping,
gauge quotienting, gravitational gluing, quantization, and entanglement under one projection
architecture.

\subsection{Locality in total space and nonlocality as shadow}
\label{subsec:intro-locality-shadow}

The geometric interpretation also clarifies the status of nonlocality.

MTT does not begin with arbitrary nonlocal influence in \(Y^4\). It begins with finite coherent
structure in the total fibered setting. When this structure is projected to the observed
four-dimensional base, it may appear as a finite-width source, finite detector profile, form
factor, or nonlocal-looking effective kernel.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{four-dimensional nonlocality is a projection shadow of controlled finite structure
  in the total/fibered space.}
  }
  \label{eq:intro-nonlocality-shadow}
\end{equation}

In Cauchy-slice formulations, finite kernels may be evaluated on equal-time spatial slices.
When such kernels enter as lower-order bounded terms and do not change the principal
hyperbolic symbol, the finite speed of propagation and domains of dependence remain those
of the underlying local hyperbolic system. This gives the Lorentzian implementation a
causal interpretation without invoking \(\ee^{-\tau\Box}\).

Thus MTT separates two notions that are often confused:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent support}
  \neq
  \text{acausal signal propagation}.
  }
\end{equation}

Finite support or finite overlap in an admissible projection context can produce non-pointlike
effective shadows without violating relativistic causal propagation.

\subsection{Circle--Lens--Nil grammar}
\label{subsec:intro-CLN}

The interpretive grammar of MTT is the Modal Triplet:
\begin{equation}
  \Circle,\qquad \Lens,\qquad \Nil.
\end{equation}

Circle denotes phase, return, recurrence, spectral closure, and holonomy:
\begin{equation}
  \Circle:
  \quad
  \text{phase, loop, recurrence, spectral mode, coherent return}.
\end{equation}

Lens denotes projection, representation, quotienting, gauge choice, coordinate choice, and
measurement context:
\begin{equation}
  \Lens:
  \quad
  \text{projection, representative, quotient, gauge, context}.
\end{equation}

Nil denotes damping, termination, branch selection, survivor basins, and discrete records:
\begin{equation}
  \Nil:
  \quad
  \text{damping, threshold, basin, record, survivor}.
\end{equation}

The finite coherent admissibility operator has the corresponding reading:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Badm
  =
  \Circle\text{-compatible}
  \;\Lens\text{-consistent}
  \;\Nil\text{-surviving}
  \text{ finite identity}.
  }
  \label{eq:intro-CLN-Badm}
\end{equation}

This grammar does not replace the mathematics. It explains why the same mathematical
architecture appears in wave mechanics, measurement, gauge theory, gravity, quantization,
and entanglement.

\subsection{What is unified}
\label{subsec:intro-what-unified}

The unification proposed here is structural and conditional. It does not claim that all
Standard Model parameters, masses, coupling constants, detector basin measures, or
quantum-gravity sectors have already been derived. It claims that once a physical sector
supplies admissible data
\begin{equation}
  A,\qquad P,\qquad \chi,\qquad \tau,
\end{equation}
and the relevant quotient, positivity, and measurement conditions are satisfied, the same
finite coherent admissibility architecture produces multiple physical shadows.

The main shadows are:
\begin{align}
  \text{particle}
  &:=
  \text{local delta-limit shadow},
  \\
  \text{wave}
  &:=
  \text{spectral phase-coherent shadow},
  \\
  \text{measurement record}
  &:=
  \text{finite survivor-basin shadow},
  \\
  \text{scattering form factor}
  &:=
  \text{finite overlap or damped internal-mode shadow},
  \\
  \text{gauge}
  &:=
  \text{Lens quotient consistency},
  \\
  \text{electromagnetism}
  &:=
  \text{gauge Lens plus phase holonomy plus photon records},
  \\
  \text{gravity}
  &:=
  \text{diffeomorphism-compatible geometric gluing},
  \\
  \text{quantization}
  &:=
  \text{admissible survivor labels},
  \\
  \text{superposition}
  &:=
  \text{coherent branch coexistence before selection},
  \\
  \text{entanglement}
  &:=
  \text{non-factorizable coherent support in a joint sector}.
\end{align}

The central claim is therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{many familiar physical structures are distinct projection shadows of finite coherent
  admissibility.}
  }
  \label{eq:intro-central-claim}
\end{equation}

\subsection{What the paper does not claim}
\label{subsec:intro-not-claim}

The paper does not claim that every sector has already been numerically closed. In particular,
it does not yet derive:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the full Standard Model gauge group and matter representations from first principles;
  \item all particle masses and coupling constants;
  \item a complete nonperturbative quantum-gravity theory;
  \item exact collider bounds for every internal excitation;
  \item all detector basin measures;
  \item a full derivation of Born weights in every measurement context.
\end{enumerate}

Those are execution tasks.

The result established here is more modest and more precise:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT supplies a common finite admissibility architecture that sectoral theories must
  instantiate and test.}
  }
  \label{eq:intro-modest-claim}
\end{equation}

This distinction is important. The unification is structural; prediction requires sectoral
execution.

\subsection{Outline of the paper}
\label{subsec:intro-outline}

Section~\ref{sec:geometric-setup} states the geometric setup, including the FP realization on
\(M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6\), compact internal fibers, nonnegative fiber Laplacians, and the joint
fiber-harmonic coherent projector.

Section~\ref{sec:operator-setup} defines the finite coherent admissibility operator
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
and distinguishes the commuting spectral case from noncommuting sectoral projections.

Section~\ref{sec:cln-kernel} gives the Circle--Lens--Nil interpretation of the operator.

Section~\ref{sec:delta-wave-measurement} develops the central wave--particle result:
Dirac delta localization and spectral wave behavior are two shadows of the same finite
coherent kernel.

Section~\ref{sec:scattering} treats scattering, finite propagators, Euclidean scalar kernels,
fiber damping, and the distinction between internal-mode damping and naive Lorentzian
damping.

Section~\ref{sec:cauchy-locality} explains the Lorentzian admissibility principle, Cauchy-slice
finite kernels, and locality in total space versus nonlocality as a four-dimensional projection
shadow.

Section~\ref{sec:gauge-em} applies the architecture to gauge theory and electromagnetism.

Section~\ref{sec:gravity} treats gravity as diffeomorphism-compatible geometric gluing and
relates the admissibility operator to spatial/geometric data rather than to an indefinite
spacetime d'Alembertian.

Section~\ref{sec:quantization} reframes quantization as the emergence of admissible survivor
labels.

Section~\ref{sec:superposition-entanglement} treats superposition and entanglement as
coherent-sector and joint-sector structure.

Section~\ref{sec:unification-theorem} separates the analytic filter theorem, the branch-channel
theorem, the scalar benchmark theorem, and the structural unification corollary.

Section~\ref{sec:relation-existing} compares the framework to complementarity, decoherence,
consistent histories, relational quantum mechanics, quantum field theory, gauge theory,
Kaluza--Klein theory, string theory, loop/holonomy approaches, holography, and other
structural programs.

Section~\ref{sec:phenomenology} states the phenomenological execution program, including
the geometric origin of the coherence scale
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
\end{equation}
and the relation
\begin{equation}
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R\sqrt{\log(C_Q/\epsadm)}
\end{equation}
when the internal spectral gap scales as \(\lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2}\).

Section~\ref{sec:failure-modes} records the domain of validity and failure modes, including
noncommuting projectors, invalid damping channels, gauge violation, diffeomorphism violation,
and inadmissible Lorentzian operators.

Section~\ref{sec:conclusion} summarizes the result:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent admissibility}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{projection shadows}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{effective physics}.
  }
\end{equation}

% ============================================================
\section{Geometric setup and fixed-point realization}
\label{sec:geometric-setup}
% ============================================================

The introduction stated the finite coherent admissibility operator in abstract form,
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}
This section gives the geometric realization that will be used as the default model throughout
the paper.

The key point is that \(A\) should not be thought of first as an arbitrary smoothing operator
on four-dimensional spacetime. In the fixed-point realization of MTT, \(A\) is naturally a
positive internal or fiberwise operator. This turns the finite kernel from an ad hoc regulator
into a geometric admissibility filter.

The geometric picture is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6,
  }
\end{equation}
or, more generally, a compact internal fiber geometry over a four-dimensional Lorentzian
base. The observed four-dimensional physics is a projection shadow of coherent admissible
structure on the total/fibered space.

\subsection{The total space}
\label{subsec:total-space}

Let
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6.
  \label{eq:M10-section2}
\end{equation}
Here \(Y^4\) is the observed four-dimensional base and \(X^6\) is a compact internal
Riemannian space.

The base \(Y^4\) is Lorentzian. It carries the macroscopic causal structure, time evolution,
and the effective four-dimensional field description. The internal space \(X^6\) is compact
and Riemannian. It carries the fiber spectra, internal harmonics, and coherent admissibility
structure.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  Y^4:
  \text{Lorentzian base},
  \qquad
  X^6:
  \text{compact Riemannian internal space}.
  }
\end{equation}

The product notation is a clean model. More generally, one may allow a fiber bundle
\begin{equation}
  \pi:M_{10}\to Y^4,
\end{equation}
with compact internal fiber
\begin{equation}
  X_y^6=\pi^{-1}(y)
\end{equation}
over each base point \(y\in Y^4\). The product case is recovered when all fibers are
canonically identified with one fixed compact space \(X^6\).

The essential requirement is not strict product geometry. The essential requirement is that
the internal directions carry positive elliptic operators with controlled spectra.

\subsection{Internal bundle directions}
\label{subsec:internal-bundle-directions}

In the fixed-point realization, the internal space is organized into three compact bundle
directions
\begin{equation}
  B_n\to Y^4,
  \qquad
  n=1,2,3.
  \label{eq:Bn-section2}
\end{equation}
Each \(B_n\) has compact Riemannian fibers over the base. Let
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{B_n(y)}
\end{equation}
denote the corresponding nonnegative fiber Laplacian over \(y\in Y^4\).

The nonnegativity condition is
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{B_n(y)}\ge0.
\end{equation}
This is crucial. The admissibility damping
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}
\end{equation}
is well-defined as damping only when \(A\) is positive in the relevant sector.

The internal admissibility operator is then modeled as
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}(y)
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3
  \kappa_n\Delta_{B_n(y)},
  \qquad
  \kappa_n>0.
  \label{eq:Aint-section2}
\end{equation}
Since each \(\Delta_{B_n(y)}\) is nonnegative and each \(\kappa_n\) is positive, one has
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}(y)\ge0.
\end{equation}

Thus the default MTT damping operator is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A=A_{\rm int},
  \qquad
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{The Hilbert bundle}
\label{subsec:hilbert-bundle}

At each base point \(y\in Y^4\), let
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{\rm int}(y)
\end{equation}
be the internal Hilbert space of square-integrable sections over the compact internal fiber.
In the simplest product case,
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{\rm int}(y)\cong L^2(X^6,E),
\end{equation}
where \(E\) is the relevant internal bundle or representation space.

The full state space may be written schematically as
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H
  =
  L^2\!\left(Y^4;\mathcal H_{\rm int}(y)\right),
  \label{eq:Hilbert-bundle-section2}
\end{equation}
with the understanding that Lorentzian time evolution on \(Y^4\) must be treated by a
Hamiltonian, Cauchy-slice, or covariant field-theoretic formulation rather than by an
indefinite heat operator on spacetime.

For local Cauchy-slice discussions, one may instead write
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_\Sigma
  =
  L^2\!\left(\Sigma;\mathcal H_{\rm int}(x)\right),
\end{equation}
where \(\Sigma\subset Y^4\) is a spacelike Cauchy slice.

Thus the internal Hilbert structure is fibered over the observed base:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{state}
  =
  \text{base dependence}
  +
  \text{internal coherent fiber data}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Fiber spectra}
\label{subsec:fiber-spectra}

Because the internal fibers are compact Riemannian spaces, the fiber Laplacians have discrete
nonnegative spectra. For each \(n\),
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{B_n(y)}\psi_{n,j}(y)
  =
  \lambda_{n,j}(y)\psi_{n,j}(y),
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
  0=\lambda_{n,0}(y)
  \le
  \lambda_{n,1}(y)
  \le
  \lambda_{n,2}(y)
  \le
  \cdots.
\end{equation}

The zero eigenspace
\begin{equation}
  \ker\Delta_{B_n(y)}
\end{equation}
is the harmonic sector of the \(n\)-th fiber direction.

The first positive eigenvalue controls the gap between harmonic and non-harmonic internal
content:
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_{n,1}(y)>0.
\end{equation}
A uniform spectral gap means there exists
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast>0
\end{equation}
such that
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_{n,1}(y)\ge\lambda_\ast
\end{equation}
for all relevant \(n\) and \(y\).

This gap is the analytic source of finite admissibility damping.

\subsection{The coherent projector}
\label{subsec:coherent-projector-section2}

Let
\begin{equation}
  \Pi_{B_n}(y):
  \mathcal H_{\rm int}(y)\to\ker\Delta_{B_n(y)}
\end{equation}
be the fiberwise harmonic projector. The joint coherent projector is
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}(y)
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}(y)\Pi_{B_2}(y)\Pi_{B_3}(y).
  \label{eq:Pcoh-section2}
\end{equation}
When the fiber projectors commute, this is itself an orthogonal projector.

Its range is the joint fiber-harmonic sector:
\begin{equation}
  \Ran P_{\rm coh}(y)
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3
  \ker\Delta_{B_n(y)}.
  \label{eq:joint-harmonic-section2}
\end{equation}

This is the precise fixed-point meaning of the coherent sector:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \mathcal H_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Ran P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \text{joint fiber-harmonic sector}.
  }
  \label{eq:Hcoh-joint-harmonic}
\end{equation}

Thus the phrase ``retained coherent content'' is not merely interpretive. In the fixed-point
realization it is a concrete spectral subspace: the joint kernel of the internal fiber
Laplacians.

\subsection{The finite coherent admissibility operator}
\label{subsec:Badm-geometric}

With
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  P=P_{\rm coh},
\end{equation}
the fixed-point realization of the MTT admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
  \label{eq:Badm-FP-section2}
\end{equation}

The factors have the following roles:
\begin{align}
  P_{\rm coh}
  &: \text{projection to the joint fiber-harmonic sector},\\
  \chi(A_{\rm int})
  &: \text{admissible spectral window},\\
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  &: \text{damping of noncoherent internal modes},\\
  \chi(A_{\rm int})
  &: \text{readmission to the admissible spectral band},\\
  P_{\rm coh}
  &: \text{return to the coherent sector}.
\end{align}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  \text{finite coherent filtering over the internal fiber spectrum}.
  }
\end{equation}

The operator \(B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}\) should not be called a projector unless it is idempotent
in a particular limiting case. It is a positive admissibility filter built from a genuine
projector \(P_{\rm coh}\), a spectral window, and a finite damping factor.

\subsection{Commuting spectral case}
\label{subsec:commuting-spectral-case-section2}

The cleanest case occurs when
\begin{equation}
  [P_{\rm coh},A_{\rm int}]=0.
\end{equation}
This is the natural situation when \(P_{\rm coh}\) is built from the spectral calculus of the
same fiber operators that define \(A_{\rm int}\).

Let
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\lambda_j\phi_j
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}\phi_j=p_j\phi_j,
  \qquad
  p_j\in\{0,1\}.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}\phi_j
  =
  p_j\chi(\lambda_j)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_j}\phi_j.
  \label{eq:Badm-modal-action-section2}
\end{equation}
The admissible modal weight is
\begin{equation}
  w_j
  =
  p_j\chi(\lambda_j)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_j}.
  \label{eq:modal-weight-section2}
\end{equation}

This is the spectral formula used repeatedly in the paper:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{mode survives}
  \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  w_j
  \text{ remains nonzero or above threshold}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Noncommuting sectoral projectors}
\label{subsec:noncommuting-projectors-section2}

Not every physical sector has a projector that commutes with the chosen stabilization
operator. In measurement contexts, gauge-fixed sectors, approximate effective descriptions,
and gravitational projections, one may have
\begin{equation}
  [P,A]\neq0.
\end{equation}

In such cases the expression
\begin{equation}
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
may still be meaningful, but the diagonal modal formula
\begin{equation}
  w_j=p_j\chi(\lambda_j)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_j}
\end{equation}
does not automatically apply.

Therefore the paper distinguishes two levels:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the commuting spectral case, where the mode weights are diagonal and explicit;
  \item the noncommuting sectoral case, where admissibility must be checked by operator
  estimates, positivity, constraint preservation, and domain control.
\end{enumerate}

This distinction is essential. It prevents the finite coherent admissibility operator from being
mistaken for an arbitrary formal smoothing map.

\subsection{The damping scale}
\label{subsec:damping-scale-section2}

The finite damping time \(\tau\) is selected by a leakage tolerance. Let
\begin{equation}
  Q=I-P_{\rm coh}
\end{equation}
be the discarded-sector projector. Suppose the discarded-sector flow satisfies an estimate
\begin{equation}
  \|Q\Phi_tQ\|
  \le
  C_Q\ee^{-\lambda_\ast t},
  \label{eq:discarded-damping-section2}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast>0
\end{equation}
is the uniform spectral gap. Given an admissibility tolerance
\begin{equation}
  0<\epsadm<1,
\end{equation}
one chooses \(\tau\) so that
\begin{equation}
  C_Q\ee^{-\lambda_\ast\tau}
  =
  \epsadm.
\end{equation}
Thus
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}.
  }
  \label{eq:tau-gap-section2}
\end{equation}

This is the geometric origin of the coherence scale.

If the typical internal radius is \(R\), then for Laplace-type fibers
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2}.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  \sim
  R^2\log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
  \label{eq:tau-R-section2}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{\log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}}.
  \label{eq:sqrttau-R-section2}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \sqrt{\tau}
  =
  \text{internal coherent length scale}.
  }
\end{equation}

The paper will later use
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2}
\end{equation}
as the corresponding effective energy scale.

\subsection{Zero modes and massive internal modes}
\label{subsec:zero-modes-massive-modes}

The internal operator has zero modes and positive modes. If
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_0=0,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}\phi_0=\phi_0.
\end{equation}
Thus zero modes are not damped by internal admissibility.

If
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
  \qquad
  \mu_j^2>0,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}\phi_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\phi_j.
\end{equation}
Thus nonzero internal modes are suppressed.

After dimensional reduction, this gives the schematic four-dimensional tower
\begin{equation}
  G_{4D}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{Z_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}}
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
  \label{eq:4D-KK-tower-section2}
\end{equation}
The zero mode satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
\end{equation}
so its admissibility weight is
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{ordinary low-energy four-dimensional fields are recovered as undamped zero-mode
  shadows.}
  }
\end{equation}

Finite coherent corrections enter through nonzero internal modes, finite detector kernels,
threshold effects, or sector-specific form factors.

\subsection{Fiber damping versus Lorentzian damping}
\label{subsec:fiber-vs-lorentzian-damping}

The distinction between internal damping and Lorentzian damping is central.

The FP/MTT default is
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}},
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}

It is not the naive Lorentzian expression
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}.
\end{equation}
The d'Alembertian \(\Box\) is indefinite and is not a positive elliptic damping operator on
Lorentzian spacetime.

Thus the fundamental Lorentzian admissibility principle is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT damping acts through positive fiber, positive spatial, or constraint-compatible
  operators, not through naive } \ee^{-\tau\Box}.
  }
  \label{eq:no-naive-box-section2}
\end{equation}

This principle will be used later in the scattering, gauge, and gravity sections.

\subsection{Locality in the total space}
\label{subsec:locality-total-space-section2}

The finite kernel should not be read as arbitrary four-dimensional nonlocality. In the geometric
realization, finite coherent width arises from internal fiber structure.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{four-dimensional nonlocal-looking behavior can be a projection shadow of finite
  coherent structure in the total/fibered space.}
  }
\end{equation}

At the level of the observed base \(Y^4\), one may see finite source profiles, finite detector
effects, or form factors. At the level of the full fibered geometry, these are shadows of
internal coherent admissibility.

This gives the conceptual rule:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{locality in the total admissible structure}
  \quad\longrightarrow\quad
  \text{finite non-pointlike shadows in four dimensions}.
  }
\end{equation}

This is why Dirac deltas are not taken as primitive. They are sharp limits of finite coherent
kernels.

\subsection{Relation to Kaluza--Klein structure}
\label{subsec:KK-structure-section2}

The fixed-point realization has a Kaluza--Klein-style geometric backbone. Compact internal
directions produce zero modes and massive internal excitations. This connection should be
acknowledged directly.

However, MTT is not merely ordinary Kaluza--Klein theory with different terminology. The
distinctive MTT structure is the combination of:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item finite coherent admissibility filtering;
  \item joint fiber-harmonic projection;
  \item damping-selected survivor sectors;
  \item Circle--Lens--Nil interpretation;
  \item measurement as finite effects plus survivor basins;
  \item gauge as quotient-compatible Lens projection;
  \item gravity as diffeomorphism-compatible geometric projection;
  \item quantization as admissible survivor labels;
  \item entanglement as non-factorizable coherent support.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT is KK-compatible, but its central contribution is the finite coherent projection
  architecture across physical sectors.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Abstract sectoral generalization}
\label{subsec:abstract-sectoral-generalization}

Although the FP realization is the default geometric model, the formal architecture can be
stated sectorally. A sector supplies:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item a Hilbert space or effective state space \(\mathcal H\);
  \item a positive or sector-compatible admissibility operator \(A\);
  \item an idempotent projector \(P\) onto retained coherent or physical content;
  \item a spectral window \(\chi(A)\);
  \item a damping scale \(\tau>0\);
  \item additional quotient, measurement, or constraint data if required.
\end{enumerate}

Then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
is the finite coherent admissibility operator of that sector.

In scalar Euclidean benchmark cases,
\begin{equation}
  A=-\Delta.
\end{equation}
In the fixed-point internal realization,
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int}.
\end{equation}
In gauge sectors,
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm gauge}
\end{equation}
must be quotient-compatible. In gravitational sectors,
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm grav}
\end{equation}
must be diffeomorphism-compatible or constraint-compatible. In measurement sectors,
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\Meas}
\end{equation}
is a detector-context stabilization operator.

Thus the abstract architecture is common, but the operator data are sectoral.

\subsection{What is not assumed}
\label{subsec:what-not-assumed-section2}

The geometric setup does not assume that all physics has already been derived from
\(M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6\). It does not by itself determine:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the complete Standard Model representation content;
  \item all particle masses;
  \item all coupling constants;
  \item the exact internal geometry;
  \item the exact collider spectrum of internal modes;
  \item all measurement basin measures;
  \item a complete nonperturbative quantum-gravity sector.
\end{enumerate}

Those are execution tasks.

What is assumed for this paper is more precise:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{there exists a sectoral positive admissibility operator and coherent projector from
  which finite projection shadows can be studied.}
  }
\end{equation}

The FP realization supplies a concrete and mathematically controlled version of that
assumption.

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:geometric-summary}

The default geometric realization of MTT is:
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6,
\end{equation}
with \(Y^4\) Lorentzian and \(X^6\) compact Riemannian.

The internal admissibility operator is:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3
  \kappa_n\Delta_{B_n},
  \qquad
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}

The coherent projector is:
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}\Pi_{B_2}\Pi_{B_3},
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
  \Ran P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3\ker\Delta_{B_n}.
\end{equation}

The finite coherent admissibility operator is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
  }
\end{equation}

The damping scale is set by the internal spectral gap:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}.
  }
\end{equation}

This geometric setup preserves the central duality:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent fiber kernel}
  \longrightarrow
  \begin{cases}
    \text{Dirac delta local shadow},\\
    \text{spectral wave shadow}.
  \end{cases}
  }
\end{equation}

It also fixes the Lorentzian interpretation:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT damping is positive fiber/spatial/Cauchy-slice damping, not naive }
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}\text{ damping}.
  }
\end{equation}

% ============================================================
\section{The finite coherent admissibility operator}
\label{sec:operator-setup}
% ============================================================

The previous section introduced the geometric setting in which MTT is realized as finite
coherent admissibility over a compact internal fiber geometry. We now define the central
operator in its general form and record the analytic assumptions under which it is used.

The basic object is
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
  \label{eq:Badm-section3}
\end{equation}
This operator is not generally a projector. The map \(P\) is the projector. The full operator
\(\Badm\) is a finite coherent admissibility operator, or admissible coherent filter.

The purpose of \(\Badm\) is to retain the coherent sector, apply an admissible spectral
window, damp inadmissible high or noncoherent modes, and return the result to the retained
sector. It is the analytic engine behind the paper's projection-shadow claims.

\subsection{Basic sectoral data}
\label{subsec:basic-sectoral-data}

A sectoral MTT construction begins with the following data:
\begin{equation}
  (\mathcal H,A,P,\chi,\tau).
\end{equation}
Here \(\mathcal H\) is a Hilbert space or effective state space,
\begin{equation}
  A:\mathcal D(A)\subset\mathcal H\to\mathcal H
\end{equation}
is a nonnegative self-adjoint or sector-compatible positive stabilization operator,
\begin{equation}
  P=P^\ast=P^2
\end{equation}
is an idempotent projector onto coherent or physical content,
\begin{equation}
  \chi(A)
\end{equation}
is a bounded spectral window, and
\begin{equation}
  \tau>0
\end{equation}
is the damping-selected admissibility scale.

The clean self-adjoint case assumes
\begin{equation}
  A\ge0,
\end{equation}
so that
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}
\end{equation}
is a contraction:
\begin{equation}
  0\le \ee^{-\tau A}\le I.
\end{equation}
If the spectral window satisfies
\begin{equation}
  0\le\chi(A)\le I,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)
\end{equation}
is positive and bounded.

The finite coherent admissibility operator is then
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
  }
\end{equation}

In the fixed-point realization,
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int},
  \qquad
  P=P_{\rm coh},
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3\kappa_n\Delta_{B_n}
\end{equation}
is the positive internal fiber operator and
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}\Pi_{B_2}\Pi_{B_3}
\end{equation}
is the joint fiber-harmonic projector.

\subsection{Why \(\Badm\) is not called a projector}
\label{subsec:not-projector}

A projector \(Q\) satisfies
\begin{equation}
  Q^2=Q.
\end{equation}
Although \(P\) is a projector, the full operator
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
does not generally satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \Badm^2=\Badm.
\end{equation}

Indeed, in a diagonal spectral basis one obtains eigenvalues
\begin{equation}
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
\end{equation}
Unless each \(w_n\) is exactly \(0\) or \(1\), the corresponding operator is not idempotent.

Thus the precise terminology is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  P
  =
  \text{coherent projector},
  }
\end{equation}
while
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Badm
  =
  \text{finite coherent admissibility operator}.
  }
\end{equation}

This distinction matters because the physical claim is not that \(\Badm\) sharply projects
once and for all. The physical claim is that \(\Badm\) filters finite coherent admissibility.

\subsection{Spectral calculus}
\label{subsec:spectral-calculus-section3}

Assume for the moment that \(A\) is nonnegative and self-adjoint. By the spectral theorem,
\begin{equation}
  A
  =
  \int_0^\infty
  \lambda\,\dd E_A(\lambda),
\end{equation}
where \(E_A\) is the projection-valued spectral measure of \(A\). For a bounded Borel function
\(f\),
\begin{equation}
  f(A)
  =
  \int_0^\infty
  f(\lambda)\,\dd E_A(\lambda).
\end{equation}

Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \chi(A)
  =
  \int_0^\infty
  \chi(\lambda)\,\dd E_A(\lambda),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}
  =
  \int_0^\infty
  \ee^{-\tau\lambda}\,\dd E_A(\lambda).
\end{equation}

If \(P\) is a spectral projector of \(A\), or if \(P\) commutes with \(A\), then \(P\) also
commutes with \(\chi(A)\) and \(\ee^{-\tau A}\). In that case the operator \(\Badm\) is a
spectral multiplier on the retained sector.

This commuting spectral case is the cleanest analytic setting and is the default in the
fiber-harmonic FP realization.

\subsection{Commuting case}
\label{subsec:commuting-case-section3}

Suppose
\begin{equation}
  [P,A]=0.
  \label{eq:P-commutes-A-section3}
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{equation}
  A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  P\phi_n=p_n\phi_n,
  \qquad
  p_n\in\{0,1\}.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \chi(A)\phi_n=\chi(\lambda_n)\phi_n,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}\phi_n=\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
  \label{eq:Badm-action-section3}
\end{equation}

Define the admissible modal weight
\begin{equation}
  w_n
  :=
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
  \label{eq:weight-section3}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n|\phi_n\rangle\langle\phi_n|
  \label{eq:Badm-spectral-sum-section3}
\end{equation}
in the discrete spectral case.

The interpretation is direct:
\begin{align}
  p_n&=0
  &&\Rightarrow
  \text{mode outside coherent sector},\\
  \chi(\lambda_n)=0
  &&\Rightarrow
  \text{mode outside admissible spectral window},\\
  \ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\ll1
  &&\Rightarrow
  \text{mode strongly damped},\\
  w_n\approx1
  &&\Rightarrow
  \text{mode retained as coherent admissible content}.
\end{align}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{coherent survivor mode}
  =
  \text{mode with nonzero or threshold-surviving admissible weight}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Noncommuting case}
\label{subsec:noncommuting-case-section3}

In more general sectors, the projector \(P\) need not commute with \(A\):
\begin{equation}
  [P,A]\neq0.
\end{equation}
This may occur for detector projections, approximate effective sectors, constrained gauge
systems, or gravitational reductions.

Then the diagonal expression
\begin{equation}
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}
\end{equation}
is not generally valid. The operator
\begin{equation}
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
may still be bounded and physically meaningful, but its admissibility must be justified by
operator estimates rather than by scalar spectral weights.

In the noncommuting case, one must check:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item domain preservation;
  \item positivity or complete positivity where relevant;
  \item boundedness on the intended Sobolev or Hilbert scale;
  \item compatibility with constraints;
  \item preservation of gauge or diffeomorphism equivalence;
  \item stability of the retained sector under the effective dynamics.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{commuting case}
  =
  \text{spectral multiplier};
  }
\end{equation}
whereas
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{noncommuting case}
  =
  \text{sectoral admissibility problem}.
  }
\end{equation}

This distinction will be important in the gauge, gravity, and measurement sections.

\subsection{Positivity and boundedness}
\label{subsec:positivity-boundedness-section3}

In the commuting self-adjoint setting, \(\Badm\) is positive. Indeed, if \(P\) commutes with
\(A\), then
\begin{align}
  \langle f,\Badm f\rangle
  &=
  \langle f,P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P f\rangle\\
  &=
  \left\|
  \ee^{-\tau A/2}\chi(A)Pf
  \right\|^2\\
  &\ge0.
\end{align}

If
\begin{equation}
  0\le\chi(A)\le I,
\end{equation}
and \(P\) is an orthogonal projector, then
\begin{equation}
  \|\Badm\|\le1.
\end{equation}

Therefore, in the clean spectral case,
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  0\le\Badm\le I.
  }
\end{equation}

This inequality gives a precise sense in which \(\Badm\) is an admissibility filter: it does not
amplify retained modes beyond the identity; it preserves, windows, and damps.

\subsection{Kernel representation}
\label{subsec:kernel-representation-section3}

When \(\Badm\) admits an integral kernel, write
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)=\langle x|\Badm|y\rangle.
  \label{eq:Kadm-section3}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  (\Badm f)(x)
  =
  \int
  \Kadm(x,y)f(y)\,\dd\mu(y),
  \label{eq:kernel-action-section3}
\end{equation}
where \(\dd\mu\) is the relevant measure.

In a discrete spectral expansion,
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y).
  \label{eq:Kadm-spectral-section3}
\end{equation}

This equation is the source of both primary shadows.

The local reading fixes one argument:
\begin{equation}
  x\mapsto\Kadm(x,x_0).
\end{equation}
This is the finite coherent response to a localized source at \(x_0\). In the sharp limit it
becomes a delta response:
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)\to\delta(x-y).
\end{equation}

The spectral reading uses the expansion
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y),
\end{equation}
which displays retained coherent modes and their phase relations.

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Kadm
  =
  \text{one finite kernel with a local delta shadow and a spectral wave shadow}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Sharp limit}
\label{subsec:sharp-limit-section3}

The Dirac delta is recovered only in a controlled sharp limit. In the simplest case this means:
\begin{equation}
  P\to I,
  \qquad
  \chi(A)\to I,
  \qquad
  \tau\downarrow0.
  \label{eq:sharp-limit-section3}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\to I
\end{equation}
in the relevant strong or distributional sense, and the kernel of the identity is
\begin{equation}
  \delta(x-y).
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \delta(x-y)
  =
  \text{identity-kernel shadow of the sharp admissibility limit}.
  }
\end{equation}

This is why MTT does not treat point particles or point sources as native primitive objects.
They are idealized sharp shadows of finite coherent kernels.

\subsection{Finite-width regime}
\label{subsec:finite-width-regime-section3}

The physically important regime is usually not the exact sharp limit but the finite-width
regime. The coherent width is
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau}.
  \label{eq:lcoh-section3}
\end{equation}
The corresponding energy scale is
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2}.
  \label{eq:Lambdaeff-section3}
\end{equation}

If a probe has resolution length \(\ell_{\rm res}\), then the finite kernel appears pointlike
when
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\ll\ell_{\rm res}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, if the probe energy is \(E_{\rm probe}\), the pointlike approximation holds when
\begin{equation}
  E_{\rm probe}\ll\Lambda_{\rm eff}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{pointlike}
  =
  \text{finite coherent support below experimental resolution}.
  }
\end{equation}

In the FP realization, \(\ell_{\rm coh}\) is tied to the internal fiber scale through
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
\end{equation}
and, for typical internal radius \(R\),
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{\log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Finite filter versus arbitrary regulator}
\label{subsec:not-arbitrary-regulator-section3}

It is important to distinguish the finite coherent admissibility operator from an arbitrary
regulator.

An arbitrary regulator is imposed externally to tame divergences. By contrast, \(\Badm\) is
intended to arise from sectoral admissibility data:
\begin{equation}
  A,\qquad P,\qquad \chi,\qquad \tau.
\end{equation}
In the FP realization, these data have geometric meaning:
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int},
  \qquad
  P=P_{\rm coh},
  \qquad
  \tau=\lambda_\ast^{-1}\log(C_Q/\epsadm).
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent damping is not arbitrary smoothing;}
  }
\end{equation}
it is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{damping generated by a positive admissibility operator and a coherent-sector
  projector}.
  }
\end{equation}

This distinction becomes essential in gauge and gravity sectors. A Gaussian-looking factor is
not automatically admissible. It must preserve quotient consistency, constraints, positivity,
and causal structure.

\subsection{Lorentzian admissibility}
\label{subsec:lorentzian-admissibility-section3}

The operator \(A\) in
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}
\end{equation}
is required to be positive in the relevant admissibility sector.

Therefore, in Lorentzian field theory, MTT does not take
\begin{equation}
  A=\Box
\end{equation}
as the fundamental damping choice. The d'Alembertian is indefinite and does not generate a
positive heat kernel in Lorentzian signature.

The admissible alternatives are:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item positive internal fiber operators such as \(A_{\rm int}\);
  \item positive spatial elliptic operators on Cauchy slices;
  \item positive Hamiltonian or constraint-compatible operators;
  \item gauge-quotiented physical operators;
  \item diffeomorphism-compatible geometric operators;
  \item detector-context stabilization operators.
\end{enumerate}

Thus the Lorentzian rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A\neq\Box
  \quad
  \text{unless a positive physical-sector replacement has been defined.}
  }
\end{equation}

This principle is not optional. It is what prevents the finite kernel from becoming an
uncontrolled nonlocal Lorentzian modification.

\subsection{Sectoral examples}
\label{subsec:sectoral-examples-section3}

The same formal architecture appears in several sectors.

\paragraph{FP internal sector.}
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int},
  \qquad
  P=P_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}
Here \(\Badm\) filters compact internal fiber modes.

\paragraph{Scalar Euclidean benchmark.}
\begin{equation}
  A=-\Delta,
  \qquad
  P=I,
  \qquad
  \chi=1.
\end{equation}
Then \(\Badm=e^{\tau\Delta}\) and the kernel is Gaussian.

\paragraph{Gauge sector.}
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys}.
\end{equation}
Here \(P_{\rm phys}\) projects to gauge-admissible content.

\paragraph{Gravity sector.}
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
\end{equation}
Here \(P_{\rm diff}\) projects to diffeomorphism-compatible geometric content.

\paragraph{Measurement sector.}
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\Meas}
  =
  P_{\Meas}\chi(A_{\Meas})
  \ee^{-\tau_{\Meas}A_{\Meas}}
  \chi(A_{\Meas})P_{\Meas}.
\end{equation}
Here \(A_{\Meas}\) and \(P_{\Meas}\) are detector-context data.

The formal shape is common. The sectoral content differs.

\subsection{Projection shadows}
\label{subsec:projection-shadows-section3}

A projection shadow is an effective physical structure obtained by reading \(\Badm\) through
a particular representation, limit, quotient, or measurement context.

The most important shadows are:
\begin{align}
  \text{local kernel}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{particle/delta shadow},
  \\
  \text{spectral expansion}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{wave/interference shadow},
  \\
  \text{finite detector effects}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{measurement-record shadow},
  \\
  \text{fiber-mode damping}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{finite internal excitation shadow},
  \\
  \text{quotient-compatible projection}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{gauge or diffeomorphism shadow},
  \\
  \text{joint coherent sector}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{entanglement shadow}.
\end{align}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{effective physics}
  =
  \text{finite coherent admissibility read through a projection context}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:operator-summary-section3}

The central operator is
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
  }
\end{equation}
It is not generally a projector. It is a finite coherent admissibility operator.

In the commuting spectral case,
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
\end{equation}
The modal weight is
\begin{equation}
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
\end{equation}

When a kernel exists,
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y).
\end{equation}
The same kernel has two primary shadows:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{local reading}
  \Rightarrow
  \text{Dirac delta / particle shadow},
  }
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{spectral reading}
  \Rightarrow
  \text{wave / interference shadow}.
  }
\end{equation}

In the FP realization,
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int},
  \qquad
  P=P_{\rm coh},
\end{equation}
so the finite admissibility operator is grounded in compact internal fiber geometry rather
than arbitrary four-dimensional smearing.

The Lorentzian rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT damping uses positive fiber/spatial/constraint-compatible operators, not naive }
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}.
  }
\end{equation}

% ============================================================
\section{Circle--Lens--Nil reading of finite coherent kernels}
\label{sec:cln-kernel}
% ============================================================

The previous sections defined the geometric setting and the finite coherent admissibility
operator
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}
This section records the Modal Triplet interpretation of that operator. The purpose is not to
replace the analytic construction with metaphor, but to identify the three structural roles
played by the factors of \(\Badm\).

The triplet is
\begin{equation}
  \Circle,\qquad \Lens,\qquad \Nil.
\end{equation}
Circle denotes phase, recurrence, closure, spectral coherence, and holonomy. Lens denotes
projection, representation, quotienting, context, gauge choice, and coordinate choice. Nil
denotes damping, loss, thresholding, basin selection, and survivor records.

In this language,
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Badm
  =
  \Circle\text{-compatible}
  \;\Lens\text{-consistent}
  \;\Nil\text{-surviving}
  \text{ finite admissibility operator}.
  }
  \label{eq:cln-Badm-reading}
\end{equation}

This is the structural grammar that will later organize wave--particle duality, measurement,
scattering, gauge theory, electromagnetism, gravity, quantization, superposition, and
entanglement.

\subsection{Circle}
\label{subsec:circle}

Circle is the phase and return component of the theory. It appears whenever physical content
is organized by coherent modes, periodicity, recurrence, spectral phase, holonomy, or closure
conditions.

In a spectral representation,
\begin{equation}
  A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n,
\end{equation}
the retained modes
\begin{equation}
  \phi_n
\end{equation}
carry coherent phase structure. In ordinary wave mechanics this appears as
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{\ii k\cdot x},
\end{equation}
or, under time evolution,
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\ii E_nt}.
\end{equation}
In gauge theory it appears as electromagnetic phase and holonomy:
\begin{equation}
  \exp\left(\ii q\oint_\gamma A\right).
\end{equation}
In gravity it appears as frame holonomy and curvature:
\begin{equation}
  [\nabla_\mu,\nabla_\nu]v^\rho
  =
  R^\rho{}_{\sigma\mu\nu}v^\sigma.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Circle
  =
  \text{phase, recurrence, closure, spectral coherence, holonomy}.
  }
\end{equation}

In the finite coherent admissibility operator, Circle is represented by the spectral modes of
\(A\), together with the phase relations among retained modes. The modal expansion
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y)
  \label{eq:cln-spectral-kernel}
\end{equation}
is therefore the Circle-readable form of the kernel.

The wave shadow of MTT is a Circle shadow:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{wave}
  =
  \Circle\text{-coherent spectral shadow}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Lens}
\label{subsec:lens}

Lens is the projection and representation component of the theory. It appears whenever the
same physical content can be read through different coordinate systems, bases, gauges,
measurement contexts, or quotient representatives.

In the finite coherent admissibility operator, Lens appears first as the projector
\begin{equation}
  P.
\end{equation}
The projector selects the retained coherent or physical sector:
\begin{equation}
  P:\mathcal H\to\mathcal H_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}

In the fixed-point realization,
\begin{equation}
  P=P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}\Pi_{B_2}\Pi_{B_3},
\end{equation}
and the retained sector is the joint fiber-harmonic sector:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{\rm coh}
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3\ker\Delta_{B_n}.
\end{equation}

In gauge theory, Lens appears as quotienting:
\begin{equation}
  A_\mu\sim A_\mu+\partial_\mu\alpha.
\end{equation}
The local potential is a representative; the physical content is gauge-equivalence data.

In gravity, Lens appears as diffeomorphism redundancy:
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}\sim\varphi^\ast g_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}
Coordinates are representatives; the physical content is diffeomorphism-compatible geometry.

In measurement, Lens appears as the chosen observable basis or detector context:
\begin{equation}
  \Meas=
  (A_{\Meas},P_{\Meas},\chi_{\Meas},\tau_{\Meas},
  \{E_i^{(\Meas)}\},\frakB_{\Meas}).
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Lens
  =
  \text{projection, representation, quotient, gauge, coordinate, context}.
  }
\end{equation}

The particle shadow also has a Lens aspect. The same finite kernel can be read locally,
spectrally, through a detector, through a gauge quotient, or through a geometric projection.
The observed physical shadow depends on the projection context.

\subsection{Nil}
\label{subsec:nil}

Nil is the damping, loss, threshold, and survivor component of the theory. It appears whenever
some formal possibilities are suppressed, excluded, terminated, or stabilized into records.

In the finite coherent admissibility operator, Nil appears in the factor
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}\phi_n
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
\end{equation}
Modes with large \(\lambda_n\) are suppressed. Modes in the coherent zero or low sector
survive.

In the FP realization,
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int},
\end{equation}
so Nil damping acts on internal fiber excitations:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}\phi_n
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_n^2}\phi_n.
\end{equation}
The zero mode is undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
Nonzero internal modes are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_n^2>0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_n^2}<1.
\end{equation}

In measurement theory, Nil appears as branch damping:
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}\mapsto D_{ab}\rho_{ab},
\end{equation}
and as survivor-basin capture:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

In gravity, Nil appears as horizons, singularities, boundary conditions, topology selection,
and survivor geometries.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Nil
  =
  \text{damping, threshold, termination, basin, survivor, record}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{The triplet in \(\Badm\)}
\label{subsec:triplet-in-Badm}

The finite coherent admissibility operator has the form
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}
Each factor has a triplet interpretation.

The projectors \(P\) are Lens factors:
\begin{equation}
  P:
  \quad
  \Lens\text{-selection of coherent or physical content}.
\end{equation}

The spectral windows \(\chi(A)\) are Lens--Nil factors:
\begin{equation}
  \chi(A):
  \quad
  \Lens\text{-selected admissible band}
  +
  \Nil\text{-exclusion of inadmissible modes}.
\end{equation}

The heat factor \(\ee^{-\tau A}\) is Nil damping:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}:
  \quad
  \Nil\text{-suppression of noncoherent spectral content}.
\end{equation}

The retained spectral modes and their phases are Circle:
\begin{equation}
  \phi_n,\quad \ee^{\ii\theta_n}:
  \quad
  \Circle\text{-coherent modal content}.
\end{equation}

Thus the whole operator can be read as:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
  =
  \Lens\text{-project}
  \to
  \Lens/\Nil\text{-window}
  \to
  \Nil\text{-damp}
  \to
  \Lens/\Nil\text{-readmit}
  \to
  \Lens\text{-return}.
  }
\end{equation}

Circle is carried by the coherent spectral content that survives this sequence.

\subsection{Circle--Lens--Nil in the fixed-point realization}
\label{subsec:cln-FP}

In the fixed-point realization,
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3\kappa_n\Delta_{B_n}.
\end{equation}
The triplet interpretation becomes concrete.

Circle is the harmonic and spectral coherence of the compact internal fibers:
\begin{equation}
  \Circle_{\rm FP}
  =
  \text{fiber-harmonic phases, internal spectral closure, coherent zero modes}.
\end{equation}

Lens is the projection onto the joint fiber-harmonic sector:
\begin{equation}
  \Lens_{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}\Pi_{B_2}\Pi_{B_3}.
\end{equation}

Nil is the damping of nonzero internal modes:
\begin{equation}
  \Nil_{\rm FP}
  =
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  \text{fiber-Circle coherence}
  +
  \text{joint-harmonic Lens projection}
  +
  \text{internal-mode Nil damping}.
  }
\end{equation}

This gives the MTT triplet a concrete geometric backbone.

\subsection{The local shadow}
\label{subsec:cln-local-shadow}

When \(\Badm\) has a kernel,
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)=\langle x|\Badm|y\rangle,
\end{equation}
one may read it locally. Fixing a source point \(y=x_0\), the section
\begin{equation}
  x\mapsto\Kadm(x,x_0)
\end{equation}
is a finite coherent response to a localized input.

In the sharp limit,
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)\to\delta(x-y).
\end{equation}
Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Dirac delta}
  =
  \text{sharp local Lens shadow of finite coherent admissibility}.
  }
\end{equation}

The point particle is not a primitive object in this reading. It is the result of taking a finite
coherent kernel below resolution:
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\ll\ell_{\rm res}.
\end{equation}

Thus particle-like localization is a Nil/Lens shadow:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{particle shadow}
  =
  \Lens\text{-localized}
  \;\Nil\text{-surviving}
  \text{ finite kernel event}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{The spectral shadow}
\label{subsec:cln-spectral-shadow}

The same kernel also has a spectral reading:
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y).
\end{equation}
This expression displays retained modes and their coherent phase relations. When those
relative phases contribute to probabilities, interference appears.

For a density matrix \(\rho\) and effect \(E\),
\begin{equation}
  \tr(\rho E)
  =
  \sum_a\rho_{aa}E_{aa}
  +
  \sum_{a\neq b}\rho_{ab}E_{ba}.
\end{equation}
The off-diagonal terms are Circle-coherent interference terms.

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{wave shadow}
  =
  \Circle\text{-coherent spectral reading of the same finite kernel}.
  }
\end{equation}

Thus wave and particle are not separate substances. They are two projection shadows:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Kadm
  \quad
  \begin{cases}
  \text{local Lens/Nil reading}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{particle shadow},\\
  \text{spectral Circle reading}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{wave shadow}.
  \end{cases}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Measurement as triplet interaction}
\label{subsec:measurement-triplet}

Measurement uses all three triplet components.

A measurement context
\begin{equation}
  \Meas
  =
  (A_{\Meas},P_{\Meas},\chi_{\Meas},\tau_{\Meas},
  \{E_i^{(\Meas)}\}_{i\in I},\frakB_{\Meas})
\end{equation}
chooses a Lens decomposition:
\begin{equation}
  P_{\Meas},\quad \{E_i^{(\Meas)}\}.
\end{equation}

It may preserve or suppress Circle coherence:
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}\mapsto D_{ab}^{(\Meas)}\rho_{ab}.
\end{equation}

It stabilizes a Nil survivor record:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{measurement}
  =
  \Lens\text{-context}
  +
  \Circle\text{-coherence preservation or loss}
  +
  \Nil\text{-survivor record}.
  }
\end{equation}

This reading will be developed in the next section.

\subsection{Gauge as Lens with Circle holonomy}
\label{subsec:gauge-triplet-preview}

Gauge theory is mainly Lens structure. A gauge potential is a local representative:
\begin{equation}
  A_\mu\sim A_\mu+\partial_\mu\alpha.
\end{equation}
The physical content is obtained after quotienting.

However, gauge theory also contains Circle structure. A charged system transported around a
loop may acquire the holonomy
\begin{equation}
  \exp\left(\ii q\oint_\gamma A\right).
\end{equation}

Nil appears through admissible charge sectors, photon records, detector events, and damping
of nonphysical or noncoherent modes.

Thus:
\begin{align}
  \Lens_{\rm gauge}
  &: \text{gauge quotient},\\
  \Circle_{\rm gauge}
  &: \text{phase and holonomy},\\
  \Nil_{\rm gauge}
  &: \text{charge sectors and photon records}.
\end{align}

This will later require a gauge-compatible admissibility operator:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gravity as Lens-on-locality}
\label{subsec:gravity-triplet-preview}

Gravity is the deepest Lens sector because the redundancy acts on the representation of
locality itself:
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}\sim\varphi^\ast g_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}

Circle appears as curvature and frame holonomy:
\begin{equation}
  [\nabla_\mu,\nabla_\nu]v^\rho
  =
  R^\rho{}_{\sigma\mu\nu}v^\sigma.
\end{equation}

Nil appears as horizons, singularities, boundaries, and survivor geometries.

Thus:
\begin{align}
  \Lens_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{diffeomorphism quotient and chart gluing},\\
  \Circle_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{curvature and frame holonomy},\\
  \Nil_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{boundary, horizon, singularity, survivor geometry}.
\end{align}

A gravitational admissibility operator must therefore have the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff},
\end{equation}
where \(A_{\rm grav}\) is positive or constraint-compatible on geometric data and is not the
naive Lorentzian d'Alembertian.

\subsection{Quantization as triplet closure}
\label{subsec:quantization-triplet-preview}

Quantization also has a triplet reading.

Circle gives phase closure:
\begin{equation}
  \psi(\theta+2\pi)=\psi(\theta)
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  n\in\mathbb Z.
\end{equation}

Lens gives quotient and representation consistency:
\begin{equation}
  \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_\Sigma F\in\mathbb Z.
\end{equation}

Nil gives survivor labels:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{quantization}
  =
  \Circle\text{-closure}
  +
  \Lens\text{-quotient consistency}
  +
  \Nil\text{-survivor selection}.
  }
\end{equation}

In the FP realization, compact internal spectra provide an additional geometric source of
discrete labels:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_n=\mu_n^2\phi_n.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Entanglement as non-factorizing triplet structure}
\label{subsec:entanglement-triplet-preview}

For a composite system,
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{AB}=\mathcal H_A\otimes\mathcal H_B
\end{equation}
may be only a kinematic starting point. The admissible coherent sector may fail to factor:
\begin{equation}
  P_{AB}\neq P_A\otimes P_B,
\end{equation}
or
\begin{equation}
  B_{AB}\neq B_A\otimes B_B.
\end{equation}

In triplet terms:
\begin{align}
  \Circle &: \text{joint phase coherence},\\
  \Lens &: \text{subsystem decomposition and projection context},\\
  \Nil &: \text{joint or correlated survivor records}.
\end{align}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{entanglement}
  =
  \text{non-factorizable coherent admissibility across a joint Lens decomposition}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Manifest covariance as Lens}
\label{subsec:manifest-covariance-lens}

The Lorentzian implementation of MTT often requires a Cauchy-slice or Hamiltonian
description. Such descriptions may not be manifestly Lorentz covariant at every intermediate
step. This should be understood as a Lens feature, not as a physical violation of relativity.

Gauge theory provides the analogy. Coulomb gauge is not manifestly Lorentz covariant, yet
it can isolate physical transverse degrees of freedom. The loss of manifest covariance in the
representative does not by itself imply a loss of physical covariance.

Similarly, a Cauchy-slice finite kernel may select a Lens representation adapted to a time
foliation. The physical requirement is not manifest covariance of every intermediate formula.
The physical requirements are:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item preservation of the hyperbolic principal symbol;
  \item finite propagation speed;
  \item compatibility with constraints;
  \item foliation-independent or consistently transformed observable content.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{manifest covariance is a representation property;}
  }
\end{equation}
whereas
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{physical covariance is an observable and constraint-level requirement}.
  }
\end{equation}

This distinction is part of the Lens grammar.

\subsection{Triplet failure modes}
\label{subsec:triplet-failure-modes}

The Circle--Lens--Nil reading also identifies failure modes.

Circle failure occurs when the claimed coherent phase structure is not actually preserved:
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}\to0
  \qquad
  (a\neq b).
\end{equation}

Lens failure occurs when the projection context is invalid:
\begin{equation}
  P
  \text{ does not select a physical or admissible sector}.
\end{equation}
In gauge theory this means quotient failure. In gravity it means diffeomorphism or constraint
failure.

Nil failure occurs when damping is not physically admissible:
\begin{equation}
  \rho\mapsto D\circ\rho
\end{equation}
but
\begin{equation}
  D\nsucceq0.
\end{equation}
It also occurs when no survivor basin stabilizes.

Thus the triplet grammar is not decorative. It tells us what must be checked:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Circle:
  \text{coherence},
  \qquad
  \Lens:
  \text{valid projection},
  \qquad
  \Nil:
  \text{admissible damping and survivor selection}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:cln-summary}

The finite coherent admissibility operator
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
has a natural Circle--Lens--Nil reading.

Circle is the coherent spectral and phase content:
\begin{equation}
  \Circle
  =
  \text{phase, return, closure, holonomy, spectral coherence}.
\end{equation}

Lens is the projection and representation structure:
\begin{equation}
  \Lens
  =
  \text{projector, quotient, gauge, coordinate, measurement context}.
\end{equation}

Nil is the damping and survivor structure:
\begin{equation}
  \Nil
  =
  \text{damping, threshold, basin, record, survivor}.
\end{equation}

In the fixed-point realization:
\begin{equation}
  \Circle
  =
  \text{fiber spectral coherence},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
  \Lens
  =
  P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}\Pi_{B_2}\Pi_{B_3},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \Nil
  =
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}.
\end{equation}

The first and most important physical application is wave--particle projection duality:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Kadm
  \quad
  \begin{cases}
  \text{local Lens/Nil reading}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{Dirac delta / particle shadow},\\
  \text{spectral Circle reading}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{wave / interference shadow}.
  \end{cases}
  }
\end{equation}

The next section develops this result in detail.

% ============================================================
\section{Delta, wave, and measurement shadows}
\label{sec:delta-wave-measurement}
% ============================================================

The previous section gave the Circle--Lens--Nil reading of the finite coherent admissibility
operator
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}
This section develops the first physical consequence in detail: particle-like localization,
wave-like interference, and measurement records are three downstream shadows of the same
finite coherent kernel.

The result is not that a quantum object is sometimes literally a point particle and sometimes
literally a wave. The result is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{particle, wave, and measurement record are three projection shadows of finite coherent admissibility.}
  }
  \label{eq:delta-wave-measurement-thesis}
\end{equation}

The particle shadow comes from the local kernel and its sharp Dirac-delta limit. The wave
shadow comes from the spectral expansion and its retained phase coherence. The measurement
shadow comes from finite effects, branch damping, and survivor-basin stabilization.

In the fixed-point realization, these shadows are grounded in the internal/fiber geometry. The
native object is not an ideal point in four-dimensional spacetime. The native object is finite
coherent admissibility over a compact internal/fiber structure, projected into the observed
four-dimensional base.

\subsection{The finite coherent kernel}
\label{subsec:finite-coherent-kernel}

Assume first that \(\Badm\) admits a kernel:
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \langle x|\Badm|y\rangle.
  \label{eq:Kadm-section5}
\end{equation}
Here \(x,y\) denote the variables of the representation being used. In a purely four-dimensional
effective reading, \(x,y\in Y^4\) or on a Cauchy slice \(\Sigma\subset Y^4\). In the full
fibered reading, the variables may include internal/fiber coordinates or may represent a
fiber-projected effective kernel on the base.
