The kernel acts by
\begin{equation}
  (\Badm f)(x)
  =
  \int
  \Kadm(x,y)f(y)\,\dd\mu(y).
  \label{eq:kernel-action-section5}
\end{equation}

The two primary readings are:
\begin{align}
  \text{local reading}
  &: \quad
  x\mapsto\Kadm(x,x_0),
  \\
  \text{spectral reading}
  &: \quad
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y).
\end{align}

The local reading gives the particle/delta shadow. The spectral reading gives the wave
shadow.

In the fixed-point realization,
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}
Thus the finite kernel is generated by:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the joint fiber-harmonic coherent projector \(P_{\rm coh}\);
  \item the positive internal admissibility operator \(A_{\rm int}\);
  \item the spectral window \(\chi(A_{\rm int})\);
  \item the finite damping scale \(\tau\).
\end{enumerate}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Kadm
  =
  \text{finite coherent response kernel generated by internal/fiber admissibility}.
  }
  \label{eq:finite-kernel-generated-by-fiber}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Particle shadow}
\label{subsec:particle-shadow}

A point particle in ordinary idealized language is represented by a Dirac delta:
\begin{equation}
  x\longmapsto \delta(x-x_0).
\end{equation}
This is a zero-width object. It is not square-integrable and is not a finite physical profile.

MTT reverses the explanatory order. The finite coherent kernel section
\begin{equation}
  x\longmapsto\Kadm(x,x_0)
  \label{eq:finite-kernel-section}
\end{equation}
is the native admissible object. The Dirac delta is recovered only as a sharp limit:
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)\to\delta(x-y).
  \label{eq:delta-limit-section5}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{particle}
  =
  \text{local Dirac-delta shadow of finite coherent support}.
  }
  \label{eq:particle-shadow-box-section5}
\end{equation}

This does not mean that a particle is a tiny classical ball. The kernel section
\(\Kadm(x,x_0)\) is a finite coherent response profile. Depending on the sector, it may be a
source kernel, amplitude kernel, detector kernel, or effective local response.

A positive detection probability requires a positive effect. For example, a finite position-sensitive
detector may use
\begin{equation}
  E_x=|k_x\rangle\langle k_x|,
  \qquad
  k_x(y)=K_{\rm det}(y,x).
  \label{eq:finite-position-effect-section5}
\end{equation}
The detector event is then localized by the finite width of \(k_x\), not by a primitive
mathematical point.

\subsection{Resolution-relative pointlikeness}
\label{subsec:resolution-relative-pointlikeness}

Pointlikeness is resolution-relative. Let
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau}
\end{equation}
be the coherent width of the finite kernel, and let
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm res}
\end{equation}
be the detector or probe resolution.

The system appears pointlike when
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\ll\ell_{\rm res}.
  \label{eq:pointlike-resolution-section5}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, with
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2},
\end{equation}
the pointlike approximation holds when
\begin{equation}
  E_{\rm probe}\ll\Lambda_{\rm eff}.
  \label{eq:pointlike-energy-section5}
\end{equation}

In the fixed-point realization, the coherent width is tied to the internal spectral gap:
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}.
  \label{eq:tau-section5}
\end{equation}
If the typical internal radius is \(R\), then
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2},
\end{equation}
so
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{\log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}}.
  \label{eq:lcoh-R-section5}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{pointlike behavior in four dimensions is the below-resolution shadow of finite internal coherent width}.
  }
  \label{eq:pointlike-fiber-shadow}
\end{equation}

This is why high-resolution scattering may probe finite coherent structure, while low-energy
experiments see ordinary pointlike behavior.

\subsection{The Dirac delta as a sharp shadow}
\label{subsec:dirac-delta-sharp-shadow}

The sharp delta limit requires a controlled limiting procedure. In the simplest spectral form,
one takes
\begin{equation}
  P\to I,
  \qquad
  \chi(A)\to I,
  \qquad
  \tau\downarrow0.
  \label{eq:sharp-limit-section5}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\to I,
\end{equation}
and the kernel of the identity is the Dirac delta:
\begin{equation}
  I(x,y)=\delta(x-y).
\end{equation}

In flat Euclidean space, the benchmark example is
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}\right).
  \label{eq:flat-heat-kernel-section5}
\end{equation}
As \(\tau\downarrow0\),
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  \to
  \delta^{(d)}(x-y)
\end{equation}
distributionally.

But in the FP realization the primary damping is not a naive four-dimensional Lorentzian
smearing. The clean physical interpretation is internal/fiber damping:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}},
\end{equation}
with the four-dimensional pointlike shadow emerging after projection and dimensional
reduction.

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \delta
  =
  \text{sharp local shadow, not the native object}.
  }
  \label{eq:delta-shadow-not-native-section5}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Wave shadow}
\label{subsec:wave-shadow}

The same kernel has a spectral representation. In the commuting spectral case,
\begin{equation}
  A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n
  =
  w_n\phi_n,
  \qquad
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y).
  \label{eq:wave-shadow-spectral-section5}
\end{equation}

This expansion is the wave side of the same finite object. It displays:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item which modes survive;
  \item which modes are windowed out;
  \item which modes are damped;
  \item which phase relations remain coherent.
\end{enumerate}

In the FP realization, if
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_n=\mu_n^2\phi_n,
\end{equation}
then the internal modal weights include
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_n^2}.
\end{equation}
The zero mode satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
\end{equation}
and is undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
Nonzero internal modes are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_n^2>0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_n^2}<1.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{wave}
  =
  \text{spectral phase-coherent shadow of finite coherent admissibility}.
  }
  \label{eq:wave-shadow-box-section5}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Off-diagonal coherence}
\label{subsec:offdiagonal-coherence-section5}

Wave-like behavior is operationally visible through off-diagonal coherence. Let
\begin{equation}
  |\psi\rangle
  =
  \sum_a c_a|a\rangle
\end{equation}
be a coherent branch state in a chosen Lens context. Its density matrix is
\begin{equation}
  \rho
  =
  |\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
  =
  \sum_{a,b}
  c_a c_b^\ast
  |a\rangle\langle b|.
\end{equation}

The diagonal terms
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{aa}=|c_a|^2
\end{equation}
are branch weights. The off-diagonal terms
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}=c_a c_b^\ast,
  \qquad
  a\neq b,
\end{equation}
carry relative phase coherence.

For an effect \(E\),
\begin{equation}
  \tr(\rho E)
  =
  \sum_a\rho_{aa}E_{aa}
  +
  \sum_{a\neq b}\rho_{ab}E_{ba}.
  \label{eq:trace-interference-section5}
\end{equation}
The second term is the interference contribution.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{interference}
  =
  \text{observable contribution of off-diagonal coherent-sector terms}.
  }
  \label{eq:interference-offdiagonal-section5}
\end{equation}

When the off-diagonal terms survive, the wave shadow is visible. When they are damped,
the downstream description becomes particle-like or mixture-like relative to the measurement
context.

\subsection{The common-source duality}
\label{subsec:common-source-duality}

The particle and wave readings are not two different underlying objects. They are two
representations of the same finite coherent kernel:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Kadm(x,y)
  \quad
  \begin{cases}
    \text{local reading}
    &\Rightarrow
    \text{Dirac delta / particle shadow},
    \\
    \text{spectral reading}
    &\Rightarrow
    \text{phase-coherent wave shadow}.
  \end{cases}
  }
  \label{eq:common-source-duality-section5}
\end{equation}

This is the core wave--particle projection-duality claim.

In the FP realization, the common source is not merely formal. It is the finite coherent
admissibility structure generated by the internal/fiber operator:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{wave--particle duality}
  =
  \text{two shadows of one finite coherent fiber-generated admissibility kernel}.
  }
  \label{eq:wave-particle-FP-section5}
\end{equation}

This is the central physical result of the paper.

\subsection{Measurement shadow}
\label{subsec:measurement-shadow-section5}

Measurement introduces a detector context. In MTT a measurement is not merely the selection
of an abstract observable. It is a finite physical projection context:
\begin{equation}
  \Meas
  =
  (A_{\Meas},P_{\Meas},\chi_{\Meas},\tau_{\Meas},
  \{E_i^{(\Meas)}\}_{i\in I},\frakB_{\Meas}).
  \label{eq:measurement-context-section5}
\end{equation}

Here:
\begin{align}
  A_{\Meas}
  &: \text{detector-sector stabilization operator},\\
  P_{\Meas}
  &: \text{detector-context projector},\\
  \chi_{\Meas}
  &: \text{detector spectral window},\\
  \tau_{\Meas}
  &: \text{measurement damping or stabilization scale},\\
  E_i^{(\Meas)}
  &: \text{positive detector effects},\\
  \frakB_{\Meas}
  &: \text{survivor-basin partition}.
\end{align}

The measurement-sector finite admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\Meas}
  =
  P_{\Meas}\chi_{\Meas}(A_{\Meas})
  \ee^{-\tau_{\Meas}A_{\Meas}}
  \chi_{\Meas}(A_{\Meas})P_{\Meas}.
  \label{eq:measurement-filter-section5}
\end{equation}

A measurement record is then a downstream survivor-basin event:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi
  \to
  B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{measurement record}
  =
  \text{finite Nil survivor-basin shadow of a detector context}.
  }
  \label{eq:measurement-record-shadow-section5}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Finite detector effects}
\label{subsec:finite-detector-effects-section5}

Detector outcomes are represented by positive effects:
\begin{equation}
  E_i^{(\Meas)}\succeq0.
\end{equation}
For a closed discrete detector,
\begin{equation}
  \sum_i E_i^{(\Meas)}=I_{\rm coh}.
  \label{eq:POVM-normalization-section5}
\end{equation}
For a continuous position-sensitive detector,
\begin{equation}
  \int E_x\,\dd x=I_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}

A finite position-sensitive effect may be written as
\begin{equation}
  E_x=|k_x\rangle\langle k_x|,
  \qquad
  k_x(y)=K_{\rm det}(y,x).
  \label{eq:finite-effect-section5}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  p(x)=\tr(\rho E_x)
\end{equation}
is the detection density.

This shows that localized detection does not require an infinitely sharp position projector as
a primitive. The detector has finite resolution. The sharp PVM limit is recovered only when
the detector kernel becomes infinitely narrow.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{localized detector click}
  =
  \text{finite detector effect captured below resolution}.
  }
  \label{eq:localized-click-section5}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Branch damping}
\label{subsec:branch-damping-section5}

A measurement context may preserve or destroy coherence between branches. In a finite
branch basis, branch damping is represented by a Schur channel:
\begin{equation}
  \rho\mapsto D^{(\Meas)}\circ\rho,
  \label{eq:schur-damping-section5}
\end{equation}
or entrywise,
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}\mapsto D_{ab}^{(\Meas)}\rho_{ab}.
\end{equation}

For this to be a valid quantum channel, the damping matrix must satisfy
\begin{equation}
  D^{(\Meas)}\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}^{(\Meas)}=1.
  \label{eq:D-positive-section5}
\end{equation}
Then \(\rho\mapsto D^{(\Meas)}\circ\rho\) is completely positive and trace preserving.

In a two-branch experiment,
\begin{equation}
  D
  =
  \begin{pmatrix}
    1 & d\\
    d^\ast & 1
  \end{pmatrix},
  \qquad
  |d|\le1.
\end{equation}
The channel acts as
\begin{equation}
  \begin{pmatrix}
    \rho_{11} & \rho_{12}\\
    \rho_{21} & \rho_{22}
  \end{pmatrix}
  \mapsto
  \begin{pmatrix}
    \rho_{11} & d\rho_{12}\\
    d^\ast\rho_{21} & \rho_{22}
  \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}

For real \(d\in[0,1]\), the visibility law is
\begin{equation}
  V_{\rm MTT}=dV_0.
  \label{eq:visibility-law-section5}
\end{equation}

More generally, one may use an exponential form
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}^{(\Meas)}
  =
  \ee^{-\tau_{\Meas}\Lambda_{ab}^{(\Meas)}},
  \label{eq:exponential-damping-section5}
\end{equation}
provided \(\Lambda^{(\Meas)}\) satisfies the appropriate conditional negative-definiteness
condition that makes \(D^{(\Meas)}\) positive semidefinite.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{visibility loss}
  =
  \text{Nil damping of off-diagonal Circle coherence}.
  }
  \label{eq:visibility-loss-section5}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Survivor-basin records}
\label{subsec:survivor-basin-records-section5}

Branch damping is not the same as a definite record. A record requires survivor-basin
stabilization.

The measurement chain is:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi
  \xrightarrow{\text{device coupling}}
  \Psi'
  \xrightarrow{\text{finite effects}}
  \rho'
  \xrightarrow{\text{branch damping}}
  D^{(\Meas)}\circ\rho'
  \xrightarrow{\text{basin capture}}
  B_i^{(\Meas)}.
  \label{eq:measurement-chain-section5}
\end{equation}

The survivor-basin partition is
\begin{equation}
  \frakB_{\Meas}
  =
  \{B_i^{(\Meas)}\}_{i\in I}.
\end{equation}
An outcome is exact relative to the measurement context when the downstream state is
captured by one basin:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{collapse shadow}
  =
  \text{branch damping plus survivor-basin selection}.
  }
  \label{eq:collapse-shadow-section5}
\end{equation}

This avoids treating collapse as a primitive conversion of a wave into a particle. It is a
finite downstream transition from coherent branch structure to stabilized record.

\subsection{Born weights versus visibility damping}
\label{subsec:born-vs-visibility-section5}

MTT separates outcome probabilities from interference visibility.

Visibility damping answers:
\begin{equation}
  \text{How much off-diagonal coherence remains?}
\end{equation}
It is controlled by
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Outcome probability answers:
\begin{equation}
  \text{Which record occurs, and how often?}
\end{equation}
In the basin-measure account, if \(\mu\) is the admissibility-weighted measure on the
measurement ensemble, then
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(i)
  =
  \frac{\mu(B_i^{(\Meas)})}
  {\sum_j\mu(B_j^{(\Meas)})}.
  \label{eq:basin-probability-section5}
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{branch damping controls visibility;}
  }
\end{equation}
whereas
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{basin measure controls outcome frequency.}
  }
\end{equation}

This distinction is essential. A detector may completely suppress interference while leaving
the diagonal branch weights unchanged. The eventual frequencies require the basin-measure
layer.

\subsection{Double slit}
\label{subsec:double-slit-section5}

The double-slit experiment is the canonical example. Let the two path branches be
\begin{equation}
  |1\rangle,\qquad |2\rangle.
\end{equation}
After the slits, a coherent state has the form
\begin{equation}
  |\psi\rangle
  =
  \alpha|\psi_1\rangle+\beta|\psi_2\rangle.
  \label{eq:double-slit-state-section5}
\end{equation}
The density matrix contains the off-diagonal terms
\begin{equation}
  \alpha\beta^\ast|\psi_1\rangle\langle\psi_2|
  +
  \alpha^\ast\beta|\psi_2\rangle\langle\psi_1|.
\end{equation}

A finite screen effect \(E_x\) gives detection density
\begin{align}
  I(x)
  &=
  |\alpha|^2\langle\psi_1|E_x|\psi_1\rangle
  +
  |\beta|^2\langle\psi_2|E_x|\psi_2\rangle
  \nonumber\\
  &\quad+
  2\operatorname{Re}
  \left[
    \alpha\beta^\ast
    \langle\psi_2|E_x|\psi_1\rangle
  \right].
  \label{eq:double-slit-intensity-section5}
\end{align}
The final term is interference.

If a which-way detector damps the off-diagonal branch coherence, then
\begin{align}
  I_{\Meas}(x)
  &=
  |\alpha|^2\langle\psi_1|E_x|\psi_1\rangle
  +
  |\beta|^2\langle\psi_2|E_x|\psi_2\rangle
  \nonumber\\
  &\quad+
  2D_{12}^{(\Meas)}
  \operatorname{Re}
  \left[
    \alpha\beta^\ast
    \langle\psi_2|E_x|\psi_1\rangle
  \right].
  \label{eq:double-slit-damped-section5}
\end{align}

Thus:
\begin{align}
  D_{12}^{(\Meas)}\approx1
  &\quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \text{interference visible},
  \\
  D_{12}^{(\Meas)}\approx0
  &\quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \text{which-way record dominates}.
\end{align}

Each screen event is localized by a finite detector effect. Across many trials, the ensemble
distribution shows or loses interference depending on branch coherence.

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{double-slit behavior}
  =
  \text{finite detector records plus retained or damped branch coherence}.
  }
  \label{eq:double-slit-summary-section5}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Measurement devices disturb differently}
\label{subsec:device-disturbance-section5}

Different measurement methods disturb different branch structures. This is not an experimental
loophole. It is part of the MTT architecture.

A measurement context is not only an observable label. It contains concrete device data:
\begin{equation}
  \Meas
  =
  (A_{\Meas},P_{\Meas},\chi_{\Meas},\tau_{\Meas},
  \{E_i^{(\Meas)}\},\frakB_{\Meas}).
\end{equation}
Two devices that nominally measure the same branch alternative may still have different
damping matrices:
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}^{(\Meas_1)}
  \neq
  D_{ab}^{(\Meas_2)}.
\end{equation}
They may also have different detector effects:
\begin{equation}
  E_i^{(\Meas_1)}
  \neq
  E_i^{(\Meas_2)},
\end{equation}
and different survivor-basin partitions:
\begin{equation}
  \frakB_{\Meas_1}
  \neq
  \frakB_{\Meas_2}.
\end{equation}

Thus a weak, reversible marker may reduce visibility only partially, while a strong
macroscopic detector may stabilize a durable which-way record:
\begin{align}
  \text{weak marker}
  &: \quad
  0<D_{12}<1,
  \\
  \text{strong which-way detector}
  &: \quad
  D_{12}\approx0.
\end{align}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the measurement device is part of the projection context}.
  }
  \label{eq:device-context-section5}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Other benchmark experiments}
\label{subsec:other-benchmark-experiments-section5}

The same pattern appears beyond the double slit.

\paragraph{Mach--Zehnder interferometer.}
The two arms form a branch basis. If path coherence survives recombination, interference
appears at the output ports. If which-path information is marked, the off-diagonal terms are
damped:
\begin{equation}
  V_{\rm MZ}=D_{12}^{(\rm MZ)}V_0.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Stern--Gerlach experiment.}
The magnet orientation \(\hat n\) defines a Lens context:
\begin{equation}
  \{|\uparrow_{\hat n}\rangle,|\downarrow_{\hat n}\rangle\}.
\end{equation}
The apparatus couples these branches to spatially separated detector basins. The outcome is
exact relative to \(\hat n\), not a context-free pre-existing classical spin value.

\paragraph{Delayed-choice and quantum eraser arrangements.}
The final measurement context determines which branch decomposition is stabilized. Conditional
interference may reappear when marker degrees of freedom are projected into a recombining
basis. This does not require retroactive alteration of a past path. It reflects a change in Lens
decomposition and Nil survivor partition.

\paragraph{Matter-wave diffraction.}
Massive particles produce interference when coherent branch structure survives. Individual
detections remain localized finite records. Increasing mass or complexity suppresses
interference only when environmental or detector coupling damps off-diagonal coherence.

\paragraph{Hong--Ou--Mandel interference.}
The relevant branches are multi-particle alternatives. Indistinguishable alternatives retain
joint coherence; distinguishability damps the off-diagonal terms responsible for the interference
dip.

All these experiments follow:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{coherent branches}
  \to
  \text{measurement context}
  \to
  \text{branch damping or recombination}
  \to
  \text{survivor records}.
  }
  \label{eq:benchmark-pattern-section5}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Connection to internal/fiber geometry}
\label{subsec:connection-fiber-geometry-section5}

The wave--particle result can be stated abstractly, but the FP realization gives it a geometric
source.

The finite coherent kernel is generated by internal admissibility:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}
The observed four-dimensional shadows arise after projecting this finite coherent structure
onto the base \(Y^4\), detector contexts, and effective sectoral descriptions.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{four-dimensional pointlike events are local projection shadows of finite fiber-coherent
  admissibility}.
  }
\end{equation}
And:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{four-dimensional wave interference is the spectral phase shadow of the same admissible
  coherent sector}.
  }
\end{equation}

This is the common-source claim in its strongest form:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Dirac delta and wave behavior have one finite geometric source.}
  }
  \label{eq:delta-wave-one-source-section5}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:delta-wave-measurement-summary}

The finite coherent admissibility operator
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
has three immediate physical shadows.

First, the local kernel gives the particle shadow:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{particle}
  =
  \text{local Dirac-delta shadow of finite coherent support}.
  }
\end{equation}

Second, the spectral expansion gives the wave shadow:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{wave}
  =
  \text{spectral phase-coherent shadow of finite coherent support}.
  }
\end{equation}

Third, finite detector effects, branch damping, and survivor basins give the measurement
shadow:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{measurement record}
  =
  \text{finite survivor-basin shadow}.
  }
\end{equation}

Together:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{wave, particle, and measurement are three projection shadows of finite coherent
  admissibility}.
  }
\end{equation}

In the fixed-point realization, this finite coherent admissibility is generated by compact
internal/fiber geometry:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
  }
\end{equation}

Therefore the Dirac delta and the wave are not rival primitives. They are the first two
observable shadows of the same finite coherent fiber-generated kernel.

% ============================================================
\section{Scattering, finite propagators, and fiber damping}
\label{sec:scattering}
% ============================================================

The previous section established the first three projection shadows of finite coherent
admissibility: the local Dirac-delta shadow, the spectral wave shadow, and the measurement
record shadow. This section applies the same structure to propagation and scattering.

The main point is subtle but important. In a flat Euclidean benchmark, the finite coherent
operator produces the familiar Gaussian momentum factor
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}.
\end{equation}
However, in the fixed-point realization of MTT this should not be confused with a fundamental
Lorentzian damping factor
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}.
\end{equation}
The default physical interpretation is instead fiber damping:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}},
\end{equation}
where \(A_{\rm int}\) is a positive internal elliptic operator. After dimensional reduction,
this gives ordinary four-dimensional zero-mode propagation plus damped massive internal
or Kaluza--Klein-like excitations.

Thus the central message of this section is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite scattering corrections arise from admissible finite kernels, internal-mode
  damping, and finite overlaps, not from naive Lorentzian } \ee^{-\tau\Box}.
  }
  \label{eq:scattering-main-message}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Euclidean scalar benchmark}
\label{subsec:euclidean-scalar-benchmark}

The cleanest worked example is the Euclidean scalar heat-kernel model. Let
\begin{equation}
  A=-\Delta
\end{equation}
on \(\mathbb R^d\), with
\begin{equation}
  P=I,
  \qquad
  \chi=1.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  \ee^{-\tau A}
  =
  \ee^{\tau\Delta}.
\end{equation}

The Fourier modes obey
\begin{equation}
  -\Delta \ee^{\ii k\cdot x}
  =
  k^2\ee^{\ii k\cdot x}.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}\ee^{\ii k\cdot x}
  =
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}\ee^{\ii k\cdot x}.
  \label{eq:euclidean-momentum-damping-section6}
\end{equation}

The heat kernel is
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  \frac{\dd^dk}{(2\pi)^d}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
  \ee^{\ii k\cdot(x-y)}.
  \label{eq:euclidean-heat-fourier-section6}
\end{equation}
Evaluating the Gaussian integral gives
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
  \label{eq:euclidean-heat-kernel-section6}
\end{equation}

As \(\tau\downarrow0\),
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  \to
  \delta^{(d)}(x-y)
\end{equation}
distributionally.

Thus the Euclidean scalar benchmark gives:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \delta^{(d)}(x-y)
  \rightsquigarrow
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right),
  }
  \label{eq:delta-to-gaussian-section6}
\end{equation}
and, in momentum space,
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  1
  \rightsquigarrow
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}.
  }
  \label{eq:one-to-gaussian-momentum-section6}
\end{equation}

This benchmark is mathematically useful because it gives a closed model of finite coherent
width, delta recovery, and high-mode damping.

\subsection{Finite scalar propagator shadow}
\label{subsec:finite-scalar-propagator-shadow}

The ordinary Euclidean scalar propagator is
\begin{equation}
  \Delta(k)
  =
  \frac{1}{k^2+m^2}.
  \label{eq:ordinary-euclidean-propagator-section6}
\end{equation}
Inserting one finite coherent heat-kernel factor gives the finite propagator shadow
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}(k)
  =
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2+m^2}.
  \label{eq:finite-euclidean-propagator-section6}
\end{equation}

In position space,
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}(x-y)
  =
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  \frac{\dd^dk}{(2\pi)^d}
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2+m^2}
  \ee^{\ii k\cdot(x-y)}.
  \label{eq:finite-position-propagator-section6}
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}
  =
  K_\tau^{(d)}\ast\Delta
\end{equation}
in the distributional sense.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite propagator shadow}
  =
  \text{ordinary propagator convolved with finite coherent kernel}.
  }
  \label{eq:finite-propagator-convolution-section6}
\end{equation}

For small \(\tau k^2\),
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
  =
  1-\tau k^2+\frac12\tau^2k^4+O(\tau^3k^6).
  \label{eq:gaussian-expansion-section6}
\end{equation}
Therefore the leading correction to the propagator is controlled by
\begin{equation}
  \tau k^2.
\end{equation}
At low momentum,
\begin{equation}
  \tau k^2\ll1,
\end{equation}
ordinary pointlike propagation is recovered.

At high momentum,
\begin{equation}
  \tau k^2\gtrsim1,
\end{equation}
the finite coherent structure becomes resolvable.

\subsection{Dimensional scale}
\label{subsec:scattering-dimensional-scale}

For a Laplace-type operator,
\begin{equation}
  [A]=L^{-2}=E^2.
\end{equation}
The damping factor
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}
\end{equation}
is dimensionless only if
\begin{equation}
  [\tau]=L^2=E^{-2}.
\end{equation}

The coherent length scale is
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau},
  \label{eq:scattering-lcoh-section6}
\end{equation}
and the corresponding effective energy scale is
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2}.
  \label{eq:scattering-Lambda-section6}
\end{equation}

The finite-width expansion parameter is
\begin{equation}
  \tau E_{\rm probe}^2
  =
  \left(
    \frac{E_{\rm probe}}{\Lambda_{\rm eff}}
  \right)^2.
  \label{eq:scattering-expansion-parameter-section6}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  E_{\rm probe}\ll\Lambda_{\rm eff}
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \text{pointlike effective behavior}.
  }
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  E_{\rm probe}\sim\Lambda_{\rm eff}
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \text{finite coherent structure may become observable}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Flat four-dimensional Euclidean case}
\label{subsec:flat-four-dimensional-euclidean}

For \(d=4\), the Euclidean heat kernel becomes
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(4)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
  \label{eq:4d-heat-kernel-section6}
\end{equation}
Its Fourier transform is
\begin{equation}
  \widehat K_\tau^{(4)}(k)=\ee^{-\tau k^2}.
\end{equation}

The finite Euclidean scalar propagator shadow is
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}^{(4)}(k)
  =
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2+m^2}.
  \label{eq:4d-finite-propagator-section6}
\end{equation}

This expression is useful as a benchmark because all pieces can be computed explicitly. It
will be elevated to a complete benchmark theorem later in the paper.

However, it must be interpreted carefully. Equation
\eqref{eq:4d-finite-propagator-section6} is a Euclidean scalar model. It is not the default
Lorentzian MTT prescription.

\begin{remark}[Euclidean benchmark versus Lorentzian physics]
\label{rem:euclidean-benchmark-lorentzian}
The factor \(\ee^{-\tau k^2}\) is clean when \(k^2\ge0\), as in Euclidean signature or positive
spectral charts. In Lorentzian signature, replacing \(k^2\) by the indefinite invariant
associated with \(\Box\) is not automatically causal, unitary, or admissible. The physical MTT
implementation uses positive fiber, spatial, Hamiltonian, or constraint-compatible operators.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Why \(e^{-\tau\Box}\) is not the fundamental object}
\label{subsec:not-exp-box-section6}

The Lorentzian d'Alembertian is indefinite. In flat spacetime, depending on sign convention,
\begin{equation}
  \Box
  =
  -\partial_t^2+\nabla^2
\end{equation}
or the opposite sign. Its Fourier symbol is not nonnegative. Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}
\end{equation}
is not a heat-kernel damping factor in the same sense as
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{\tau\Delta}
\end{equation}
on Euclidean space.

Naively inserting
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}
\end{equation}
can threaten:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item causality;
  \item unitarity;
  \item pole structure;
  \item reflection positivity;
  \item gauge identities;
  \item constraint preservation.
\end{enumerate}

MTT therefore imposes the Lorentzian admissibility principle:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the damping operator must be positive in the admissible physical sector}.
  }
  \label{eq:lorentzian-admissibility-principle-section6}
\end{equation}

In the fixed-point realization this means:
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int},
  \qquad
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}

In Cauchy-slice formulations this means:
\begin{equation}
  A=A_\Sigma,
  \qquad
  A_\Sigma\ge0
\end{equation}
on a spatial slice or constrained physical data.

In gauge theory this means:
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm gauge}
\end{equation}
after quotienting or in a gauge-covariant physical sector.

In gravity this means:
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm grav}
\end{equation}
on diffeomorphism-compatible or constraint-compatible geometric data.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A\neq\Box
  \quad
  \text{unless a positive physical-sector replacement has been constructed}.
  }
  \label{eq:A-not-box-section6}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Fiber damping in the fixed-point realization}
\label{subsec:fiber-damping-section6}

In the fixed-point realization, the admissibility operator is the positive internal operator
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3
  \kappa_n\Delta_{B_n},
  \qquad
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}\phi_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\phi_j.
  \label{eq:fiber-damping-eigenmode-section6}
\end{equation}

The zero mode satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
\end{equation}
and is undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}

A nonzero internal mode satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0,
\end{equation}
and is damped:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT damping suppresses internal nonzero modes while leaving coherent zero modes
  undamped.}
  }
  \label{eq:fiber-damping-summary-section6}
\end{equation}

This is the clean physical replacement for naive Lorentzian smoothing.

\subsection{Dimensional reduction and effective four-dimensional propagation}
\label{subsec:dimensional-reduction-section6}

After dimensional reduction, internal eigenmodes appear as effective four-dimensional fields.
Schematically, a field on the total space may be expanded as
\begin{equation}
  \Phi(x,z)
  =
  \sum_j
  \varphi_j(x)\phi_j(z),
  \label{eq:KK-expansion-section6}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  x\in Y^4,
  \qquad
  z\in X^6.
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
\end{equation}
then the internal eigenvalue contributes to the effective four-dimensional mass scale:
\begin{equation}
  m_j^2
  =
  m_0^2+\mu_j^2
\end{equation}
in the simplest scalar model.

The finite admissibility factor assigns weight
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
\end{equation}
to the \(j\)-th internal mode. Thus the effective four-dimensional propagator has schematic
Källén--Lehmann-like form
\begin{equation}
  G_{4D}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{Z_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}}
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
  \label{eq:4D-effective-propagator-section6}
\end{equation}
Here the residues \(Z_j\) depend on the internal geometry, wavefunction overlap, and sectoral
normalization.

If
\begin{equation}
  Z_j\ge0
\end{equation}
and the internal sector is ghost-free, then the spectral representation is positive. Positivity
is therefore not automatic for every formal sector; it is a consistency requirement of the
sectoral realization.

The zero mode has
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
\end{equation}
so its finite admissibility weight is
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{ordinary four-dimensional low-energy propagation is recovered from undamped zero modes.}
  }
  \label{eq:zero-mode-recovery-section6}
\end{equation}

The massive internal modes are suppressed by
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}
Therefore finite coherent corrections appear as suppressed internal-tower contributions,
threshold effects, loop effects, finite overlaps, or detector-dependent form factors.

\subsection{Fiber damping versus base damping}
\label{subsec:fiber-versus-base-damping-section6}

One may formally decompose a total Euclidean momentum as
\begin{equation}
  k^2=k_{\rm base}^2+k_{\rm fiber}^2.
\end{equation}
A total Euclidean Gaussian factor would then split as
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
  =
  \ee^{-\tau k_{\rm base}^2}
  \ee^{-\tau k_{\rm fiber}^2}.
\end{equation}

However, this is not the default Lorentzian FP/MTT interpretation. The clean fixed-point
choice is fiber damping:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k_{\rm fiber}^2}
  \quad
  \text{or spectrally}
  \quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

The base dynamics remain Lorentzian and are treated by standard hyperbolic or Hamiltonian
evolution. There is no fundamental base factor
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k_{\rm base}^2}
\end{equation}
unless a positive spatial/Cauchy-slice operator is explicitly introduced and justified.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the default MTT finite filter is internal/fiber damping, not four-dimensional Lorentzian
  momentum damping.}
  }
  \label{eq:fiber-not-base-section6}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Contact interactions as finite overlaps}
\label{subsec:contact-interactions-section6}

Pointlike quantum field theory often uses local contact interactions. For example, a scalar
quartic interaction is written as
\begin{equation}
  S_{\rm int}
  =
  \frac{\lambda}{4!}
  \int
  \phi(x)^4\,\dd^dx.
  \label{eq:local-contact-section6}
\end{equation}
This expression assumes exact coincidence at one spacetime point.

In finite coherent admissibility, exact coincidence is replaced by finite coherent overlap.
A schematic finite version is
\begin{equation}
  S_{\rm int}^{\rm adm}
  =
  \frac{\lambda}{4!}
  \int
  \prod_{i=1}^4\dd^dx_i\,
  K_\tau(x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4)
  \phi(x_1)\phi(x_2)\phi(x_3)\phi(x_4),
  \label{eq:finite-contact-section6}
\end{equation}
where \(K_\tau\) is sharply peaked near coincidence but finite.

In the sharp limit,
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau(x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4)
  \to
  \delta(x_1-x_2)\delta(x_1-x_3)\delta(x_1-x_4),
\end{equation}
and the local contact interaction is recovered.

In the FP/fiber realization, the finite overlap may arise from internal wavefunction overlap:
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_{ijkl}^{\rm eff}
  =
  \lambda_{10D}
  \int_{X^6}
  \phi_i(z)\phi_j(z)\phi_k(z)\phi_l(z)\,\dd z
\end{equation}
together with admissibility weights
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_i^2},
  \quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2},
  \quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_k^2},
  \quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_l^2}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{local contact}
  =
  \text{sharp shadow of finite coherent overlap}.
  }
  \label{eq:contact-sharp-shadow-section6}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Scattering amplitudes}
\label{subsec:scattering-amplitudes-section6}

In a scalar Euclidean benchmark, a tree-level exchange amplitude may contain
\begin{equation}
  \frac{1}{k^2+m^2}.
\end{equation}
A finite coherent insertion gives
\begin{equation}
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2+m^2}.
\end{equation}
At low momentum,
\begin{equation}
  \tau k^2\ll1,
\end{equation}
the standard amplitude is recovered.

In the FP/fiber realization, the four-dimensional amplitude instead receives a tower of
internal-mode contributions:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal A_{4D}(p)
  \sim
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  g_j^2
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}}
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
  \label{eq:fiber-scattering-amplitude-section6}
\end{equation}
Here \(g_j\) is an effective coupling determined by internal wavefunction overlap.

The zero mode contributes with no internal damping:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
The higher internal modes contribute with suppressed weights:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent scattering corrections are suppressed internal-mode, overlap, or
  threshold effects.}
  }
  \label{eq:finite-scattering-corrections-section6}
\end{equation}

This is the physically safe way to connect MTT damping to observed four-dimensional
scattering.

\subsection{Amplitude weights and cross-section caution}
\label{subsec:cross-section-caution-section6}

If the first nonzero internal eigenvalue is
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1^2\sim\lambda_\ast,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \tau=\lambda_\ast^{-1}\log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
\end{equation}
then its admissibility weight is
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_1^2}
  \sim
  \ee^{-\log(C_Q/\epsadm)}
  =
  \frac{\epsadm}{C_Q}.
  \label{eq:first-mode-weight-section6}
\end{equation}
This gives a clean relationship between the first internal-mode amplitude weight and the
admissibility tolerance.

However, an observable cross section depends on more than this single factor. It depends on:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item production couplings;
  \item decay couplings;
  \item resonance widths;
  \item phase space;
  \item internal wavefunction overlaps;
  \item gauge and representation factors;
  \item interference with Standard Model backgrounds.
\end{enumerate}

Therefore the paper does not assert a universal numerical cross-section suppression such as
\(\epsadm\) or \(\epsadm^2\). The safe claim is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the \(j\)-th internal excitation carries an amplitude-level admissibility weight }
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
  }
  \label{eq:amplitude-level-weight-section6}
\end{equation}

The observable collider prediction must be computed from the derived four-dimensional
effective action.

\subsection{Bounds from pointlike scattering}
\label{subsec:scattering-bounds-section6}

In the Euclidean scalar benchmark, if no deviation from a pointlike amplitude is observed up
to energy \(E_{\max}\) with tolerance \(\eta\), the leading estimate is
\begin{equation}
  \tau E_{\max}^2\lesssim\eta.
  \label{eq:scalar-scattering-bound-section6}
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}
  \gtrsim
  \frac{E_{\max}}{\sqrt{\eta}}.
  \label{eq:Lambda-bound-section6}
\end{equation}

In the FP/fiber realization, the corresponding bound is on the internal scale:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1\gtrsim E_{\rm threshold},
\end{equation}
up to coupling, representation, and damping effects. Since
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1\sim R^{-1},
\end{equation}
this translates into
\begin{equation}
  R\lesssim E_{\rm threshold}^{-1}.
\end{equation}

The coherence scale is then
\begin{equation}
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{\log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{scattering constrains the finite coherent internal length scale.}
  }
  \label{eq:scattering-constrains-length-section6}
\end{equation}

Precise numerical bounds require specifying the internal geometry, gauge embedding, matter
representations, and effective couplings.

\subsection{Loop regularization and open tasks}
\label{subsec:loop-regularization-section6}

The damping factor
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
\end{equation}
suppresses high internal modes. This suggests a natural finite-mode control mechanism in
loop sums over internal excitations:
\begin{equation}
  \sum_j
  F_j
  \quad
  \rightsquigarrow
  \quad
  \sum_j
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}F_j.
  \label{eq:loop-sum-damping-section6}
\end{equation}

However, this does not automatically complete renormalization. One must still prove:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item compatibility with gauge identities;
  \item unitarity of the reduced theory;
  \item positivity of the spectral representation;
  \item stability under radiative corrections;
  \item correct low-energy effective matching;
  \item anomaly cancellation in chiral sectors;
  \item preservation of Lorentzian causal propagation.
\end{enumerate}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{internal damping is a candidate ultraviolet-control mechanism, not a completed
  renormalization theorem by itself.}
  }
  \label{eq:not-renormalization-theorem-section6}
\end{equation}

This is an execution task for sector-specific MTT field theory.

\subsection{Gauge-sector caution}
\label{subsec:scattering-gauge-caution}

Gauge scattering cannot be modified by inserting damping factors arbitrarily. A gauge-sector
finite propagator must be constructed from physical or quotient-compatible data:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys}.
  \label{eq:gauge-Badm-preview-section6}
\end{equation}

For Abelian conserved-current exchange, a schematic transverse form may be
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal A_{\rm adm}(k)
  \sim
  e^2J_\mu(k)
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2}
  P_T^{\mu\nu}(k)
  J'_\nu(-k),
\end{equation}
but this is safe only in a positive Euclidean or physical transverse chart. In the FP/fiber
realization, the safer statement is that internal gauge excitations carry weights
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
\end{equation}
after gauge quotienting.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{filter after quotienting, or filter covariantly before quotienting}.
  }
  \label{eq:gauge-safe-rule-preview-section6}
\end{equation}

This rule will be developed in the gauge section.

\subsection{Gravity-sector caution}
\label{subsec:scattering-gravity-caution}

Gravity requires even more care. A gravitational finite filter cannot simply damp all metric
components. Metric components include diffeomorphism redundancy.

The schematic form is
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
  \label{eq:grav-Badm-preview-section6}
\end{equation}
Here \(A_{\rm grav}\) must be positive or constraint-compatible on geometric data, and
\(P_{\rm diff}\) must respect diffeomorphism equivalence or the gravitational constraint
surface.

In weak-field settings, \(A_{\rm grav}\) may be a positive spatial or transverse-traceless
operator on Cauchy-slice data. In full gravity, this is a much harder nonperturbative
construction.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravitational damping must act on diffeomorphism-compatible geometry, not coordinate
  components.}
  }
  \label{eq:gravity-safe-rule-preview-section6}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Scattering as a projection shadow}
\label{subsec:scattering-projection-shadow}

The finite coherent interpretation of scattering can now be summarized.

In the Euclidean scalar benchmark:
\begin{equation}
  \frac{1}{k^2+m^2}
  \quad
  \rightsquigarrow
  \quad
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2+m^2}.
\end{equation}

In the FP/fiber realization:
\begin{equation}
  \frac{1}{p^2-m_0^2+\ii\epsilon}
  \quad
  \rightsquigarrow
  \quad
  \sum_j
  \frac{Z_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}}
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
\end{equation}

In contact interactions:
\begin{equation}
  \delta\text{-coincidence}
  \quad
  \rightsquigarrow
  \quad
  \text{finite coherent overlap}.
\end{equation}

In all cases:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{pointlike scattering}
  =
  \text{sharp or low-resolution shadow of finite coherent admissibility}.
  }
  \label{eq:pointlike-scattering-shadow-section6}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:scattering-summary}

This section separated three different ideas that must not be conflated.

First, the Euclidean scalar benchmark gives the clean Gaussian formula:
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \widehat K_\tau(k)=\ee^{-\tau k^2}.
\end{equation}

Second, the FP realization uses positive internal fiber damping:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}\phi_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\phi_j.
\end{equation}
The zero mode is undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
Massive internal modes are damped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
\end{equation}

Third, Lorentzian MTT does not use naive
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}
\end{equation}
as its fundamental damping operator. The admissibility operator must be positive in the
physical sector:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A=A_{\rm int},
  \quad
  A=A_\Sigma,
  \quad
  A=A_{\rm gauge},
  \quad
  A=A_{\rm grav},
  \quad
  \text{or another positive constraint-compatible operator}.
  }
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite scattering corrections are projection shadows of positive coherent
  admissibility, not arbitrary Lorentzian nonlocality}.
  }
\end{equation}
% ============================================================
\section{Cauchy-slice kernels and Lorentzian admissibility}
\label{sec:cauchy-locality}
% ============================================================

The previous section separated the Euclidean scalar benchmark from the physical Lorentzian
implementation of MTT. The distinction is essential. The factor
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
\end{equation}
is mathematically clean in Euclidean or positive spectral settings, but the naive Lorentzian
expression
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}
\end{equation}
is not the fundamental MTT prescription.

This section gives the Lorentzian replacement. In Lorentzian sectors, finite coherent
admissibility is implemented through positive internal operators, positive spatial operators
on Cauchy slices, Hamiltonian or constraint-compatible operators, or equal-time kernels that
enter as lower-order bounded terms without changing the hyperbolic principal symbol.

The guiding principle is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Lorentzian MTT uses positive admissibility data on physical sectors, not indefinite
  spacetime heat flow.}
  }
  \label{eq:lorentzian-principle-section7}
\end{equation}

This preserves the central finite-kernel logic while avoiding the usual pathologies of
nonlocal Lorentzian exponential operators.

\subsection{The Lorentzian divide}
\label{subsec:lorentzian-divide}

Euclidean heat flow is generated by a positive elliptic operator:
\begin{equation}
  A=-\Delta\ge0.
\end{equation}
The heat operator
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}=\ee^{\tau\Delta}
\end{equation}
is smoothing, bounded, and contractive on suitable spaces.

Lorentzian wave propagation is different. The d'Alembertian
\begin{equation}
  \Box
\end{equation}
is hyperbolic and indefinite. It is not a positive elliptic operator. Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}
\end{equation}
is not a heat operator in the same sense.

A naive Lorentzian exponential can introduce:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item acausal tails;
  \item uncontrolled infinite time derivatives;
  \item ghost-like poles or extra degrees of freedom;
  \item loss of unitarity;
  \item ambiguity in analytic continuation;
  \item violation of gauge or gravitational constraints.
\end{enumerate}

MTT therefore rejects the naive identification
\begin{equation}
  A=\Box
\end{equation}
as the fundamental damping rule.

Instead:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A
  \text{ must be positive or constraint-compatible in the admissible sector.}
  }
  \label{eq:A-positive-sector-section7}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Positive admissibility operators}
\label{subsec:positive-admissibility-operators}

The allowed Lorentzian implementations include the following.

\paragraph{Internal/fiber operators.}
In the fixed-point realization,
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3\kappa_n\Delta_{B_n},
  \qquad
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}
This is the cleanest case. Damping acts on compact internal fiber modes:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}\phi_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\phi_j.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Spatial Cauchy-slice operators.}
On a globally hyperbolic spacetime, choose a Cauchy slice \(\Sigma_t\). A positive spatial
elliptic operator
\begin{equation}
  A_\Sigma\ge0
\end{equation}
may act on data on \(\Sigma_t\). For example, in a flat spatial chart,
\begin{equation}
  A_\Sigma=-\Delta_\Sigma.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Hamiltonian or energy operators.}
In a canonical formulation, one may use a positive Hamiltonian, positive energy operator, or
constraint-compatible quadratic form where such an object is available.

\paragraph{Gauge-quotiented operators.}
In gauge theory, the operator must act on gauge-admissible physical content:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys}.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Diffeomorphism-compatible operators.}
In gravity, the operator must act on geometric or constraint-compatible data:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
\end{equation}

Thus the Lorentzian admissibility rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{damp only positive admissible directions, not arbitrary Lorentzian spacetime
  directions.}
  }
  \label{eq:damp-positive-directions-section7}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Global hyperbolicity and Cauchy slices}
\label{subsec:global-hyperbolicity-cauchy}

Let \(Y^4\) be globally hyperbolic. Then it admits a foliation by Cauchy slices
\begin{equation}
  \Sigma_t,
\end{equation}
so that
\begin{equation}
  Y^4
  \cong
  \mathbb R\times\Sigma.
\end{equation}
A field \(\phi\) can be represented by Cauchy data
\begin{equation}
  \left(\phi|_{\Sigma_t},\,n^\mu\nabla_\mu\phi|_{\Sigma_t}\right),
\end{equation}
where \(n^\mu\) is the future-directed unit normal to the slice.

A hyperbolic equation has finite propagation speed when its principal symbol defines a
causal cone and lower-order terms do not alter the characteristic structure.

A typical scalar hyperbolic equation has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g\phi
  +
  m^2\phi
  =
  F(\phi,\nabla\phi).
  \label{eq:hyperbolic-scalar-section7}
\end{equation}
The causal domain of dependence is determined by the principal part
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g.
\end{equation}

MTT finite kernels are Lorentzian-admissible when they do not change this principal
hyperbolic structure.

\subsection{Equal-time finite kernels}
\label{subsec:equal-time-kernels}

A Cauchy-slice finite kernel acts on fields at equal time:
\begin{equation}
  (\mathcal K_t f)(x)
  =
  \int_{\Sigma_t}
  K_t(x,y)f(y)\,\dd\mu_{\Sigma_t}(y),
  \qquad
  x,y\in\Sigma_t.
  \label{eq:equal-time-kernel-section7}
\end{equation}
Here \(K_t(x,y)\) is a spatial kernel on the slice \(\Sigma_t\).

The kernel is finite and admissible if it is bounded in the relevant Sobolev scale. A simple
sufficient condition is that \(K_t\) is integrable in each variable:
\begin{equation}
  \sup_x
  \int_{\Sigma_t}
  |K_t(x,y)|\,\dd\mu_{\Sigma_t}(y)
  <
  \infty,
  \label{eq:kernel-L1-x-section7}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \sup_y
  \int_{\Sigma_t}
  |K_t(x,y)|\,\dd\mu_{\Sigma_t}(x)
  <
  \infty.
  \label{eq:kernel-L1-y-section7}
\end{equation}
Then \(\mathcal K_t\) is bounded on \(L^2(\Sigma_t)\) by Schur-type estimates.

More generally, if \(K_t\) is smooth enough and has controlled spatial decay or compact
support, it defines a bounded lower-order operator on Sobolev spaces:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal K_t:H^s(\Sigma_t)\to H^s(\Sigma_t)
\end{equation}
for the relevant range of \(s\).

Such a kernel can represent finite detector resolution, finite spatial overlap, finite source
profile, or finite coherent internal projection read on a Cauchy slice.

\subsection{Lower-order nonlocality}
\label{subsec:lower-order-nonlocality}

Consider a hyperbolic equation modified by an equal-time finite kernel:
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g\phi
  +
  m^2\phi
  +
  \mathcal K_t\phi
  =
  J.
  \label{eq:kernel-modified-hyperbolic-section7}
\end{equation}
The operator \(\mathcal K_t\) acts only on spatial variables at fixed time and is bounded on
the relevant energy space.

The principal part remains
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g.
\end{equation}
Therefore the characteristic cone is unchanged.

The finite kernel modifies the lower-order dynamics, dispersion, or effective source response,
but it does not replace the hyperbolic principal symbol. Hence:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{equal-time bounded kernels do not by themselves change the causal characteristic
  cone.}
  }
  \label{eq:principal-symbol-unchanged-section7}
\end{equation}

This is the key Lorentzian distinction. MTT finite kernels are admissible when they behave as
bounded lower-order terms relative to the hyperbolic equation.

\subsection{Domain of dependence}
\label{subsec:domain-of-dependence}

For a local hyperbolic system, the solution in a region depends only on initial data inside
the causal past determined by the principal symbol. If a finite kernel enters as a bounded
lower-order equal-time term, the principal causal cone remains the same.

Let \(D(\mathcal O)\) denote the domain of dependence of a region \(\mathcal O\subset\Sigma_0\)
under the original hyperbolic operator. If the finite kernel is lower-order and compatible with
the energy estimates, then the modified equation has the same characteristic propagation
speed.

The energy estimate has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  E(t)
  \le
  C
  \left[
    E(0)
    +
    \int_0^t
    \|J(s)\|^2\,\dd s
  \right]
  \exp(C_Kt),
  \label{eq:energy-estimate-kernel-section7}
\end{equation}
where \(C_K\) depends on the operator norm of \(\mathcal K_t\), but not on new superluminal
principal characteristics.

Thus the finite kernel may alter growth rates, mode mixing, or finite-width responses, but it
does not create a new principal propagation cone.

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent Cauchy kernels can preserve relativistic finite propagation speed.}
  }
  \label{eq:finite-propagation-preserved-section7}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Causality versus finite overlap}
\label{subsec:causality-versus-finite-overlap}

Finite overlap should not be confused with acausal signaling.

A spatial kernel may correlate or average data over a finite region of a Cauchy slice:
\begin{equation}
  (\mathcal K_t f)(x)
  =
  \int_{\Sigma_t}
  K_t(x,y)f(y)\,\dd y.
\end{equation}
This means that the effective state at \(x\) includes a finite spatial profile. But if the
kernel is part of the initial data, detector response, internal projection, or lower-order
source term, it does not imply that a signal is propagating outside the causal cone.

The distinction is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite spatial support or overlap}
  \neq
  \text{superluminal signal propagation}.
  }
  \label{eq:finite-support-not-superluminal-section7}
\end{equation}

A detector with finite spatial resolution samples a finite region. That is not acausal. A
finite internal wavefunction projects to an effective finite profile. That is not acausal. A
bounded equal-time kernel may enter the equations without changing the characteristic cone.
That is not acausal.

Acausality would arise only if the construction allowed controllable influence outside the
domain of dependence or changed the principal hyperbolic structure in a way that creates
superluminal characteristics.

\subsection{Manifest covariance as Lens}
\label{subsec:manifest-covariance-lens-section7}

A Cauchy-slice construction is not always manifestly Lorentz covariant. It singles out a
foliation
\begin{equation}
  \{\Sigma_t\}_{t\in\mathbb R}.
\end{equation}
In MTT this is a Lens choice: a representation used to define positive admissibility data.

This is analogous to gauge fixing. Coulomb gauge is not manifestly Lorentz covariant, but it
can isolate physical transverse modes. The loss of manifest covariance in the representative
does not by itself imply loss of physical covariance.

Similarly, a Cauchy-slice kernel may be a non-manifestly covariant representative of a
physically admissible finite projection. The physical requirements are:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the principal causal structure is preserved;
  \item constraints are preserved;
  \item observable predictions are independent of unphysical representative choices or
  transform consistently under changes of foliation;
  \item no controllable superluminal signaling is introduced.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{manifest covariance is a Lens property;}
  }
\end{equation}
whereas
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{physical covariance is a constraint and observable-level requirement.}
  }
\end{equation}

This distinction allows MTT to use positive Cauchy-slice operators without confusing a
non-manifest representation with a physical violation of relativity.

\subsection{Internal damping and Lorentzian base dynamics}
\label{subsec:internal-damping-base-dynamics}

The fixed-point realization gives the cleanest version of Lorentzian admissibility. The base
\(Y^4\) is Lorentzian and carries hyperbolic dynamics. The internal space \(X^6\) is compact
Riemannian and carries the positive damping operator:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}

Thus the finite filter is
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}},
\end{equation}
not
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box_Y}.
\end{equation}

The four-dimensional base dynamics remain governed by Lorentzian equations. The internal
modes appear after dimensional reduction as effective mass towers:
\begin{equation}
  m_j^2=m_0^2+\mu_j^2,
\end{equation}
with admissibility weights
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the Lorentzian base remains hyperbolic while internal modes are finitely filtered.}
  }
  \label{eq:base-hyperbolic-internal-filtered-section7}
\end{equation}

This is the clean FP/MTT answer to the Lorentzian divide.

\subsection{The role of \(\varepsilon\)-base regularization}
\label{subsec:epsilon-base-regularization}

Some fixed-point constructions introduce a regularized operator of the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  A_\varepsilon
  =
  A_{\rm int}
  +
  \varepsilon A_{\rm base},
  \qquad
  \varepsilon\ge0.
  \label{eq:Aepsilon-section7}
\end{equation}
Here \(A_{\rm base}\) may be a positive spatial or Euclideanized base operator used for
analytic control.

The physical Lorentzian interpretation must distinguish two cases.

If
\begin{equation}
  \varepsilon=0,
\end{equation}
then the finite damping is purely internal:
\begin{equation}
  A_\varepsilon=A_{\rm int}.
\end{equation}
This is the clean fiber-damping reading.

If
\begin{equation}
  \varepsilon>0,
\end{equation}
then base regularization has been introduced. This may be a useful analytic device, but it is
not automatically a physical Lorentzian damping mechanism. One must show that the base
operator is positive on a Cauchy slice or otherwise physically admissible.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \varepsilon>0
  \text{ requires a separate Lorentzian admissibility justification.}
  }
  \label{eq:epsilon-requires-justification-section7}
\end{equation}

The safest physical default is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \varepsilon=0
  \quad
  \text{for fundamental fiber damping.}
  }
  \label{eq:epsilon-zero-section7}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Spatial filters and Hamiltonian evolution}
\label{subsec:spatial-filters-hamiltonian}

A second Lorentzian-admissible route uses spatial filters on Hamiltonian data. Let
\begin{equation}
  (\phi,\pi)
\end{equation}
be canonical data on \(\Sigma_t\). A positive spatial operator
\begin{equation}
  A_\Sigma\ge0
\end{equation}
may define a finite filter
\begin{equation}
  B_\Sigma
  =
  P_\Sigma\chi(A_\Sigma)
  \ee^{-\tau A_\Sigma}
  \chi(A_\Sigma)P_\Sigma.
  \label{eq:spatial-filter-section7}
\end{equation}

The filtered data are
\begin{equation}
  (\phi,\pi)
  \mapsto
  (B_\Sigma\phi,B_\Sigma\pi),
\end{equation}
or a sector-specific variant.

The subsequent time evolution remains hyperbolic:
\begin{equation}
  \partial_t
  \begin{pmatrix}
    \phi\\
    \pi
  \end{pmatrix}
  =
  \mathcal H
  \begin{pmatrix}
    \phi\\
    \pi
  \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}

In this picture, finite coherent projection prepares or constrains admissible data, while the
base dynamics remain Lorentzian.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{spatial filtering of Cauchy data is admissible when it preserves the physical constraint
  and energy spaces.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gauge constraints on Cauchy slices}
\label{subsec:gauge-cauchy-section7}

Gauge fields have constraints on Cauchy data. For electromagnetism, Gauss law is
\begin{equation}
  \nabla\cdot\mathbf E=\rho.
\end{equation}
A finite spatial or internal filter must preserve this constraint or act after projection to the
physical transverse sector.

Thus a gauge-safe Cauchy-slice filter has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_\Sigma^{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_\Sigma^{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_\Sigma^{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys}.
  \label{eq:gauge-cauchy-filter-section7}
\end{equation}

Here \(P_{\rm phys}\) may be a transverse projector, BRST cohomology projection, or another
quotient-compatible physical-sector map.

The rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Cauchy-slice filtering must not turn constraint-violating gauge data into physical data.}
  }
  \label{eq:gauge-cauchy-rule-section7}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gravity constraints on Cauchy slices}
\label{subsec:gravity-cauchy-section7}

In gravity, Cauchy data consist of spatial geometry and extrinsic curvature:
\begin{equation}
  (h_{ij},K_{ij})
\end{equation}
on a spatial slice \(\Sigma_t\). These data must satisfy the Hamiltonian and momentum
constraints:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H=0,
  \qquad
  \mathcal H_i=0.
\end{equation}

A gravitational finite filter must preserve the constraint surface or act after reduction to
physical/diffeomorphism-compatible data:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_\Sigma^{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_\Sigma^{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_\Sigma^{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
  \label{eq:gravity-cauchy-filter-section7}
\end{equation}

In weak-field settings, \(P_{\rm diff}\) may reduce to a transverse-traceless projector. In full
gravity, it must encode diffeomorphism and Hamiltonian constraint compatibility.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravitational finite filtering must act on admissible spatial geometry, not arbitrary
  metric components.}
  }
  \label{eq:gravity-cauchy-rule-section7}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Locality in total space}
\label{subsec:locality-total-space-section7}

The fixed-point realization gives a geometric reading of effective nonlocality.

In the total space
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6,
\end{equation}
finite coherent structure may be local or controlled in the internal/fiber directions. When
projected to the observed base \(Y^4\), it may appear as:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item finite source profiles;
  \item finite detector response;
  \item form factors;
  \item suppressed internal-mode towers;
  \item non-pointlike local shadows;
  \item effective finite overlaps.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{four-dimensional nonlocality can be the shadow of locality or finite controlled support
  in the total/fibered space.}
  }
  \label{eq:nonlocality-shadow-section7}
\end{equation}

This is not arbitrary action at a distance. It is projection-induced finite support.

\subsection{Finite coherent width}
\label{subsec:finite-coherent-width-section7}

The coherent width is
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau}.
\end{equation}
In the fixed-point realization,
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
\end{equation}
and if
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2},
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{\log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}}.
\end{equation}

Thus the finite coherent width is an internal geometric length scale. The four-dimensional
delta approximation is valid when this internal width is below observational resolution:
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\ll\ell_{\rm res}.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Dirac deltas are low-resolution shadows of finite internal coherent width.}
  }
  \label{eq:deltas-low-resolution-section7}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Lorentzian admissibility checklist}
\label{subsec:lorentzian-checklist}

A Lorentzian finite-kernel construction is admissible only if the following checks pass:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item The damping operator is positive in the relevant sector.
  \item No naive indefinite \(\ee^{-\tau\Box}\) is used as a fundamental filter.
  \item Equal-time kernels are bounded on the relevant energy or Sobolev spaces.
  \item The hyperbolic principal symbol is unchanged.
  \item The finite propagation cone is preserved.
  \item Gauge constraints are preserved in gauge sectors.
  \item Diffeomorphism and Hamiltonian constraints are preserved in gravity.
  \item The construction does not allow controllable superluminal signaling.
  \item Observable predictions are independent of unphysical Lens choices or transform
  consistently under changes of representative.
\end{enumerate}

This checklist is part of the theory, not an afterthought.

\subsection{Failure modes}
\label{subsec:lorentzian-failure-modes-section7}

Lorentzian admissibility fails if:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item \(A=\Box\) is used without a positive physical-sector replacement;
  \item a finite kernel modifies the principal hyperbolic symbol in an uncontrolled way;
  \item an equal-time kernel is unbounded on the energy space;
  \item gauge or gravitational constraints are broken;
  \item base diffusion is treated as physical without a Lorentzian justification;
  \item foliation dependence enters observable predictions without compensation;
  \item finite overlap is used to transmit controllable signals outside the causal cone.
\end{enumerate}

In these cases, the finite kernel is not an admissible MTT object. It is an invalid Lorentzian
nonlocal modification.

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:cauchy-summary}

MTT resolves the Lorentzian divide by refusing to identify finite coherent damping with
naive spacetime heat flow:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A\neq\Box
  }
\end{equation}
as the fundamental Lorentzian damping operator.

Instead, admissible Lorentzian MTT uses:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{positive internal fiber operators, positive spatial Cauchy-slice operators, Hamiltonian
  or constraint-compatible operators, and bounded equal-time kernels.}
  }
\end{equation}

In the fixed-point realization:
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int}\ge0,
\end{equation}
so damping acts on internal fiber modes:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}\phi_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\phi_j.
\end{equation}

Equal-time Cauchy kernels are admissible when they are bounded lower-order operators that
do not change the hyperbolic principal symbol. Therefore they can preserve finite propagation
speed and the usual domains of dependence.

The conceptual result is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{four-dimensional finite nonlocality is a projection shadow of controlled finite
  coherent structure, not a license for acausal propagation.}
  }
\end{equation}

This Lorentzian admissibility principle will be used in the gauge and gravity sections that
follow.
% ============================================================
\section{Gauge and electromagnetism as quotient-compatible projection shadows}
\label{sec:gauge-em}
% ============================================================

The previous sections established the finite coherent admissibility operator, its
Circle--Lens--Nil reading, the delta/wave/measurement shadows, the scattering interpretation,
and the Lorentzian admissibility principle. We now apply the same architecture to gauge
theory and electromagnetism.

Gauge theory is the paradigmatic Lens sector. Its defining feature is that the variables used
locally are not themselves unique physical objects. They are representatives of equivalence
classes. Therefore an MTT finite filter is admissible in a gauge sector only if it respects the
gauge quotient.

The central rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{filter after quotienting, or filter covariantly before quotienting}.
  }
  \label{eq:gauge-safe-rule-section8}
\end{equation}

Equivalently, the gauge-sector finite coherent admissibility operator must have the schematic
form
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys}.
  \label{eq:gauge-Badm-section8}
\end{equation}
Here \(P_{\rm phys}\) projects to gauge-admissible physical content, and \(A_{\rm gauge}\) is a
positive or gauge-compatible admissibility operator on that physical content.

This section develops this rule and explains electromagnetism as a combined Lens--Circle--Nil
projection shadow.

\subsection{Gauge redundancy as Lens structure}
\label{subsec:gauge-redundancy-lens}

In Abelian gauge theory, the gauge potential transforms as
\begin{equation}
  A_\mu
  \mapsto
  A_\mu+\partial_\mu\alpha.
  \label{eq:U1-gauge-transform-section8}
\end{equation}
The field strength is
\begin{equation}
  F_{\mu\nu}
  =
  \partial_\mu A_\nu-\partial_\nu A_\mu.
\end{equation}
It is invariant under \eqref{eq:U1-gauge-transform-section8}:
\begin{equation}
  F_{\mu\nu}
  \mapsto
  F_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}

Thus \(A_\mu\) is not itself a unique physical object. It is a local representative. The physical
content is encoded in gauge-invariant or gauge-covariant structures such as:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item field strengths;
  \item Wilson loops and holonomies;
  \item conserved-current amplitudes;
  \item physical transverse modes;
  \item BRST cohomology classes;
  \item gauge-invariant composite observables.
\end{enumerate}

In MTT language:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Lens_{\rm gauge}
  =
  \text{local representative modulo gauge equivalence}.
  }
\end{equation}

Therefore, a finite coherent filter cannot treat all components of \(A_\mu\) as physical
degrees of freedom. Pure-gauge directions are Lens redundancy, not physical modes to be
damped or retained.

\subsection{Physical-sector projection}
\label{subsec:physical-sector-projection}

The gauge-sector projector
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm phys}
\end{equation}
selects physical, gauge-admissible content. In a simple Abelian flat-space setting this may be
represented by projection to transverse modes. In momentum space, define
\begin{equation}
  P_T^{\mu\nu}(k)
  =
  \eta^{\mu\nu}
  -
  \frac{k^\mu k^\nu}{k^2},
  \qquad
  k^2\neq0,
  \label{eq:transverse-projector-section8}
\end{equation}
with the appropriate Euclidean or physical transverse interpretation.

It satisfies
\begin{equation}
  k_\mu P_T^{\mu\nu}(k)=0,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  P_T^2=P_T.
\end{equation}

The transverse projector removes longitudinal representative components. Thus a simple
gauge-safe Abelian finite filter may be written schematically as
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm Abelian}
  =
  P_T\chi(A_T)\ee^{-\tau A_T}\chi(A_T)P_T,
  \label{eq:abelian-transverse-filter-section8}
\end{equation}
where \(A_T\) is positive on the transverse physical sector.

This is the gauge analogue of the coherent projector \(P_{\rm coh}\) in the FP internal
realization. In both cases, \(P\) removes inadmissible directions before finite damping is
interpreted physically.

\subsection{Conserved currents}
\label{subsec:conserved-currents-section8}

Electromagnetic amplitudes couple to conserved currents:
\begin{equation}
  \partial_\mu J^\mu=0,
\end{equation}
or in momentum space,
\begin{equation}
  k_\mu J^\mu(k)=0.
  \label{eq:current-conservation-section8}
\end{equation}
Because of \eqref{eq:current-conservation-section8}, longitudinal components do not
contribute to physical conserved-current amplitudes:
\begin{equation}
  J_\mu k^\mu=0.
\end{equation}

A gauge-safe finite current-exchange amplitude may therefore be written schematically as
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal A_{\rm adm}(k)
  \sim
  e^2
  J_\mu(k)
  \frac{F_{\rm adm}(k)}{k^2}
  P_T^{\mu\nu}(k)
  J'_\nu(-k),
  \label{eq:gauge-safe-current-amplitude-section8}
\end{equation}
where \(F_{\rm adm}\) is an admissible finite factor.

In a Euclidean scalar-like benchmark,
\begin{equation}
  F_{\rm adm}(k)=\ee^{-\tau k^2}.
\end{equation}
In the FP/fiber realization, the safer physical interpretation is instead an internal-mode
sum:
\begin{equation}
  F_{\rm adm}
  \quad
  \leadsto
  \quad
  \sum_j Z_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge-safe amplitudes damp physical or quotient-compatible content, not pure
  representative directions.}
  }
  \label{eq:gauge-safe-amplitudes-section8}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Fiber realization of gauge sectors}
\label{subsec:fiber-gauge-realization}

In the FP realization, gauge degrees of freedom may arise from internal geometry, internal
harmonics, bundle connections, representation data, or fiber-mode sectors. The finite coherent
filter acts on internal spectral data:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
\end{equation}
with weight
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

A four-dimensional gauge boson zero mode corresponds to an undamped internal zero mode:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
Massive internal gauge excitations carry weights
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2},
  \qquad
  \mu_j^2>0.
\end{equation}

Thus the effective four-dimensional gauge propagator may take the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  D_{\mu\nu}^{\rm eff}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{Z_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}}
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}
  P_{\mu\nu}^{(j)}(p),
  \label{eq:gauge-KK-effective-propagator-section8}
\end{equation}
where \(P_{\mu\nu}^{(j)}\) denotes the appropriate physical polarization or quotient-compatible
projector for the \(j\)-th effective mode.

The zero mode recovers ordinary low-energy gauge propagation. The nonzero internal modes
are admissibility-suppressed.

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{four-dimensional gauge fields are undamped zero-mode shadows plus suppressed
  internal gauge excitations.}
  }
  \label{eq:gauge-zero-mode-shadow-section8}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gauge covariance before quotienting}
\label{subsec:gauge-covariant-before-quotienting}

Sometimes it is useful to filter before explicitly projecting to the physical Hilbert space. In
that case the filter must be gauge-covariant.

For a non-Abelian gauge theory with connection
\begin{equation}
  A_\mu=A_\mu^aT_a,
\end{equation}
the covariant derivative is
\begin{equation}
  D_\mu=\partial_\mu+[A_\mu,\cdot].
\end{equation}
A natural gauge-covariant positive operator is a covariant Laplacian of the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm YM}
  =
  -D_iD^i
  \label{eq:YM-spatial-covariant-laplacian-section8}
\end{equation}
on a Cauchy slice, or a positive elliptic operator on internal/gauge-covariant fiber data.

A covariant filter
\begin{equation}
  \chi(A_{\rm YM})\ee^{-\tau A_{\rm YM}}\chi(A_{\rm YM})
\end{equation}
can be meaningful before quotienting only if it transforms compatibly under gauge
transformations. The resulting physical content must then be projected or evaluated through
gauge-invariant observables.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{before quotienting, the filter must be gauge-covariant;}
  }
\end{equation}
and:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{after quotienting, the filter must act on physical gauge content.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{BRST-compatible formulation}
\label{subsec:BRST-compatible-section8}

In covariant quantization, gauge redundancy is often handled by BRST symmetry. Let
\begin{equation}
  Q_{\rm BRST}^2=0.
\end{equation}
The physical Hilbert space is represented by cohomology:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{\rm phys}
  =
  \ker Q_{\rm BRST}/\operatorname{im}Q_{\rm BRST}.
  \label{eq:BRST-cohomology-section8}
\end{equation}

A finite coherent filter is BRST-compatible if it preserves physical cohomology. A sufficient
schematic condition is
\begin{equation}
  [A_{\rm gauge},Q_{\rm BRST}]=0,
  \qquad
