  [P_{\rm phys},Q_{\rm BRST}]=0,
  \label{eq:BRST-compatibility-section8}
\end{equation}
with domain and gauge-fixing details handled in the specific theory.

If a finite filter maps BRST-closed states to non-closed states, or turns BRST-exact redundancy
into physical content, it is not gauge-admissible.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{BRST-compatible MTT filtering preserves physical cohomology.}
  }
  \label{eq:BRST-compatible-filtering-section8}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Ward and Slavnov--Taylor constraints}
\label{subsec:ward-slavnov-section8}

Gauge theory is constrained by identities that express gauge consistency. In Abelian theory,
Ward identities encode current conservation and gauge invariance. In non-Abelian theory,
Slavnov--Taylor identities encode the corresponding BRST/gauge constraints.

A finite coherent filter must preserve these identities. Therefore \(A_{\rm gauge}\), \(P_{\rm
phys}\), \(\chi\), and \(\tau\) cannot be chosen arbitrarily.

A filter that damps physical transverse modes while preserving Ward identities may be
admissible. A filter that treats longitudinal pure-gauge modes as physical, or breaks current
conservation, is not.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge admissibility requires identity preservation, not merely high-mode damping.}
  }
  \label{eq:gauge-identity-preservation-section8}
\end{equation}

This is one of the central ways in which MTT finite filtering differs from arbitrary regularization.

\subsection{Electromagnetism as Lens--Circle--Nil structure}
\label{subsec:em-cln-section8}

Electromagnetism realizes all three MTT modes.

Lens is gauge redundancy:
\begin{equation}
  A_\mu\sim A_\mu+\partial_\mu\alpha.
\end{equation}
The potential is a local representative. The physical content is gauge-compatible.

Circle is electromagnetic phase and holonomy:
\begin{equation}
  \exp\left(\ii q\oint_\gamma A\right).
  \label{eq:EM-holonomy-section8}
\end{equation}
This phase can be physically observable even when the local field strength vanishes along
the path, as in holonomy-type effects.

Nil is charge-sector selection, photon-number records, detector clicks, and damping of
inadmissible or noncoherent gauge modes:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{align}
  \Lens_{\rm EM}
  &: \text{gauge representative and quotient},\\
  \Circle_{\rm EM}
  &: \text{phase, flux, holonomy},\\
  \Nil_{\rm EM}
  &: \text{charge sectors, photon records, detector outcomes}.
\end{align}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{electromagnetism}
  =
  \Lens\text{-gauge}
  +
  \Circle\text{-phase/holonomy}
  +
  \Nil\text{-charge and photon records}.
  }
  \label{eq:EM-CLN-box-section8}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Aharonov--Bohm-type phase as Circle shadow}
\label{subsec:AB-circle-shadow}

The phase
\begin{equation}
  \exp\left(\ii q\oint_\gamma A\right)
\end{equation}
is gauge-compatible because under
\begin{equation}
  A\mapsto A+\dd\alpha
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}
  \oint_\gamma \dd\alpha=0
\end{equation}
for a closed loop with single-valued gauge function.

Thus the loop phase is not merely a local representative artifact. It is a Circle holonomy
shadow of gauge structure:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{electromagnetic phase}
  =
  \Circle\text{-holonomy read through }\Lens\text{-gauge representatives}.
  }
\end{equation}

This is why gauge potentials cannot simply be dismissed as unphysical. They are representatives,
but their global holonomy can encode physical phase information.

\subsection{Flux quantization and charge sectors}
\label{subsec:flux-quantization-charge-sectors}

Gauge theory also exhibits quantized global labels. For suitable closed two-surfaces
\(\Sigma\), flux quantization has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_\Sigma F\in\mathbb Z.
  \label{eq:flux-quantization-section8}
\end{equation}
For a magnetic monopole, Dirac quantization gives
\begin{equation}
  eg=2\pi n,
  \qquad
  n\in\mathbb Z,
  \label{eq:dirac-monopole-section8}
\end{equation}
in units where \(\hbar=c=1\).

In MTT language, these integers are Lens--Circle consistency labels. They arise because local
gauge representatives must glue globally in a phase-consistent way.

Charge-sector records and photon-number detections add the Nil component:
\begin{equation}
  \text{charge or photon record}
  =
  \text{survivor label in a detector or sectoral basin}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge quantization}
  =
  \Circle\text{-phase closure}
  +
  \Lens\text{-bundle consistency}
  +
  \Nil\text{-sector survival}.
  }
  \label{eq:gauge-quantization-section8}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Finite charge sources}
\label{subsec:finite-charge-sources-section8}

A point charge is usually written as
\begin{equation}
  \rho(\mathbf r)=q\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf r).
\end{equation}
MTT treats this as a sharp shadow of a finite coherent charge profile. A simple Euclidean
finite-source model is
\begin{equation}
  \rho_\tau(\mathbf r)
  =
  q(4\pi\tau)^{-3/2}
  \exp\left(-\frac{r^2}{4\tau}\right).
  \label{eq:finite-charge-profile-section8}
\end{equation}
It preserves total charge:
\begin{equation}
  \int_{\mathbb R^3}\rho_\tau(\mathbf r)\,\dd^3r=q.
\end{equation}

The corresponding Coulomb-like potential is
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_\tau(r)
  =
  \frac{q}{4\pi r}
  \operatorname{erf}
  \left(
    \frac{r}{2\sqrt{\tau}}
  \right).
  \label{eq:finite-coulomb-section8}
\end{equation}
At large radius,
\begin{equation}
  r\gg\sqrt{\tau},
\end{equation}
one recovers the usual Coulomb potential:
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_\tau(r)\sim\frac{q}{4\pi r}.
\end{equation}
At small radius, the singularity is softened.

However, in the FP/fiber realization this formula should be read as a benchmark finite-source
model, not automatically as a universal four-dimensional Coulomb correction. If the finite
width is primarily internal, then four-dimensional Coulomb corrections arise through internal
mode exchange and finite overlap, not by direct spatial smearing of \(\mathbf r\) unless a
physical four-dimensional finite source is derived.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite Coulomb smearing is a benchmark; FP-realized electromagnetic corrections are
  internal-mode and overlap effects unless a 4D source profile is derived.}
  }
  \label{eq:finite-coulomb-caution-section8}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Photon records}
\label{subsec:photon-records-section8}

A photon detection event is not merely the presence of a mode. It is a measurement record.
In MTT terms, photon detection involves:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item a gauge-compatible electromagnetic field sector;
  \item a finite detector effect \(E_i^{(\Meas)}\);
  \item branch or mode damping due to the detector/environment;
  \item survivor-basin stabilization into a record.
\end{enumerate}

The measurement chain is
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{\rm EM}
  \to
  D^{(\Meas)}\circ\rho_{\rm EM}
  \to
  B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{photon}
  =
  \text{gauge-compatible excitation shadow plus detector survivor record}.
  }
  \label{eq:photon-record-section8}
\end{equation}

The wave aspect of light is the Circle-coherent spectral field. The particle aspect is the
Nil-stabilized detector record. Gauge structure is the Lens quotient behind both.

\subsection{Non-Abelian gauge sectors}
\label{subsec:nonabelian-section8}

For non-Abelian gauge theory, the gauge potential is Lie-algebra-valued:
\begin{equation}
  A_\mu=A_\mu^aT_a.
\end{equation}
Gauge transformations are nonlinear, and the field strength is
\begin{equation}
  F_{\mu\nu}
  =
  \partial_\mu A_\nu-\partial_\nu A_\mu+[A_\mu,A_\nu].
\end{equation}

The finite admissibility operator must respect the non-Abelian quotient structure:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm YM}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm YM})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm YM}}
  \chi(A_{\rm YM})P_{\rm phys}.
  \label{eq:YM-Badm-section8}
\end{equation}

Compared with Abelian theory, several new issues appear:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item field-dependent gauge orbits;
  \item ghost sectors;
  \item Gribov ambiguity;
  \item BRST cohomology;
  \item Slavnov--Taylor identities;
  \item anomaly cancellation in chiral sectors;
  \item confinement or nonperturbative sector structure.
\end{enumerate}

Thus non-Abelian MTT filtering is not obtained by simply multiplying propagators by a
Gaussian factor. It must be derived in a gauge-compatible physical sector.

\subsection{Standard Model gauge execution}
\label{subsec:SM-gauge-execution-section8}

A full Standard Model gauge implementation must recover
\begin{equation}
  SU(3)\times SU(2)\times U(1),
\end{equation}
matter representations, chirality, anomaly cancellation, electroweak symmetry breaking,
mixing, confinement, and precision scattering data.

In MTT terms, this requires deriving or specifying:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm SM},
  \qquad
  P_{\rm phys}^{\rm SM},
  \qquad
  \chi_{\rm SM},
  \qquad
  \tau_{\rm SM}.
\end{equation}

The fixed-point/fiber realization may provide a geometric source of representation and
mode data, but the present paper does not claim to complete the Standard Model derivation.
It identifies the form such a derivation must take.

The minimum requirements are:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item correct gauge group;
  \item correct representations;
  \item anomaly cancellation;
  \item gauge-identity preservation;
  \item correct low-energy zero-mode physics;
  \item admissibly suppressed nonzero internal modes;
  \item compatibility with collider and precision constraints.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Standard Model closure is an execution task, not assumed by the structural theorem.}
  }
  \label{eq:SM-execution-task-section8}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gauge-sector failure modes}
\label{subsec:gauge-failure-modes-section8}

The MTT gauge construction fails if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item pure-gauge modes are treated as physical;
  \item \(P_{\rm phys}\) is not quotient-compatible;
  \item \(A_{\rm gauge}\) breaks gauge covariance or gauge constraints;
  \item the spectral window \(\chi(A_{\rm gauge})\) breaks gauge identities;
  \item Ward or Slavnov--Taylor identities fail;
  \item current conservation is violated;
  \item BRST cohomology is not preserved;
  \item anomaly cancellation is broken;
  \item Lorentzian damping is implemented by an inadmissible \(\ee^{-\tau\Box}\) factor.
\end{enumerate}

In such cases, the finite filter is not an MTT gauge object. It is an inadmissible regulator.

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge-sector admissibility is a quotient and identity-preservation condition.}
  }
  \label{eq:gauge-admissibility-summary-section8}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:gauge-em-summary}

Gauge theory is the paradigmatic Lens sector of MTT. The local variables are representatives,
not unique physical objects:
\begin{equation}
  A_\mu\sim A_\mu+\partial_\mu\alpha.
\end{equation}

Therefore the finite coherent admissibility operator must be gauge-compatible:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys}.
  }
\end{equation}

The safe rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{filter after quotienting, or filter covariantly before quotienting}.
  }
\end{equation}

Electromagnetism combines all three MTT components:
\begin{align}
  \Lens_{\rm EM}
  &: \text{gauge representatives and quotienting},\\
  \Circle_{\rm EM}
  &: \text{phase, flux, and holonomy},\\
  \Nil_{\rm EM}
  &: \text{charge sectors, photon records, and detector outcomes}.
\end{align}

In the fixed-point/fiber realization, ordinary four-dimensional gauge fields arise as
undamped zero-mode shadows, while nonzero internal gauge excitations are admissibility
suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{electromagnetism is gauge-compatible finite coherent admissibility read through
  Lens quotienting, Circle phase, and Nil records.}
  }
\end{equation}
% ============================================================
\section{Gravity as diffeomorphism-compatible geometric projection}
\label{sec:gravity}
% ============================================================

Gauge theory is Lens structure over fields on spacetime. Gravity is deeper: it is Lens
structure acting on spacetime geometry itself. In general relativity, the metric is not a field
living on a fixed physical stage in the same way as an ordinary matter field. The metric helps
define the stage. Coordinate-related metric descriptions represent the same physical geometry:
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}\sim\varphi^\ast g_{\mu\nu}.
  \label{eq:diff-equivalence-section9}
\end{equation}

Therefore, a finite coherent admissibility operator in gravity cannot act on arbitrary metric
components as if they were all physical. It must act on diffeomorphism-compatible geometric
content.

The schematic gravitational operator is
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
  \label{eq:grav-Badm-section9}
\end{equation}
Here \(P_{\rm diff}\) is a diffeomorphism-compatible projector or reduction map, and
\(A_{\rm grav}\) is a positive or constraint-compatible geometric admissibility operator.

The central rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent filtering in gravity must preserve diffeomorphism-compatible
  geometric content.}
  }
  \label{eq:gravity-safe-rule-section9}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Diffeomorphism redundancy as Lens structure}
\label{subsec:diffeomorphism-lens}

A diffeomorphism
\begin{equation}
  \varphi:M\to M
\end{equation}
acts on the metric by pullback:
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}\mapsto\varphi^\ast g_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}
The two metric representatives describe the same geometry if they are related by an allowed
diffeomorphism.

Thus coordinates are not physical labels by themselves. They are Lens representatives.

In MTT language:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Lens_{\rm grav}
  =
  \text{coordinate representation modulo diffeomorphism equivalence}.
  }
\end{equation}

This makes gravity the most radical Lens sector. Gauge theory has redundancy over a fixed
base. Gravity has redundancy in the representation of the base geometry itself.

Therefore the gravitational finite filter cannot be:
\begin{equation}
  \text{``smooth each metric component in coordinates.''}
\end{equation}
That would be coordinate-dependent and generally inadmissible. The admissible operation must
be geometric:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{filter geometric content, not coordinate artifacts.}
  }
  \label{eq:filter-geometric-content-section9}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Why \(A_{\rm grav}\neq\Box_g\) as a naive heat operator}
\label{subsec:grav-not-box}

The Lorentzian d'Alembertian
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g
\end{equation}
is hyperbolic and indefinite. A naive expression
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box_g}
\end{equation}
is not a positive heat-kernel damping operator. In gravity this is even more dangerous than
in ordinary field theory, because the operator itself depends on the dynamical metric.

Thus MTT does not define gravitational finite admissibility by naive spacetime heat flow:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A_{\rm grav}\neq\Box_g
  \quad
  \text{as a fundamental Lorentzian damping operator.}
  }
  \label{eq:Agrav-not-box-section9}
\end{equation}

Instead, \(A_{\rm grav}\) must be one of the following:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item a positive spatial operator on Cauchy-slice geometric data;
  \item a positive operator on transverse-traceless perturbative modes;
  \item a constraint-compatible operator on canonical data;
  \item a geometric elliptic operator in a Euclidean or positive spectral chart;
  \item an internal/fiber geometric operator that contributes to effective gravitational sectors;
  \item a reduced operator acting after diffeomorphism quotienting.
\end{enumerate}

The safe gravitational rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{use positive geometric admissibility data after constraints or quotienting, not indefinite
  Lorentzian damping.}
  }
  \label{eq:positive-grav-data-section9}
\end{equation}

\subsection{ADM/Cauchy-slice formulation}
\label{subsec:ADM-cauchy-gravity}

On a globally hyperbolic spacetime, one may foliate the geometry by spacelike Cauchy slices:
\begin{equation}
  Y^4\cong\mathbb R\times\Sigma.
\end{equation}
The gravitational data on a slice \(\Sigma_t\) are the spatial metric and extrinsic curvature:
\begin{equation}
  (h_{ij},K_{ij}).
\end{equation}

Equivalently, in Hamiltonian variables, one uses
\begin{equation}
  (h_{ij},\pi^{ij}),
\end{equation}
where \(\pi^{ij}\) is the momentum conjugate to \(h_{ij}\).

The data cannot be arbitrary. They must satisfy the gravitational constraints:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H=0,
  \qquad
  \mathcal H_i=0.
  \label{eq:ADM-constraints-section9}
\end{equation}
The Hamiltonian constraint \(\mathcal H=0\) and momentum constraints \(\mathcal H_i=0\)
encode the diffeomorphism structure of general relativity.

Thus a gravitational finite filter must act on:
\begin{equation}
  \text{constraint-compatible Cauchy data}
\end{equation}
rather than arbitrary metric components.

The Cauchy-slice gravitational filter has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
  \label{eq:grav-cauchy-filter-section9}
\end{equation}

Here:
\begin{align}
  P_{\rm diff}
  &: \text{projects to diffeomorphism- or constraint-compatible content},\\
  A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}
  &: \text{positive spatial/geometric admissibility operator},\\
  \chi(A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav})
  &: \text{admissible spectral window},\\
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}}
  &: \text{finite damping of inadmissible geometric modes}.
\end{align}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravity-sector MTT filtering is naturally ADM/Cauchy-slice compatible.}
  }
  \label{eq:ADM-compatible-filtering-section9}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Constraint preservation}
\label{subsec:constraint-preservation-gravity}

The constraint surface is not optional. Let
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal C_{\rm grav}
  =
  \{(h_{ij},\pi^{ij}):\mathcal H=0,\mathcal H_i=0\}.
\end{equation}
A gravitational finite filter is admissible only if it preserves this surface or acts on a reduced
physical quotient:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}:
  \mathcal C_{\rm grav}\to\mathcal C_{\rm grav},
  \label{eq:constraint-preservation-section9}
\end{equation}
or, more generally,
\begin{equation}
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}:
  \mathcal C_{\rm grav}/\mathrm{Diff}
  \to
  \mathcal C_{\rm grav}/\mathrm{Diff}.
\end{equation}

In an operator language, one expects compatibility conditions of the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  [A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav},\widehat{\mathcal H}]\approx0,
  \qquad
  [A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav},\widehat{\mathcal H}_i]\approx0,
  \label{eq:constraint-commutators-section9}
\end{equation}
or a weaker condition that the full projected operator preserves physical equivalence classes.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{a gravitational finite filter is admissible only if it preserves the constraint structure.}
  }
  \label{eq:constraint-structure-box-section9}
\end{equation}

This is the gravitational analogue of preserving Ward or Slavnov--Taylor identities in gauge
theory.

\subsection{Linearized gravity and transverse-traceless projection}
\label{subsec:linearized-gravity-TT}

In weak-field gravity, one expands around a background metric:
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}
  =
  \bar g_{\mu\nu}+h_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}
For a flat background,
\begin{equation}
  \bar g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}

At linear order, infinitesimal diffeomorphisms act as
\begin{equation}
  h_{\mu\nu}
  \mapsto
  h_{\mu\nu}
  +
  \partial_\mu\xi_\nu
  +
  \partial_\nu\xi_\mu.
  \label{eq:linearized-diff-section9}
\end{equation}
Thus not all components of \(h_{\mu\nu}\) are physical.

In a flat perturbative setting, the physical graviton degrees of freedom are represented by
transverse-traceless data:
\begin{equation}
  h_{ij}^{\rm TT}.
\end{equation}
They satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \partial^i h_{ij}^{\rm TT}=0,
  \qquad
  \delta^{ij}h_{ij}^{\rm TT}=0.
\end{equation}

A weak-field gravitational finite filter can therefore be written schematically as
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm grav}^{\rm lin}
  =
  P_{\rm TT}\chi(A_{\rm TT})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm TT}}
  \chi(A_{\rm TT})P_{\rm TT},
  \label{eq:linearized-grav-filter-section9}
\end{equation}
where \(P_{\rm TT}\) is the transverse-traceless projector and \(A_{\rm TT}\ge0\) is a positive
operator on the TT sector.

For example, on a spatial Cauchy slice one may use
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm TT}=-\Delta_\Sigma
\end{equation}
restricted to TT data, or a Lichnerowicz-type operator in a curved background.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{weak-field gravitational filtering acts on physical spin-2 data, not pure
  diffeomorphism modes.}
  }
  \label{eq:weak-field-spin2-filtering-section9}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Lichnerowicz-type geometric operators}
\label{subsec:Lichnerowicz-section9}

On a curved background \((M,\bar g)\), a natural elliptic operator on symmetric two-tensors
is a Lichnerowicz-type operator. In one common convention,
\begin{equation}
  (\Delta_L h)_{\mu\nu}
  =
  -\nabla^2 h_{\mu\nu}
  -
  2R_{\mu\rho\nu\sigma}h^{\rho\sigma}
  +
  R_\mu{}^\rho h_{\rho\nu}
  +
  R_\nu{}^\rho h_{\mu\rho}.
  \label{eq:Lichnerowicz-section9}
\end{equation}
The exact form depends on sign conventions and on the chosen gauge-fixed gravitational
sector.

The important point is not this particular formula. The important point is that \(A_{\rm grav}\)
must be:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item geometric;
  \item positive or controlled on the relevant sector;
  \item compatible with constraints;
  \item compatible with diffeomorphism quotienting;
  \item compatible with the Lorentzian admissibility principle.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A_{\rm grav}
  =
  \text{positive or constraint-compatible geometric stabilization operator}.
  }
  \label{eq:Agrav-geometric-section9}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Finite graviton propagator shadow}
\label{subsec:finite-graviton-propagator-shadow}

In a weak-field positive spectral chart, one may write a schematic finite graviton propagator
shadow:
\begin{equation}
  D_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{\rm adm}(k)
  \sim
  \frac{F_{\rm adm}(k)}{k^2}
  P_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{\rm TT}(k),
  \label{eq:finite-graviton-propagator-section9}
\end{equation}
where \(P^{\rm TT}\) is the physical spin-2 projector and \(F_{\rm adm}\) is an admissible finite
factor.

In a Euclidean or positive spatial benchmark,
\begin{equation}
  F_{\rm adm}(k)=\ee^{-\tau k^2}.
\end{equation}
In the FP/fiber realization, the safer interpretation is internal-mode damping:
\begin{equation}
  F_{\rm adm}
  \leadsto
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
\end{equation}
for gravitational or geometry-coupled internal modes.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite graviton propagation is meaningful only after physical spin-2 or geometric
  projection.}
  }
  \label{eq:finite-graviton-after-projection-section9}
\end{equation}

A damping factor applied directly to all metric components is not admissible.

\subsection{Geometry-dependent heat kernels}
\label{subsec:geometry-dependent-kernels-section9}

For a fixed Riemannian geometry and a positive geometric operator \(A_g\), the finite heat
kernel has the form
\begin{equation}
  K_g(x,y;\tau)
  =
  \langle x|
  \ee^{-\tau A_g}
  |y\rangle.
\end{equation}
For small \(\tau\), the local heat-kernel expansion has the schematic structure
\begin{equation}
  K_g(x,y;\tau)
  \sim
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{\sigma_g(x,y)}{2\tau}
  \right)
  \Delta_g^{1/2}(x,y)
  \left[
    1+\tau a_1(x,y)+\tau^2a_2(x,y)+\cdots
  \right],
  \label{eq:geometry-heat-kernel-section9}
\end{equation}
where \(\sigma_g(x,y)\) is Synge's world function and \(\Delta_g(x,y)\) is the Van Vleck
determinant.

This expression shows that finite kernels in gravity are shaped by geometry. They depend on:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item geodesic distance;
  \item curvature;
  \item volume measure;
  \item parallel transport;
  \item boundary conditions;
  \item topology.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{a gravitational finite kernel is a geometry-dependent coherent response, not a flat
  Gaussian pasted onto spacetime.}
  }
  \label{eq:geometry-dependent-response-section9}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Curvature as Circle holonomy}
\label{subsec:curvature-circle-section9}

Gravity has a natural Circle component: curvature is holonomy.

Parallel transport around a loop \(\gamma\) acts as
\begin{equation}
  v^\mu
  \mapsto
  \mathcal P
  \exp\left(
    \oint_\gamma \Gamma
  \right)
  v^\mu.
\end{equation}
Infinitesimally,
\begin{equation}
  [\nabla_\mu,\nabla_\nu]v^\rho
  =
  R^\rho{}_{\sigma\mu\nu}v^\sigma.
  \label{eq:curvature-commutator-section9}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  R^\rho{}_{\sigma\mu\nu}
  =
  \text{infinitesimal Circle holonomy of local frame transport}.
  }
  \label{eq:curvature-circle-box-section9}
\end{equation}

The gravitational triplet becomes:
\begin{align}
  \Lens_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{diffeomorphism quotient and coordinate representation},\\
  \Circle_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{curvature and frame holonomy},\\
  \Nil_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{boundary, horizon, singularity, survivor geometry}.
\end{align}

\subsection{Nil boundaries and survivor geometries}
\label{subsec:nil-boundaries-section9}

Nil appears in gravity whenever geometric continuation, admissibility, or observation reaches
a boundary or survivor condition.

Examples include:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item event horizons;
  \item apparent horizons;
  \item trapped surfaces;
  \item singularity formation;
  \item cosmological boundaries;
  \item asymptotic boundary conditions;
  \item topology-change restrictions;
  \item black-hole endpoint sectors.
\end{enumerate}

In MTT language, a gravitational solution is a survivor geometry:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravitational solution}
  =
  \text{constraint-compatible survivor geometry}.
  }
  \label{eq:survivor-geometry-section9}
\end{equation}

A finite gravitational filter must therefore preserve or select admissible geometric survivors.
It cannot arbitrarily smooth away a singularity, horizon, or boundary and claim success unless
the resulting geometry satisfies the constraints and boundary conditions.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{singularity smoothing is meaningful only when it produces an admissible survivor
  geometry.}
  }
  \label{eq:singularity-smoothing-caution-section9}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Stress-energy compatibility}
\label{subsec:stress-energy-compatibility-section9}

Finite coherent matter sources coupled to gravity must respect stress-energy conservation:
\begin{equation}
  \nabla_\mu T^{\mu\nu}=0.
  \label{eq:stress-energy-conservation-section9}
\end{equation}
A point-mass source idealization may be written schematically as
\begin{equation}
  T_{\mu\nu}
  \sim
  m u_\mu u_\nu \delta^{(3)}(\mathbf x-\mathbf x_0).
\end{equation}
MTT may replace this with a finite coherent profile:
\begin{equation}
  T_{\mu\nu}^{\rm adm}
  \sim
  m u_\mu u_\nu K_{\rm adm}(x,x_0).
\end{equation}

But this replacement is admissible only if the resulting source satisfies the gravitational
constraints and conservation equations. Finite smearing is not arbitrary:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite stress-energy must be geometrically and dynamically consistent.}
  }
  \label{eq:finite-stress-energy-section9}
\end{equation}

This is the gravitational analogue of charge conservation in electromagnetism.

\subsection{Gravity and the FP internal realization}
\label{subsec:gravity-FP-realization}

In the fixed-point realization, the internal compact geometry contributes coherent zero modes
and damped nonzero internal excitations. Gravity may couple to this structure in two ways.

First, the four-dimensional gravitational sector may arise as a zero-mode geometric shadow:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
Second, nonzero internal geometric or matter excitations may affect effective gravity through
suppressed tower contributions:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2},
  \qquad
  \mu_j^2>0.
\end{equation}

Thus, in a schematic effective gravitational sector,
\begin{equation}
  G_{\rm grav}^{\rm eff}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{Z_j^{\rm grav}\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}}
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}
  P_j^{\rm phys}(p),
  \label{eq:grav-effective-tower-section9}
\end{equation}
where \(P_j^{\rm phys}\) is the appropriate physical spin/geometric projector.

This should be read as an execution template, not as a completed quantum-gravity derivation.

The important principle is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the observed gravitational sector is the diffeomorphism-compatible zero-mode and
  survivor-geometric shadow of finite internal admissibility.}
  }
  \label{eq:gravity-zero-mode-shadow-section9}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Emergent locality and geometric projection}
\label{subsec:emergent-locality-gravity}

Because gravity defines geometry, locality itself is projection-sensitive. In ordinary field
theory one asks whether a field is local on a fixed spacetime. In gravity, the meaning of
``local'' depends on the geometric sector.

MTT therefore treats gravitational locality as a Lens outcome:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{locality in gravity is defined after diffeomorphism-compatible geometric projection.}
  }
  \label{eq:locality-after-projection-section9}
\end{equation}

This matters for:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item horizons;
  \item entanglement across regions;
  \item black-hole interiors;
  \item bulk/boundary reconstruction;
  \item singularity resolution;
  \item spatial subregion factorization.
\end{enumerate}

Thus, in gravity, the Lens component acts not only on field representation but on the
representation of spacetime regions themselves.

\subsection{Entanglement and gravitational factorization}
\label{subsec:gravity-entanglement-factorization}

In non-gravitational quantum mechanics one often begins with
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{AB}
  =
  \mathcal H_A\otimes\mathcal H_B.
\end{equation}
In gravity, this factorization can be subtle or fail. The decomposition into subregions depends
on geometry, boundary data, constraints, and diffeomorphism-invariant definitions of location.

Thus a gravitational coherent sector may not factor:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{\rm grav}^{AB}
  \neq
  \mathcal H_{\rm grav}^{A}
  \otimes
  \mathcal H_{\rm grav}^{B}.
\end{equation}
Similarly,
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{AB}
  \neq
  B_{\rm adm}^{A}
  \otimes
  B_{\rm adm}^{B}.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravitational entanglement must be defined after geometric Lens projection.}
  }
  \label{eq:gravitational-entanglement-lens-section9}
\end{equation}

This will be revisited in the section on superposition and entanglement.

\subsection{Weak-field phenomenological template}
\label{subsec:weak-field-phenomenology-section9}

A near-term benchmark for gravitational MTT is weak-field linearized gravity. The execution
program is:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item choose a background \(\bar g_{\mu\nu}\);
  \item decompose perturbations \(h_{\mu\nu}\);
  \item project to physical transverse-traceless or constraint-compatible data;
  \item choose a positive operator \(A_{\rm TT}\) on that sector;
  \item construct
  \begin{equation}
    B_{\rm grav}^{\rm lin}
    =
    P_{\rm TT}\chi(A_{\rm TT})
    \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm TT}}
    \chi(A_{\rm TT})P_{\rm TT};
  \end{equation}
  \item compute finite corrections to propagation or source coupling;
  \item compare with gravitational-wave and weak-field constraints.
\end{enumerate}

This is a controlled benchmark. It does not claim to solve full quantum gravity.

The leading correction scale is again
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau},
  \qquad
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2},
\end{equation}
with \(\tau\) determined by the relevant sectoral gap or admissibility scale.

\subsection{Full nonperturbative execution task}
\label{subsec:nonperturbative-gravity-task}

Full gravity is harder. A nonperturbative gravitational MTT sector must supply:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm grav},
  \qquad
  P_{\rm diff},
  \qquad
  \chi,
  \qquad
  \tau.
\end{equation}
It must also satisfy:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item diffeomorphism compatibility;
  \item Hamiltonian and momentum constraint preservation;
  \item recovery of classical general relativity at large scales;
  \item controlled Lorentzian causal structure;
  \item admissible boundary conditions;
  \item stress-energy compatibility;
  \item a physical Hilbert or state-space interpretation;
  \item no unphysical coordinate-dependent smoothing.
\end{enumerate}

This paper does not complete that task. It states the form such a construction must take.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravity-sector MTT is a geometric projection program, not a completed quantum-gravity
  calculation by itself.}
  }
  \label{eq:gravity-execution-task-section9}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gravity-sector failure modes}
\label{subsec:gravity-failure-modes-section9}

The gravitational MTT construction fails if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item \(A_{\rm grav}\) is taken to be an indefinite Lorentzian \(\Box_g\) heat operator;
  \item metric components are damped as coordinate components rather than geometric data;
  \item pure diffeomorphism modes are treated as physical;
  \item the Hamiltonian or momentum constraints are violated;
  \item the constraint algebra is broken;
  \item stress-energy conservation fails;
  \item a weak-field projector is used outside its perturbative regime;
  \item singularities or horizons are smoothed without producing admissible survivor geometries;
  \item observable predictions depend on arbitrary coordinate or foliation choices.
\end{enumerate}

In such cases, the finite filter is not an admissible gravitational MTT object.

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:gravity-summary}

Gravity is the deepest Lens sector because it acts on the representation of locality and
geometry itself:
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}\sim\varphi^\ast g_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}

The gravitational finite coherent admissibility operator must therefore be
diffeomorphism-compatible:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
  }
\end{equation}

The Lorentzian rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A_{\rm grav}\neq\Box_g
  \quad
  \text{as a naive fundamental heat operator}.
  }
\end{equation}
Instead, \(A_{\rm grav}\) must be a positive or constraint-compatible geometric operator.

In ADM/Cauchy form:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff},
\end{equation}
with preservation of the Hamiltonian and momentum constraints.

The Circle--Lens--Nil reading is:
\begin{align}
  \Lens_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{diffeomorphism quotient and geometric representation},\\
  \Circle_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{curvature and frame holonomy},\\
  \Nil_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{boundary, horizon, singularity, survivor geometry}.
\end{align}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravity is diffeomorphism-compatible finite coherent admissibility read as geometric
  projection.}
  }
\end{equation}

% ============================================================
\section{Quantization as admissible survivor-label structure}
\label{sec:quantization}
% ============================================================

The previous sections treated particles, waves, measurement records, scattering, gauge
structure, electromagnetism, and gravity as projection shadows of finite coherent
admissibility. We now turn to quantization.

In ordinary presentations, quantization often appears as a separate mystery: why do some
physical quantities come in discrete units? In MTT, quantization is not treated as a primitive
postulate. It is treated as the emergence of stable admissible labels from the combined action
of Circle closure, Lens quotient consistency, and Nil survivor selection.

The guiding thesis is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{quantization}
  =
  \Circle\text{-closure}
  +
  \Lens\text{-quotient consistency}
  +
  \Nil\text{-survivor selection}.
  }
  \label{eq:quantization-thesis-section10}
\end{equation}

In the fixed-point realization, compact internal fiber geometry adds a concrete source of
discrete labels: the spectrum of positive fiber operators and the joint harmonic coherent
sector.

\subsection{Quantization as survivor-label discreteness}
\label{subsec:quantization-survivor-labels}

A quantized label is a stable label that survives admissibility constraints. Such labels may
come from:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item eigenvalues of a self-adjoint operator;
  \item compact-fiber spectra;
  \item phase closure conditions;
  \item topological winding numbers;
  \item gauge flux sectors;
  \item representation labels;
  \item particle-number records;
  \item measurement survivor basins;
  \item boundary or horizon sectors.
\end{enumerate}

In MTT, these are not all identical mechanisms. They are different ways in which admissible
structure becomes discretely labeled.

The common form is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{continuous formal possibility}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{admissibility constraint}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{stable discrete survivor labels}.
  }
  \label{eq:formal-to-discrete-section10}
\end{equation}

A quantized label is therefore not merely a number. It is a Nil-surviving label compatible
with Circle closure and Lens representation consistency.

\subsection{Spectral quantization}
\label{subsec:spectral-quantization}

The simplest form of quantization is spectral. Let \(A\) be a self-adjoint operator with
eigenvalue equation
\begin{equation}
  A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n.
  \label{eq:spectral-eigenvalue-section10}
\end{equation}
If the spectrum is discrete, then admissible modes are labeled by \(n\).

The finite coherent admissibility operator acts by
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n
  \label{eq:Badm-spectral-quantization-section10}
\end{equation}
in the commuting spectral case.

The label \(n\) survives when
\begin{equation}
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}
\end{equation}
is nonzero or above the relevant admissibility threshold.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{spectral quantization}
  =
  \text{discrete eigenlabels surviving finite admissibility filtering}.
  }
  \label{eq:spectral-quantization-box-section10}
\end{equation}

This is the analytic prototype for MTT quantization.

\subsection{Compact fiber spectra}
\label{subsec:compact-fiber-spectra}

In the fixed-point realization, quantization has a geometric source. The internal fibers are
compact Riemannian spaces. Their Laplace-type operators have discrete nonnegative spectra:
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{B_n(y)}\psi_{n,j}(y)
  =
  \lambda_{n,j}(y)\psi_{n,j}(y),
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
  0=\lambda_{n,0}(y)
  \le
  \lambda_{n,1}(y)
  \le
  \lambda_{n,2}(y)
  \le
  \cdots.
\end{equation}

The internal admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3\kappa_n\Delta_{B_n}.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
\end{equation}
then the internal labels \(j\) are discrete in compact sectors.

The zero-mode sector is
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
\end{equation}
and is undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
The nonzero internal modes satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0,
\end{equation}
and are damped:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
\end{equation}

Thus the fixed-point realization gives:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{compact fiber geometry}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{discrete internal labels}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{zero-mode shadows and damped excitation towers}.
  }
  \label{eq:fiber-quantization-box-section10}
\end{equation}

This is the Kaluza--Klein-compatible source of one class of MTT quantized labels.

\subsection{The joint harmonic coherent sector}
\label{subsec:joint-harmonic-quantization}

The coherent projector in the FP realization is
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}\Pi_{B_2}\Pi_{B_3},
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
  \Ran P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3
  \ker\Delta_{B_n}.
\end{equation}

This means that the coherent low-energy sector is not arbitrary. It is the joint fiber-harmonic
sector. The zero-mode labels are selected by simultaneous harmonicity across internal bundle
directions.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{coherent zero-mode quantization}
  =
  \text{joint survival in the fiber-harmonic kernel}.
  }
  \label{eq:joint-harmonic-quantization-box-section10}
\end{equation}

The nonzero internal modes are not absent in principle. They are present as possible
excitations, but admissibility suppresses them by
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Therefore, the FP realization separates:
\begin{align}
  \text{zero modes}
  &: \text{coherent low-energy survivor labels},\\
  \text{nonzero internal modes}
  &: \text{damped excitation labels}.
\end{align}

\subsection{Phase closure and integer labels}
\label{subsec:phase-closure-integers}

Circle closure gives another basic route to quantization. If a phase variable lives on a circle,
then single-valuedness requires
\begin{equation}
  \psi(\theta+2\pi)=\psi(\theta).
\end{equation}
The eigenfunctions are
\begin{equation}
  \psi_n(\theta)=\ee^{\ii n\theta},
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
  n\in\mathbb Z.
\end{equation}

Thus integer labels arise from phase closure:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Circle\text{-closure}
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  n\in\mathbb Z.
  }
  \label{eq:circle-integer-section10}
\end{equation}

This is not merely a formal fact. It is the simplest example of quantization as admissible
return. A phase that fails to return consistently is not a valid global state.

In this sense:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{integer quantization}
  =
  \text{stable Circle return label}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gauge quantization}
\label{subsec:gauge-quantization-section10}

Gauge theory adds Lens quotient consistency. Local representatives must glue globally in a
way that preserves phase and bundle structure.

For a \(U(1)\) gauge field, flux quantization may take the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_\Sigma F\in\mathbb Z.
  \label{eq:flux-quantization-section10}
\end{equation}
For electric and magnetic charges, Dirac quantization gives
\begin{equation}
  eg=2\pi n,
  \qquad
  n\in\mathbb Z,
  \label{eq:Dirac-quantization-section10}
\end{equation}
in units where \(\hbar=c=1\).

In MTT language:
\begin{align}
  \Circle &: \text{phase closure around loops},\\
  \Lens &: \text{bundle patching and gauge quotient consistency},\\
  \Nil &: \text{stable charge or flux sector}.
\end{align}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge quantization}
  =
  \Circle\text{-phase closure}
  +
  \Lens\text{-bundle consistency}
  +
  \Nil\text{-sector survival}.
  }
  \label{eq:gauge-quantization-box-section10}
\end{equation}

Gauge quantized labels are therefore not arbitrary labels attached to fields. They are global
consistency labels of the gauge Lens structure.

\subsection{Representation labels}
\label{subsec:representation-labels}

Quantum systems often carry representation labels. A particle may transform under a
representation of a symmetry group:
\begin{equation}
  \rho:G\to\mathrm{Aut}(V).
\end{equation}
For compact groups, irreducible representation labels are discrete.

In MTT terms, representation labels are Lens-compatible survivor labels. They specify how
states transform under symmetry while remaining admissible under quotient and phase
constraints.

For a gauge group \(G\), a matter sector must choose representation data:
\begin{equation}
  V_R.
\end{equation}
The representation label \(R\) survives only if it is compatible with:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item gauge quotienting;
  \item anomaly cancellation;
  \item physical positivity;
  \item observed low-energy sectors;
  \item admissible coupling to measurement records.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{representation quantization}
  =
  \text{symmetry-compatible Lens survivor labeling}.
  }
  \label{eq:representation-quantization-section10}
\end{equation}

The present paper does not derive the full Standard Model representation content. It states
the MTT form such a derivation must have.

\subsection{Particle number}
\label{subsec:particle-number-section10}

Particle number is another quantized label, but it is not always fundamental. In quantum
field theory, particle number is often a Fock-space label associated with excitations relative
to a chosen vacuum or asymptotic context.

In MTT, particle number is a survivor label in a sector where:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item a coherent mode decomposition is available;
  \item excitations can be counted;
  \item the measurement context stabilizes number records;
  \item interactions do not destroy the relevant asymptotic interpretation.
\end{enumerate}

For a mode \(a^\dagger\), Fock labels are
\begin{equation}
  |n\rangle
  =
  \frac{(a^\dagger)^n}{\sqrt{n!}}|0\rangle,
  \qquad
  n=0,1,2,\dots.
\end{equation}

In MTT language:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{particle number}
  =
  \text{Nil-stable count of excitations in a chosen Circle/Lens mode context}.
  }
  \label{eq:particle-number-section10}
\end{equation}

This is why particle number can be exact in one regime and ambiguous in another. It depends
on the admissible sector and the measurement context.

\subsection{Measurement outcomes as quantized records}
\label{subsec:measurement-outcomes-quantized}

Measurement outcomes are discrete when the detector survivor basins are discrete:
\begin{equation}
  \frakB_{\Meas}
  =
  \{B_i^{(\Meas)}\}_{i\in I}.
\end{equation}
An individual record is
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

The outcome label \(i\) is a Nil survivor label. It is exact relative to the measurement context
when the detector dynamics stabilize the basin.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{measurement quantization}
  =
  \text{discrete survivor-basin labeling}.
  }
  \label{eq:measurement-quantization-section10}
\end{equation}

This clarifies the difference between a continuous underlying amplitude and a discrete record.
The detector context may map continuous or coherent input structure into a discrete basin
partition.

The basin probabilities are not supplied merely by the label set. They require a measure:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(i)
  =
  \frac{\mu(B_i^{(\Meas)})}
  {\sum_j\mu(B_j^{(\Meas)})}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{quantized outcomes require survivor labels; probabilities require basin measure.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Energy quantization}
\label{subsec:energy-quantization-section10}

Energy quantization depends on boundary conditions, potentials, geometry, and the relevant
Hamiltonian. For a bound system with Hamiltonian \(H\),
\begin{equation}
  H\psi_n=E_n\psi_n.
\end{equation}
If the resolvent is compact or the bound-state spectrum is discrete, then
\begin{equation}
  E_n
\end{equation}
are discrete labels.

In MTT terms, the discrete energies are Circle/Lens/Nil-compatible survivor labels:
\begin{align}
  \Circle &: \text{standing-wave phase closure},\\
  \Lens &: \text{boundary conditions and representation choice},\\
  \Nil &: \text{normalizable bound-state survival}.
\end{align}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{bound-state energy quantization}
  =
  \text{phase-closed normalizable survivor modes}.
  }
  \label{eq:energy-quantization-box-section10}
\end{equation}

The same logic applies to compact internal fiber spectra, where compactness and ellipticity
produce discrete internal energies or mass contributions.

\subsection{Topological quantization}
\label{subsec:topological-quantization}

Topological sectors provide robust quantized labels. Examples include winding number,
Chern number, instanton number, monopole charge, and flux sectors.

A typical topological integer has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  Q_{\rm top}
  =
  \int_M \omega
  \in\mathbb Z,
\end{equation}
where \(\omega\) is a characteristic density normalized so that the integral is integer-valued
on admissible configurations.

In MTT language:
\begin{align}
  \Circle &: \text{closed cycles and return},\\
  \Lens &: \text{global patching and quotient consistency},\\
  \Nil &: \text{topological sector survival}.
\end{align}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{topological quantization}
  =
  \text{integer-valued survivor labels of global admissibility}.
  }
  \label{eq:topological-quantization-section10}
\end{equation}

These labels are often stable under continuous deformation because changing them requires
leaving the admissible sector or crossing a singular configuration.

\subsection{Gravity and geometric quantization labels}
\label{subsec:gravity-quantization-labels}

Gravity introduces geometric labels. In canonical or holonomy-based approaches, such labels
may include:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item spin-network labels;
  \item holonomy representations;
  \item flux labels;
  \item area and volume spectra;
  \item boundary charges;
  \item horizon microstate labels.
\end{enumerate}

MTT does not assume a specific quantum-gravity realization. Instead, it states the structural
condition:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravitational quantized labels must be diffeomorphism-compatible survivor labels of
  geometric admissibility.}
  }
  \label{eq:gravity-quantization-section10}
\end{equation}

Thus a gravitational label is not physical merely because it is discrete in a coordinate
description. It must survive the Lens quotient:
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}\sim\varphi^\ast g_{\mu\nu},
\end{equation}
and must preserve the gravitational constraints.

\subsection{Quantization and the Born layer}
\label{subsec:quantization-born-layer}

Quantized labels are not the same as probabilities. A theory may identify possible discrete
outcomes
\begin{equation}
  i\in I,
\end{equation}
but still need to assign outcome weights.

In MTT, survivor labels are supplied by Nil basin structure:
\begin{equation}
  \frakB_{\Meas}
  =
  \{B_i^{(\Meas)}\}_{i\in I}.
\end{equation}
Probabilities require a basin measure:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(i)
  =
  \frac{\mu(B_i^{(\Meas)})}
  {\sum_j\mu(B_j^{(\Meas)})}.
  \label{eq:born-basin-section10}
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{quantization gives possible labels;}
  }
\end{equation}
while:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the Born layer gives frequencies over labels.}
  }
\end{equation}

This distinction prevents the common mistake of treating discreteness itself as a probability
law.

\subsection{Quantization and finite admissibility}
\label{subsec:quantization-finite-admissibility}

Finite coherent admissibility modifies quantization by distinguishing possible formal labels
from surviving admissible labels.

In the commuting spectral case:
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n
  =
  w_n\phi_n,
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
\end{equation}
A formal eigenlabel \(n\) is strongly suppressed if
\begin{equation}
  w_n\ll1.
\end{equation}
It survives as part of the effective coherent sector if
\begin{equation}
  w_n
\end{equation}
is nonzero or above the relevant admissibility threshold.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite admissibility turns a formal spectrum into an effective survivor spectrum.}
  }
  \label{eq:effective-survivor-spectrum-section10}
\end{equation}

In the FP realization:
\begin{equation}
  w_j
  =
  p_j\chi(\mu_j^2)^2\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}
The zero modes survive undamped. The nonzero modes are suppressed according to their
internal eigenvalues.

\subsection{Continuous spectra and approximate quantization}
\label{subsec:continuous-spectra-approx-quantization}

Not every sector has a purely discrete spectrum. Scattering states often form continuous
spectra. In such cases, quantization may appear only after:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item imposing boundary conditions;
  \item compactifying a region;
  \item selecting resonances;
  \item measuring discrete detector records;
  \item projecting onto finite-resolution bins;
  \item restricting to bound states or stable sectors.
\end{enumerate}

Thus MTT distinguishes:
\begin{equation}
  \text{spectral discreteness}
\end{equation}
from
\begin{equation}
  \text{record discreteness}.
\end{equation}

A continuous scattering spectrum can still produce discrete detector clicks because the
detector has survivor basins:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{continuous wave spectra can produce discrete records through Nil basin selection.}
  }
  \label{eq:continuous-to-discrete-records-section10}
\end{equation}

This is another way in which MTT unifies wave-like and particle-like behavior.

\subsection{Classical discreteness versus quantum discreteness}
\label{subsec:classical-vs-quantum-discreteness}

Not all discreteness is quantum. A classical system can have discrete stable states due to
nonlinear attractors, thresholds, or boundary conditions. Quantum discreteness is distinguished
by its relation to coherent phase structure, Hilbert-space representation, and measurement
statistics.

In MTT, quantum discreteness requires:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item coherent spectral or phase structure;
  \item admissible Lens representation;
  \item Nil survivor stability;
  \item a compatible probability or basin-measure layer.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{quantum labels are not merely discrete states;}
  }
\end{equation}
they are:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{coherence-compatible survivor labels in an admissible projection architecture}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Relation to wave--particle duality}
\label{subsec:quantization-wave-particle}

The delta/wave duality remains central. Quantization adds a third aspect to the same kernel.

The local reading gives:
\begin{equation}
  \text{particle/delta shadow}.
\end{equation}

The spectral reading gives:
\begin{equation}
  \text{wave/interference shadow}.
\end{equation}

The survivor-label reading gives:
\begin{equation}
  \text{quantized label shadow}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Kadm
  \quad
  \begin{cases}
    \text{local reading}
    &\Rightarrow
    \text{particle shadow},\\
    \text{spectral reading}
    &\Rightarrow
    \text{wave shadow},\\
    \text{survivor-label reading}
    &\Rightarrow
    \text{quantization shadow}.
  \end{cases}
  }
  \label{eq:particle-wave-quantization-section10}
\end{equation}

This is why quantization belongs in the same paper. It is not an unrelated add-on. It is another
projection shadow of finite coherent admissibility.

\subsection{What is not yet derived}
\label{subsec:quantization-not-derived}

The paper does not claim to derive all observed quantum numbers from the present structural
argument. In particular, it does not yet derive:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item all Standard Model representations;
  \item all fermion generations;
  \item all masses;
  \item all coupling constants;
  \item all anomaly cancellations;
  \item all quantum-gravity labels;
  \item all detector basin measures.
\end{enumerate}

Those are sectoral execution tasks.

The claim established here is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{quantization fits the finite coherent admissibility architecture as survivor-label
  structure.}
  }
  \label{eq:quantization-structural-claim-section10}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:quantization-summary}

MTT interprets quantization as admissible survivor-label structure:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{quantization}
  =
  \Circle\text{-closure}
  +
  \Lens\text{-quotient consistency}
  +
  \Nil\text{-survivor selection}.
  }
\end{equation}

In spectral sectors:
\begin{equation}
  A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n
\end{equation}
gives discrete modal labels, and finite admissibility weights them by
\begin{equation}
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
\end{equation}

In the fixed-point realization:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j
\end{equation}
gives compact-fiber labels. Zero modes survive undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1,
\end{equation}
while nonzero internal modes are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
\end{equation}

Measurement outcomes are quantized when detector survivor basins are discrete:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Gauge and topological labels arise from global phase closure and quotient consistency:
\begin{equation}
  \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_\Sigma F\in\mathbb Z.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{quantized labels are finite coherent admissibility labels that survive Circle closure,
  Lens consistency, and Nil selection.}
  }
\end{equation}

% ============================================================
\section{Superposition and entanglement as coherent-sector structure}
\label{sec:superposition-entanglement}
% ============================================================

The previous section treated quantization as admissible survivor-label structure. We now turn
to superposition and entanglement. These are often treated as uniquely quantum mysteries:
how can a system be in several states at once, and how can separated systems share
nonclassical correlations?

In MTT, superposition and entanglement are not separate primitives. They are coherent-sector
structures before Nil survivor selection. Superposition is coherent coexistence of admissible
branches in a given Lens context. Entanglement is non-factorizable coherent support across a
joint admissible sector.

The guiding thesis is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{superposition}
  =
  \text{coherent branch coexistence before Nil selection},
  }
  \label{eq:superposition-thesis-section11}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{entanglement}
  =
  \text{non-factorizable coherent admissibility in a joint sector}.
  }
  \label{eq:entanglement-thesis-section11}
\end{equation}

Both are naturally expressed through the finite coherent admissibility operator:
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Superposition as coherent branch coexistence}
\label{subsec:superposition-branch-coexistence}

Let a measurement or representation context select a branch basis:
\begin{equation}
  \{|a\rangle\}_{a\in I}.
\end{equation}
A superposed state has the form
\begin{equation}
  |\psi\rangle
  =
  \sum_{a\in I}c_a|a\rangle.
  \label{eq:superposition-state-section11}
\end{equation}
The density matrix is
\begin{equation}
  \rho
  =
  |\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
  =
  \sum_{a,b}
  c_a c_b^\ast
  |a\rangle\langle b|.
  \label{eq:superposition-density-section11}
\end{equation}

The diagonal terms
\begin{equation}
  |c_a|^2|a\rangle\langle a|
\end{equation}
are branch weights. The off-diagonal terms
\begin{equation}
  c_a c_b^\ast|a\rangle\langle b|,
  \qquad
  a\neq b,
\end{equation}
are branch coherences.

In MTT, the state \eqref{eq:superposition-state-section11} is not read as a classical object
being in many classical states simultaneously. It is read as a coherent admissible state whose
branch decomposition depends on the Lens context.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{superposition is basis-relative coherent admissibility, not classical multiplicity.}
  }
  \label{eq:superposition-basis-relative-section11}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Circle, Lens, and Nil in superposition}
\label{subsec:CLN-superposition}

Superposition involves all three components of the Modal Triplet.

Circle carries relative phase:
\begin{equation}
  c_a c_b^\ast
  =
  |c_a||c_b|\ee^{\ii(\theta_a-\theta_b)}.
\end{equation}
These relative phases are what allow interference.

Lens supplies the branch decomposition:
\begin{equation}
  \{|a\rangle\}_{a\in I}.
\end{equation}
A different measurement basis produces a different branch expansion.

Nil selects or stabilizes one record downstream:
\begin{equation}
  |\psi\rangle
  \to
  B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{superposition}
  =
  \Circle\text{-relative phase}
  +
  \Lens\text{-branch decomposition}
  \quad
  \text{before}
  \quad
  \Nil\text{-record selection}.
  }
  \label{eq:superposition-CLN-section11}
\end{equation}

This is why superposition and measurement are inseparable in interpretation. The state is
coherent relative to one Lens decomposition, and the record is stabilized relative to a
measurement context.

\subsection{Superposition and finite admissibility}
\label{subsec:superposition-finite-admissibility}

Not every formal branch decomposition is physically admissible. The finite coherent
admissibility operator determines which components survive the sectoral filtering:
\begin{equation}
  \Badm|\psi\rangle
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P|\psi\rangle.
\end{equation}

In a commuting spectral basis,
\begin{equation}
  |\psi\rangle
  =
  \sum_n c_n|\phi_n\rangle,
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
  A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n,
\end{equation}
one obtains
\begin{equation}
  \Badm|\psi\rangle
  =
  \sum_n
  c_n
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2
  \ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}
  |\phi_n\rangle.
  \label{eq:filtered-superposition-section11}
\end{equation}

The mode weights are
\begin{equation}
  w_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
\end{equation}
Thus the admissible superposition is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Badm|\psi\rangle
  =
  \sum_n c_n w_n|\phi_n\rangle.
  }
\end{equation}

This shows that superposition in MTT is not unconstrained formal addition. It is finite
coherent addition inside an admissible sector.

\subsection{Decoherence and branch damping}
\label{subsec:decoherence-branch-damping-section11}

When a system couples to a detector or environment, off-diagonal coherence may be damped.
In a finite branch basis this is described by
\begin{equation}
  \rho\mapsto D\circ\rho,
  \label{eq:branch-damping-section11}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  (D\circ\rho)_{ab}=D_{ab}\rho_{ab}.
\end{equation}

Physical admissibility requires
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}=1.
\end{equation}
Then the Schur map is completely positive and trace preserving.

If
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}\approx1,
  \qquad
  a\neq b,
\end{equation}
then branch coherence survives. If
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}\approx0,
  \qquad
  a\neq b,
\end{equation}
then the branches are effectively decohered in that Lens context.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{decoherence}
  =
  \Nil\text{-damping of off-diagonal Circle coherence in a Lens-selected branch basis}.
  }
  \label{eq:decoherence-CLN-section11}
\end{equation}

This is not yet a definite outcome. A definite outcome also requires survivor-basin selection:
\begin{equation}
  D\circ\rho
  \to
  B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Superposition versus mixture}
\label{subsec:superposition-versus-mixture}

A coherent superposition
\begin{equation}
  |\psi\rangle
  =
  \sum_a c_a|a\rangle
\end{equation}
has density matrix
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{\rm sup}
  =
  \sum_{a,b}
  c_a c_b^\ast
  |a\rangle\langle b|.
\end{equation}
An incoherent mixture with the same branch weights has density matrix
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{\rm mix}
  =
  \sum_a
  |c_a|^2
  |a\rangle\langle a|.
\end{equation}

The difference is the off-diagonal part:
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{\rm sup}-\rho_{\rm mix}
  =
  \sum_{a\neq b}
  c_a c_b^\ast
  |a\rangle\langle b|.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{superposition}
  -
  \text{mixture}
  =
  \text{off-diagonal coherent structure}.
  }
  \label{eq:superposition-mixture-difference-section11}
\end{equation}

Measurement devices that erase or preserve these off-diagonal terms produce different
experimental shadows.

\subsection{Entanglement as non-factorizable coherence}
\label{subsec:entanglement-nonfactorizable}

Let a joint system have Hilbert space
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{AB}
  =
  \mathcal H_A\otimes\mathcal H_B
\end{equation}
in a regime where such a tensor product decomposition is valid. A general pure state is
\begin{equation}
  |\Psi\rangle
  =
  \sum_{a,b}
  c_{ab}
  |a\rangle_A\otimes|b\rangle_B.
\end{equation}

The state is separable if
\begin{equation}
  c_{ab}=\alpha_a\beta_b,
\end{equation}
so that
\begin{equation}
  |\Psi\rangle=|\psi\rangle_A\otimes|\varphi\rangle_B.
\end{equation}
It is entangled if it cannot be written in this factorized form.

In MTT language:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{entanglement}
  =
  \text{joint coherent admissibility that does not factor into local coherent sectors}.
  }
  \label{eq:entanglement-nonfactor-box-section11}
\end{equation}

At the operator level, this means that the joint admissibility structure may fail to factor:
\begin{equation}
  P_{AB}\neq P_A\otimes P_B,
  \label{eq:PAB-nonfactor-section11}
\end{equation}
or
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{AB}
  \neq
  B_{\rm adm}^{A}\otimes B_{\rm adm}^{B}.
  \label{eq:Badm-nonfactor-section11}
\end{equation}

Thus entanglement is not merely a surprising correlation after measurement. It is a structure
of the joint coherent sector before measurement.

\subsection{Joint finite admissibility}
\label{subsec:joint-finite-admissibility}

For a joint system, the finite coherent admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{AB}
  =
  P_{AB}\chi(A_{AB})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{AB}}
  \chi(A_{AB})P_{AB}.
  \label{eq:joint-Badm-section11}
\end{equation}

If the system factorizes cleanly, then one may have
\begin{equation}
  A_{AB}=A_A\otimes I_B+I_A\otimes A_B,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  P_{AB}=P_A\otimes P_B.
\end{equation}
In that case, under compatible windows and scales, the finite filter may factor.

But in interacting, constrained, gauge, gravitational, or globally coherent sectors, one may
have
\begin{equation}
  A_{AB}
  \neq
  A_A\otimes I_B+I_A\otimes A_B,
\end{equation}
or
\begin{equation}
  P_{AB}\neq P_A\otimes P_B.
\end{equation}

Then the joint finite admissibility operator is genuinely non-factorizing.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{entanglement can arise from non-factorizing admissibility data }(A_{AB},P_{AB}).
  }
  \label{eq:entanglement-admissibility-data-section11}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Entangled records}
\label{subsec:entangled-records}

Entanglement becomes operationally visible through correlated measurement records. Let
Alice and Bob measure settings \(x\) and \(y\), producing outcomes \(a\) and \(b\). The joint
effects are
\begin{equation}
  E_{a|x}^{A},
  \qquad
  E_{b|y}^{B}.
\end{equation}
The joint probability is
\begin{equation}
  p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \tr\left[
    \rho_{AB}
    \left(
      E_{a|x}^{A}\otimes E_{b|y}^{B}
    \right)
  \right],
  \label{eq:joint-probability-section11}
\end{equation}
when a tensor factorization is valid.

In MTT, the measurement process also includes branch damping and survivor-basin selection:
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{AB}
  \to
  D^{(x,y)}\circ\rho_{AB}
  \to
  B_{ab}^{(x,y)}.
\end{equation}

Thus an entangled experiment has:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item a joint coherent sector;
  \item local Lens settings \(x,y\);
  \item possible non-factorizing phase structure;
  \item local detector effects;
  \item correlated survivor records.
\end{enumerate}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{entangled correlations}
  =
  \text{joint coherent-sector structure read through local measurement Lenses}.
  }
  \label{eq:entangled-correlations-section11}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Bell-type correlations}
\label{subsec:bell-type-correlations}

Bell-type experiments show that quantum correlations cannot be reproduced by local hidden
variables satisfying the usual factorization assumptions. MTT does not evade this by adding
classical hidden variables. The nonclassical feature is the non-factorizing joint coherent
sector.

The key MTT statement is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{joint admissible coherence need not factor into pre-existing local survivor labels.}
  }
  \label{eq:bell-MTT-statement-section11}
\end{equation}

Before measurement, the joint state can be coherent in a way that is not equivalent to a
probability distribution over local classical records. Measurement settings select Lens
decompositions, and Nil survivor basins stabilize outcomes.

The local outcome records are definite only after measurement:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi_{AB}
  \to
  B_{ab}^{(x,y)}.
\end{equation}

Thus Bell-type violation is not surprising in MTT. It reflects the failure of the assumption:
\begin{equation}
  \text{joint coherent admissibility}
  =
  \text{pre-existing distribution over local survivor labels}.
\end{equation}

MTT instead says:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{local records are Nil shadows of a joint coherent structure, not pre-existing classical
  variables.}
  }
  \label{eq:local-records-nil-shadows-section11}
\end{equation}

\subsection{No-signaling constraint}
\label{subsec:no-signaling-section11}

Entanglement must not permit controllable superluminal signaling. Therefore the local
marginals must not depend on the remote measurement setting.

For Alice,
\begin{equation}
  p(a|x,y)
  =
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y)
\end{equation}
must satisfy
\begin{equation}
  p(a|x,y)=p(a|x,y')
  \label{eq:no-signaling-Alice-section11}
\end{equation}
for all Bob settings \(y,y'\). Similarly,
\begin{equation}
  p(b|x,y)=p(b|x',y)
  \label{eq:no-signaling-Bob-section11}
\end{equation}
for all Alice settings \(x,x'\).

In MTT terms:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{joint coherence may be non-factorizing, but local survivor statistics must satisfy
  no-signaling.}
  }
  \label{eq:no-signaling-MTT-section11}
\end{equation}

This is a required admissibility condition on any MTT entanglement model.

The presence of joint coherent structure does not by itself transmit a signal. A signal would
require controllable modulation of local marginals by a remote choice. MTT forbids this in
admissible physical sectors.

\subsection{Gauge and gravitational factorization subtleties}
\label{subsec:gauge-gravity-factorization}

The tensor product
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{AB}
  =
  \mathcal H_A\otimes\mathcal H_B
\end{equation}
is not always fundamental.

In gauge theory, Gauss constraints can obstruct naive factorization across spatial regions.
Boundary or edge modes may be required. The physical Hilbert space may not factor without
including shared boundary data.

In gravity, the issue is deeper. Spatial regions themselves are defined only after geometric
Lens projection. Diffeomorphism constraints and gravitational boundary charges can obstruct
simple factorization.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{subsystem factorization is itself a Lens-dependent structure.}
  }
  \label{eq:factorization-lens-dependent-section11}
\end{equation}

Therefore, entanglement claims in gauge or gravity sectors must first justify the subsystem
decomposition:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{AB}\stackrel{?}{=}\mathcal H_A\otimes\mathcal H_B.
\end{equation}

If this decomposition is invalid, then apparent entanglement or separability may be an artifact
of a bad Lens choice.

\subsection{Internal/fiber entanglement}
\label{subsec:internal-fiber-entanglement}

In the fixed-point realization, the internal fiber structure may also contribute to entanglement.
A total state may involve both base and internal variables:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi(x,z),
  \qquad
  x\in Y^4,
  \quad
  z\in X^6.
\end{equation}
Expanding in internal modes:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi(x,z)
  =
  \sum_j
  \psi_j(x)\phi_j(z).
  \label{eq:base-fiber-expansion-section11}
\end{equation}

If this expansion cannot be written as a product
\begin{equation}
  \Psi(x,z)\neq \psi(x)\phi(z),
\end{equation}
then base and internal degrees of freedom are entangled.

After projection to the four-dimensional base, such internal coherence may appear as:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item effective mixed states;
  \item suppressed internal towers;
  \item finite form factors;
  \item correlated sector labels;
  \item apparent nonlocal finite-width shadows.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{some four-dimensional effective mixedness or finite structure can be a shadow of
  base--fiber entanglement.}
  }
  \label{eq:base-fiber-entanglement-section11}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Superposition in the FP zero-mode sector}
\label{subsec:superposition-FP-zero-mode}

In the FP realization, the coherent low-energy sector is the joint fiber-harmonic sector:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Ran P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3\ker\Delta_{B_n}.
\end{equation}

A low-energy superposition is therefore a coherent combination inside this zero-mode sector:
\begin{equation}
  |\psi_{\rm coh}\rangle
  =
  \sum_\alpha c_\alpha|\alpha\rangle_{\rm coh},
  \label{eq:coherent-zero-mode-superposition-section11}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}|\alpha\rangle_{\rm coh}
  =
  |\alpha\rangle_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}

Nonzero internal modes may also appear formally:
\begin{equation}
  |\psi\rangle
  =
  \sum_\alpha c_\alpha|\alpha\rangle_{\rm coh}
  +
  \sum_j d_j|\phi_j\rangle_{\rm nonzero}.
\end{equation}
The finite admissibility operator suppresses them:
\begin{equation}
  d_j
  \mapsto
  d_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{effective low-energy superposition lives primarily in the joint fiber-harmonic
  coherent sector.}
  }
  \label{eq:FP-superposition-box-section11}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Measurement of entangled systems}
\label{subsec:measurement-entangled-systems}

For an entangled system, measurement must be described jointly. A measurement context
includes local or joint settings:
\begin{equation}
  \Meas_{AB}
  =
  (x,y,A_{\Meas},P_{\Meas},\chi_{\Meas},\tau_{\Meas},
  \{E_{ab}^{(x,y)}\},\frakB_{AB}^{(x,y)}).
\end{equation}

The survivor basins are joint:
\begin{equation}
  \frakB_{AB}^{(x,y)}
  =
  \{B_{ab}^{(x,y)}\}_{a,b}.
\end{equation}
The basin probability is
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \frac{
    \mu(B_{ab}^{(x,y)})
  }{
    \sum_{a',b'}\mu(B_{a'b'}^{(x,y)})
  }.
  \label{eq:joint-basin-probability-section11}
\end{equation}

If the basin measure agrees with the quantum trace rule, then
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \tr\left[
    \rho_{AB}
    E_{ab}^{(x,y)}
  \right].
\end{equation}

The important distinction remains:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{joint coherence determines possible correlations;}
  }
\end{equation}
whereas
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{joint basin measure determines observed frequencies.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Reversibility and erasure}
\label{subsec:reversibility-erasure}

Some apparent loss of coherence is reversible. If branch information is stored in a marker
system but not irreversibly amplified into a stable survivor record, then a later measurement
may erase or recombine the marker basis.

Let the system-marker state be
\begin{equation}
  |\Psi\rangle
  =
  \alpha|\psi_1\rangle|m_1\rangle
  +
  \beta|\psi_2\rangle|m_2\rangle.
\end{equation}
The branch coherence visible in the system alone is controlled by the marker overlap:
\begin{equation}
  \langle m_2|m_1\rangle.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  \langle m_2|m_1\rangle=0,
\end{equation}
the system alone shows no interference in that basis. But if the marker is later measured in
a recombining basis, conditional interference can reappear.

In MTT language:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{reversible marker entanglement}
  \neq
  \text{irreversible Nil survivor-basin capture}.
  }
  \label{eq:reversibility-vs-capture-section11}
\end{equation}

This explains quantum eraser behavior without retrocausal alteration. The Lens context and
Nil basin partition are changed before a final survivor record is stabilized.

\subsection{Classical correlations versus entanglement}
\label{subsec:classical-correlations-vs-entanglement}

A classically correlated state has the form
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{\rm class}
  =
  \sum_\lambda
  p(\lambda)
  \rho_A^\lambda\otimes\rho_B^\lambda.
\end{equation}
It represents a probability distribution over local states.

An entangled state cannot be written in this form. It contains joint coherent structure that
does not reduce to pre-existing local alternatives.

In MTT:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{classical correlation}
  =
  \text{shared Nil label distribution};
  }
\end{equation}
whereas
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{entanglement}
  =
  \text{joint Circle coherence before local Nil labels exist}.
  }
\end{equation}

This distinction is central to Bell-type experiments. Classical correlation assumes local
survivor labels already exist. Entanglement says the joint coherent sector precedes the local
record decomposition.

\subsection{Entanglement and the delta/wave duality}
\label{subsec:entanglement-delta-wave}

The delta/wave duality extends naturally to entanglement.

For a single system:
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  \quad
  \begin{cases}
    \text{local reading}
    &\Rightarrow
    \text{particle/delta shadow},\\
    \text{spectral reading}
    &\Rightarrow
    \text{wave shadow}.
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}

For a joint system:
\begin{equation}
  K_{AB}(x_A,x_B;y_A,y_B)
\end{equation}
may fail to factor:
\begin{equation}
  K_{AB}
  \neq
  K_AK_B.
\end{equation}
Then local readings produce correlated records, while spectral readings show joint coherent
modes.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{entanglement}
  =
  \text{non-factorizing extension of the same finite kernel logic behind delta/wave duality}.
  }
  \label{eq:entanglement-delta-wave-section11}
\end{equation}

This is why entanglement belongs naturally in the same finite coherent projection framework.

\subsection{Execution tasks}
\label{subsec:entanglement-execution-tasks}

A complete MTT account of entanglement must supply:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the joint Hilbert or physical state space;
  \item the admissible joint operator \(A_{AB}\);
  \item the joint coherent projector \(P_{AB}\);
  \item proof or disproof of factorization \(P_{AB}=P_A\otimes P_B\);
  \item local measurement effects \(E_{a|x}^{A}\), \(E_{b|y}^{B}\);
  \item joint survivor basins \(B_{ab}^{(x,y)}\);
  \item the basin measure \(\mu\);
  \item no-signaling of local marginals;
  \item agreement with Bell-type quantum correlations in benchmark cases.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT identifies the structure of entanglement; sectoral execution must derive the
  quantitative correlations.}
  }
  \label{eq:entanglement-execution-section11}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:superposition-entanglement-summary}
