Superposition is coherent branch coexistence:
\begin{equation}
  |\psi\rangle
  =
  \sum_a c_a|a\rangle.
\end{equation}
It depends on a Lens branch decomposition and carries Circle phase coherence.

Decoherence is Nil damping of off-diagonal terms:
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}\mapsto D_{ab}\rho_{ab}.
\end{equation}
A definite outcome requires survivor-basin capture:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Entanglement is non-factorizable joint coherent admissibility:
\begin{equation}
  P_{AB}\neq P_A\otimes P_B,
\end{equation}
or
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{AB}
  \neq
  B_{\rm adm}^{A}\otimes B_{\rm adm}^{B}.
\end{equation}

Bell-type correlations reflect the fact that joint coherent admissibility need not be a
pre-existing distribution over local survivor labels. Admissible MTT entanglement must still
satisfy no-signaling:
\begin{equation}
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y')
\end{equation}
and similarly for Bob.

In the fixed-point realization, effective low-energy superpositions live primarily in the joint
fiber-harmonic sector:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{\rm coh}
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3\ker\Delta_{B_n},
\end{equation}
while nonzero internal modes are suppressed by
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{superposition and entanglement are coherent-sector shadows of the same finite
  admissibility structure that produces delta and wave shadows.}
  }
\end{equation}

% ============================================================
\section{Analytic theorems and structural unification corollary}
\label{sec:unification-theorem}
% ============================================================

The preceding sections developed the physical shadows of finite coherent admissibility:
Dirac-delta localization, spectral wave behavior, measurement records, scattering form
factors, gauge quotienting, electromagnetic holonomy, gravitational projection,
quantization, superposition, and entanglement. We now separate the rigorous analytic claims
from the broader structural unification claim.

This separation is important. The paper does not assert one vague theorem that proves all
physics at once. It establishes a hierarchy:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item an analytic finite-filter theorem;
  \item a branch-channel theorem for admissible damping;
  \item a scalar heat-kernel benchmark theorem;
  \item a fixed-point/fiber realization statement;
  \item a structural unification corollary.
\end{enumerate}

The first three are mathematical statements. The fourth identifies the geometric realization.
The fifth is the interpretive MTT conclusion.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{analytic control}
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \text{sectoral admissibility}
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \text{projection-shadow unification}.
  }
  \label{eq:theorem-hierarchy-section12}
\end{equation}

\subsection{The finite-filter theorem}
\label{subsec:finite-filter-theorem}

Let \(\mathcal H\) be a Hilbert space. Let
\begin{equation}
  A:\mathcal D(A)\subset\mathcal H\to\mathcal H
\end{equation}
be a nonnegative self-adjoint operator:
\begin{equation}
  A\ge0.
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{equation}
  P=P^\ast=P^2
\end{equation}
be an orthogonal projector. Let \(\chi\) be a bounded Borel function satisfying
\begin{equation}
  0\le\chi(\lambda)\le1.
\end{equation}
Assume the commuting spectral condition:
\begin{equation}
  [P,A]=0.
  \label{eq:commuting-condition-section12}
\end{equation}
For \(\tau>0\), define
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
  \label{eq:Badm-theorem-section12}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Finite coherent admissibility filter]
\label{thm:finite-filter}
Under the assumptions above, \(\Badm\) is a bounded positive contraction:
\begin{equation}
  0\le \Badm\le I.
\end{equation}
If \(A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n\) and \(P\phi_n=p_n\phi_n\) with \(p_n\in\{0,1\}\), then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
  \label{eq:finite-filter-modal-action-section12}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since \(A\ge0\), the spectral theorem gives
\begin{equation}
  0\le \ee^{-\tau A}\le I.
\end{equation}
Since \(0\le\chi\le1\),
\begin{equation}
  0\le\chi(A)\le I.
\end{equation}
Because \([P,A]=0\), \(P\) commutes with \(\chi(A)\) and \(\ee^{-\tau A}\). Therefore, for
any \(f\in\mathcal H\),
\begin{align}
  \langle f,\Badm f\rangle
  &=
  \langle f,P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P f\rangle\\
  &=
  \left\|
    \ee^{-\tau A/2}\chi(A)P f
  \right\|^2\\
  &\ge0.
\end{align}
Thus \(\Badm\ge0\).

Moreover,
\begin{equation}
  \|\chi(A)\|\le1,
  \qquad
  \|\ee^{-\tau A}\|\le1,
  \qquad
  \|P\|\le1.
\end{equation}
Hence
\begin{equation}
  \|\Badm\|\le1.
\end{equation}
Since \(\Badm\ge0\), this implies
\begin{equation}
  0\le\Badm\le I.
\end{equation}

If \(A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n\), then
\begin{equation}
  \chi(A)\phi_n=\chi(\lambda_n)\phi_n,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}\phi_n=\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
\end{equation}
If \(P\phi_n=p_n\phi_n\), then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
\end{equation}
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The theorem is stated in the commuting spectral case. If \([P,A]\neq0\), the formula
\eqref{eq:finite-filter-modal-action-section12} need not hold. The expression
\(P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P\) may still be meaningful, but admissibility must then be
checked by domain, positivity, constraint, and boundedness estimates.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Kernel-shadow corollary}
\label{subsec:kernel-shadow-corollary}

Assume now that \(\Badm\) has an integral kernel
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)=\langle x|\Badm|y\rangle.
\end{equation}
In the commuting discrete spectral case,
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}
  \phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y).
  \label{eq:kernel-shadow-formula-section12}
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[Local and spectral shadows]
\label{cor:local-spectral-shadows}
When a kernel representation exists, the same finite coherent admissibility operator has two
primary readings:
\begin{align}
  \text{local reading}
  &: \quad
  x\mapsto\Kadm(x,x_0),
  \\
  \text{spectral reading}
  &: \quad
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y).
\end{align}
The local reading gives the finite source or particle-shadow profile. The spectral reading
gives the wave-shadow profile.
\end{corollary}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{one finite kernel}
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \begin{cases}
    \text{Dirac-delta/local shadow},\\
    \text{spectral/wave shadow}.
  \end{cases}
  }
  \label{eq:one-kernel-two-shadows-section12}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Sharp delta corollary}
\label{subsec:sharp-delta-corollary}

The Dirac delta is recovered when the finite coherent admissibility operator approaches the
identity.

\begin{corollary}[Sharp delta limit]
\label{cor:sharp-delta-limit}
Suppose a family
\begin{equation}
  \Badm^{(N)}
  =
  P_N\chi_N(A)\ee^{-\tau_N A}\chi_N(A)P_N
\end{equation}
satisfies
\begin{equation}
  P_N\to I,
  \qquad
  \chi_N(A)\to I,
  \qquad
  \tau_N\downarrow0
\end{equation}
strongly on the relevant test-function domain. Then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm^{(N)}f\to f.
\end{equation}
If \(\Badm^{(N)}\) has kernels \(K_N(x,y)\), then
\begin{equation}
  K_N(x,y)\to\delta(x-y)
\end{equation}
distributionally.
\end{corollary}

This establishes the precise MTT reading:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \delta(x-y)
  =
  \text{sharp identity-kernel shadow of finite coherent admissibility}.
  }
  \label{eq:delta-sharp-shadow-section12}
\end{equation}

The Dirac delta is therefore not primitive in the theory. It is the zero-width limit of an
admissible finite kernel.

\subsection{Schur branch-channel theorem}
\label{subsec:schur-channel-theorem-section12}

Measurement and decoherence require a separate theorem. Branch damping is not described
by the same operator \(\Badm\) acting on wavefunctions. It is described by a channel acting
on density matrices.

Let \(D\) be an \(N\times N\) matrix and define the Schur product map
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_D(\rho)=D\circ\rho,
  \qquad
  (\Phi_D(\rho))_{ab}=D_{ab}\rho_{ab}.
  \label{eq:Schur-map-section12}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Schur damping channel]
\label{thm:schur-damping-channel-section12}
If
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}=1
\end{equation}
for all \(a\), then
\begin{equation}
  \rho\mapsto D\circ\rho
\end{equation}
is completely positive and trace preserving.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since \(D\succeq0\), there exist vectors \(v_a\) in an auxiliary Hilbert space \(\mathcal K\)
such that
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}=\langle v_b,v_a\rangle.
\end{equation}
The condition \(D_{aa}=1\) implies
\begin{equation}
  \|v_a\|=1.
\end{equation}
Define an isometry
\begin{equation}
  V:\mathbb C^N\to\mathbb C^N\otimes\mathcal K
\end{equation}
by
\begin{equation}
  V|a\rangle=|a\rangle\otimes v_a.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  V^\ast V=I.
\end{equation}
The reduced channel
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_D(\rho)
  =
  \tr_{\mathcal K}(V\rho V^\ast)
\end{equation}
has matrix elements
\begin{align}
  \langle a|\Phi_D(\rho)|b\rangle
  &=
  \rho_{ab}\langle v_b,v_a\rangle\\
  &=
  D_{ab}\rho_{ab}.
\end{align}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_D(\rho)=D\circ\rho.
\end{equation}
Because \(\Phi_D\) has a Stinespring representation, it is completely positive. Because \(V\)
is an isometry, it is trace preserving.
\end{proof}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  D\succeq0,\quad D_{aa}=1
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \text{branch damping is a valid quantum channel}.
  }
  \label{eq:branch-channel-box-section12}
\end{equation}

This theorem is why MTT does not permit arbitrary visibility factors.

\subsection{Exponential damping and Schoenberg admissibility}
\label{subsec:schoenberg-admissibility-section12}

A common measurement damping model is
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}(\tau)
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\Lambda_{ab}}.
  \label{eq:exponential-damping-section12}
\end{equation}
For this to define a valid Schur channel for all \(\tau\ge0\), one needs
\begin{equation}
  D(\tau)\succeq0
\end{equation}
for all \(\tau\ge0\), together with
\begin{equation}
  D_{aa}(\tau)=1.
\end{equation}

A standard sufficient condition is that \(\Lambda\) is conditionally negative definite and has
zero diagonal:
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{aa}=0,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \sum_{a,b}\overline{c_a}c_b\Lambda_{ab}\le0
  \qquad
  \text{whenever}
  \qquad
  \sum_a c_a=0.
  \label{eq:CND-section12}
\end{equation}

Then Schoenberg's theorem implies
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Lambda}\succeq0
\end{equation}
for all \(\tau\ge0\).

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Lambda\text{ conditionally negative definite}
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  D_{ab}=\ee^{-\tau\Lambda_{ab}}
  \text{ is admissible branch damping}.
  }
  \label{eq:Schoenberg-box-section12}
\end{equation}

In MTT language, \(\Lambda_{ab}\) measures branch distinguishability, while \(D_{ab}\) measures
surviving coherence.

\subsection{Scalar heat-kernel benchmark theorem}
\label{subsec:scalar-benchmark-theorem-section12}

The scalar Euclidean heat kernel provides a complete closed benchmark. Let
\begin{equation}
  A=-\Delta
\end{equation}
on \(\mathbb R^d\), with
\begin{equation}
  P=I,
  \qquad
  \chi=1.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=\ee^{\tau\Delta}.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Flat scalar heat-kernel benchmark]
\label{thm:scalar-heat-benchmark}
For \(A=-\Delta\) on \(\mathbb R^d\), \(P=I\), and \(\chi=1\), the finite coherent kernel is
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
  \label{eq:scalar-heat-kernel-theorem-section12}
\end{equation}
Its Fourier transform is
\begin{equation}
  \widehat K_\tau^{(d)}(k)=\ee^{-\tau k^2}.
  \label{eq:scalar-heat-transform-section12}
\end{equation}
It converges distributionally to the Dirac delta:
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)\to\delta^{(d)}(x-y)
  \qquad
  \text{as}
  \qquad
  \tau\downarrow0.
  \label{eq:scalar-delta-limit-section12}
\end{equation}
The coherent length and effective energy scale are
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau},
  \qquad
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2}.
  \label{eq:scalar-scales-section12}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The Fourier representation is
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  \frac{\dd^dk}{(2\pi)^d}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
  \ee^{\ii k\cdot(x-y)}.
\end{equation}
The \(d\)-dimensional Gaussian integral gives
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
\end{equation}
By construction its Fourier transform is
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}.
\end{equation}
For every test function \(f\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^d)\),
\begin{equation}
  \lim_{\tau\downarrow0}
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)f(y)\,\dd^dy
  =
  f(x),
\end{equation}
so \(K_\tau^{(d)}\to\delta^{(d)}\) distributionally. Since \([\tau]=L^2\), the coherent length
is \(\sqrt{\tau}\), and the inverse energy scale is \(\tau^{-1/2}\).
\end{proof}

This benchmark proves explicitly how a finite kernel yields both:
\begin{equation}
  \delta^{(d)}(x-y)
\end{equation}
as a sharp local shadow and
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
\end{equation}
as a spectral damping shadow.

\subsection{Finite propagator benchmark}
\label{subsec:finite-propagator-benchmark-section12}

In the Euclidean scalar benchmark, the ordinary propagator is
\begin{equation}
  \Delta(k)
  =
  \frac{1}{k^2+m^2}.
\end{equation}
A finite coherent insertion gives
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}(k)
  =
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2+m^2}.
  \label{eq:finite-propagator-benchmark-section12}
\end{equation}

This has the position-space form
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}
  =
  K_\tau^{(d)}\ast\Delta.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite propagator}
  =
  \text{ordinary propagator convolved with finite coherent kernel}.
  }
  \label{eq:finite-propagator-box-section12}
\end{equation}

However, as emphasized earlier, this is a Euclidean scalar benchmark. It is not the fundamental
Lorentzian prescription. Lorentzian MTT uses positive internal, spatial, Hamiltonian, or
constraint-compatible operators.

\subsection{Fixed-point/fiber realization statement}
\label{subsec:FP-realization-statement-section12}

In the fixed-point realization, the total geometric model is
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6,
\end{equation}
or a compact internal fiber geometry over a Lorentzian base \(Y^4\). The internal admissibility
operator is
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3\kappa_n\Delta_{B_n},
  \qquad
  \kappa_n>0,
  \label{eq:Aint-theorem-section12}
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}

The coherent projector is
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}\Pi_{B_2}\Pi_{B_3},
  \label{eq:Pcoh-theorem-section12}
\end{equation}
with range
\begin{equation}
  \Ran P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3\ker\Delta_{B_n}.
  \label{eq:joint-harmonic-theorem-section12}
\end{equation}

The fixed-point coherent admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
  \label{eq:FP-Badm-theorem-section12}
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}\phi_j
  =
  p_j\chi(\mu_j^2)^2
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\phi_j.
  \label{eq:FP-modal-action-section12}
\end{equation}

The zero modes satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
\end{equation}
and are undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
The nonzero internal modes satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0,
\end{equation}
and are damped:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{FP/MTT finite admissibility}
  =
  \text{joint harmonic zero-mode survival plus damped internal excitations}.
  }
  \label{eq:FP-admissibility-box-section12}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gap-selected damping scale}
\label{subsec:gap-selected-damping-section12}

Assume the discarded sector has a uniform gap:
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast>0.
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{equation}
  Q=I-P
\end{equation}
be the discarded-sector projector. Suppose the discarded-sector flow satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \|Q\Phi_tQ\|
  \le
  C_Q\ee^{-\lambda_\ast t}.
\end{equation}
Given admissibility tolerance
\begin{equation}
  0<\epsadm<1,
\end{equation}
choose \(\tau\) by
\begin{equation}
  C_Q\ee^{-\lambda_\ast\tau}=\epsadm.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}.
  }
  \label{eq:gap-selected-tau-section12}
\end{equation}

If the typical internal radius is \(R\), then for Laplace-type fibers
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2}.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  }.
  \label{eq:gap-radius-scale-section12}
\end{equation}

Thus the finite coherent width has a geometric origin:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  \sim
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R\sqrt{\log(C_Q/\epsadm)}.
  }
  \label{eq:coherent-width-geometric-section12}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Lorentzian admissibility theorem schema}
\label{subsec:lorentzian-theorem-schema-section12}

The Lorentzian result is not a heat-kernel theorem for \(\Box\). It is an admissibility schema.

A Lorentzian finite-kernel construction is admissible when:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the damping operator is positive in the relevant physical sector;
  \item no naive indefinite \(e^{-\tau\Box}\) is used as a fundamental heat operator;
  \item equal-time kernels are bounded on the relevant energy/Sobolev spaces;
  \item the hyperbolic principal symbol is unchanged;
  \item gauge and gravitational constraints are preserved where relevant;
  \item no controllable superluminal signaling is introduced.
\end{enumerate}

For an equation of the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g\phi+m^2\phi+\mathcal K_t\phi=J,
  \label{eq:kernel-hyperbolic-schema-section12}
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal K_t\) is a bounded equal-time spatial operator, the principal part remains
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g.
\end{equation}
Therefore the characteristic cone is unchanged.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{bounded equal-time finite kernels can preserve Lorentzian finite propagation speed
  because they do not change the principal symbol.}
  }
  \label{eq:lorentzian-schema-box-section12}
\end{equation}

This is the Lorentzian replacement for naive \(e^{-\tau\Box}\).

\subsection{Gauge and gravity admissibility schemas}
\label{subsec:gauge-gravity-schema-section12}

Gauge admissibility requires
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys}.
  \label{eq:gauge-schema-section12}
\end{equation}
The operator must preserve gauge quotienting, Ward identities, BRST cohomology, and current
conservation.

Gravity admissibility requires
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
  \label{eq:gravity-schema-section12}
\end{equation}
The operator must preserve diffeomorphism-compatible geometric content and the gravitational
constraint structure.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite filtering is physical only after the relevant quotient and constraint structure
  has been respected.}
  }
  \label{eq:quotient-constraint-box-section12}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Structural unification corollary}
\label{subsec:structural-unification-corollary}

We can now state the structural MTT conclusion.

\begin{corollary}[Structural projection-shadow unification]
\label{cor:structural-unification}
Suppose a physical sector supplies admissible data
\begin{equation}
  (\mathcal H,A,P,\chi,\tau),
\end{equation}
with \(A\) positive or sector-compatible, \(P\) a valid coherent/physical projector, \(\chi(A)\)
an admissible spectral window, and \(\tau\) a finite damping scale. Suppose further that
gauge, gravitational, measurement, and Lorentzian constraints are respected where relevant.
Then the finite coherent admissibility operator
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
generates sectoral projection shadows. These include:
\begin{align}
  \text{local kernel shadow}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{particle/delta behavior},\\
  \text{spectral kernel shadow}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{wave/interference behavior},\\
  \text{finite detector shadow}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{measurement records},\\
  \text{branch-channel shadow}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{visibility damping},\\
  \text{internal-mode shadow}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{suppressed excitation towers},\\
  \text{quotient-compatible shadow}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{gauge structure},\\
  \text{geometric quotient shadow}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{gravity},\\
  \text{survivor-label shadow}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{quantization},\\
  \text{joint-sector shadow}
  &\Rightarrow
  \text{entanglement}.
\end{align}
\end{corollary}

In compact form:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent admissibility}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{projection shadows}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{effective physics}.
  }
  \label{eq:structural-unification-box-section12}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Why this is not a trivial theorem}
\label{subsec:not-trivial-theorem-section12}

The result is not merely the observation that physics uses Hilbert spaces, operators, or
spectra. Those are already standard. The nontrivial content is the assembly.

The same finite coherent architecture accounts for:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the Dirac-delta limit of local particle-like behavior;
  \item the spectral representation of wave-like behavior;
  \item detector records through finite effects and survivor basins;
  \item decoherence through admissible Schur channels;
  \item scattering form factors through finite kernels or internal-mode damping;
  \item gauge theory through quotient-compatible projection;
  \item electromagnetism through gauge Lens, Circle holonomy, and Nil records;
  \item gravity through diffeomorphism-compatible geometric projection;
  \item quantization through survivor-label discreteness;
  \item entanglement through non-factorizing joint coherent sectors.
\end{enumerate}

The individual pieces are familiar. The contribution is that they are organized by a single
projection architecture:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{coherent sector}
  \xrightarrow{\;\text{finite admissibility}\;}
  \text{physical shadow}.
  }
  \label{eq:coherent-sector-to-shadow-section12}
\end{equation}

\subsection{What the theorem does not prove}
\label{subsec:what-theorem-not-prove-section12}

The structural corollary does not prove all sectoral physics automatically. It does not by
itself derive:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item all Standard Model particles and representations;
  \item all masses and couplings;
  \item the full nonperturbative quantum-gravity state space;
  \item exact collider phenomenology of internal excitations;
  \item all measurement basin measures;
  \item the Born rule in every detector context;
  \item all cosmological or black-hole predictions.
\end{enumerate}

Those require sectoral execution.

The theorem gives the admissibility architecture:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{derive }A,\;P,\;\chi,\;\tau
  \quad
  \text{then compute the sectoral shadows}.
  }
  \label{eq:execution-architecture-section12}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:unification-theorem-summary}

This section separated the paper's claims into precise layers.

The finite-filter theorem states that, under clean spectral assumptions,
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
is a positive bounded admissibility filter with modal weights
\begin{equation}
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
\end{equation}

The Schur-channel theorem states that
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}=1
\end{equation}
makes
\begin{equation}
  \rho\mapsto D\circ\rho
\end{equation}
a valid completely positive trace-preserving branch-damping channel.

The scalar benchmark theorem proves explicitly that
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}\right)
\end{equation}
has Fourier transform
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
\end{equation}
and sharp limit
\begin{equation}
  \delta^{(d)}(x-y).
\end{equation}

The fixed-point realization grounds the abstract operator in compact internal geometry:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}

The structural corollary is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{wave, particle, measurement, scattering, gauge, gravity, quantization, and
  entanglement are projection shadows of finite coherent admissibility.}
  }
\end{equation}

% ============================================================
\section{Relation to existing programs}
\label{sec:relation-existing}
% ============================================================

The previous section separated the analytic theorems from the structural unification
corollary. We now locate the framework relative to existing physical programs.

The purpose of this section is not to claim that MTT replaces all existing approaches. Many
ingredients used here are familiar: spectral theory, heat kernels, decoherence, gauge
quotienting, Kaluza--Klein reduction, constrained Hamiltonian dynamics, and geometric
projection all have long histories. The contribution of MTT is the assembly:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent admissibility}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{projection shadows}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{effective physics}.
  }
\end{equation}

The central novelty is that particle localization, wave interference, measurement records,
gauge structure, gravity, quantization, and entanglement are treated as different shadows of
one finite coherent admissibility architecture.

\subsection{Complementarity}
\label{subsec:relation-complementarity}

Bohr complementarity states that wave-like and particle-like descriptions are mutually
exclusive experimental descriptions. One experimental arrangement reveals interference;
another reveals localized path or detector information.

MTT agrees with the operational lesson but changes the explanatory order.

Complementarity says:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{wave and particle are complementary descriptions.}
  }
\end{equation}

MTT says:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{wave and particle are two projection shadows of one finite coherent kernel.}
  }
\end{equation}

The common source is
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}
The local reading gives the particle shadow:
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)\to\delta(x-y).
\end{equation}
The spectral reading gives the wave shadow:
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y).
\end{equation}

Thus MTT does not deny complementarity. It refines complementarity into projection duality:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{complementary appearances arise from different Lens readings of the same finite
  coherent admissibility structure.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Decoherence}
\label{subsec:relation-decoherence}

Decoherence explains the suppression of interference through entanglement with environmental
degrees of freedom. MTT incorporates this mechanism but distinguishes it from record
selection.

In MTT, branch damping is represented by
\begin{equation}
  \rho\mapsto D\circ\rho,
\end{equation}
with physical admissibility condition
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}=1.
\end{equation}
This is precisely the Schur-channel layer.

However, MTT separates:
\begin{equation}
  \text{coherence loss}
\end{equation}
from
\begin{equation}
  \text{record stabilization}.
\end{equation}
The first is described by
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}\mapsto D_{ab}\rho_{ab}.
\end{equation}
The second is described by survivor-basin capture:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{decoherence explains visibility loss;}
  }
\end{equation}
whereas:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT measurement requires visibility loss plus Nil survivor-basin stabilization.}
  }
\end{equation}

In this sense, MTT is compatible with decoherence but does not identify decoherence alone
with a completed measurement theory.

\subsection{Consistent histories}
\label{subsec:relation-consistent-histories}

Consistent-histories approaches assign probabilities to sets of histories when interference
between histories is sufficiently suppressed. The key condition is that a set of histories must
satisfy a consistency or decoherence condition before classical probabilities can be assigned.

MTT has a closely related structure. A measurement or history context supplies a Lens
decomposition, branch damping suppresses off-diagonal interference, and Nil survivor basins
stabilize records.

In MTT notation:
\begin{equation}
  \text{history branches}
  \quad\leadsto\quad
  \rho_{ab}
  \quad\leadsto\quad
  D_{ab}\rho_{ab}
  \quad\leadsto\quad
  B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

The distinction is that MTT embeds this logic in the broader finite admissibility operator
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}
Thus histories are not the only context in which projection matters. They are one instance
of the more general Circle--Lens--Nil grammar.

\subsection{Relational quantum mechanics}
\label{subsec:relation-relational-QM}

Relational quantum mechanics emphasizes that quantum states and facts are relative to
systems or interactions. MTT agrees that measurement context matters. In MTT language,
this context-dependence is Lens structure.

A measurement record is not an absolute context-free primitive. It is a finite survivor record
relative to a measurement context:
\begin{equation}
  \Meas
  =
  (A_{\Meas},P_{\Meas},\chi_{\Meas},\tau_{\Meas},
  \{E_i^{(\Meas)}\},\frakB_{\Meas}).
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{facts are Lens-relative and Nil-stabilized.}
  }
\end{equation}

The difference is that MTT gives an explicit finite-kernel architecture for this contextuality:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\Meas}
  =
  P_{\Meas}\chi(A_{\Meas})
  \ee^{-\tau_{\Meas}A_{\Meas}}
  \chi(A_{\Meas})P_{\Meas}.
\end{equation}

Therefore MTT can be read as a finite coherent projection realization of a relational idea.

\subsection{Quantum field theory}
\label{subsec:relation-QFT}

Quantum field theory already treats particles as excitations of fields rather than classical
point objects. MTT is compatible with this. The MTT contribution is not to reintroduce
classical particles but to reinterpret pointlike field-theoretic objects as sharp projection
shadows.

In ordinary QFT, one often uses:
\begin{equation}
  \delta(x-y),
\end{equation}
local fields,
\begin{equation}
  \phi(x),
\end{equation}
and pointlike vertices,
\begin{equation}
  \int \phi(x)^4\,\dd^dx.
\end{equation}

MTT reads these as limiting structures:
\begin{equation}
  \delta(x-y)
  =
  \text{sharp local shadow},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
  \phi(x)
  =
  \text{local field representative},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \text{point vertex}
  =
  \text{sharp limit of finite coherent overlap}.
\end{equation}

In the Euclidean scalar benchmark, this replacement is explicit:
\begin{equation}
  \delta^{(d)}(x-y)
  \rightsquigarrow
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}\right).
\end{equation}

In the FP/fiber realization, the more physical statement is:
\begin{equation}
  \text{4D local QFT}
  =
  \text{zero-mode low-resolution shadow of fiber-coherent admissibility}.
\end{equation}

Thus MTT does not reject QFT. It reframes the ideal local objects of QFT as effective shadows
of finite coherent structure.

\subsection{Renormalization group and effective field theory}
\label{subsec:relation-RG-EFT}

The renormalization group already teaches that physics depends on scale. Effective field
theory organizes low-energy descriptions without requiring the same variables to remain
valid at arbitrarily high energy.

MTT agrees with this scale-dependent view. The finite coherent scale
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau},
\end{equation}
or
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2},
\end{equation}
marks the scale at which finite admissibility becomes resolvable.

At energies
\begin{equation}
  E\ll\Lambda_{\rm eff},
\end{equation}
the pointlike effective description is valid. At energies
\begin{equation}
  E\sim\Lambda_{\rm eff},
\end{equation}
finite coherent structure may become visible.

The difference from ordinary EFT is that \(\tau\) is not merely an arbitrary cutoff. In the
FP realization,
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
\end{equation}
so the coherent scale is tied to the internal spectral gap:
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT is compatible with EFT, but gives the finite scale a geometric admissibility
  interpretation.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Kaluza--Klein theory}
\label{subsec:relation-KK}

The fixed-point realization of MTT has an explicit Kaluza--Klein-style backbone. The total
space is
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6,
\end{equation}
or a compact internal fiber geometry over a four-dimensional Lorentzian base. Internal
spectra produce zero modes and massive internal excitations:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j.
\end{equation}

After dimensional reduction, the schematic four-dimensional propagator is
\begin{equation}
  G_{4D}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{Z_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}}
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
\end{equation}

The zero mode satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
\end{equation}
and is undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
Nonzero internal modes are damped:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1,
  \qquad
  \mu_j^2>0.
\end{equation}

Thus MTT should be honest about the KK connection:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{FP-realized MTT is KK-compatible and uses a compact internal spectral backbone.}
  }
\end{equation}

The distinction is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{ordinary KK: compact dimensions produce mode towers;}
  }
\end{equation}
whereas:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT/FP: compact dimensions plus finite coherent admissibility produce projection
  shadows across sectors.}
  }
\end{equation}

MTT is therefore not ``unrelated to KK.'' It is a projection-theoretic extension of a
KK-compatible geometric structure.

\subsection{String theory}
\label{subsec:relation-string-theory}

String theory also replaces point particles with extended objects and uses extra dimensions,
compactification, worldsheet conformal structure, and dualities. It provides one of the most
developed frameworks in which pointlike particles emerge as effective low-energy shadows.

MTT shares several broad themes:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item pointlike objects are not fundamental primitives;
  \item compact internal geometry matters;
  \item observed four-dimensional physics may be a lower-dimensional shadow;
  \item dualities can relate apparently different descriptions;
  \item gauge and gravity are deeply geometric.
\end{enumerate}

However, MTT does not assume that the fundamental object is a string or brane. Its central
object is the finite coherent admissibility operator:
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}

Thus the comparison is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{string theory: extended fundamental objects and worldsheet consistency;}
  }
\end{equation}
while:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT: finite coherent admissibility and projection shadows.}
  }
\end{equation}

The two are not necessarily incompatible. A string compactification could, in principle,
supply sectoral data \(A,P,\chi,\tau\) for an MTT-style finite admissibility analysis. But MTT
does not require the full string-theoretic machinery in order to state its structural claim.

\subsection{Holography}
\label{subsec:relation-holography}

Holography teaches that bulk gravitational physics may be encoded in lower-dimensional
boundary data. It emphasizes that locality and spacetime geometry can be emergent or
representation-dependent.

MTT is compatible with this lesson. In MTT language, holography is a powerful Lens
principle:
\begin{equation}
  \text{bulk geometry}
  \quad
  \leftrightarrow
  \quad
  \text{boundary representation}.
\end{equation}

The MTT contribution is to place this among a broader class of projection shadows. The same
logic that makes geometry representation-dependent also appears in:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item wave/particle duality;
  \item gauge quotienting;
  \item measurement context;
  \item entanglement factorization;
  \item finite internal-mode projection.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{holography is a deep geometric Lens duality;}
  }
\end{equation}
whereas:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT treats Lens projection as a general structural principle across sectors.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Loop and holonomy approaches}
\label{subsec:relation-loop-holonomy}

Loop quantum gravity and related holonomy approaches emphasize connections, holonomies,
spin networks, fluxes, and diffeomorphism-compatible geometric labels.

MTT shares the importance of:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item holonomy as Circle structure;
  \item diffeomorphism quotienting as Lens structure;
  \item discrete geometric labels as Nil survivor labels;
  \item constraint compatibility in gravity.
\end{enumerate}

The gravitational MTT operator has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
\end{equation}

This is not a replacement for a full loop quantization. Rather, it states what any
MTT-compatible gravitational sector must supply:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm grav},
  \qquad
  P_{\rm diff},
  \qquad
  \chi,
  \qquad
  \tau.
\end{equation}

A loop or holonomy theory could potentially provide these data. MTT then supplies the finite
coherent projection grammar.

\subsection{Causal set and discrete spacetime approaches}
\label{subsec:relation-causal-discrete}

Discrete spacetime approaches posit that spacetime may be fundamentally discrete or
partially ordered. MTT does not begin by assuming spacetime discreteness. Instead, it treats
discrete labels as admissible survivor labels.

Thus MTT distinguishes:
\begin{equation}
  \text{discrete substrate}
\end{equation}
from
\begin{equation}
  \text{discrete survivor shadow}.
\end{equation}

A finite detector record is discrete, but this does not automatically imply that spacetime is a
lattice. A compact fiber spectrum is discrete, but this does not automatically imply that the
base is discrete.

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT allows discrete effective labels without requiring spacetime to be fundamentally
  discrete.}
  }
\end{equation}

If a discrete spacetime model supplies a sectoral admissibility operator and coherent
projector, it may be expressible in MTT terms. But discreteness is not the starting axiom of
MTT.

\subsection{Nonlocal field theories}
\label{subsec:relation-nonlocal-field-theories}

Many nonlocal field theories introduce form factors such as
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}
\end{equation}
or other functions of the d'Alembertian. These theories often face difficulties with causality,
unitarity, analyticity, and extra degrees of freedom.

MTT differs sharply from naive Lorentzian nonlocal field theory. The fundamental Lorentzian
principle is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A\neq\Box
  \quad
  \text{as a naive indefinite heat operator}.
  }
\end{equation}

Instead, MTT uses:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item positive internal/fiber operators;
  \item positive spatial Cauchy-slice operators;
  \item Hamiltonian or constraint-compatible operators;
  \item gauge-quotiented physical operators;
  \item diffeomorphism-compatible geometric operators;
  \item bounded equal-time kernels that preserve the principal hyperbolic symbol.
\end{enumerate}

Thus MTT finite kernels are not arbitrary acausal nonlocal modifications. They are admissible
projection kernels constrained by positivity, quotienting, and causal structure.

\subsection{Open quantum systems}
\label{subsec:relation-open-systems}

Open quantum systems provide the mathematical language of channels, decoherence, reduced
states, and environment-induced dynamics. MTT uses this language in the measurement and
branch-damping layer.

The Schur damping theorem is an open-system result:
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}=1
  \quad
  \Rightarrow
  \quad
  \rho\mapsto D\circ\rho
  \text{ is CPTP}.
\end{equation}

MTT adds the survivor-basin layer:
\begin{equation}
  D\circ\rho
  \to
  B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{open-system channels describe admissible damping;}
  }
\end{equation}
while:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT adds record stabilization as Nil basin selection.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Hidden-variable and collapse theories}
\label{subsec:relation-hidden-collapse}

Hidden-variable theories attempt to restore definite underlying variables. Collapse theories
modify the dynamics to produce definite outcomes.

MTT is different from both.

It does not introduce pre-existing local classical variables for every possible measurement
context. In Bell-type situations, MTT treats local records as Nil shadows of a joint coherent
sector, not as pre-existing classical values.

It also does not postulate a universal stochastic collapse law as the primitive dynamics.
Instead, it treats record formation as survivor-basin stabilization in a finite measurement
context:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT is not a local hidden-variable theory and not a primitive collapse theory.}
  }
\end{equation}

It is a finite coherent projection theory with contextual survivor records.

\subsection{Relation to ordinary quantum mechanics}
\label{subsec:relation-ordinary-QM}

Ordinary quantum mechanics is recovered when:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the coherent sector is fixed;
  \item the relevant finite width is below experimental resolution;
  \item the zero modes dominate;
  \item measurement effects reproduce the usual POVM or PVM predictions;
  \item basin measures reproduce Born probabilities.
\end{enumerate}

Then MTT reduces operationally to the usual formalism:
\begin{equation}
  p(i)=\tr(\rho E_i).
\end{equation}

The difference is interpretive and structural. Ordinary quantum mechanics begins with states,
observables, and Born probabilities. MTT asks what finite admissibility structure produces:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item local particle records;
  \item wave interference;
  \item detector effects;
  \item branch damping;
  \item survivor records;
  \item quantized labels;
  \item entangled joint sectors.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{ordinary quantum mechanics is the effective operational shadow of MTT in sectors
  where the standard POVM/Born structure is recovered.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{What MTT adds}
\label{subsec:what-MTT-adds}

MTT adds the following structural claims:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item Point particles are Dirac-delta shadows of finite coherent kernels.
  \item Waves are spectral phase shadows of the same kernels.
  \item Measurement records are survivor-basin shadows.
  \item Visibility loss is admissible Schur damping.
  \item Gauge theory is Lens quotient structure.
  \item Electromagnetism combines gauge Lens, Circle holonomy, and Nil records.
  \item Gravity is diffeomorphism-compatible geometric projection.
  \item Quantization is admissible survivor-label structure.
  \item Entanglement is non-factorizing joint coherent admissibility.
  \item The fixed-point realization gives a KK-compatible compact-fiber source for the finite
  coherent scale.
\end{enumerate}

The core addition is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
  }
\end{equation}
as a common finite admissibility engine, together with:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Circle/\Lens/\Nil
  }
\end{equation}
as the structural grammar of its physical shadows.

\subsection{What MTT does not add}
\label{subsec:what-MTT-does-not-add}

MTT does not claim that every existing theory is wrong. It does not claim to have already
computed every sectoral observable. It does not claim to replace:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item quantum field theory as a computational framework;
  \item gauge theory as the structure of known interactions;
  \item general relativity as the classical theory of gravity;
  \item effective field theory as the low-energy expansion method;
  \item decoherence as the mechanism of visibility loss;
  \item KK or compactification methods where internal geometry is relevant.
\end{enumerate}

Instead, MTT reframes these as sectoral shadows of finite coherent admissibility where the
operator data can be supplied:
\begin{equation}
  A,\qquad P,\qquad \chi,\qquad \tau.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT is an organizing and execution framework, not a declaration that existing
  machinery is obsolete.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:relation-existing-summary}

MTT is compatible with many existing insights but assembles them differently.

It refines complementarity by giving wave and particle a common finite kernel source.

It incorporates decoherence but distinguishes coherence loss from survivor-record selection.

It is compatible with QFT and EFT but treats pointlike local objects as sharp shadows.

It honestly acknowledges its KK-compatible FP backbone:
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6,
\end{equation}
with internal-mode damping:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

It avoids naive Lorentzian nonlocality by rejecting \(e^{-\tau\Box}\) as the fundamental
damping rule.

It aligns with gauge and gravitational quotient structures by requiring:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
\end{equation}

The unifying statement is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT is a finite coherent admissibility architecture whose physical content appears as
  projection shadows across quantum, gauge, geometric, and measurement sectors.}
  }
\end{equation}
% ============================================================
\section{Phenomenology and execution tasks}
\label{sec:phenomenology}
% ============================================================

The preceding sections established the structural claim: finite coherent admissibility gives
rise to local delta shadows, spectral wave shadows, measurement records, scattering form
factors, gauge quotienting, gravitational projection, quantized survivor labels, and
entanglement structure. This section turns to the execution question:

\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{What would the framework predict, constrain, or require quantitatively?}
  }
\end{equation}

The answer depends on the sectoral data:
\begin{equation}
  A,\qquad P,\qquad \chi,\qquad \tau,
\end{equation}
and, in the fixed-point realization, on the internal geometry:
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6.
\end{equation}

The main phenomenological message is:

\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{ordinary four-dimensional low-energy physics is recovered by undamped zero modes,
  while finite coherent corrections enter through suppressed internal modes, finite overlaps,
  detector profiles, and threshold effects.}
  }
  \label{eq:phenomenology-main-message}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Geometric origin of the coherence scale}
\label{subsec:geometric-origin-coherence-scale}

In the fixed-point realization, the admissibility operator is the positive internal operator
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3
  \kappa_n\Delta_{B_n},
  \qquad
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}
The internal fibers are compact, so the nonzero fiber spectrum has a positive gap. Let
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast>0
\end{equation}
denote the relevant uniform spectral gap.

The damping-selected admissibility time is
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
  \label{eq:tau-phenomenology}
\end{equation}
where \(C_Q\) is the discarded-sector constant and \(\epsadm\) is the admissibility tolerance.

If the typical internal radius is \(R\), then for Laplace-type internal operators,
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2}.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  \sim
  R^2
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
  \label{eq:tau-R-phenomenology}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  }.
  \label{eq:lcoh-R-phenomenology}
\end{equation}

The effective energy scale is
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}
  =
  \tau^{-1/2}
  \sim
  \frac{1}{R}
  \left(
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  \right)^{-1/2}.
  \label{eq:Lambdaeff-R-phenomenology}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the finite coherent scale is not arbitrary; it is tied to the internal spectral gap.}
  }
  \label{eq:coherence-scale-geometric-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Zero-mode recovery}
\label{subsec:zero-mode-recovery-phenomenology}

Let
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j.
\end{equation}
The admissibility weight is
\begin{equation}
  W_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
  \label{eq:mode-weight-phenomenology}
\end{equation}

The zero mode satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  W_0=\ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}

This gives the central low-energy recovery statement:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{zero modes are not suppressed by internal finite admissibility.}
  }
  \label{eq:zero-mode-not-suppressed}
\end{equation}

Hence ordinary four-dimensional low-energy propagation is recovered from zero-mode sectors.
In a scalar benchmark, the effective four-dimensional propagator has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  G_{4D}(p)
  =
  \frac{Z_0}{p^2-m_0^2+\ii\epsilon}
  +
  \sum_{j\ge1}
  \frac{Z_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}}
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
  \label{eq:4D-propagator-phenomenology}
\end{equation}

The first term is the ordinary zero-mode contribution. The remaining terms are suppressed
internal excitations.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT corrections are not generic low-energy distortions of the zero mode; they are
  suppressed excitation, overlap, and threshold effects.}
  }
  \label{eq:not-low-energy-distortion}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Internal excitation scale}
\label{subsec:internal-excitation-scale}

The first nonzero internal eigenvalue defines a mass scale
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1^2\sim\lambda_\ast.
\end{equation}
The corresponding internal excitation mass is
\begin{equation}
  M_{\rm int}\sim\mu_1\sim R^{-1}.
  \label{eq:Mint-R}
\end{equation}

The admissibility weight of the first internal excitation is
\begin{equation}
  W_1
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_1^2}.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1^2\simeq\lambda_\ast,
\end{equation}
then using \eqref{eq:tau-phenomenology},
\begin{equation}
  W_1
  \simeq
  \exp\left(
    -
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  \right)
  =
  \frac{\epsadm}{C_Q}.
  \label{eq:first-mode-weight-phenomenology}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the first internal excitation carries amplitude-level admissibility weight }
  W_1\simeq\epsadm/C_Q
  \text{ when } \mu_1^2\simeq\lambda_\ast.
  }
  \label{eq:first-mode-weight-box}
\end{equation}

This is a clean structural prediction. However, an observed cross section depends on more
than \(W_1\). It also depends on effective couplings, widths, internal wavefunction overlaps,
phase space, spin, gauge representation, background interference, and detector efficiencies.

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  W_j=\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  \text{ is an amplitude-level admissibility weight, not by itself a universal cross-section
  prediction.}
  }
  \label{eq:weight-not-cross-section}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Effective four-dimensional tower}
\label{subsec:effective-4D-tower}

The reduced four-dimensional theory contains zero modes and nonzero internal excitations.
A generic propagator or response function has the schematic tower form
\begin{equation}
  G_{\rm eff}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{
    Z_j
    \chi(\mu_j^2)^2
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
  \label{eq:generic-effective-tower}
\end{equation}

The residues \(Z_j\) encode internal overlap and normalization. The window
\begin{equation}
  \chi(\mu_j^2)
\end{equation}
selects the admissible spectral band. The damping
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
\end{equation}
suppresses nonzero internal modes.

For \(j=0\),
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
\end{equation}
and therefore
\begin{equation}
  \chi(\mu_0^2)^2\ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}
  =
  \chi(0)^2.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  \chi(0)=1,
\end{equation}
then the zero mode is fully retained.

Thus the low-energy consistency condition is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \chi(0)=1,
  \qquad
  Z_0>0,
  \qquad
  \mu_0=0.
  }
  \label{eq:zero-mode-consistency}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Phenomenological bound template}
\label{subsec:phenomenological-bound-template}

Suppose an experiment excludes internal excitations below a scale
\begin{equation}
  M_{\rm exp}.
\end{equation}
Then one obtains the template bound
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1\gtrsim M_{\rm exp}.
\end{equation}
Since
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1\sim R^{-1},
\end{equation}
this gives
\begin{equation}
  R\lesssim M_{\rm exp}^{-1}.
  \label{eq:R-bound-template}
\end{equation}

The coherence length then satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \lesssim
  M_{\rm exp}^{-1}
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  }.
  \label{eq:lcoh-bound-template}
\end{equation}

Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}
  =
  \tau^{-1/2}
  \gtrsim
  M_{\rm exp}
  \left(
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  \right)^{-1/2}.
  \label{eq:Lambda-bound-template}
\end{equation}

This is the general translation from experimental non-observation to MTT internal scale:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{bounds on internal excitations}
  \Rightarrow
  \text{bounds on }R,\sqrt{\tau},\Lambda_{\rm eff}.
  }
  \label{eq:bound-translation-box}
\end{equation}

Precise numerical values require a specified internal geometry, gauge embedding, coupling
normalization, and detector channel.

\subsection{Illustrative tolerance benchmark}
\label{subsec:illustrative-tolerance-benchmark}

As an illustrative benchmark, suppose
\begin{equation}
  C_Q\simeq1
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \epsadm=10^{-3}.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  =
  \log(10^3)
  \simeq
  6.9.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \simeq
  2.6R.
  \label{eq:illustrative-sqrttau}
\end{equation}
The first-mode admissibility weight, if \(\mu_1^2\simeq\lambda_\ast\), is
\begin{equation}
  W_1\simeq10^{-3}.
  \label{eq:illustrative-W1}
\end{equation}

This benchmark is useful for scale intuition, but it is not a universal prediction. A different
\(\epsadm\), \(C_Q\), or internal spectrum changes the numerical coefficient.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \epsadm
  \text{ must ultimately be fixed by sectoral admissibility, not chosen by hand.}
  }
  \label{eq:epsadm-execution-task}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Collider template}
\label{subsec:collider-template}

Collider signatures arise if internal excitations couple to visible four-dimensional fields.
The generic amplitude contribution from an internal excitation \(j\) is
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal A_j(p)
  \sim
  g_j^{\rm in}g_j^{\rm out}
  \frac{
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii m_j\Gamma_j},
  \label{eq:collider-amplitude-template}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  g_j^{\rm in},\quad g_j^{\rm out}
\end{equation}
are effective production and decay couplings, and \(\Gamma_j\) is the width.

The corresponding observable cross section depends on
\begin{equation}
  |\mathcal A_{\rm SM}+\sum_j\mathcal A_j|^2.
\end{equation}
Therefore, even if the amplitude-level MTT weight is
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2},
\end{equation}
the cross-section effect may scale differently depending on interference, widths, and
coupling suppression.

The execution tasks for collider phenomenology are:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item specify the internal geometry \(X^6\);
  \item compute \(\mu_j^2\);
  \item compute internal wavefunction overlaps;
  \item derive effective couplings \(g_j\);
  \item impose gauge and representation constraints;
  \item compute widths \(\Gamma_j\);
  \item calculate observable cross sections;
  \item compare with collider exclusions.
\end{enumerate}

Thus the robust MTT collider statement is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{internal excitations appear, if at all, as tower-like states with admissibility weights }
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
  }
  \label{eq:collider-robust-statement}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Precision-electroweak and QED templates}
\label{subsec:precision-QED-template}

Low-energy precision observables receive corrections only if internal excitations couple to
the relevant sector. A generic heavy-mode correction to a low-energy observable \(\mathcal O\)
has the form
\begin{equation}
  \delta\mathcal O
  \sim
  \sum_{j\ge1}
  C_j
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  \left(
    \frac{E_{\rm obs}}{\mu_j}
  \right)^{d_j},
  \label{eq:precision-correction-template}
\end{equation}
where:
\begin{align}
  C_j &: \text{effective coupling/overlap coefficient},\\
  E_{\rm obs} &: \text{observable energy scale},\\
  d_j &: \text{operator-dimension or decoupling power}.
\end{align}

When
\begin{equation}
  E_{\rm obs}\ll\mu_1,
\end{equation}
such corrections are suppressed both by heavy-mode decoupling and by the admissibility factor
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{precision low-energy tests constrain MTT primarily through suppressed virtual internal
  modes and effective operators.}
  }
  \label{eq:precision-tests-box}
\end{equation}

A complete analysis must derive the coefficients \(C_j\) from the internal geometry and gauge
embedding.

\subsection{Coulomb-law and finite-source tests}
\label{subsec:Coulomb-tests}

The Euclidean finite-source benchmark replaces a point charge by
\begin{equation}
  \rho_\tau(\mathbf r)
  =
  q(4\pi\tau)^{-3/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{r^2}{4\tau}
  \right),
\end{equation}
with potential
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_\tau(r)
  =
  \frac{q}{4\pi r}
  \operatorname{erf}
  \left(
    \frac{r}{2\sqrt{\tau}}
  \right).
  \label{eq:finite-Coulomb-template}
\end{equation}

This benchmark shows how a finite four-dimensional source profile would soften a Coulomb
singularity.

However, in the FP/fiber realization, the finite width is primarily internal unless a physical
four-dimensional finite source is derived. Therefore four-dimensional Coulomb deviations
should be described by internal-mode exchange:
\begin{equation}
  \Phi(r)
  =
  \frac{q}{4\pi r}
  \left[
    1+
    \sum_{j\ge1}
    c_j
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
    \ee^{-\mu_j r}
  \right],
  \label{eq:Coulomb-internal-mode-template}
\end{equation}
where \(c_j\) depends on the internal gauge-sector overlap.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{direct Gaussian Coulomb smearing is a benchmark; FP-realized Coulomb corrections are
  internal-mode Yukawa-type corrections unless a 4D finite source is derived.}
  }
  \label{eq:Coulomb-caution-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gravitational short-distance template}
\label{subsec:gravitational-short-distance-template}

A gravitational analogue involves finite stress-energy profiles or internal-mode corrections.
A point-mass source may be replaced schematically by
\begin{equation}
  T_{\mu\nu}^{\rm adm}(x)
  \sim
  m u_\mu u_\nu K_{\rm adm}(x,x_0),
\end{equation}
but only if the resulting source satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \nabla_\mu T^{\mu\nu}=0
\end{equation}
and the gravitational constraints.

In the FP/fiber realization, gravitational corrections may instead appear through suppressed
internal modes:
\begin{equation}
  V_{\rm grav}(r)
  =
  -\frac{Gm_1m_2}{r}
  \left[
    1+
    \sum_{j\ge1}
    a_j
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
    \ee^{-\mu_j r}
  \right].
  \label{eq:gravity-yukawa-template}
\end{equation}

The coefficients \(a_j\) depend on the gravitational sector, internal geometry, boundary data,
and physical spin/geometric projection.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{short-distance gravity constrains internal geometric modes only after the
  diffeomorphism-compatible sector is derived.}
  }
  \label{eq:gravity-phenomenology-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Measurement phenomenology}
\label{subsec:measurement-phenomenology}

Measurement experiments constrain the branch-damping and survivor-basin layer.

For a two-branch interferometer, the visibility law is
\begin{equation}
  V_{\rm obs}
  =
  |D_{12}|V_0.
  \label{eq:visibility-phenomenology}
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  D_{12}
  =
  \ee^{-\tau_{\Meas}\Lambda_{12}^{(\Meas)}},
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \log\frac{V_0}{V_{\rm obs}}
  =
  \tau_{\Meas}\Lambda_{12}^{(\Meas)}.
  \label{eq:visibility-log-law}
\end{equation}

Thus interferometry can constrain:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item detector-induced branch distinguishability \(\Lambda_{12}^{(\Meas)}\);
  \item measurement damping scale \(\tau_{\Meas}\);
  \item whether damping is reversible;
  \item whether survivor-basin capture has occurred.
\end{enumerate}

The key distinction is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{visibility loss measures branch damping, not directly outcome probability.}
  }
\end{equation}

Outcome frequencies require basin measures:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(i)
  =
  \frac{
    \mu(B_i^{(\Meas)})
  }{
    \sum_j\mu(B_j^{(\Meas)})
  }.
\end{equation}

Thus a measurement execution program must separately fit:
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}^{(\Meas)}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \mu(B_i^{(\Meas)}).
\end{equation}

\subsection{Quantum eraser and weak-measurement tests}
\label{subsec:quantum-eraser-weak-tests}

Weak measurement and quantum eraser experiments are useful because they separate branch
marking from irreversible record formation.

Let marker states be
\begin{equation}
  |m_1\rangle,
  \qquad
  |m_2\rangle.
\end{equation}
The surviving coherence is controlled by
\begin{equation}
  D_{12}
  =
  \langle m_2|m_1\rangle.
  \label{eq:marker-overlap-phenomenology}
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  |D_{12}|<1,
\end{equation}
visibility is reduced. If the marker degrees of freedom are later measured in a recombining
basis, conditional interference may reappear.

MTT predicts the distinction:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{reversible branch marking}
  \neq
  \text{irreversible survivor-basin capture}.
  }
  \label{eq:weak-eraser-distinction}
\end{equation}

Thus the relevant experimental observables are:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item raw visibility;
  \item conditional visibility after erasure;
  \item marker overlap;
  \item record stability;
  \item environmental irreversibility;
  \item basin capture probability.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Entanglement and no-signaling tests}
\label{subsec:entanglement-phenomenology}

Entanglement phenomenology constrains the joint coherent-sector and measurement-basin
structure.

For local settings \(x,y\), outcomes \(a,b\), and joint probabilities \(p(a,b|x,y)\), MTT must
reproduce:
\begin{equation}
  p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \tr\left[
    \rho_{AB}
    E_{ab}^{(x,y)}
  \right]
\end{equation}
in standard quantum benchmark sectors.

It must also satisfy no-signaling:
\begin{equation}
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y')
  \label{eq:no-signaling-phenomenology-A}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \sum_a p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \sum_a p(a,b|x',y).
  \label{eq:no-signaling-phenomenology-B}
\end{equation}

The MTT execution task is to identify:
\begin{equation}
  A_{AB},
  \qquad
  P_{AB},
  \qquad
  \chi_{AB},
  \qquad
  \tau_{AB},
\end{equation}
and the joint survivor basins
\begin{equation}
  B_{ab}^{(x,y)}
\end{equation}
such that the basin measure reproduces quantum correlations without enabling signaling.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{entanglement tests constrain non-factorizing admissibility and joint basin measure.}
  }
  \label{eq:entanglement-tests-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gauge-sector execution tasks}
\label{subsec:gauge-execution-tasks-phenomenology}

A phenomenologically viable gauge sector must supply:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm gauge},
  \qquad
  P_{\rm phys},
  \qquad
  \chi_{\rm gauge},
  \qquad
  \tau_{\rm gauge}.
\end{equation}
It must also satisfy:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item correct gauge group;
  \item correct representation content;
  \item anomaly cancellation;
  \item Ward or Slavnov--Taylor identity preservation;
  \item current conservation;
  \item positive physical-state space;
  \item undamped observed zero modes;
  \item admissibly suppressed internal excitations;
  \item agreement with precision and collider data.
\end{enumerate}

The effective gauge-sector tower has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  D_{\mu\nu}^{\rm eff}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{
    Z_j
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}
  P_{\mu\nu}^{(j)}(p).
\end{equation}

The zero-mode consistency condition is:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1,
\end{equation}
with the correct observed gauge boson sector after symmetry breaking.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge phenomenology requires quotient-compatible towers, not arbitrary damped
  propagators.}
  }
  \label{eq:gauge-phenomenology-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gravity-sector execution tasks}
\label{subsec:gravity-execution-tasks-phenomenology}

A phenomenologically viable gravity sector must supply:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm grav},
  \qquad
  P_{\rm diff},
  \qquad
  \chi_{\rm grav},
  \qquad
  \tau_{\rm grav}.
\end{equation}
It must also satisfy:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item diffeomorphism compatibility;
  \item Hamiltonian and momentum constraint preservation;
  \item recovery of classical general relativity;
  \item positive physical graviton sector in perturbative regimes;
  \item stress-energy conservation;
  \item causal propagation;
  \item consistency with weak-field and gravitational-wave observations;
  \item admissible treatment of horizons and boundaries.
\end{enumerate}

The weak-field benchmark is:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm grav}^{\rm lin}
  =
  P_{\rm TT}\chi(A_{\rm TT})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm TT}}
  \chi(A_{\rm TT})P_{\rm TT}.
  \label{eq:weak-gravity-execution}
\end{equation}

The full nonperturbative task is much harder and requires a genuine diffeomorphism-compatible
state space.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravity phenomenology must be computed after geometric projection and constraint
  preservation.}
  }
  \label{eq:gravity-execution-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Standard Model embedding tasks}
\label{subsec:SM-embedding-tasks}

A complete Standard Model embedding requires the internal geometry to generate or support:
\begin{equation}
  SU(3)\times SU(2)\times U(1),
\end{equation}
fermion representations, chirality, generations, Yukawa structure, anomaly cancellation,
symmetry breaking, and observed low-energy couplings.

In MTT terms, the execution problem is to derive:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm SM},
  \qquad
  P_{\rm SM},
  \qquad
  \chi_{\rm SM},
  \qquad
  \tau_{\rm SM},
\end{equation}
from the internal/fiber geometry and quotient structure.

The minimal checklist is:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item recover the correct gauge group;
  \item recover the correct chiral matter;
  \item cancel anomalies;
  \item produce three observed generations or explain their effective emergence;
  \item derive Yukawa and mixing structures;
  \item recover observed gauge couplings at low energy;
  \item suppress unobserved internal excitations;
  \item preserve Lorentzian and gauge admissibility.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Standard Model closure is a concrete execution program, not an assumption of the
  structural theorem.}
  }
  \label{eq:SM-closure-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Predictions versus templates}
\label{subsec:predictions-versus-templates}

It is useful to distinguish three levels of phenomenological statement.

\paragraph{Level 1: structural predictions.}
These follow from the architecture:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item zero modes are undamped by internal admissibility;
  \item nonzero internal modes carry weights \(\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\);
  \item \(\tau\) is tied to the spectral gap;
  \item pointlike behavior is recovered below resolution;
  \item branch visibility is controlled by admissible damping matrices;
  \item gauge and gravity filters must preserve quotient/constraint structure.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Level 2: model templates.}
These require a chosen internal geometry and sectoral operators:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item effective tower propagators;
  \item Yukawa corrections;
  \item collider resonance templates;
  \item precision observable corrections;
  \item weak-field gravity corrections.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Level 3: numerical predictions.}
These require full sectoral execution:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item internal spectrum;
  \item wavefunction overlaps;
  \item gauge representation content;
  \item couplings;
  \item widths;
  \item detector acceptances;
  \item renormalization and matching.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{this paper establishes Level 1 and supplies Level 2 templates; Level 3 is future
  sectoral computation.}
  }
  \label{eq:levels-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{A compact execution table}
\label{subsec:execution-table}

The core phenomenological quantities are:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|c|c}
Quantity & Symbol & MTT/FP meaning\\
\hline
Internal radius & \(R\) & compact fiber size\\
Fiber spectral gap & \(\lambda_\ast\) & first nonzero admissibility gap\\
Coherent time & \(\tau\) & damping-selected admissibility scale\\
Coherent length & \(\ell_{\rm coh}\) & \(\sqrt{\tau}\)\\
Effective scale & \(\Lambda_{\rm eff}\) & \(\tau^{-1/2}\)\\
First excitation & \(\mu_1\) & first nonzero internal mass scale\\
Mode weight & \(W_j\) & \(\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\)\\
Visibility factor & \(D_{ab}\) & branch coherence survival\\
Basin measure & \(\mu(B_i)\) & outcome-frequency weight
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The key relations are:
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \sqrt{\tau},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}
  =
  \tau^{-1/2},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  W_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{What would falsify or constrain the framework}
\label{subsec:falsification-constraints}

The framework can be constrained or falsified at several levels.

\paragraph{Analytic falsification.}
If a proposed sectoral \(A\), \(P\), \(\chi\), \(\tau\) fails positivity, boundedness, quotient
compatibility, or causal admissibility, that sectoral implementation fails.

\paragraph{Gauge falsification.}
If the finite filter violates Ward identities, Slavnov--Taylor identities, BRST cohomology, or
anomaly cancellation, the gauge implementation fails.

\paragraph{Gravity falsification.}
If the gravitational filter violates constraints, stress-energy conservation, or diffeomorphism
compatibility, the gravitational implementation fails.

\paragraph{Phenomenological falsification.}
If a specified internal geometry predicts unsuppressed excitations, deviations, or new
couplings already excluded by experiment, that model is ruled out or constrained.

\paragraph{Measurement falsification.}
If a proposed damping matrix is not positive semidefinite, or if its visibility predictions fail
controlled interferometry, that measurement model fails.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT is not immune to falsification; each sectoral realization must satisfy analytic,
  quotient, causal, and observational tests.}
  }
  \label{eq:falsification-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:phenomenology-summary}

The phenomenological content of the fixed-point realization is organized by the internal
spectral gap:
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}.
\end{equation}
For an internal scale \(R\),
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2},
\end{equation}
so
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  }.
\end{equation}

Zero modes are undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
Nonzero internal modes are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
\end{equation}

The generic effective tower is:
\begin{equation}
  G_{\rm eff}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{
    Z_j\chi(\mu_j^2)^2\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
\end{equation}

The robust phenomenological predictions are structural:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item low-energy zero-mode recovery;
  \item suppressed internal excitations;
  \item finite coherent scale tied to the internal spectral gap;
  \item visibility damping controlled by positive Schur channels;
  \item gauge and gravity corrections constrained by quotient compatibility;
  \item pointlike behavior recovered below resolution.
\end{enumerate}

Precise numerical predictions require a completed sectoral model.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT becomes predictive when the internal geometry, sectoral projectors, admissibility
  windows, and basin measures are explicitly computed.}
  }
\end{equation}
% ============================================================
\section{Domain of validity and failure modes}
\label{sec:failure-modes}
% ============================================================

The previous section described the phenomenological execution program. This section records
the domain of validity of the framework and the main ways in which a proposed MTT
realization can fail.

This is necessary because the finite coherent admissibility operator
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
is not automatically physical for arbitrary choices of \(A\), \(P\), \(\chi\), and \(\tau\). The
operator is meaningful only when the data are admissible in the relevant sector.

The central rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent filtering is physical only when positivity, quotient consistency,
  Lorentzian admissibility, and sectoral constraints are preserved.}
  }
  \label{eq:failure-central-rule}
\end{equation}

Thus this section is not merely defensive. It identifies what must be proven in any serious
sectoral realization.

\subsection{General admissibility requirements}
\label{subsec:general-admissibility-requirements}

A sectoral MTT construction begins with
\begin{equation}
  (\mathcal H,A,P,\chi,\tau).
\end{equation}
The basic admissibility requirements are:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item \(A\) must be positive, or positive after projection to the physical sector:
  \begin{equation}
    A\ge0.
  \end{equation}

  \item \(P\) must be a genuine projector:
  \begin{equation}
    P=P^\ast=P^2.
  \end{equation}

  \item \(\chi(A)\) must be a bounded admissible spectral window:
  \begin{equation}
    0\le\chi(A)\le I
  \end{equation}
  in the clean spectral case.

  \item \(\tau\) must have the correct dimension and physical origin:
  \begin{equation}
    [\tau]=L^2=E^{-2}.
  \end{equation}

  \item The finite operator
  \begin{equation}
    \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
  \end{equation}
  must be bounded on the intended Hilbert or Sobolev scale.

  \item Gauge, diffeomorphism, and measurement constraints must be preserved where relevant.

  \item The Lorentzian implementation must not use a naive indefinite heat operator
  \begin{equation}
    \ee^{-\tau\Box}
  \end{equation}
  as its fundamental damping rule.
\end{enumerate}

If any of these fail, the proposed construction is not an admissible MTT sector.

\subsection{Failure mode I: calling \(\Badm\) a projector}
\label{subsec:failure-calling-Badm-projector}

The first failure is conceptual but important. The full operator
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
is not generally a projector. A projector must satisfy
\begin{equation}
  Q^2=Q.
\end{equation}
In a diagonal spectral case, \(\Badm\) has weights
\begin{equation}
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
\end{equation}
Unless
\begin{equation}
  w_n\in\{0,1\}
\end{equation}
for every mode, one has
\begin{equation}
  \Badm^2\neq\Badm.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  P\text{ is the projector; }\Badm\text{ is the finite coherent admissibility operator.}
  }
  \label{eq:Badm-not-projector-failure}
\end{equation}

Calling \(\Badm\) a projector obscures the finite filtering role of \(\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)\).

\subsection{Failure mode II: nonpositive admissibility operator}
\label{subsec:failure-nonpositive-A}

The damping factor
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}
\end{equation}
is a genuine damping factor only when \(A\) is positive in the relevant sector.

If \(A\) has negative spectrum, then modes with
\begin{equation}
  \lambda<0
\end{equation}
are amplified:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\lambda}>1.
\end{equation}
This is not Nil damping. It is instability.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A\ge0
  \text{ is not optional in the clean finite-filter theorem.}
  }
  \label{eq:positive-A-required-failure}
\end{equation}

In gauge and gravity sectors, positivity may only hold after physical projection or constraint
reduction. In those cases, the reduced or projected operator must be positive on the physical
sector.

\subsection{Failure mode III: naive Lorentzian heat flow}
\label{subsec:failure-naive-lorentzian}

The most serious failure is to set
\begin{equation}
  A=\Box
\end{equation}
in Lorentzian signature and write
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}
\end{equation}
as if it were an ordinary heat operator.

The d'Alembertian is hyperbolic and indefinite. Therefore \(\ee^{-\tau\Box}\) is not the
Lorentzian analogue of Euclidean heat flow. A naive expression of this type may introduce:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item acausal tails;
  \item infinite time-derivative instabilities;
  \item ghost-like poles;
  \item loss of unitarity;
  \item analytic continuation ambiguity;
  \item violation of gauge or gravitational constraints.
\end{enumerate}

The Lorentzian admissibility principle is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A\neq\Box
  \quad
  \text{as a fundamental damping operator.}
  }
  \label{eq:A-not-box-failure}
\end{equation}
