The admissible alternatives are positive internal/fiber operators, positive spatial Cauchy-slice
operators, Hamiltonian or constraint-compatible operators, gauge-quotiented operators, and
diffeomorphism-compatible geometric operators.

\subsection{Failure mode IV: uncontrolled base diffusion}
\label{subsec:failure-base-diffusion}

Some fixed-point constructions may introduce a regularized operator
\begin{equation}
  A_\varepsilon
  =
  A_{\rm int}
  +
  \varepsilon A_{\rm base},
  \qquad
  \varepsilon\ge0.
\end{equation}
This can be useful analytically. However, if \(A_{\rm base}\) represents base diffusion in a
Lorentzian direction, then it cannot automatically be interpreted as physical damping.

The clean physical FP/MTT reading is:
\begin{equation}
  \varepsilon=0,
\end{equation}
so that
\begin{equation}
  A_\varepsilon=A_{\rm int}.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  \varepsilon>0,
\end{equation}
then the base term must be justified as:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item a positive spatial Cauchy-slice operator;
  \item a Euclidean analytic regulator later removed;
  \item a Hamiltonian or constraint-compatible positive operator;
  \item or a physically derived detector/source profile.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \varepsilon>0
  \text{ is not automatically physical Lorentzian damping.}
  }
  \label{eq:epsilon-failure}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode V: invalid sharp-limit reasoning}
\label{subsec:failure-invalid-sharp-limit}

The Dirac delta appears as a sharp limit:
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)\to\delta(x-y).
\end{equation}
This does not mean that every finite kernel may be replaced by a delta in every calculation.

The delta approximation is valid only when the coherent width is below the relevant resolution:
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\ll\ell_{\rm res},
\end{equation}
or equivalently,
\begin{equation}
  E_{\rm probe}\ll\Lambda_{\rm eff}.
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  E_{\rm probe}\sim\Lambda_{\rm eff},
\end{equation}
then finite-width corrections may matter.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \delta\text{-locality is a low-resolution approximation, not an exact physical primitive.}
  }
  \label{eq:delta-approximation-failure}
\end{equation}

A proposed MTT calculation fails if it uses the delta limit outside its resolution domain.

\subsection{Failure mode VI: arbitrary spectral windows}
\label{subsec:failure-arbitrary-window}

The spectral window
\begin{equation}
  \chi(A)
\end{equation}
cannot be chosen arbitrarily if the goal is physical prediction. It must reflect sectoral
admissibility.

In the clean bounded case one requires
\begin{equation}
  0\le\chi(A)\le I.
\end{equation}
If \(\chi\) amplifies modes, breaks gauge identities, violates constraints, or excludes required
zero modes, the construction fails.

In particular, low-energy recovery usually requires
\begin{equation}
  \chi(0)=1.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  \chi(0)=0,
\end{equation}
then the zero mode is removed and ordinary low-energy physics is not recovered.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \chi(A)
  \text{ must be an admissibility window, not an arbitrary regulator.}
  }
  \label{eq:window-failure}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode VII: noncommuting projectors treated as diagonal}
\label{subsec:failure-noncommuting-projectors}

The modal formula
\begin{equation}
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}
\end{equation}
is valid only when
\begin{equation}
  [P,A]=0.
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  [P,A]\neq0,
\end{equation}
then \(P\) and \(A\) cannot generally be diagonalized together. The operator
\begin{equation}
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
may still be meaningful, but its behavior must be checked by operator estimates.

A calculation fails if it assumes diagonal weights in a noncommuting sector without proof.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{noncommuting sectoral projections require operator-level admissibility checks.}
  }
  \label{eq:noncommuting-failure}
\end{equation}

This matters especially in measurement, gauge, and gravity sectors.

\subsection{Failure mode VIII: invalid branch damping}
\label{subsec:failure-invalid-branch-damping}

Branch damping is represented by
\begin{equation}
  \rho\mapsto D\circ\rho.
\end{equation}
This map is physically valid only if
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}=1.
\end{equation}
If \(D\) is not positive semidefinite, then the map may fail to be completely positive. If
\(D_{aa}\neq1\), then trace preservation may fail unless additional loss channels are included.

For exponential damping
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}=\ee^{-\tau\Lambda_{ab}},
\end{equation}
one cannot choose \(\Lambda_{ab}\) arbitrarily. A sufficient condition is that \(\Lambda\) is
conditionally negative definite with zero diagonal.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{not every visibility law is a valid quantum damping law.}
  }
  \label{eq:visibility-law-failure}
\end{equation}

A proposed measurement model fails if its damping matrix does not define a completely
positive trace-preserving channel.

\subsection{Failure mode IX: confusing decoherence with outcome selection}
\label{subsec:failure-decoherence-collapse}

Decoherence suppresses off-diagonal terms:
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}\mapsto D_{ab}\rho_{ab}.
\end{equation}
It does not by itself specify which detector record occurs.

MTT separates:
\begin{equation}
  \text{branch damping}
\end{equation}
from
\begin{equation}
  \text{survivor-basin capture}.
\end{equation}

A measurement record requires:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}
Outcome probabilities require a basin measure:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(i)
  =
  \frac{\mu(B_i^{(\Meas)})}
  {\sum_j\mu(B_j^{(\Meas)})}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{visibility damping is not the same thing as outcome probability.}
  }
  \label{eq:decoherence-not-outcome-failure}
\end{equation}

A proposed measurement account fails if it treats decoherence alone as a complete record
selection mechanism.

\subsection{Failure mode X: ignoring device dependence}
\label{subsec:failure-device-dependence}

Different measurement devices disturb different branch structures. A detector context includes:
\begin{equation}
  \Meas
  =
  (A_{\Meas},P_{\Meas},\chi_{\Meas},\tau_{\Meas},
  \{E_i^{(\Meas)}\},\frakB_{\Meas}).
\end{equation}

Two devices may have different effects:
\begin{equation}
  E_i^{(\Meas_1)}\neq E_i^{(\Meas_2)},
\end{equation}
different damping matrices:
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}^{(\Meas_1)}
  \neq
  D_{ab}^{(\Meas_2)},
\end{equation}
and different survivor basins:
\begin{equation}
  \frakB_{\Meas_1}\neq\frakB_{\Meas_2}.
\end{equation}

Thus a measurement model fails if it treats “measuring the same observable” as sufficient to
determine the same disturbance, visibility loss, or basin structure.

The rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the measurement device is part of the Lens context.}
  }
  \label{eq:device-context-failure}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode XI: gauge quotient violation}
\label{subsec:failure-gauge-quotient}

Gauge theory requires quotient compatibility. The gauge-sector operator must have the form
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys},
\end{equation}
or an equivalent gauge-covariant construction before quotienting.

A gauge-sector realization fails if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item pure-gauge modes are treated as physical;
  \item \(P_{\rm phys}\) is not quotient-compatible;
  \item Ward identities are violated;
  \item Slavnov--Taylor identities are violated;
  \item BRST cohomology is not preserved;
  \item current conservation is broken;
  \item anomaly cancellation fails.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge filtering must preserve gauge quotient structure and gauge identities.}
  }
  \label{eq:gauge-failure-box}
\end{equation}

A Gaussian factor inserted into a gauge propagator is not automatically gauge-admissible.

\subsection{Failure mode XII: gravitational diffeomorphism violation}
\label{subsec:failure-gravity-diffeomorphism}

Gravity requires diffeomorphism compatibility. The gravitational operator must have the form
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
\end{equation}

A gravity-sector realization fails if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item metric components are damped as coordinate components;
  \item pure diffeomorphism modes are treated as physical;
  \item the Hamiltonian constraint is violated;
  \item the momentum constraints are violated;
  \item the constraint algebra is broken;
  \item stress-energy conservation fails;
  \item a weak-field projector is used outside its domain;
  \item singularities or horizons are smoothed without an admissible survivor-geometry
  principle.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravitational filtering must act on geometric content, not coordinate artifacts.}
  }
  \label{eq:gravity-failure-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode XIII: invalid subsystem factorization}
\label{subsec:failure-factorization}

Entanglement claims require a valid subsystem decomposition. In ordinary quantum mechanics,
one often writes
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{AB}
  =
  \mathcal H_A\otimes\mathcal H_B.
\end{equation}
But in gauge and gravity sectors this factorization may fail or require boundary/edge data.

A proposed entanglement analysis fails if it assumes
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{AB}
  =
  \mathcal H_A\otimes\mathcal H_B
\end{equation}
without checking constraints, gauge redundancy, diffeomorphism equivalence, or boundary
structure.

The rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{subsystem factorization is a Lens structure and must be justified.}
  }
  \label{eq:factorization-failure-box}
\end{equation}

This is especially important in gravitational entanglement.

\subsection{Failure mode XIV: no-signaling violation}
\label{subsec:failure-no-signaling}

Entanglement may produce nonclassical correlations, but it must not enable controllable
superluminal signaling. Therefore local marginals must be independent of remote settings:
\begin{equation}
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y')
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \sum_a p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \sum_a p(a,b|x',y).
\end{equation}

A proposed MTT entanglement model fails if joint basin measures or detector contexts allow
remote settings to change local outcome statistics in a controllable way.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{joint coherent admissibility must satisfy no-signaling at the record-statistics level.}
  }
  \label{eq:no-signaling-failure-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode XV: overclaiming Standard Model closure}
\label{subsec:failure-SM-overclaim}

The structural framework does not by itself derive:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the Standard Model gauge group;
  \item chiral matter representations;
  \item three generations;
  \item Yukawa couplings;
  \item mixing matrices;
  \item anomaly cancellation;
  \item symmetry breaking;
  \item observed masses and coupling constants.
\end{enumerate}

A paper or model overclaims if it suggests that the finite admissibility architecture alone has
already produced these details.

The correct statement is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Standard Model closure is an execution task.}
  }
  \label{eq:SM-overclaim-failure}
\end{equation}

MTT supplies the form of the required data:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm SM},
  \qquad
  P_{\rm SM},
  \qquad
  \chi_{\rm SM},
  \qquad
  \tau_{\rm SM},
\end{equation}
but the data must still be derived or specified.

\subsection{Failure mode XVI: overclaiming quantum gravity}
\label{subsec:failure-QG-overclaim}

Similarly, the structural gravity section does not complete quantum gravity. It states that a
gravitational MTT sector must supply:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm grav},
  \qquad
  P_{\rm diff},
  \qquad
  \chi_{\rm grav},
  \qquad
  \tau_{\rm grav}.
\end{equation}
It must preserve diffeomorphism equivalence, constraints, causal structure, and stress-energy
compatibility.

A model overclaims if it treats the schematic operator
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}
\end{equation}
as a completed nonperturbative quantum-gravity theory without constructing the physical
state space and constraints.

The correct statement is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT gives a gravitational projection architecture, not a completed nonperturbative
  quantum-gravity calculation by itself.}
  }
  \label{eq:QG-overclaim-failure}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode XVII: arbitrary phenomenological numbers}
\label{subsec:failure-arbitrary-phenomenology}

The relations
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  }
\end{equation}
give a geometric scale relation. But precise numerical predictions require:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item internal geometry;
  \item spectrum \(\mu_j^2\);
  \item wavefunction overlaps;
  \item gauge representations;
  \item couplings;
  \item widths;
  \item detector acceptances;
  \item renormalization and matching.
\end{enumerate}

Thus a model fails if it asserts exact collider cross-section suppressions, precision-test
deviations, or mass spectra without deriving the effective four-dimensional action.

The safe universal statement is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  W_j=\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
\end{equation}
is an amplitude-level admissibility weight. Observable cross sections require further
calculation.

\subsection{Failure mode XVIII: treating benchmarks as universal predictions}
\label{subsec:failure-benchmark-universal}

The flat Euclidean Gaussian kernel
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right)
\end{equation}
is a benchmark theorem. It is not automatically the universal physical kernel in every sector.

Similarly, the finite Coulomb potential
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_\tau(r)
  =
  \frac{q}{4\pi r}
  \operatorname{erf}
  \left(
    \frac{r}{2\sqrt{\tau}}
  \right)
\end{equation}
is a useful benchmark for a finite four-dimensional charge profile. But in the FP/fiber
realization, electromagnetic corrections may instead appear through internal-mode exchange:
\begin{equation}
  \Phi(r)
  =
  \frac{q}{4\pi r}
  \left[
    1+
    \sum_{j\ge1}
    c_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\ee^{-\mu_j r}
  \right].
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{benchmark kernels are examples, not universal sectoral predictions.}
  }
  \label{eq:benchmark-failure-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode XIX: loss of low-energy recovery}
\label{subsec:failure-low-energy-recovery}

A viable MTT sector must recover ordinary low-energy physics. In the FP realization this
requires undamped zero modes:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
It also requires:
\begin{equation}
  \chi(0)=1.
\end{equation}

If the zero mode is removed, over-damped, or assigned the wrong representation, the low-energy
sector fails.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{zero-mode recovery is a mandatory consistency condition.}
  }
  \label{eq:low-energy-recovery-failure}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode XX: unbounded equal-time kernels}
\label{subsec:failure-unbounded-cauchy-kernels}

Equal-time Cauchy kernels are Lorentzian-admissible only if they are bounded on the relevant
energy or Sobolev spaces. A kernel
\begin{equation}
  (\mathcal K_t f)(x)
  =
  \int_{\Sigma_t}K_t(x,y)f(y)\,\dd y
\end{equation}
is not automatically safe.

A sufficient boundedness condition is a Schur-type condition:
\begin{equation}
  \sup_x
  \int_{\Sigma_t}|K_t(x,y)|\,\dd y<\infty,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \sup_y
  \int_{\Sigma_t}|K_t(x,y)|\,\dd x<\infty.
\end{equation}

If the kernel is unbounded or changes the hyperbolic principal symbol, it may spoil well-posedness
or causality.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Cauchy-slice kernels must be bounded lower-order operators.}
  }
  \label{eq:unbounded-kernel-failure}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode XXI: changing the hyperbolic principal symbol}
\label{subsec:failure-principal-symbol}

A Lorentzian finite kernel may be admissible when it enters as a lower-order bounded term:
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g\phi+m^2\phi+\mathcal K_t\phi=J.
\end{equation}
The principal part remains
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g.
\end{equation}

But if the finite modification changes the highest-derivative hyperbolic structure, the causal
cone may change. This can introduce superluminal characteristics or ill-posedness.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Lorentzian finite kernels must not alter the principal causal structure unless a new
  causal theory is explicitly derived.}
  }
  \label{eq:principal-symbol-failure}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode XXII: treating finite overlap as signaling}
\label{subsec:failure-overlap-signaling}

Finite spatial support or overlap is not the same as signal propagation. A finite detector
samples a finite region. A finite source has a finite profile. An internal fiber state may project
to a finite effective base profile.

These facts do not by themselves imply superluminal signaling.

A failure occurs if the theory allows controllable influence outside the causal domain of
dependence. That is a stronger condition than finite support.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite overlap is admissible; controllable acausal signaling is not.}
  }
  \label{eq:overlap-signaling-failure}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode XXIII: ignoring positivity of spectral representation}
\label{subsec:failure-spectral-positivity}

After dimensional reduction, a schematic propagator may be written
\begin{equation}
  G_{4D}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{Z_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}}
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
\end{equation}
A positive Källén--Lehmann-type interpretation requires
\begin{equation}
  Z_j\ge0
\end{equation}
in the relevant physical sector.

If negative-norm states or ghost residues appear, the sector is not physical unless a consistent
constraint or gauge mechanism removes them.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{spectral damping does not repair negative-norm sectors.}
  }
  \label{eq:spectral-positivity-failure}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode XXIV: topology or boundary inconsistency}
\label{subsec:failure-topology-boundary}

Topological and boundary data can carry physical labels. A finite coherent filter must respect
global consistency.

Failure occurs if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item flux quantization is broken;
  \item bundle patching is inconsistent;
  \item boundary charges are not preserved;
  \item horizon or asymptotic boundary conditions are violated;
  \item topological sectors are mixed without an admissible transition rule.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite admissibility must preserve global topological and boundary data unless a
  lawful transition is derived.}
  }
  \label{eq:topology-boundary-failure}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Failure mode XXV: confusing structural unification with completed prediction}
\label{subsec:failure-structure-vs-prediction}

The structural corollary says:
\begin{equation}
  \text{finite coherent admissibility}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{projection shadows}.
\end{equation}
It does not say:
\begin{equation}
  \text{all predictions are already computed}.
\end{equation}

A correct use of MTT must distinguish:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item structural architecture;
  \item sectoral operator data;
  \item analytic admissibility;
  \item numerical prediction;
  \item experimental test.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT is meaningful when it guides sectoral execution, not when it replaces it.}
  }
  \label{eq:structure-vs-prediction-failure}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{subsec:failure-modes-summary}

A finite coherent admissibility operator is physical only when its data are admissible:
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}

The most important failure modes are:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item treating \(\Badm\) as a projector;
  \item using a nonpositive \(A\);
  \item using naive \(\ee^{-\tau\Box}\) in Lorentzian signature;
  \item interpreting base diffusion as physical without justification;
  \item using the delta limit outside its resolution regime;
  \item choosing arbitrary spectral windows;
  \item assuming diagonal weights when \([P,A]\neq0\);
  \item using non-CPTP branch damping;
  \item confusing decoherence with outcome selection;
  \item ignoring detector-context dependence;
  \item violating gauge quotienting;
  \item violating diffeomorphism constraints;
  \item assuming invalid subsystem factorization;
  \item violating no-signaling;
  \item overclaiming Standard Model or quantum-gravity closure;
  \item treating benchmark kernels as universal predictions.
\end{enumerate}

The central domain-of-validity statement is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT is valid only where finite coherent filtering preserves positivity, quotient
  consistency, Lorentzian causality, sectoral constraints, and empirical low-energy recovery.}
  }
\end{equation}
% ============================================================
\section{Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}
% ============================================================

This paper developed finite coherent admissibility as a structural architecture for Modal
Triplet Theory. The central operator is
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}
The projector is \(P\). The full operator \(\Badm\) is not generally a projector; it is a finite
coherent admissibility operator, or admissible coherent filter.

The main result is that many familiar physical structures can be read as projection shadows
of this finite coherent operator:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent admissibility}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{projection shadows}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{effective physics}.
  }
  \label{eq:conclusion-main-map}
\end{equation}

The first and most important shadows are the Dirac-delta particle shadow and the spectral
wave shadow:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Kadm(x,y)
  \quad
  \begin{cases}
    \text{local reading}
    &\Rightarrow
    \text{Dirac delta / particle shadow},\\
    \text{spectral reading}
    &\Rightarrow
    \text{wave / interference shadow}.
  \end{cases}
  }
  \label{eq:conclusion-delta-wave}
\end{equation}

Thus wave--particle duality is not treated as a primitive paradox. It is treated as projection
duality: two readings of one finite coherent kernel.

\subsection{The geometric backbone}
\label{subsec:conclusion-geometric-backbone}

The paper also clarified the geometric realization used as the default physical model. In the
fixed-point realization, the total space is
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6,
\end{equation}
or more generally a compact internal fiber geometry over a four-dimensional Lorentzian base.

The base \(Y^4\) carries the observed Lorentzian dynamics. The internal space \(X^6\), or the
internal bundle fibers, carry positive elliptic operators. The internal admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3
  \kappa_n\Delta_{B_n},
  \qquad
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}

The coherent projector is
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}\Pi_{B_2}\Pi_{B_3},
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
  \Ran P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3\ker\Delta_{B_n}.
\end{equation}

Therefore the fixed-point version of the finite coherent admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
  }
  \label{eq:conclusion-FP-Badm}
\end{equation}

This gives the abstract MTT operator a concrete geometric source. The coherent sector is not
a vague retained subspace. It is the joint fiber-harmonic sector.

\subsection{The Lorentzian resolution}
\label{subsec:conclusion-lorentzian-resolution}

A central correction made in this paper is the rejection of naive Lorentzian heat flow. MTT
does not use
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}
\end{equation}
as its fundamental physical damping operator.

The Lorentzian d'Alembertian is indefinite. Therefore the expression
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}
\end{equation}
does not play the same role as Euclidean heat flow generated by a positive operator.

The Lorentzian admissibility principle is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A\neq\Box
  \quad
  \text{as a naive fundamental damping operator.}
  }
  \label{eq:conclusion-A-not-box}
\end{equation}

Instead, admissible Lorentzian implementations use:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item positive internal/fiber operators;
  \item positive spatial Cauchy-slice operators;
  \item Hamiltonian or constraint-compatible operators;
  \item gauge-quotiented physical operators;
  \item diffeomorphism-compatible geometric operators;
  \item bounded equal-time kernels that preserve the hyperbolic principal symbol.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT finite kernels are not arbitrary Lorentzian nonlocality.}
  }
\end{equation}

They are admissible only when positivity, constraints, quotient structure, and causal
propagation are preserved.

\subsection{Locality and projection}
\label{subsec:conclusion-locality-projection}

The finite-kernel structure does not imply uncontrolled action at a distance. In the fixed-point
realization, finite coherent width is tied to internal geometry:
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
\end{equation}
and, for internal radius \(R\),
\begin{equation}
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  }.
\end{equation}

Thus four-dimensional nonlocal-looking behavior may be a projection shadow of controlled
finite support in the total/fibered structure:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{locality in total/fibered space}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{finite non-pointlike shadows in four dimensions}.
  }
  \label{eq:conclusion-locality-shadow}
\end{equation}

Point particles are recovered when the finite coherent width is below resolution:
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\ll\ell_{\rm res}.
\end{equation}

Equivalently:
\begin{equation}
  E_{\rm probe}\ll\Lambda_{\rm eff},
  \qquad
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{pointlike behavior is a low-resolution shadow of finite coherent support.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Scattering and internal-mode damping}
\label{subsec:conclusion-scattering}

In the Euclidean scalar benchmark, the finite kernel is
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right),
\end{equation}
with Fourier transform
\begin{equation}
  \widehat K_\tau^{(d)}(k)=\ee^{-\tau k^2}.
\end{equation}
This gives the benchmark finite propagator
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}(k)
  =
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2+m^2}.
\end{equation}

But in the physical fixed-point realization, the default damping is internal:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
\end{equation}
so
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}\phi_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\phi_j.
\end{equation}

After dimensional reduction, a typical four-dimensional effective propagator has the schematic
form
\begin{equation}
  G_{4D}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{
    Z_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
\end{equation}

The zero mode is undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}

Nonzero internal modes are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{ordinary low-energy four-dimensional physics is recovered by undamped zero modes.}
  }
\end{equation}

Finite coherent corrections enter through suppressed internal excitations, finite overlaps,
threshold effects, detector profiles, and sector-specific form factors.

\subsection{Gauge and electromagnetism}
\label{subsec:conclusion-gauge-em}

Gauge theory was identified as the paradigmatic Lens sector. Gauge potentials are local
representatives:
\begin{equation}
  A_\mu\sim A_\mu+\partial_\mu\alpha.
\end{equation}

Therefore a finite coherent filter is admissible in a gauge theory only if it respects the gauge
quotient:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{filter after quotienting, or filter covariantly before quotienting}.
  }
\end{equation}

The gauge-sector operator must have the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys}.
  }
\end{equation}

Electromagnetism then has a natural Circle--Lens--Nil reading:
\begin{align}
  \Lens_{\rm EM}
  &: \text{gauge representatives and quotienting},\\
  \Circle_{\rm EM}
  &: \text{phase, flux, and holonomy},\\
  \Nil_{\rm EM}
  &: \text{charge sectors, photon records, and detector outcomes}.
\end{align}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{electromagnetism is gauge-compatible finite coherent admissibility read through
  Lens quotienting, Circle phase, and Nil records.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gravity}
\label{subsec:conclusion-gravity}

Gravity is the deepest Lens sector because it acts on the representation of geometry itself:
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}\sim\varphi^\ast g_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}

Therefore a gravitational finite filter cannot act on arbitrary coordinate components of the
metric. It must act on diffeomorphism-compatible geometric content:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
  }
\end{equation}

In ADM/Cauchy form, the relevant data are spatial geometry and momentum:
\begin{equation}
  (h_{ij},\pi^{ij}),
\end{equation}
subject to the Hamiltonian and momentum constraints:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H=0,
  \qquad
  \mathcal H_i=0.
\end{equation}

Thus gravitational MTT filtering must preserve the constraint surface or act on a reduced
physical quotient.

The gravitational triplet is:
\begin{align}
  \Lens_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{diffeomorphism quotient and geometric representation},\\
  \Circle_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{curvature and frame holonomy},\\
  \Nil_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{boundary, horizon, singularity, survivor geometry}.
\end{align}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravity is diffeomorphism-compatible finite coherent admissibility read as geometric
  projection.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Quantization, superposition, and entanglement}
\label{subsec:conclusion-quantum-structures}

Quantization was interpreted as admissible survivor-label structure:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{quantization}
  =
  \Circle\text{-closure}
  +
  \Lens\text{-quotient consistency}
  +
  \Nil\text{-survivor selection}.
  }
\end{equation}

In spectral sectors:
\begin{equation}
  A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n
\end{equation}
gives possible labels, and finite admissibility weights them by
\begin{equation}
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
\end{equation}

In the fixed-point realization:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j
\end{equation}
gives compact-fiber labels. Zero modes survive undamped, while nonzero modes are suppressed.

Superposition was interpreted as coherent branch coexistence before Nil selection:
\begin{equation}
  |\psi\rangle
  =
  \sum_a c_a|a\rangle.
\end{equation}

Entanglement was interpreted as non-factorizable coherent admissibility in a joint sector:
\begin{equation}
  P_{AB}\neq P_A\otimes P_B
\end{equation}
or
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{AB}
  \neq
  B_{\rm adm}^{A}\otimes B_{\rm adm}^{B}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{superposition and entanglement are coherent-sector shadows of finite admissibility.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Measurement and records}
\label{subsec:conclusion-measurement}

Measurement was described by finite detector effects, branch damping, and survivor-basin
capture. A measurement context is
\begin{equation}
  \Meas
  =
  (A_{\Meas},P_{\Meas},\chi_{\Meas},\tau_{\Meas},
  \{E_i^{(\Meas)}\},\frakB_{\Meas}).
\end{equation}

Branch damping is represented by a Schur channel:
\begin{equation}
  \rho\mapsto D\circ\rho.
\end{equation}
Physical admissibility requires
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}=1.
\end{equation}

A definite measurement record requires survivor-basin stabilization:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{visibility damping}
  \neq
  \text{outcome selection}.
  }
\end{equation}

Visibility is controlled by off-diagonal damping:
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}\mapsto D_{ab}\rho_{ab}.
\end{equation}

Outcome frequencies require a basin measure:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(i)
  =
  \frac{
    \mu(B_i^{(\Meas)})
  }{
    \sum_j\mu(B_j^{(\Meas)})
  }.
\end{equation}

This distinction is essential to the MTT measurement account.

\subsection{What has been achieved}
\label{subsec:conclusion-achieved}

The paper achieved the following structural synthesis.

First, it gave a common-source account of wave--particle duality:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Dirac delta and wave behavior are two shadows of one finite coherent kernel.}
  }
\end{equation}

Second, it grounded the finite kernel in a concrete fixed-point/fiber geometry:
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6.
\end{equation}

Third, it resolved the Lorentzian ambiguity by rejecting naive
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}
\end{equation}
as a fundamental damping rule and replacing it with positive fiber, spatial, Hamiltonian, or
constraint-compatible admissibility operators.

Fourth, it showed that scattering corrections should be interpreted as finite overlap,
Euclidean benchmark damping, or internal-mode suppression, depending on the sector.

Fifth, it showed that gauge theory and gravity require quotient-compatible finite filtering:
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm phys}
  \quad
  \text{for gauge sectors},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm diff}
  \quad
  \text{for gravitational sectors}.
\end{equation}

Sixth, it placed quantization, superposition, and entanglement inside the same finite coherent
architecture.

Seventh, it separated rigorous analytic theorems from structural claims and from future
phenomenological execution.

The resulting framework is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{not a completed calculation of all sectoral physics, but a unified admissibility
  architecture for deriving and testing sectoral physics.}
  }
  \label{eq:conclusion-achievement-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{What remains open}
\label{subsec:conclusion-open}

Several tasks remain open.

A complete Standard Model execution must derive or specify:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm SM},
  \qquad
  P_{\rm SM},
  \qquad
  \chi_{\rm SM},
  \qquad
  \tau_{\rm SM},
\end{equation}
together with the observed gauge group, matter representations, chirality, anomaly
cancellation, masses, couplings, and mixing data.

A complete gravitational execution must derive or specify:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm grav},
  \qquad
  P_{\rm diff},
  \qquad
  \chi_{\rm grav},
  \qquad
  \tau_{\rm grav},
\end{equation}
on a physical diffeomorphism-compatible state space.

A complete measurement execution must derive detector basin measures:
\begin{equation}
  \mu(B_i^{(\Meas)}),
\end{equation}
and show when they reproduce Born weights.

A complete phenomenological execution must compute internal spectra:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2,
\end{equation}
effective couplings:
\begin{equation}
  Z_j,\quad g_j,
\end{equation}
and observable cross sections or precision corrections.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the next stage is sectoral execution.}
  }
  \label{eq:conclusion-next-stage}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Final statement}
\label{subsec:conclusion-final}

The framework developed here can be summarized in one sentence:

\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent admissibility is the common source from which particle, wave,
  measurement, gauge, gravity, quantization, and entanglement appear as projection shadows.}
  }
  \label{eq:conclusion-final-statement}
\end{equation}

The Dirac delta is not fundamental. It is the sharp local shadow.

The wave is not a rival substance. It is the spectral phase shadow.

The measurement record is not primitive collapse. It is Nil survivor-basin stabilization.

Gauge is not optional redundancy. It is Lens quotient structure.

Electromagnetism is gauge Lens plus Circle holonomy plus Nil records.

Gravity is the Lens structure of geometry itself.

Quantization is admissible survivor-label discreteness.

Entanglement is non-factorizable coherent admissibility.

All of these are organized by:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
  }
\end{equation}

In the fixed-point realization:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
  }
\end{equation}

Thus the paper's final claim is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{effective physics is the shadow structure of finite coherent admissibility.}
  }
\end{equation}
% ============================================================
\appendix
\section{Spectral calculus for finite coherent admissibility}
\label{app:spectral-calculus}
% ============================================================

This appendix records the analytic background for the finite coherent admissibility operator
used throughout the paper:
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
  \label{eq:appA-Badm}
\end{equation}
The main purpose is to clarify when the simple modal weight formula is valid and when it is
not.

The clean case is the commuting spectral case:
\begin{equation}
  A\ge0,
  \qquad
  P=P^\ast=P^2,
  \qquad
  [P,A]=0.
\end{equation}
In this case, \(\Badm\) is a positive bounded contraction and acts diagonally on common
eigenmodes of \(A\) and \(P\).

\subsection{Nonnegative self-adjoint operators}
\label{appsubsec:nonnegative-self-adjoint}

Let \(\mathcal H\) be a Hilbert space and let
\begin{equation}
  A:\mathcal D(A)\subset\mathcal H\to\mathcal H
\end{equation}
be a nonnegative self-adjoint operator:
\begin{equation}
  A=A^\ast,
  \qquad
  A\ge0.
\end{equation}
Nonnegativity means
\begin{equation}
  \langle f,Af\rangle\ge0
\end{equation}
for all \(f\in\mathcal D(A)\).

By the spectral theorem, there is a projection-valued spectral measure \(E_A(\lambda)\) such
that
\begin{equation}
  A
  =
  \int_0^\infty
  \lambda\,\dd E_A(\lambda).
  \label{eq:appA-spectral-resolution}
\end{equation}
For every bounded Borel function \(f\), one defines
\begin{equation}
  f(A)
  =
  \int_0^\infty
  f(\lambda)\,\dd E_A(\lambda).
  \label{eq:appA-functional-calculus}
\end{equation}

In particular,
\begin{equation}
  \chi(A)
  =
  \int_0^\infty
  \chi(\lambda)\,\dd E_A(\lambda),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}
  =
  \int_0^\infty
  \ee^{-\tau\lambda}\,\dd E_A(\lambda).
\end{equation}

Since
\begin{equation}
  0\le\ee^{-\tau\lambda}\le1
\end{equation}
for \(\lambda\ge0\), one has
\begin{equation}
  0\le\ee^{-\tau A}\le I.
\end{equation}

If also
\begin{equation}
  0\le\chi(\lambda)\le1,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  0\le\chi(A)\le I.
\end{equation}

\subsection{The finite coherent admissibility operator}
\label{appsubsec:finite-coherent-operator}

Let
\begin{equation}
  P=P^\ast=P^2
\end{equation}
be an orthogonal projector. Define
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}
The operator \(P\) is idempotent. The full operator \(\Badm\) is generally not idempotent:
\begin{equation}
  \Badm^2\neq\Badm.
\end{equation}
Therefore \(\Badm\) is called a finite coherent admissibility operator, not a projector.

If
\begin{equation}
  [P,A]=0,
\end{equation}
then \(P\) also commutes with \(\chi(A)\) and \(\ee^{-\tau A}\). In that case,
\begin{align}
  \langle f,\Badm f\rangle
  &=
  \langle f,P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P f\rangle\\
  &=
  \left\|
    \ee^{-\tau A/2}\chi(A)Pf
  \right\|^2\\
  &\ge0.
\end{align}
Thus
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\ge0.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
  \|\Badm\|\le1.
\end{equation}
Hence
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  0\le\Badm\le I.
  }
  \label{eq:appA-positive-contraction}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Discrete spectral case}
\label{appsubsec:discrete-spectral-case}

Suppose \(A\) has a discrete orthonormal eigenbasis
\begin{equation}
  A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n,
  \qquad
  \lambda_n\ge0.
\end{equation}
Assume also that \(P\) commutes with \(A\), so that the eigenbasis can be chosen to diagonalize
\(P\):
\begin{equation}
  P\phi_n=p_n\phi_n,
  \qquad
  p_n\in\{0,1\}.
\end{equation}

Then
\begin{equation}
  \chi(A)\phi_n=\chi(\lambda_n)\phi_n,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}\phi_n
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
  \label{eq:appA-modal-action}
\end{equation}

Define
\begin{equation}
  w_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
  \label{eq:appA-modal-weight}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n|\phi_n\rangle\langle\phi_n|.
  \label{eq:appA-spectral-Badm}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the commuting spectral case gives explicit admissibility weights }w_n.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Why \(\Badm\) is usually not idempotent}
\label{appsubsec:not-idempotent}

Using the spectral expression,
\begin{equation}
  \Badm^2\phi_n=w_n^2\phi_n.
\end{equation}
Meanwhile,
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n=w_n\phi_n.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \Badm^2=\Badm
\end{equation}
only if
\begin{equation}
  w_n^2=w_n
\end{equation}
for every \(n\), i.e.
\begin{equation}
  w_n\in\{0,1\}.
\end{equation}

But generally
\begin{equation}
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}
\end{equation}
takes values strictly between \(0\) and \(1\). Hence \(\Badm\) is generally not a projector.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  P\text{ is a projector; }\Badm\text{ is a finite filter.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Kernel representation}
\label{appsubsec:kernel-representation}

If \(\Badm\) admits an integral kernel, write
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \langle x|\Badm|y\rangle.
\end{equation}
In the discrete spectral case,
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y).
  \label{eq:appA-kernel-spectral}
\end{equation}

The same kernel has two primary readings:
\begin{align}
  x\mapsto\Kadm(x,x_0)
  &: \text{local finite response profile},\\
  \sum_n w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y)
  &: \text{spectral coherent-mode expansion}.
\end{align}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Kadm
  =
  \text{one kernel with local and spectral shadows}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Sharp identity limit}
\label{appsubsec:sharp-identity-limit}

Suppose
\begin{equation}
  P\to I,
  \qquad
  \chi(A)\to I,
  \qquad
  \tau\downarrow0.
\end{equation}
Then formally
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\to I.
\end{equation}
If the convergence holds strongly on a suitable test-function space, then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm f\to f.
\end{equation}
At the kernel level, this means
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)\to\delta(x-y)
\end{equation}
distributionally.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \delta(x-y)
  =
  \text{kernel of the identity reached in the sharp admissibility limit}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Noncommuting case}
\label{appsubsec:noncommuting-case}

If
\begin{equation}
  [P,A]\neq0,
\end{equation}
then \(P\), \(\chi(A)\), and \(\ee^{-\tau A}\) cannot generally be diagonalized together.
The formula
\begin{equation}
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}
\end{equation}
is then not valid.

The operator
\begin{equation}
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
may still be meaningful. However, its admissibility must be checked by:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item boundedness on the intended Hilbert or Sobolev scale;
  \item positivity or complete positivity where relevant;
  \item preservation of constraints;
  \item preservation of gauge or diffeomorphism equivalence;
  \item domain stability;
  \item compatibility with the physical measurement context.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{noncommuting projections require operator estimates, not scalar modal weights.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{FP commuting case}
\label{appsubsec:FP-commuting-case}

In the fixed-point realization,
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3\kappa_n\Delta_{B_n},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  P=P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}\Pi_{B_2}\Pi_{B_3}.
\end{equation}
The coherent projector is built from the fiber-harmonic projectors. In the clean fiber-spectral
setting, this gives the commuting relation
\begin{equation}
  [P_{\rm coh},A_{\rm int}]=0.
\end{equation}

Therefore the modal formula applies:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}\phi_j
  =
  p_j\chi(\mu_j^2)^2\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\phi_j,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j.
\end{equation}

The zero modes satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
\end{equation}
and are undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
The nonzero internal modes satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0,
\end{equation}
and are damped:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the FP realization is the clean commuting spectral model of MTT admissibility.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{appsubsec:spectral-summary}

The finite coherent admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}
In the clean commuting case,
\begin{equation}
  A\ge0,
  \qquad
  [P,A]=0,
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}
  0\le\Badm\le I.
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n,
  \qquad
  P\phi_n=p_n\phi_n,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
\end{equation}

The modal weight is
\begin{equation}
  w_n=p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
\end{equation}

The operator \(\Badm\) is generally not idempotent and therefore is not generally a projector.

When a kernel exists,
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y).
\end{equation}
Its sharp identity limit gives the Dirac delta:
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)\to\delta(x-y).
\end{equation}

This is the analytic foundation for the paper's central claim:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent kernels have both local particle shadows and spectral wave shadows.}
  }
\end{equation}
% ============================================================
\section{Dirac delta limits and finite coherent kernels}
\label{app:delta-limits}
% ============================================================

This appendix records the distributional meaning of the Dirac delta limit used throughout the
paper. The central point is that the Dirac delta is not treated as a primitive physical object.
It is the sharp local shadow of a family of finite coherent kernels.

The guiding relation is
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau(x,y)\to\delta(x-y)
  \qquad
  \text{as}
  \qquad
  \tau\downarrow0,
  \label{eq:appB-delta-limit}
\end{equation}
where the convergence is distributional or weak, not pointwise.

This appendix makes precise what that means.

\subsection{The delta distribution}
\label{appsubsec:delta-distribution}

Let \(M\) be a smooth manifold with measure \(\dd\mu\). The Dirac delta distribution
\begin{equation}
  \delta_x(y)
\end{equation}
is defined by its action on test functions:
\begin{equation}
  \int_M \delta_x(y)f(y)\,\dd\mu(y)
  =
  f(x).
  \label{eq:appB-delta-def}
\end{equation}

Equivalently, the identity operator has kernel
\begin{equation}
  I(x,y)=\delta(x-y),
\end{equation}
because
\begin{equation}
  (If)(x)
  =
  \int_M \delta(x-y)f(y)\,\dd\mu(y)
  =
  f(x).
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \delta(x-y)
  =
  \text{kernel of the identity operator}.
  }
  \label{eq:appB-delta-identity}
\end{equation}

In MTT, this identity kernel appears only as the sharp limit of finite coherent admissibility.

\subsection{Approximate identities}
\label{appsubsec:approximate-identities}

A family of kernels
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau(x,y),
  \qquad
  \tau>0,
\end{equation}
is an approximate identity if
\begin{equation}
  \lim_{\tau\downarrow0}
  \int_M K_\tau(x,y)f(y)\,\dd\mu(y)
  =
  f(x)
  \label{eq:appB-approx-identity}
\end{equation}
for all test functions \(f\) in the relevant class.

This is equivalent to saying
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau(x,y)\to\delta(x-y)
\end{equation}
distributionally.

A typical approximate identity satisfies:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item normalization:
  \begin{equation}
    \int_M K_\tau(x,y)\,\dd\mu(y)=1;
  \end{equation}

  \item concentration near \(x=y\) as \(\tau\downarrow0\);

  \item decay away from the diagonal;

  \item boundedness or positivity sufficient for convergence on the chosen function space.
\end{enumerate}

The physical meaning is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent response becomes pointlike only in the sharp limit.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Flat Gaussian approximate identity}
\label{appsubsec:flat-gaussian-approx-identity}

On \(\mathbb R^d\), the standard heat kernel is
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
  \label{eq:appB-flat-gaussian}
\end{equation}

It is normalized:
\begin{equation}
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)\,\dd^dy
  =
  1.
\end{equation}

For every test function \(f\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^d)\),
\begin{equation}
  \lim_{\tau\downarrow0}
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)f(y)\,\dd^dy
  =
  f(x).
  \label{eq:appB-gaussian-convergence}
\end{equation}

Therefore
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  \to
  \delta^{(d)}(x-y)
\end{equation}
distributionally.

This is the simplest mathematical model of the MTT particle shadow:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite Gaussian kernel}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{Dirac delta shadow}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Fourier representation}
\label{appsubsec:fourier-representation-delta}

The same kernel has Fourier representation
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  \frac{\dd^dk}{(2\pi)^d}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
  \ee^{\ii k\cdot(x-y)}.
  \label{eq:appB-fourier-gaussian}
\end{equation}

As \(\tau\downarrow0\),
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}\to1.
\end{equation}
Formally,
\begin{equation}
  \lim_{\tau\downarrow0}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  \frac{\dd^dk}{(2\pi)^d}
  \ee^{\ii k\cdot(x-y)}
  =
  \delta^{(d)}(x-y).
\end{equation}

Thus the same object has two readings:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  \text{finite local kernel},
  }
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \widehat K_\tau^{(d)}(k)
  =
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
  =
  \text{spectral damping factor}.
  }
\end{equation}

This is the mathematical prototype of wave--particle projection duality.

\subsection{Finite width}
\label{appsubsec:finite-width}

The Gaussian kernel
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
\end{equation}
has characteristic width
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau}.
\end{equation}
The corresponding effective energy scale is
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}

The delta approximation is valid only when the finite width is below experimental resolution:
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\ll\ell_{\rm res}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
  E_{\rm probe}\ll\Lambda_{\rm eff}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \delta\text{-locality is a resolution-dependent approximation.}
  }
  \label{eq:appB-resolution-delta}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Sharp limit of \(\Badm\)}
\label{appsubsec:sharp-limit-Badm}

The finite coherent admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}
A sharp identity limit is obtained when
\begin{equation}
  P\to I,
  \qquad
  \chi(A)\to I,
  \qquad
  \tau\downarrow0.
\end{equation}

Under suitable convergence assumptions,
\begin{equation}
  \Badm f\to f.
\end{equation}
If \(\Badm\) has a kernel \(\Kadm(x,y)\), this implies
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)\to\delta(x-y)
\end{equation}
distributionally.

Thus the Dirac delta is the kernel of the limiting identity:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \delta(x-y)
  =
  \lim_{\rm sharp}
  \langle x|\Badm|y\rangle.
  }
  \label{eq:appB-delta-Badm-limit}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Spectral sharp limit}
\label{appsubsec:spectral-sharp-limit}

In the commuting discrete spectral case,
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  w_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
\end{equation}

The sharp identity limit corresponds to
\begin{equation}
  w_n\to1
\end{equation}
for all modes in the test-function domain. Then
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  \to
  \sum_n
  \phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y).
\end{equation}
By completeness,
\begin{equation}
  \sum_n
  \phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y)
  =
  \delta(x-y).
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \delta(x-y)
  =
  \text{complete spectral reconstruction in the sharp admissibility limit}.
  }
  \label{eq:appB-spectral-delta}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Finite kernels on manifolds}
\label{appsubsec:finite-kernels-manifolds}

On a Riemannian manifold \((M,g)\), a positive elliptic operator \(A_g\) defines a heat kernel
\begin{equation}
  K_g(x,y;\tau)
  =
  \langle x|\ee^{-\tau A_g}|y\rangle.
\end{equation}
For small \(\tau\), the heat kernel has an asymptotic expansion of the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  K_g(x,y;\tau)
  \sim
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{\sigma_g(x,y)}{2\tau}
  \right)
  \Delta_g^{1/2}(x,y)
  \left[
    1+\tau a_1(x,y)+\tau^2a_2(x,y)+\cdots
  \right],
  \label{eq:appB-manifold-heat}
\end{equation}
where \(\sigma_g(x,y)\) is Synge's world function and \(\Delta_g(x,y)\) is the Van Vleck
determinant.

As \(\tau\downarrow0\),
\begin{equation}
  K_g(x,y;\tau)\to\delta_g(x,y),
\end{equation}
where \(\delta_g\) is the delta distribution with respect to the Riemannian volume measure.

Thus the delta limit persists on curved spaces, but the finite kernel is shaped by:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item geodesic distance;
  \item curvature;
  \item measure;
  \item topology;
  \item boundary conditions;
  \item bundle structure.
\end{enumerate}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent kernels are geometry-dependent before they become delta shadows.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Internal/fiber delta shadows}
\label{appsubsec:fiber-delta-shadows}

In the fixed-point realization, the natural finite kernel lives on compact internal/fiber
geometry. The positive internal operator is
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3
  \kappa_n\Delta_{B_n}.
\end{equation}
A corresponding internal heat kernel has spectral form
\begin{equation}
  K_{\rm int}(z,z';\tau)
  =
  \sum_j
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  \phi_j(z)\phi_j^\ast(z'),
  \label{eq:appB-internal-kernel}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j.
\end{equation}

The internal sharp limit is
\begin{equation}
  K_{\rm int}(z,z';\tau)\to\delta_{X^6}(z,z')
\end{equation}
as \(\tau\downarrow0\), subject to the relevant projector and window limits.

After dimensional reduction, this finite internal structure appears as four-dimensional
effective data:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item zero-mode fields;
  \item damped internal excitations;
  \item finite overlap coefficients;
  \item form factors;
  \item detector or source profiles;
  \item non-pointlike projection shadows.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{four-dimensional delta behavior may be a low-resolution shadow of finite internal
  coherent structure.}
  }
  \label{eq:appB-fiber-delta-shadow}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Delta sources and finite sources}
\label{appsubsec:delta-sources-finite-sources}

A point source is often written as
\begin{equation}
  J(x)=q\delta(x-x_0).
\end{equation}
MTT replaces this, when finite structure is relevant, by
\begin{equation}
  J_\tau(x)=qK_\tau(x,x_0).
\end{equation}
The total charge or weight is preserved if
\begin{equation}
  \int_M K_\tau(x,x_0)\,\dd\mu(x)=1.
\end{equation}

In the sharp limit,
\begin{equation}
  J_\tau(x)\to q\delta(x-x_0).
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{point source}
  =
  \text{sharp limit of finite coherent source}.
  }
\end{equation}

This is the source-side version of the particle shadow.

\subsection{Delta detectors and finite effects}
\label{appsubsec:delta-detectors-finite-effects}

Similarly, an idealized position measurement may be represented by a sharp projector:
\begin{equation}
  |x\rangle\langle x|.
\end{equation}
A finite detector instead uses a positive effect
\begin{equation}
  E_x=|k_x\rangle\langle k_x|,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  k_x(y)=K_{\rm det}(y,x).
\end{equation}

The probability density is
\begin{equation}
  p(x)=\tr(\rho E_x).
\end{equation}

The sharp position measurement is recovered only when the detector profile becomes
delta-like:
\begin{equation}
  K_{\rm det}(y,x)\to\delta(y-x).
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{localized detector clicks are finite-effect records, not primitive delta events.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Distributional convergence versus pointwise convergence}
\label{appsubsec:distributional-vs-pointwise}

It is important that
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau(x,y)\to\delta(x-y)
\end{equation}
does not mean ordinary pointwise convergence. The delta distribution is not a function.

For fixed \(x\neq y\), the Gaussian kernel satisfies
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau(x-y)\to0
\end{equation}
as \(\tau\downarrow0\). At \(x=y\), it diverges:
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau(0)\to\infty.
\end{equation}
The meaningful statement is the integrated one:
\begin{equation}
  \int K_\tau(x-y)f(y)\,\dd y\to f(x).
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the delta limit is a distributional limit, not a pointwise physical profile.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Resolution and operational indistinguishability}
\label{appsubsec:resolution-operational}

A finite kernel and a delta source are operationally indistinguishable to a probe if the probe
cannot resolve the finite width. Let the probe resolution be
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm res}.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  \sqrt{\tau}\ll\ell_{\rm res},
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau(x,y)
\end{equation}
is effectively indistinguishable from
\begin{equation}
  \delta(x-y)
\end{equation}
for that probe.

The first finite-width correction in a derivative expansion is often controlled by
\begin{equation}
  \tau\partial^2
\end{equation}
or, in momentum space,
\begin{equation}
  \tau k^2.
\end{equation}

Thus the expansion parameter is
\begin{equation}
  \tau E_{\rm probe}^2.
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  \tau E_{\rm probe}^2\ll1,
\end{equation}
the delta approximation is reliable. If
\begin{equation}
  \tau E_{\rm probe}^2\sim1,
\end{equation}
finite coherent structure may become observable.

\subsection{Summary}
\label{appsubsec:delta-summary}

The Dirac delta is defined by
\begin{equation}
  \int\delta(x-y)f(y)\,\dd\mu(y)=f(x).
\end{equation}
It is the kernel of the identity operator:
\begin{equation}
  I(x,y)=\delta(x-y).
\end{equation}

A family of finite kernels \(K_\tau(x,y)\) converges to the delta distribution when
\begin{equation}
  \int K_\tau(x,y)f(y)\,\dd\mu(y)\to f(x)
\end{equation}
for suitable test functions.

The Gaussian heat kernel provides the benchmark:
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right),
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)\to\delta^{(d)}(x-y).
\end{equation}

In MTT,
\begin{equation}
  \delta(x-y)
  =
  \lim_{\rm sharp}
  \langle x|\Badm|y\rangle.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the Dirac delta is the sharp local shadow of finite coherent admissibility.}
  }
\end{equation}

% ============================================================
\section{Schur damping, branch channels, and visibility}
\label{app:schur-damping}
% ============================================================

This appendix records the channel-theoretic details behind the MTT branch-damping layer.
The central object is a Schur product map
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_D(\rho)
  =
  D\circ\rho,
  \label{eq:appC-Schur-map}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  (D\circ\rho)_{ab}
  =
  D_{ab}\rho_{ab}.
\end{equation}

This map describes loss of branch coherence in a chosen Lens context. It is used for
decoherence, visibility loss, partial which-way marking, weak measurement, and measurement
disturbance.

The key admissibility condition is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  D\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}=1.
  }
  \label{eq:appC-D-condition}
\end{equation}
Under this condition, the Schur map is completely positive and trace preserving.

\subsection{Branch basis and density matrices}
\label{appsubsec:branch-basis-density}

Let
\begin{equation}
  \{|a\rangle\}_{a=1}^N
\end{equation}
be a finite branch basis selected by a measurement or representation context. A density
matrix has entries
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}
  =
  \langle a|\rho|b\rangle.
\end{equation}

The diagonal entries
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{aa}
\end{equation}
are branch weights. The off-diagonal entries
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab},
  \qquad
  a\neq b,
\end{equation}
carry branch coherence.

A branch-damping map acts as
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}
  \mapsto
  D_{ab}\rho_{ab}.
  \label{eq:appC-branch-damping}
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  D_{aa}=1,
\end{equation}
the diagonal branch weights are unchanged. If
\begin{equation}
  |D_{ab}|<1,
  \qquad
  a\neq b,
\end{equation}
the corresponding branch coherence is reduced.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  D_{ab}
  =
  \text{surviving coherence between branches }a\text{ and }b.
  }
  \label{eq:appC-D-meaning}
\end{equation}

\subsection{The Schur product theorem}
\label{appsubsec:Schur-product-theorem}

The Schur product theorem states that if
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \rho\succeq0,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  D\circ\rho\succeq0.
\end{equation}

This ensures positivity of the output state. However, quantum channels require complete
positivity, not only positivity. For Schur maps, positive semidefiniteness of \(D\) is also
sufficient for complete positivity.

\begin{theorem}[Schur damping channel]
\label{thm:appC-Schur-channel}
Let \(D\in M_N(\mathbb C)\). The map
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_D(\rho)=D\circ\rho
\end{equation}
is completely positive if and only if
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0.
\end{equation}
It is trace preserving if and only if
\begin{equation}
  D_{aa}=1
\end{equation}
for all \(a\). Therefore, if
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}=1,
\end{equation}
then \(\Phi_D\) is CPTP.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If \(D\succeq0\), then there exist vectors \(v_a\) in an auxiliary Hilbert space \(\mathcal K\)
such that
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}
  =
  \langle v_b,v_a\rangle.
\end{equation}
Define
\begin{equation}
  V:\mathbb C^N\to\mathbb C^N\otimes\mathcal K
\end{equation}
by
\begin{equation}
  V|a\rangle
  =
  |a\rangle\otimes v_a.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  D_{aa}=1,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \|v_a\|^2=D_{aa}=1,
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}
  V^\ast V=I.
\end{equation}

Now define
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_D(\rho)
  =
  \tr_{\mathcal K}(V\rho V^\ast).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{align}
  \langle a|\Phi_D(\rho)|b\rangle
  &=
  \rho_{ab}\langle v_b,v_a\rangle\\
  &=
  D_{ab}\rho_{ab}.
\end{align}
Thus
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_D(\rho)=D\circ\rho.
\end{equation}
Because \(\Phi_D\) has a Stinespring representation, it is completely positive. Because
\(V^\ast V=I\), it is trace preserving.

Conversely, if \(\Phi_D\) is completely positive, applying it to the maximally entangled
Choi state shows that its Choi matrix is positive. The Choi matrix of \(\Phi_D\) is supported
on the span of \(|aa\rangle\) and has entries \(D_{ab}\), so \(D\succeq0\). Trace preservation
requires
\begin{equation}
  \tr(D\circ\rho)=\tr\rho
\end{equation}
for all \(\rho\), which is equivalent to \(D_{aa}=1\) for every \(a\).
\end{proof}

Thus arbitrary branch damping is not allowed. The damping matrix must define a valid quantum
channel.

\subsection{Two-branch damping}
\label{appsubsec:two-branch-damping}

For two branches,
\begin{equation}
  D
  =
  \begin{pmatrix}
    1 & d\\
    d^\ast & 1
  \end{pmatrix}.
  \label{eq:appC-two-branch-D}
\end{equation}
The condition
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0
\end{equation}
is equivalent to
\begin{equation}
  |d|\le1.
\end{equation}

The Schur map acts as
\begin{equation}
  \begin{pmatrix}
    \rho_{11} & \rho_{12}\\
    \rho_{21} & \rho_{22}
  \end{pmatrix}
  \mapsto
  \begin{pmatrix}
    \rho_{11} & d\rho_{12}\\
    d^\ast\rho_{21} & \rho_{22}
  \end{pmatrix}.
  \label{eq:appC-two-branch-map}
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  d=1,
\end{equation}
coherence is preserved. If
\begin{equation}
  d=0,
\end{equation}
the off-diagonal terms vanish in the branch basis. If
\begin{equation}
  0<|d|<1,
\end{equation}
coherence is partially preserved.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  |d|
  =
  \text{two-branch coherence survival factor}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Visibility law}
\label{appsubsec:visibility-law}

Consider a two-branch interferometer with state
\begin{equation}
  |\psi\rangle
  =
  \alpha|\psi_1\rangle+\beta|\psi_2\rangle.
\end{equation}
A detector effect \(E_x\) gives intensity
\begin{align}
  I(x)
  &=
  |\alpha|^2
  \langle\psi_1|E_x|\psi_1\rangle
  +
  |\beta|^2
  \langle\psi_2|E_x|\psi_2\rangle
  \nonumber\\
  &\quad+
  2\operatorname{Re}
  \left[
    \alpha\beta^\ast
    \langle\psi_2|E_x|\psi_1\rangle
  \right].
  \label{eq:appC-undamped-intensity}
\end{align}

After branch damping with two-branch factor \(d\), the intensity becomes
\begin{align}
  I_D(x)
  &=
  |\alpha|^2
  \langle\psi_1|E_x|\psi_1\rangle
  +
  |\beta|^2
  \langle\psi_2|E_x|\psi_2\rangle
  \nonumber\\
  &\quad+
  2\operatorname{Re}
  \left[
    d\alpha\beta^\ast
    \langle\psi_2|E_x|\psi_1\rangle
  \right].
  \label{eq:appC-damped-intensity}
\end{align}

If \(d\) is real and nonnegative, the fringe visibility obeys
\begin{equation}
  V_D=dV_0.
  \label{eq:appC-visibility-law}
\end{equation}
If \(d\) is complex, \(|d|\) controls the visibility reduction and \(\arg(d)\) shifts the phase.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{visibility loss}
  =
  \text{off-diagonal branch damping}.
  }
  \label{eq:appC-visibility-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Marker-state model}
\label{appsubsec:marker-state-model}

A physical origin for Schur damping is entanglement with marker states. Suppose
\begin{equation}
  |\psi\rangle
  =
  \sum_a c_a|a\rangle
\end{equation}
becomes
\begin{equation}
  |\Psi\rangle
  =
  \sum_a c_a|a\rangle|m_a\rangle,
  \label{eq:appC-marker-entangled}
\end{equation}
where \(|m_a\rangle\) are marker or environment states.

Tracing out the marker system gives
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}
  \mapsto
  \langle m_b|m_a\rangle\rho_{ab}.
\end{equation}
Thus
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}
  =
  \langle m_b|m_a\rangle.
  \label{eq:appC-marker-D}
\end{equation}

Because \(D\) is a Gram matrix, it is positive semidefinite:
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0.
\end{equation}
If the marker states are normalized,
\begin{equation}
  \langle m_a|m_a\rangle=1,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  D_{aa}=1.
\end{equation}

Thus marker entanglement automatically produces an admissible Schur damping channel.

For two branches:
\begin{equation}
  d=\langle m_2|m_1\rangle.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  \langle m_2|m_1\rangle=1,
\end{equation}
no which-way information is stored. If
\begin{equation}
  \langle m_2|m_1\rangle=0,
\end{equation}
the marker states are perfectly distinguishable and branch coherence vanishes in the reduced
system.

\subsection{Weak marking and partial distinguishability}
\label{appsubsec:weak-marking}

Weak measurement corresponds to partial branch distinguishability:
\begin{equation}
  0<|\langle m_b|m_a\rangle|<1.
\end{equation}
The coherence is reduced but not eliminated:
\begin{equation}
  \rho_{ab}\mapsto
  \langle m_b|m_a\rangle\rho_{ab}.
\end{equation}

Thus weak measurement naturally gives:
\begin{equation}
  0<|D_{ab}|<1.
\end{equation}

This explains why different devices disturb the same nominal observable differently. They
produce different marker states and therefore different damping matrices:
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}^{(\Meas_1)}
  \neq
  D_{ab}^{(\Meas_2)}.
\end{equation}

In MTT language:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{device disturbance is encoded in the measurement-context damping matrix }D^{(\Meas)}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Quantum eraser}
\label{appsubsec:quantum-eraser}

If marker information is stored reversibly, later measurement of the marker in a different
basis may conditionally restore interference.

For two branches,
\begin{equation}
  |\Psi\rangle
  =
  \alpha|\psi_1\rangle|m_1\rangle
  +
  \beta|\psi_2\rangle|m_2\rangle.
\end{equation}
If \(|m_1\rangle\) and \(|m_2\rangle\) are orthogonal, the system alone has no interference
in the branch basis. But define marker superposition states
\begin{equation}
  |m_\pm\rangle
  =
  \frac{1}{\sqrt2}
  \left(
    |m_1\rangle\pm |m_2\rangle
  \right)
\end{equation}
when the marker states are orthonormal. Conditional on detecting \(m_+\) or \(m_-\), the
system state becomes proportional to
\begin{equation}
  \alpha|\psi_1\rangle\pm\beta|\psi_2\rangle.
\end{equation}
Interference reappears conditionally.

MTT reads this as a change of Lens context before irreversible Nil survivor-basin capture:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{quantum erasure changes the marker Lens before final record stabilization.}
  }
\end{equation}

It does not require retroactive alteration of a past event.

\subsection{Exponential branch damping}
\label{appsubsec:exponential-branch-damping}

A useful damping model is
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}(\tau)
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\Lambda_{ab}},
  \label{eq:appC-exponential-D}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{aa}=0.
\end{equation}
To ensure that \(D(\tau)\) is positive semidefinite for all \(\tau\ge0\), \(\Lambda\) should be
conditionally negative definite:
\begin{equation}
  \sum_{a,b}
  \overline{c_a}c_b\Lambda_{ab}
  \le0
  \qquad
  \text{whenever}
  \qquad
  \sum_a c_a=0.
  \label{eq:appC-CND}
\end{equation}

Under this condition, Schoenberg's theorem implies
\begin{equation}
  \left(
  \ee^{-\tau\Lambda_{ab}}
  \right)_{ab}
  \succeq0
\end{equation}
for all \(\tau\ge0\).

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Lambda\text{ conditionally negative definite}
  \quad
  \Rightarrow
  \quad
  D_{ab}=\ee^{-\tau\Lambda_{ab}}
  \text{ is admissible branch damping}.
  }
  \label{eq:appC-Schoenberg-box}
\end{equation}

This condition prevents arbitrary phenomenological visibility laws from being inserted
without channel consistency.

\subsection{Distance-induced damping}
\label{appsubsec:distance-induced-damping}

A common way to obtain conditionally negative definite \(\Lambda\) is through squared
distances. Suppose each branch \(a\) is assigned a vector \(r_a\) in a real Hilbert space.
Define
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{ab}
  =
  \|r_a-r_b\|^2.
  \label{eq:appC-squared-distance}
\end{equation}
Then \(\Lambda\) is conditionally negative definite.

Therefore
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}
  =
  \exp\left(
    -\tau\|r_a-r_b\|^2
  \right)
\end{equation}
is positive semidefinite for all \(\tau\ge0\).

Physically, \(r_a\) may represent the detector/environment imprint associated with branch
\(a\). Branches with more separated imprints have smaller coherence:
\begin{equation}
  \|r_a-r_b\|\text{ large}
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  |D_{ab}|\text{ small}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{branch distinguishability can generate admissible exponential coherence damping.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Relation to measurement contexts}
\label{appsubsec:Schur-measurement-contexts}

In MTT, a measurement context is
\begin{equation}
  \Meas
  =
  (A_{\Meas},P_{\Meas},\chi_{\Meas},\tau_{\Meas},
  \{E_i^{(\Meas)}\},\frakB_{\Meas}).
\end{equation}
The damping matrix
\begin{equation}
  D^{(\Meas)}
\end{equation}
is part of this context. It records how the device/environment suppresses coherence in the
chosen branch basis.

Two devices may measure the same nominal branch alternative but have different damping:
\begin{equation}
  D^{(\Meas_1)}
  \neq
  D^{(\Meas_2)}.
\end{equation}

They may also have different finite effects:
\begin{equation}
  E_i^{(\Meas_1)}
  \neq
  E_i^{(\Meas_2)},
\end{equation}
and different survivor basins:
\begin{equation}
  \frakB_{\Meas_1}
  \neq
  \frakB_{\Meas_2}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{measurement disturbance is device-specific finite admissibility data.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Visibility versus probability}
\label{appsubsec:visibility-versus-probability}

The damping matrix \(D\) controls visibility, not final outcome frequency by itself.

Visibility asks:
\begin{equation}
  \text{How much off-diagonal coherence survives?}
\end{equation}
This is determined by
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}.
\end{equation}

Outcome frequency asks:
\begin{equation}
  \text{Which record occurs, and how often?}
\end{equation}
In MTT, this requires basin measures:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(i)
  =
  \frac{
    \mu(B_i^{(\Meas)})
  }{
    \sum_j\mu(B_j^{(\Meas)})
  }.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  D_{ab}
  \text{ controls coherence survival;}
  \qquad
  \mu(B_i)
  \text{ controls outcome frequency.}
  }
  \label{eq:appC-visibility-probability-box}
\end{equation}

Confusing these two layers is a failure mode.

\subsection{Relation to decoherence}
\label{appsubsec:relation-decoherence}

Environmental decoherence is a physical mechanism that often produces Schur damping in an
approximately preferred basis. MTT incorporates this mechanism but separates it from final
record selection.

The sequence is:
\begin{equation}
  \rho
  \xrightarrow{\text{branch coupling}}
  \rho'
  \xrightarrow{\text{Schur damping}}
  D\circ\rho'
  \xrightarrow{\text{basin capture}}
  B_i^{(\Meas)}.
  \label{eq:appC-measurement-chain}
\end{equation}

Decoherence corresponds to the middle step. Measurement record formation requires the final
Nil step.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{decoherence is branch damping; measurement record is survivor-basin capture.}
  }
  \label{eq:appC-decoherence-record-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{No-signaling compatibility}
\label{appsubsec:Schur-no-signaling}

For entangled systems, local branch damping must not allow controllable superluminal
signaling. If the damping is a local CPTP channel on Alice's subsystem,
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_A\otimes I_B,
\end{equation}
then Bob's marginal remains unaffected by Alice's local choice when averaged over Alice's
outcomes:
\begin{equation}
  \rho_B'
  =
  \tr_A[(\Phi_A\otimes I_B)(\rho_{AB})]
  =
  \tr_A(\rho_{AB})
  =
  \rho_B.
\end{equation}

Thus CPTP locality is a sufficient condition for no-signaling at the channel level.

However, full measurement models must also check basin probabilities:
\begin{equation}
  p(a,b|x,y)
\end{equation}
must satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y')
\end{equation}
and similarly for Bob.

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{admissible branch damping must be CPTP and compatible with no-signaling record
  statistics.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{appsubsec:Schur-summary}

Branch damping in MTT is represented by the Schur map
\begin{equation}
  \rho\mapsto D\circ\rho.
\end{equation}
It is a valid quantum channel when
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}=1.
\end{equation}

For two branches,
\begin{equation}
  D=
  \begin{pmatrix}
    1 & d\\
    d^\ast & 1
  \end{pmatrix},
  \qquad
  |d|\le1.
\end{equation}
The visibility law is
\begin{equation}
  V_D=|d|V_0.
\end{equation}

Marker states generate damping by
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}=\langle m_b|m_a\rangle.
\end{equation}

Exponential damping
\begin{equation}
  D_{ab}=\ee^{-\tau\Lambda_{ab}}
\end{equation}
is admissible for all \(\tau\ge0\) when \(\Lambda\) is conditionally negative definite.

The main physical distinction is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Schur damping controls visibility; survivor basins control records.}
  }
\end{equation}

% ============================================================
\section{Scalar heat-kernel benchmark}
\label{app:scalar-heat-benchmark}
% ============================================================

This appendix gives the complete scalar Euclidean benchmark used throughout the paper.
It is the simplest fully closed example of finite coherent admissibility.

The benchmark data are:
\begin{equation}
  A=-\Delta,
  \qquad
  P=I,
  \qquad
  \chi=1,
\end{equation}
on \(\mathbb R^d\), with Euclidean metric.

Then the finite coherent admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  \ee^{-\tau A}
  =
  \ee^{\tau\Delta}.
  \label{eq:appD-Badm-scalar}
\end{equation}

This appendix proves:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the heat kernel formula;
  \item the Dirac delta limit;
  \item the momentum damping factor;
  \item the finite propagator shadow;
  \item the coherent length scale \(\ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau}\);
  \item the effective energy scale \(\Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2}\).
\end{enumerate}

This example is Euclidean. It is not the fundamental Lorentzian prescription of MTT. Its role
is to provide a transparent benchmark in which all formulas can be computed explicitly.

\subsection{The Euclidean scalar operator}
\label{appsubsec:euclidean-scalar-operator}

Let
\begin{equation}
  A=-\Delta
\end{equation}
on \(\mathbb R^d\), where
\begin{equation}
  \Delta
  =
  \sum_{j=1}^d
  \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_j^2}.
\end{equation}
The operator \(A=-\Delta\) is nonnegative:
\begin{equation}
  \langle f,Af\rangle
  =
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  |\nabla f(x)|^2\,\dd^dx
  \ge0
\end{equation}
for suitable \(f\).

The plane waves
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{\ii k\cdot x}
\end{equation}
are generalized eigenfunctions:
\begin{equation}
  -\Delta \ee^{\ii k\cdot x}
  =
  k^2\ee^{\ii k\cdot x},
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  k^2=|k|^2.
\end{equation}

Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}\ee^{\ii k\cdot x}
  =
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}\ee^{\ii k\cdot x}.
  \label{eq:appD-plane-wave-damping}
\end{equation}

Thus the scalar Euclidean benchmark gives the clean spectral damping factor:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \widehat K_\tau(k)=\ee^{-\tau k^2}.
  }
  \label{eq:appD-momentum-damping-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Heat kernel from Fourier transform}
\label{appsubsec:heat-kernel-fourier}

The heat kernel is defined by
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  \langle x|\ee^{\tau\Delta}|y\rangle.
\end{equation}
Using the Fourier representation, one has
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
