  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  \frac{\dd^dk}{(2\pi)^d}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
  \ee^{\ii k\cdot(x-y)}.
  \label{eq:appD-heat-fourier}
\end{equation}

The integral factorizes into \(d\) one-dimensional Gaussian integrals:
\begin{equation}
  \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}
  \frac{\dd k}{2\pi}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
  \ee^{\ii k a}
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-1/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{a^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
\end{equation}

Therefore
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
  \label{eq:appD-heat-kernel}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
  }
  \label{eq:appD-heat-kernel-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Normalization}
\label{appsubsec:heat-normalization}

The kernel is normalized:
\begin{equation}
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)\,\dd^dy
  =
  1.
  \label{eq:appD-normalization}
\end{equation}
Indeed,
\begin{align}
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right)
  \dd^dy
  &=
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  (4\pi\tau)^{d/2}\\
  &=
  1.
\end{align}

Thus \(K_\tau^{(d)}\) preserves total weight.

This is important for physical interpretation. A finite source profile built from
\(K_\tau^{(d)}\) preserves total charge, mass, or probability weight if normalized in this way.

\subsection{Delta limit}
\label{appsubsec:heat-delta-limit}

Let \(f\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^d)\). Then
\begin{equation}
  (K_\tau^{(d)}\ast f)(x)
  =
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)f(y)\,\dd^dy.
\end{equation}
As \(\tau\downarrow0\),
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}\ast f
  \to
  f
\end{equation}
smoothly on compact sets.

Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  \to
  \delta^{(d)}(x-y)
\end{equation}
distributionally.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \lim_{\tau\downarrow0}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  \delta^{(d)}(x-y)
  }
  \label{eq:appD-delta-limit-box}
\end{equation}
in the sense of distributions.

This proves the particle-shadow limit in the scalar benchmark.

\subsection{The finite kernel as delta replacement}
\label{appsubsec:finite-kernel-delta-replacement}

The benchmark replacement is:
\begin{equation}
  \delta^{(d)}(x-y)
  \quad
  \rightsquigarrow
  \quad
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y).
  \label{eq:appD-delta-replacement}
\end{equation}

Explicitly:
\begin{equation}
  \delta^{(d)}(x-y)
  \quad
  \rightsquigarrow
  \quad
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
\end{equation}

The right-hand side is finite for every \(\tau>0\), normalized, and concentrated near
\(x=y\) with width \(\sqrt{\tau}\).

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{point support}
  \quad
  \rightsquigarrow
  \quad
  \text{finite coherent support}.
  }
  \label{eq:appD-point-to-finite}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Momentum damping}
\label{appsubsec:momentum-damping}

Taking the Fourier transform of the heat kernel gives
\begin{equation}
  \widehat K_\tau^{(d)}(k)
  =
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}.
  \label{eq:appD-fourier-damping}
\end{equation}

Thus the same finite kernel that appears as a Gaussian in position space appears as a damping
factor in momentum space.

The high-momentum modes are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  k^2\gg\tau^{-1}
  \quad
  \Rightarrow
  \quad
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}\ll1.
\end{equation}

The low-momentum modes are retained:
\begin{equation}
  k^2\ll\tau^{-1}
  \quad
  \Rightarrow
  \quad
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}\approx1.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent width in position space}
  =
  \text{high-mode damping in spectral space}.
  }
  \label{eq:appD-position-spectral-duality}
\end{equation}

This is the scalar benchmark version of the MTT delta/wave duality.

\subsection{Coherence length and effective scale}
\label{appsubsec:coherence-length-effective-scale}

The Gaussian exponent is
\begin{equation}
  -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}.
\end{equation}
Therefore the characteristic spatial width is
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau}.
  \label{eq:appD-lcoh}
\end{equation}

Since
\begin{equation}
  [\tau]=L^2=E^{-2},
\end{equation}
the corresponding energy scale is
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2}.
  \label{eq:appD-Lambdaeff}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau},
  \qquad
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2}.
  }
  \label{eq:appD-scales-box}
\end{equation}

The finite-kernel correction parameter is
\begin{equation}
  \tau k^2
  =
  \left(
    \frac{k}{\Lambda_{\rm eff}}
  \right)^2.
\end{equation}

At low momentum:
\begin{equation}
  k\ll\Lambda_{\rm eff},
\end{equation}
the pointlike approximation is valid.

At high momentum:
\begin{equation}
  k\sim\Lambda_{\rm eff},
\end{equation}
finite coherent structure becomes resolvable.

\subsection{Finite propagator shadow}
\label{appsubsec:finite-propagator-shadow}

The ordinary Euclidean scalar propagator is
\begin{equation}
  \Delta(k)
  =
  \frac{1}{k^2+m^2}.
  \label{eq:appD-ordinary-propagator}
\end{equation}

Inserting the finite coherent heat-kernel factor gives
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}(k)
  =
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2+m^2}.
  \label{eq:appD-finite-propagator}
\end{equation}

In position space:
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}(x-y)
  =
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  \frac{\dd^dk}{(2\pi)^d}
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2+m^2}
  \ee^{\ii k\cdot(x-y)}.
  \label{eq:appD-finite-propagator-position}
\end{equation}

Equivalently:
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}
  =
  K_\tau^{(d)}\ast\Delta.
  \label{eq:appD-propagator-convolution}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite propagator}
  =
  \text{ordinary propagator convolved with finite coherent kernel}.
  }
  \label{eq:appD-finite-propagator-box}
\end{equation}

This is the cleanest scalar model of finite coherent scattering.

\subsection{Low-momentum expansion}
\label{appsubsec:low-momentum-expansion}

For
\begin{equation}
  \tau k^2\ll1,
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
  =
  1-\tau k^2+\frac12\tau^2k^4+O(\tau^3k^6).
  \label{eq:appD-exponential-expansion}
\end{equation}

Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}(k)
  =
  \frac{1}{k^2+m^2}
  -
  \tau
  \frac{k^2}{k^2+m^2}
  +
  \frac12\tau^2
  \frac{k^4}{k^2+m^2}
  +\cdots.
  \label{eq:appD-low-momentum-propagator}
\end{equation}

The first correction is controlled by
\begin{equation}
  \tau k^2.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite-kernel corrections are small when }\tau k^2\ll1.
  }
  \label{eq:appD-low-momentum-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{High-momentum behavior}
\label{appsubsec:high-momentum-behavior}

For
\begin{equation}
  k^2\gg\tau^{-1},
\end{equation}
the factor
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
\end{equation}
strongly suppresses high-momentum contributions.

In loop-type integrals, a scalar benchmark expression may take the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  \int
  \frac{\dd^dk}{(2\pi)^d}
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2+m^2}.
\end{equation}
The exponential factor improves ultraviolet convergence in the Euclidean benchmark.

However, this does not by itself prove a complete renormalization theorem for Lorentzian
quantum field theory. Gauge identities, unitarity, positivity, and analytic continuation must
be checked separately in each sector.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Euclidean high-mode damping is a benchmark for UV control, not by itself a full
  Lorentzian QFT proof.}
  }
  \label{eq:appD-UV-caution}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Four-dimensional Euclidean case}
\label{appsubsec:4D-euclidean-case}

For \(d=4\), the kernel is
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(4)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
  \label{eq:appD-4D-kernel}
\end{equation}

The finite propagator is
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}^{(4)}(k)
  =
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2+m^2}.
  \label{eq:appD-4D-propagator}
\end{equation}

The coherent length and effective scale are:
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau},
  \qquad
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}

The low-energy condition is:
\begin{equation}
  k^2\ll\tau^{-1}.
\end{equation}

This is the formula often useful for intuition, but it should not be confused with the physical
Lorentzian FP/MTT prescription.

\subsection{Why this is not the Lorentzian rule}
\label{appsubsec:not-lorentzian-rule}

The Euclidean benchmark uses
\begin{equation}
  A=-\Delta\ge0.
\end{equation}
The operator is positive, so
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}
\end{equation}
is a true damping operator.

In Lorentzian spacetime, the d'Alembertian
\begin{equation}
  \Box
\end{equation}
is indefinite. Therefore the expression
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box}
\end{equation}
is not the direct analogue of Euclidean heat flow.

MTT therefore imposes:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A\neq\Box
  \quad
  \text{as a naive fundamental Lorentzian damping operator}.
  }
  \label{eq:appD-not-box}
\end{equation}

The physical Lorentzian choices are instead:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item positive internal/fiber operators;
  \item positive spatial Cauchy-slice operators;
  \item Hamiltonian or constraint-compatible positive operators;
  \item gauge-quotiented physical operators;
  \item diffeomorphism-compatible geometric operators.
\end{enumerate}

Thus the scalar benchmark is a mathematical model of finite coherent damping, not the
universal physical prescription.

\subsection{Relation to FP/fiber damping}
\label{appsubsec:relation-FP-fiber}

In the fixed-point realization, the natural positive operator is
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3
  \kappa_n\Delta_{B_n}.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}\phi_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\phi_j.
\end{equation}

This is the internal/fiber analogue of
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
\end{equation}
in the Euclidean scalar benchmark.

The key difference is:
\begin{equation}
  k^2
  =
  \text{Euclidean base momentum squared}
\end{equation}
in the scalar benchmark, while
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2
  =
  \text{internal fiber eigenvalue}
\end{equation}
in the FP realization.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \ee^{-\tau k^2}
  \text{ is the Euclidean benchmark;}
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  \text{ is the FP/fiber physical analogue.}
  }
  \label{eq:appD-benchmark-vs-FP}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Finite source benchmark}
\label{appsubsec:finite-source-benchmark}

A point source in \(\mathbb R^d\) is
\begin{equation}
  J(x)=q\delta^{(d)}(x-x_0).
\end{equation}
The finite scalar benchmark replaces it by
\begin{equation}
  J_\tau(x)
  =
  qK_\tau^{(d)}(x-x_0).
  \label{eq:appD-finite-source}
\end{equation}

This source has total charge or weight:
\begin{equation}
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  J_\tau(x)\,\dd^dx
  =
  q.
\end{equation}

As \(\tau\downarrow0\),
\begin{equation}
  J_\tau(x)
  \to
  q\delta^{(d)}(x-x_0).
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{point source}
  =
  \text{sharp limit of finite coherent source}.
  }
  \label{eq:appD-source-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Finite contact benchmark}
\label{appsubsec:finite-contact-benchmark}

A local contact interaction has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  S_{\rm int}
  =
  \frac{\lambda}{4!}
  \int_{\mathbb R^d}
  \phi(x)^4\,\dd^dx.
  \label{eq:appD-local-contact}
\end{equation}

A finite coherent benchmark replaces exact coincidence by a finite overlap kernel:
\begin{equation}
  S_{\rm int}^{\rm adm}
  =
  \frac{\lambda}{4!}
  \int
  \prod_{i=1}^4\dd^dx_i\,
  K_\tau(x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4)
  \phi(x_1)\phi(x_2)\phi(x_3)\phi(x_4).
  \label{eq:appD-finite-contact}
\end{equation}

In the sharp limit:
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau(x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4)
  \to
  \delta(x_1-x_2)\delta(x_1-x_3)\delta(x_1-x_4),
\end{equation}
and the local contact interaction is recovered.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{local contact}
  =
  \text{sharp shadow of finite coherent overlap}.
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{appsubsec:scalar-benchmark-summary}

The scalar heat-kernel benchmark is defined by:
\begin{equation}
  A=-\Delta,
  \qquad
  P=I,
  \qquad
  \chi=1.
\end{equation}

The finite coherent admissibility operator is:
\begin{equation}
  \Badm=\ee^{\tau\Delta}.
\end{equation}

Its kernel is:
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
\end{equation}

Its Fourier transform is:
\begin{equation}
  \widehat K_\tau^{(d)}(k)=\ee^{-\tau k^2}.
\end{equation}

Its sharp limit is:
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)\to\delta^{(d)}(x-y).
\end{equation}

The coherent length and energy scale are:
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}\sim\sqrt{\tau},
  \qquad
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}\sim\tau^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}

The finite propagator shadow is:
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\rm adm}(k)
  =
  \frac{\ee^{-\tau k^2}}{k^2+m^2}.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the scalar benchmark proves explicitly how finite coherent kernels produce both
  Dirac-delta localization and spectral damping.}
  }
\end{equation}
% ============================================================
\section{Lorentzian admissibility, Cauchy-slice kernels, and causality}
\label{app:lorentzian-cauchy}
% ============================================================

This appendix records the Lorentzian admissibility logic used in the main text. The purpose is
to separate three ideas that are often conflated:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item Euclidean heat-kernel damping generated by a positive elliptic operator;
  \item Lorentzian hyperbolic propagation generated by a principal wave operator;
  \item finite Cauchy-slice kernels used as preparation, detector, source, internal projection,
  or lower-order effective terms.
\end{enumerate}

The central point is that MTT does not define Lorentzian finite coherent admissibility by
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box_g}.
\end{equation}
Instead, the damping operator must be positive in the relevant physical sector:
\begin{equation}
  A\ge0,
\end{equation}
or positive after projection, quotienting, or constraint reduction.

Thus the Lorentzian admissibility principle is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent damping must act through positive internal, spatial, Hamiltonian,
  gauge-physical, or diffeomorphism-compatible operators, not through naive }
  \ee^{-\tau\Box_g}.
  }
  \label{eq:appE-lorentzian-principle}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Euclidean heat flow versus Lorentzian wave flow}
\label{appsubsec:euclidean-vs-lorentzian}

In Euclidean signature, a positive elliptic operator such as
\begin{equation}
  A=-\Delta\ge0
\end{equation}
generates a heat semigroup:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}=\ee^{\tau\Delta}.
\end{equation}
This operator is smoothing and damping.

In Lorentzian signature, the d'Alembertian
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g
\end{equation}
is hyperbolic and indefinite. It is not a positive elliptic operator. Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box_g}
\end{equation}
is not a heat kernel in the same sense.

A naive Lorentzian exponential can introduce:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item acausal tails;
  \item infinite time-derivative instabilities;
  \item ghost-like poles;
  \item analytic continuation ambiguity;
  \item loss of unitarity;
  \item violation of gauge or gravitational constraints.
\end{enumerate}

Therefore MTT imposes:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A\neq\Box_g
  \quad
  \text{as a naive fundamental damping operator.}
  }
  \label{eq:appE-A-not-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Globally hyperbolic setup}
\label{appsubsec:globally-hyperbolic-setup}

Let \(Y^4\) be globally hyperbolic. Then
\begin{equation}
  Y^4\cong\mathbb R\times\Sigma,
\end{equation}
with Cauchy slices
\begin{equation}
  \Sigma_t.
\end{equation}
A scalar field is specified by Cauchy data
\begin{equation}
  \left(
  \phi|_{\Sigma_t},
  n^\mu\nabla_\mu\phi|_{\Sigma_t}
  \right),
\end{equation}
where \(n^\mu\) is the future-directed unit normal to \(\Sigma_t\).

A standard hyperbolic equation has schematic form
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g\phi+m^2\phi=J.
  \label{eq:appE-standard-hyperbolic}
\end{equation}
Its finite propagation speed is determined by the principal symbol of
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g.
\end{equation}

Thus the causal cone is controlled by the highest-derivative hyperbolic part of the equation.

\subsection{Positive Cauchy-slice operators}
\label{appsubsec:positive-cauchy-operators}

On each spatial slice \(\Sigma_t\), one may define positive spatial operators. A basic example
is
\begin{equation}
  A_\Sigma=-\Delta_{\Sigma_t}\ge0
\end{equation}
on a Riemannian slice, subject to appropriate boundary conditions.

Then one may define a finite Cauchy-slice filter
\begin{equation}
  B_\Sigma
  =
  P_\Sigma\chi(A_\Sigma)
  \ee^{-\tau A_\Sigma}
  \chi(A_\Sigma)P_\Sigma.
  \label{eq:appE-cauchy-filter}
\end{equation}

This filter acts on spatial data, not on Lorentzian spacetime by an indefinite heat flow.
It may be used to:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item prepare admissible initial data;
  \item define finite detector effects;
  \item define finite source profiles;
  \item project to physical spatial modes;
  \item filter internal/fiber data read on a Cauchy slice.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{spatial filtering of Cauchy data is Lorentzian-admissible when it preserves the
  physical energy and constraint spaces.}
  }
  \label{eq:appE-spatial-filtering-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Equal-time kernels}
\label{appsubsec:equal-time-kernels}

A finite equal-time kernel acts on spatial data by
\begin{equation}
  (\mathcal K_t f)(x)
  =
  \int_{\Sigma_t}
  K_t(x,y)f(y)\,\dd\mu_{\Sigma_t}(y).
  \label{eq:appE-equal-time-kernel}
\end{equation}
A sufficient boundedness condition is a Schur estimate:
\begin{equation}
  \sup_x
  \int_{\Sigma_t}
  |K_t(x,y)|\,\dd\mu_{\Sigma_t}(y)
  <\infty,
  \label{eq:appE-Schur-x}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \sup_y
  \int_{\Sigma_t}
  |K_t(x,y)|\,\dd\mu_{\Sigma_t}(x)
  <\infty.
  \label{eq:appE-Schur-y}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal K_t:L^2(\Sigma_t)\to L^2(\Sigma_t)
\end{equation}
is bounded.

More generally, one may require boundedness on the relevant Sobolev or energy space:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal K_t:H^s(\Sigma_t)\to H^s(\Sigma_t).
\end{equation}

This is the minimal analytic requirement for using such a kernel as a lower-order finite
admissibility term.

\subsection{Principal-symbol preservation}
\label{appsubsec:principal-symbol-preservation}

Consider a modified hyperbolic equation of the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g\phi+m^2\phi+\mathcal K_t\phi=J.
  \label{eq:appE-kernel-modified-equation}
\end{equation}
Assume \(\mathcal K_t\) is bounded on the relevant energy space and contains no additional
time derivatives.

The principal part is still
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g.
\end{equation}
Therefore the principal symbol is unchanged.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{bounded equal-time finite kernels do not change the hyperbolic principal symbol.}
  }
  \label{eq:appE-principal-symbol-box}
\end{equation}

This is the precise sense in which such kernels do not alter the local characteristic cone.

\subsection{Energy estimate}
\label{appsubsec:energy-estimate}

Let \(E(t)\) be the standard hyperbolic energy associated with Cauchy data on \(\Sigma_t\).
For the equation
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g\phi+m^2\phi+\mathcal K_t\phi=J,
\end{equation}
a schematic energy estimate has the form
\begin{equation}
  E(t)
  \le
  C
  \left[
    E(0)
    +
    \int_0^t
    \|J(s)\|^2\,\dd s
  \right]
  \exp(C_Kt),
  \label{eq:appE-energy-estimate}
\end{equation}
where \(C_K\) depends on a bound for \(\mathcal K_t\).

The finite kernel may change the energy growth constant, but it does not change the
highest-derivative hyperbolic operator.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{bounded lower-order kernels can preserve hyperbolic well-posedness while modifying
  finite response or mode mixing.}
  }
  \label{eq:appE-energy-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Important distinction: principal cone versus strict support}
\label{appsubsec:principal-cone-versus-support}

There is a subtle but important distinction.

Preserving the principal symbol means preserving the local characteristic cone. It does not
automatically mean that an arbitrary spatially nonlocal integral kernel preserves strict compact
support of solutions.

If \(K_t(x,y)\) has global spatial support and appears dynamically in the equation
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g\phi+m^2\phi+\mathcal K_t\phi=J,
\end{equation}
then the value of \(\mathcal K_t\phi\) at \(x\) may depend on \(\phi(y)\) at distant points
on the same slice. This can spoil strict support-domain statements even though the principal
symbol is unchanged.

Therefore MTT distinguishes three cases.

\paragraph{Case 1: preparation, source, detector, or readout kernels.}
If the finite kernel is used to prepare initial data, define a finite source, describe detector
resolution, or read out an internal projection, then it does not alter the Lorentzian evolution
law. The subsequent propagation is governed by the underlying hyperbolic system.

\paragraph{Case 2: local or support-controlled lower-order kernels.}
If the kernel has controlled spatial support compatible with the physical finite resolution, then
the effective domain of dependence may be enlarged only by the finite support radius, or may
be handled as part of the finite preparation/readout apparatus.

\paragraph{Case 3: globally supported dynamical kernels.}
If the kernel has unrestricted global spatial support and is inserted dynamically, then strict
support-domain preservation is not automatic. One must prove that no controllable acausal
signaling or physical domain-of-dependence violation results.

Thus the safe statement is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{principal-symbol preservation is automatic for bounded lower-order equal-time kernels;
  strict support-domain preservation requires support control or a preparation/readout
  interpretation.}
  }
  \label{eq:appE-support-caution-box}
\end{equation}

This distinction prevents MTT from overclaiming causality.

\subsection{Finite overlap versus acausal signaling}
\label{appsubsec:finite-overlap-vs-signaling}

Finite spatial overlap is not the same thing as acausal signaling.

A finite detector effect may sample a spatial region:
\begin{equation}
  E_x=|k_x\rangle\langle k_x|,
  \qquad
  k_x(y)=K_{\rm det}(y,x).
\end{equation}
This does not imply superluminal signal propagation. It means the detector has finite spatial
resolution.

Similarly, a finite source profile
\begin{equation}
  J_\tau(x)=qK_\tau(x,x_0)
\end{equation}
does not imply that information propagates outside the light cone. It means the source is not
a mathematical point.

Acausality would require controllable influence outside the domain allowed by the Lorentzian
dynamics.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent support}
  \neq
  \text{controllable acausal signaling}.
  }
  \label{eq:appE-finite-support-not-signaling}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Internal/fiber damping as the clean Lorentzian route}
\label{appsubsec:fiber-damping-clean-route}

The fixed-point realization gives the cleanest Lorentzian implementation. The total space is
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6,
\end{equation}
with \(Y^4\) Lorentzian and \(X^6\) compact Riemannian.

The finite damping operator is internal:
\begin{equation}
  A=A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3
  \kappa_n\Delta_{B_n},
  \qquad
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}\phi_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\phi_j.
\end{equation}

The Lorentzian base dynamics remain hyperbolic. The internal modes appear in four dimensions
as zero modes and massive excitations:
\begin{equation}
  G_{4D}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{Z_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}}
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
\end{equation}

The zero mode is undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{fiber damping avoids Lorentzian heat-flow pathology by damping compact internal
  spectra rather than the indefinite spacetime d'Alembertian.}
  }
  \label{eq:appE-fiber-damping-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Base regularization and the \(\varepsilon\) parameter}
\label{appsubsec:epsilon-parameter}

Some analytic constructions introduce
\begin{equation}
  A_\varepsilon
  =
  A_{\rm int}
  +
  \varepsilon A_{\rm base},
  \qquad
  \varepsilon\ge0.
\end{equation}
This can be useful as a regulator or approximation. However, it must not be interpreted
automatically as physical Lorentzian damping.

The clean FP/MTT physical choice is
\begin{equation}
  \varepsilon=0,
\end{equation}
so that
\begin{equation}
  A_\varepsilon=A_{\rm int}.
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  \varepsilon>0,
\end{equation}
then \(A_{\rm base}\) must be justified as one of:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item a positive spatial Cauchy-slice operator;
  \item a Euclidean analytic regulator to be removed;
  \item a Hamiltonian or energy operator on physical data;
  \item a detector or source profile;
  \item a constraint-compatible positive operator.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \varepsilon>0
  \text{ requires separate Lorentzian admissibility justification.}
  }
  \label{eq:appE-epsilon-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gauge constraints}
\label{appsubsec:gauge-cauchy-constraints}

Gauge fields have constraints on Cauchy data. For electromagnetism, Gauss law is
\begin{equation}
  \nabla\cdot\mathbf E=\rho.
\end{equation}
A finite Cauchy-slice filter must preserve this constraint or act after projection to physical
transverse data.

A gauge-admissible filter has schematic form
\begin{equation}
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}
  \chi(A_\Sigma^{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_\Sigma^{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_\Sigma^{\rm gauge})
  P_{\rm phys}.
  \label{eq:appE-gauge-cauchy}
\end{equation}

The requirement is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm gauge}
  \text{ must preserve gauge constraints and physical gauge equivalence.}
  }
  \label{eq:appE-gauge-constraint-box}
\end{equation}

Thus one may filter physical transverse data, BRST cohomology, or gauge-covariant internal
data. One may not arbitrarily damp gauge representatives.

\subsection{Gravity constraints}
\label{appsubsec:gravity-cauchy-constraints}

In ADM gravity, Cauchy data are
\begin{equation}
  (h_{ij},\pi^{ij}),
\end{equation}
subject to constraints
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H=0,
  \qquad
  \mathcal H_i=0.
\end{equation}

A gravitational finite filter must preserve the constraint surface or act on reduced
diffeomorphism-compatible data:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}
  \chi(A_\Sigma^{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_\Sigma^{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_\Sigma^{\rm grav})
  P_{\rm diff}.
  \label{eq:appE-grav-cauchy}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}
  \text{ must preserve gravitational constraints and diffeomorphism-compatible geometry.}
  }
  \label{eq:appE-gravity-constraint-box}
\end{equation}

A coordinate-component smoothing of the metric is not an admissible gravitational MTT filter.

\subsection{Manifest covariance as Lens}
\label{appsubsec:manifest-covariance-lens-app}

A Cauchy-slice construction selects a foliation
\begin{equation}
  \{\Sigma_t\}.
\end{equation}
This may obscure manifest Lorentz covariance. In MTT, this is interpreted as a Lens feature:
a representation chosen to define positive admissibility data.

The analogy is gauge fixing. A gauge-fixed description may not display all symmetries
manifestly, but it can still describe the correct physical content if gauge-invariant observables
and constraints are preserved.

Thus the relevant distinction is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{manifest covariance is a property of the representative;}
  }
\end{equation}
whereas:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{physical covariance is a property of observables, constraints, and transformation
  consistency.}
  }
\end{equation}

A Cauchy-slice MTT construction is admissible only if observable predictions do not depend
on arbitrary foliation choices, or if their foliation dependence is physically specified and
constraint-compatible.

\subsection{Causality checklist}
\label{appsubsec:causality-checklist}

A Lorentzian finite-kernel construction must pass the following checks:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item The damping operator is positive in the relevant physical sector.
  \item The construction does not use naive \(\ee^{-\tau\Box_g}\) as a fundamental heat flow.
  \item Cauchy-slice kernels are bounded on the intended energy/Sobolev space.
  \item The hyperbolic principal symbol is unchanged, unless a new causal theory is explicitly
  derived.
  \item Strict support-domain claims are justified by support control, locality, or a
  preparation/readout interpretation.
  \item Gauge constraints are preserved in gauge sectors.
  \item Diffeomorphism and Hamiltonian constraints are preserved in gravity.
  \item No controllable acausal signaling is introduced.
  \item Observable predictions are independent of unphysical Lens choices or transform
  consistently under changes of representative.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Lorentzian admissibility is a positivity, constraint, and causal-consistency condition.}
  }
  \label{eq:appE-causality-checklist-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{appsubsec:lorentzian-summary}

The Lorentzian d'Alembertian is not a positive damping operator:
\begin{equation}
  A\neq\Box_g
\end{equation}
as a naive fundamental MTT filter.

The admissible Lorentzian replacements are:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\ge0,
  \qquad
  A_\Sigma\ge0,
  \qquad
  A_{\rm gauge}\ge0
  \text{ on physical gauge data},
  \qquad
  A_{\rm grav}
  \text{ constraint-compatible}.
\end{equation}

A bounded equal-time kernel may preserve the hyperbolic principal symbol:
\begin{equation}
  \Box_g\phi+m^2\phi+\mathcal K_t\phi=J
  \quad
  \Rightarrow
  \quad
  \text{principal part remains }\Box_g.
\end{equation}

However, strict support-domain preservation requires additional support or interpretation
conditions. Arbitrary globally supported dynamical kernels are not automatically causal.

The safest physical realization is internal/fiber damping:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}},
\end{equation}
with the Lorentzian base remaining hyperbolic.

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT finite kernels are Lorentzian-admissible only when positivity, constraints,
  principal causal structure, and no-signaling are preserved.}
  }
\end{equation}
% ============================================================
\section{Gauge-compatible finite filters}
\label{app:gauge-compatible-filters}
% ============================================================

This appendix records the gauge-sector admissibility conditions used in the main text. The
central point is that a finite coherent filter is not gauge-admissible merely because it damps
high modes. It must respect gauge redundancy, physical-state projection, current
conservation, and the relevant Ward, Slavnov--Taylor, or BRST identities.

The gauge-sector version of the finite coherent admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  P_{\rm phys}.
  \label{eq:appF-gauge-Badm}
\end{equation}

The safe rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{filter after quotienting, or filter covariantly before quotienting.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-safe-rule}
\end{equation}

This appendix explains what that means.

\subsection{Gauge equivalence}
\label{appsubsec:gauge-equivalence}

For Abelian gauge theory, the local gauge potential transforms as
\begin{equation}
  A_\mu
  \mapsto
  A_\mu+\partial_\mu\alpha.
  \label{eq:appF-U1-transform}
\end{equation}
The field strength is
\begin{equation}
  F_{\mu\nu}
  =
  \partial_\mu A_\nu-\partial_\nu A_\mu,
\end{equation}
and is invariant under \eqref{eq:appF-U1-transform}:
\begin{equation}
  F_{\mu\nu}\mapsto F_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}

Thus \(A_\mu\) is a Lens representative, not a unique physical object. The physical content
is represented by gauge-invariant or gauge-covariant data such as:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item field strengths;
  \item Wilson loops;
  \item holonomies;
  \item conserved-current amplitudes;
  \item transverse photon modes;
  \item BRST cohomology classes;
  \item gauge-invariant composite observables.
\end{enumerate}

Therefore, a finite coherent filter cannot act on gauge representatives as if every component
were physical. Pure-gauge directions must be removed, quotiented, or handled covariantly.

\subsection{Physical projection}
\label{appsubsec:physical-projection}

Let
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm phys}
\end{equation}
denote the projection to physical gauge-admissible content. The precise form depends on the
formalism.

In a simple Abelian transverse-mode setting, one may use a transverse projector:
\begin{equation}
  P_T^{ij}(k)
  =
  \delta^{ij}
  -
  \frac{k^ik^j}{|\mathbf k|^2},
  \qquad
  \mathbf k\neq0.
  \label{eq:appF-spatial-transverse-projector}
\end{equation}
It satisfies
\begin{equation}
  k_iP_T^{ij}(k)=0
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  P_T^2=P_T.
\end{equation}

A transverse finite filter on a spatial Cauchy slice may then have the form
\begin{equation}
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm EM}
  =
  P_T
  \chi(A_T)
  \ee^{-\tau A_T}
  \chi(A_T)
  P_T,
  \label{eq:appF-transverse-filter}
\end{equation}
where \(A_T\ge0\) acts on transverse physical modes.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the finite filter must act on physical transverse data, not on arbitrary gauge
  representatives.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-transverse-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Conserved-current amplitudes}
\label{appsubsec:conserved-current-amplitudes}

Physical electromagnetic exchange amplitudes couple to conserved currents:
\begin{equation}
  \partial_\mu J^\mu=0.
\end{equation}
In momentum space,
\begin{equation}
  k_\mu J^\mu(k)=0.
  \label{eq:appF-current-conservation}
\end{equation}

Because of \eqref{eq:appF-current-conservation}, longitudinal components of the gauge
propagator do not contribute to physical conserved-current amplitudes. A gauge-compatible
finite exchange amplitude may be written schematically as
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal A_{\rm adm}(k)
  =
  e^2
  J_\mu(k)
  \frac{F_{\rm adm}(k)}{k^2+\ii\epsilon}
  P_{\rm phys}^{\mu\nu}(k)
  J'_\nu(-k),
  \label{eq:appF-current-amplitude}
\end{equation}
where \(P_{\rm phys}^{\mu\nu}\) represents the appropriate physical projector and
\(F_{\rm adm}\) is an admissible finite factor.

In a Euclidean benchmark,
\begin{equation}
  F_{\rm adm}(k)=\ee^{-\tau k^2}.
\end{equation}
In the FP/fiber realization, the safer physical statement is an internal-mode expansion:
\begin{equation}
  F_{\rm adm}
  \leadsto
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
\end{equation}
for the \(j\)-th internal gauge excitation.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge-compatible finite factors must multiply physical amplitudes, not unphysical
  gauge components.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Filter after quotienting}
\label{appsubsec:filter-after-quotienting}

The cleanest gauge procedure is:
\begin{equation}
  \text{gauge fields}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{physical quotient}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{finite admissibility filter}.
\end{equation}

At the Hilbert-space level this means:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{\rm gauge}
  \longrightarrow
  \mathcal H_{\rm phys},
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}:
  \mathcal H_{\rm phys}\to\mathcal H_{\rm phys}.
\end{equation}

The operator has the schematic form:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  P_{\rm phys}.
\end{equation}

In this case, the role of \(P_{\rm phys}\) is to ensure that pure-gauge directions are removed
before the finite filter is interpreted physically.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{after quotienting, finite damping acts only on physical gauge content.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-after-quotient-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Filter covariantly before quotienting}
\label{appsubsec:filter-before-quotienting}

Sometimes it is useful to filter before explicitly passing to the physical quotient. In that case,
the filter must transform covariantly under gauge transformations.

For a non-Abelian connection
\begin{equation}
  A_\mu=A_\mu^aT_a,
\end{equation}
the covariant derivative is
\begin{equation}
  D_\mu=\partial_\mu+[A_\mu,\cdot].
\end{equation}
A positive spatial covariant operator may be
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm cov}
  =
  -D_iD^i
  \label{eq:appF-covariant-spatial-laplacian}
\end{equation}
on a Cauchy slice, with appropriate gauge-covariant boundary conditions.

Under a gauge transformation \(g\), the operator transforms as
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm cov}
  \mapsto
  gA_{\rm cov}g^{-1}.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \chi(A_{\rm cov})\ee^{-\tau A_{\rm cov}}\chi(A_{\rm cov})
\end{equation}
also transforms covariantly.

This is admissible only if final observables are gauge-invariant or if the result is subsequently
projected to the physical quotient.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{before quotienting, the filter must be gauge-covariant and must not create
  gauge-artifact observables.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-before-quotient-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{BRST formulation}
\label{appsubsec:BRST-formulation}

In covariant quantization, physical states are often represented by BRST cohomology. Let
\begin{equation}
  Q_{\rm BRST}^2=0.
\end{equation}
The physical state space is
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{\rm phys}
  =
  \ker Q_{\rm BRST}/\operatorname{im}Q_{\rm BRST}.
  \label{eq:appF-BRST-cohomology}
\end{equation}

A finite coherent filter is BRST-compatible if it maps BRST-closed states to BRST-closed
states and does not turn BRST-exact redundancy into physical content.

A strong sufficient condition is
\begin{equation}
  [A_{\rm gauge},Q_{\rm BRST}]=0,
  \label{eq:appF-A-BRST-commute}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  [P_{\rm phys},Q_{\rm BRST}]=0,
  \label{eq:appF-P-BRST-commute}
\end{equation}
on the relevant domains.

Then
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys}
\end{equation}
preserves the physical cohomology class.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{BRST-compatible finite filtering preserves physical cohomology.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-BRST-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Ward identity condition}
\label{appsubsec:Ward-condition}

In Abelian gauge theory, Ward identities encode gauge invariance and current conservation.
A finite modification is gauge-admissible only if it preserves these identities.

For a current-exchange amplitude, this means longitudinal components must decouple:
\begin{equation}
  k_\mu\mathcal A^\mu=0
\end{equation}
where appropriate. Equivalently, current conservation
\begin{equation}
  k_\mu J^\mu=0
\end{equation}
must remain valid.

If a finite damping factor modifies transverse and longitudinal sectors differently in a way
that makes longitudinal components physical, the construction fails.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{Abelian finite filtering must preserve Ward identities and current conservation.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-Ward-box}
\end{equation}

This is why a Gaussian-looking factor is not enough. It must be inserted in a gauge-safe way.

\subsection{Slavnov--Taylor condition}
\label{appsubsec:Slavnov-Taylor-condition}

In non-Abelian gauge theory, Slavnov--Taylor identities encode the interacting gauge
constraints. A finite filter must preserve these identities or be derived from a gauge-invariant
or BRST-invariant regularized action.

A non-Abelian finite filter is inadmissible if it:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item breaks BRST symmetry;
  \item changes ghost cancellations inconsistently;
  \item spoils Slavnov--Taylor identities;
  \item violates unitarity of the physical sector;
  \item changes anomaly cancellation;
  \item turns gauge artifacts into physical poles.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{non-Abelian finite filtering must be identity-preserving, not merely high-mode
  damping.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-ST-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gauge-sector spectral weights}
\label{appsubsec:gauge-sector-spectral-weights}

In a clean physical gauge sector, suppose
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm gauge}\psi_j=\lambda_j\psi_j,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm phys}\psi_j=p_j\psi_j.
\end{equation}
Then, if
\begin{equation}
  [P_{\rm phys},A_{\rm gauge}]=0,
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}\psi_j
  =
  p_j\chi(\lambda_j)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_j}\psi_j.
  \label{eq:appF-gauge-modal-weight}
\end{equation}

This formula is valid only in the commuting physical-sector case. If
\begin{equation}
  [P_{\rm phys},A_{\rm gauge}]\neq0,
\end{equation}
the scalar weight formula is not valid without further analysis.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge-sector mode weights require physical projection and commuting spectral data.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-gauge-weight-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{FP/fiber gauge tower}
\label{appsubsec:FP-fiber-gauge-tower}

In the fixed-point realization, gauge fields may arise as zero-mode shadows of internal
geometric or bundle data. Let
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j.
\end{equation}
The internal admissibility weight is
\begin{equation}
  W_j=\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

A schematic four-dimensional gauge propagator after dimensional reduction may take the
form
\begin{equation}
  D_{\mu\nu}^{\rm eff}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{
    Z_j
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}
  P_{\mu\nu}^{(j)}(p).
  \label{eq:appF-gauge-effective-propagator}
\end{equation}
Here \(P_{\mu\nu}^{(j)}\) is the appropriate physical polarization or quotient-compatible
projector for the \(j\)-th mode.

The zero mode satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
\end{equation}
so
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}
This recovers the undamped low-energy gauge field.

Nonzero internal gauge excitations satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0,
\end{equation}
and are suppressed by
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{observed gauge fields are undamped zero-mode shadows; nonzero internal gauge
  excitations are admissibility-suppressed.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-gauge-zero-mode-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Finite electromagnetic source benchmark}
\label{appsubsec:finite-EM-source-benchmark}

A point charge is represented by
\begin{equation}
  \rho(\mathbf r)=q\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf r).
\end{equation}
A Euclidean finite-source benchmark replaces it by
\begin{equation}
  \rho_\tau(\mathbf r)
  =
  q(4\pi\tau)^{-3/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{r^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
  \label{eq:appF-finite-charge}
\end{equation}
This preserves total charge:
\begin{equation}
  \int_{\mathbb R^3}\rho_\tau(\mathbf r)\,\dd^3r=q.
\end{equation}

The corresponding potential is
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_\tau(r)
  =
  \frac{q}{4\pi r}
  \operatorname{erf}
  \left(
    \frac{r}{2\sqrt{\tau}}
  \right).
  \label{eq:appF-finite-Coulomb}
\end{equation}

This benchmark is useful for understanding finite source profiles. However, in the FP/fiber
realization, direct four-dimensional Gaussian smearing is not automatic. If the finite width is
internal, then electromagnetic corrections instead appear through internal-mode exchange:
\begin{equation}
  \Phi(r)
  =
  \frac{q}{4\pi r}
  \left[
    1+
    \sum_{j\ge1}
    c_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\ee^{-\mu_j r}
  \right],
  \label{eq:appF-Coulomb-Yukawa}
\end{equation}
where \(c_j\) depends on the internal gauge-sector overlap.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite Coulomb smearing is a benchmark; FP-realized electromagnetic corrections
  require internal-mode derivation.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-Coulomb-caution-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Holonomy and global gauge data}
\label{appsubsec:holonomy-global-gauge}

Gauge physics is not exhausted by local field strengths. A charged system transported around
a closed loop \(\gamma\) can acquire the phase
\begin{equation}
  \exp\left(
    \ii q\oint_\gamma A
  \right).
  \label{eq:appF-holonomy}
\end{equation}

Under
\begin{equation}
  A\mapsto A+\dd\alpha,
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}
  \oint_\gamma\dd\alpha=0
\end{equation}
for a single-valued gauge function on a closed loop. Therefore the holonomy is gauge-compatible.

In MTT language:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge holonomy is Circle phase read through Lens gauge representatives.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-holonomy-box}
\end{equation}

A finite gauge filter must preserve such global gauge data. It must not erase holonomies,
flux sectors, or bundle patching conditions unless a lawful transition is derived.

\subsection{Flux quantization}
\label{appsubsec:flux-quantization-gauge}

For suitable closed two-surfaces \(\Sigma\), flux quantization has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  \frac{1}{2\pi}
  \int_\Sigma F
  \in
  \mathbb Z.
  \label{eq:appF-flux-quantization}
\end{equation}
For magnetic monopoles, Dirac quantization gives
\begin{equation}
  eg=2\pi n,
  \qquad
  n\in\mathbb Z.
  \label{eq:appF-Dirac-quantization}
\end{equation}

These are global Lens--Circle consistency conditions. A finite filter that breaks flux
quantization or bundle patching is not gauge-admissible.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge finite filtering must preserve global topological consistency.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-global-consistency-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Anomaly constraints}
\label{appsubsec:anomaly-constraints}

A chiral gauge theory is consistent only if its gauge anomalies cancel. A finite coherent
filter must not spoil this cancellation.

Therefore, any Standard Model or chiral gauge-sector MTT realization must preserve:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item gauge anomaly cancellation;
  \item mixed gauge-gravity anomaly cancellation where relevant;
  \item global anomaly constraints;
  \item the correct chiral representation content.
\end{enumerate}

If the filter changes representation weights, removes required states, or introduces anomalous
unpaired modes, the gauge sector fails.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{anomaly cancellation is a mandatory gauge-sector admissibility condition.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-anomaly-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gauge failure modes}
\label{appsubsec:gauge-failure-modes}

A gauge-sector finite filter fails if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item pure-gauge modes are treated as physical;
  \item \(P_{\rm phys}\) is not a valid quotient-compatible projector;
  \item the filter breaks Ward identities;
  \item the filter breaks Slavnov--Taylor identities;
  \item BRST cohomology is not preserved;
  \item current conservation is violated;
  \item anomaly cancellation is spoiled;
  \item flux quantization or bundle patching is broken;
  \item longitudinal components acquire unphysical poles;
  \item the Lorentzian filter is implemented as naive \(\ee^{-\tau\Box}\).
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge-sector admissibility is quotient compatibility plus identity preservation.}
  }
  \label{eq:appF-failure-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{appsubsec:gauge-compatible-summary}

The gauge-sector finite coherent admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  P_{\rm phys}.
\end{equation}

The safe rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{filter after quotienting, or filter covariantly before quotienting.}
  }
\end{equation}

Gauge admissibility requires:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item physical projection or gauge covariance;
  \item Ward or Slavnov--Taylor identity preservation;
  \item BRST compatibility where relevant;
  \item current conservation;
  \item anomaly cancellation;
  \item global bundle and flux consistency;
  \item Lorentzian positivity and causal admissibility.
\end{enumerate}

In the FP/fiber realization, the zero-mode gauge sector is undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1,
\end{equation}
while nonzero internal gauge excitations are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge theory is finite coherent admissibility constrained by Lens quotient structure.}
  }
\end{equation}

% ============================================================
\section{Diffeomorphism-compatible gravitational filters}
\label{app:gravitational-filters}
% ============================================================

This appendix records the gravitational admissibility conditions used in the main text. The
central point is that a finite coherent filter in gravity cannot act on arbitrary coordinate
components of the metric. It must act on diffeomorphism-compatible geometric content.

The gravitational finite coherent admissibility operator has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})
  P_{\rm diff}.
  \label{eq:appG-grav-Badm}
\end{equation}
Here \(P_{\rm diff}\) is a diffeomorphism-compatible projection or reduction map, and
\(A_{\rm grav}\) is a positive or constraint-compatible geometric admissibility operator.

The gravitational safe rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{filter geometric content, not coordinate artifacts.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-safe-rule}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Diffeomorphism equivalence}
\label{appsubsec:diffeomorphism-equivalence}

In general relativity, metrics related by diffeomorphism represent the same physical geometry:
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}
  \sim
  \varphi^\ast g_{\mu\nu}.
  \label{eq:appG-diff-equivalence}
\end{equation}
Coordinates are therefore Lens representatives. They are not themselves physical labels.

This means that the components
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}(x)
\end{equation}
are not independently physical in a coordinate-free sense. A finite filter that smooths or damps
metric components in a chosen coordinate chart may fail to be geometrically meaningful.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{a gravitational finite filter must respect diffeomorphism equivalence.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-diff-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Why naive metric-component smoothing fails}
\label{appsubsec:naive-metric-smoothing}

A coordinate-dependent smoothing operation may have the form
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}(x)
  \mapsto
  \int K(x,y)g_{\mu\nu}(y)\,\dd y.
\end{equation}
This expression is generally not tensorial, not diffeomorphism-compatible, and not tied to the
constraint structure of general relativity.

Such an operation can:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item depend on the coordinate chart;
  \item fail to preserve the Hamiltonian and momentum constraints;
  \item smooth pure gauge directions as if they were physical;
  \item break stress-energy compatibility;
  \item produce a tensor field that no longer solves the gravitational equations;
  \item alter boundary or horizon data without an admissible geometric rule.
\end{enumerate}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{coordinate-component smoothing is not an admissible gravitational MTT filter.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-no-component-smoothing}
\end{equation}

\subsection{ADM data and constraints}
\label{appsubsec:ADM-data-constraints}

On a globally hyperbolic spacetime, one may use an ADM decomposition:
\begin{equation}
  Y^4\cong\mathbb R\times\Sigma.
\end{equation}
The gravitational Cauchy data on a slice \(\Sigma_t\) are
\begin{equation}
  (h_{ij},K_{ij}),
\end{equation}
or equivalently
\begin{equation}
  (h_{ij},\pi^{ij}),
\end{equation}
where \(h_{ij}\) is the spatial metric and \(\pi^{ij}\) is its conjugate momentum.

These data must satisfy the constraints:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H=0,
  \qquad
  \mathcal H_i=0.
  \label{eq:appG-ADM-constraints}
\end{equation}

Let
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal C_{\rm grav}
  =
  \{(h_{ij},\pi^{ij}):\mathcal H=0,\ \mathcal H_i=0\}
\end{equation}
denote the constraint surface.

A gravitational finite filter is admissible only if it preserves the constraint surface:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}:
  \mathcal C_{\rm grav}\to\mathcal C_{\rm grav},
  \label{eq:appG-constraint-surface-preservation}
\end{equation}
or acts on the reduced quotient:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal C_{\rm grav}/\mathrm{Diff}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravitational filtering must preserve the constraint surface or act after reduction.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-constraint-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Cauchy-slice gravitational filter}
\label{appsubsec:cauchy-gravitational-filter}

A Cauchy-slice gravitational filter has schematic form
\begin{equation}
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}
  \chi(A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav})
  P_{\rm diff}.
  \label{eq:appG-cauchy-grav-filter}
\end{equation}

Here:
\begin{align}
  P_{\rm diff}
  &: \text{projection to diffeomorphism- or constraint-compatible content},\\
  A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}
  &: \text{positive spatial/geometric admissibility operator},\\
  \chi(A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav})
  &: \text{admissible spectral window},\\
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}}
  &: \text{finite damping of inadmissible geometric modes}.
\end{align}

This is the gravitational analogue of:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  P_{\rm phys}
\end{equation}
in gauge theory.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravity requires diffeomorphism-compatible projection just as gauge theory requires
  gauge-compatible projection.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-gauge-gravity-analogy}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Constraint-compatibility conditions}
\label{appsubsec:constraint-compatibility}

In an operator formulation, a strong schematic compatibility condition is:
\begin{equation}
  [A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav},\widehat{\mathcal H}]\approx0,
  \qquad
  [A_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav},\widehat{\mathcal H}_i]\approx0,
  \label{eq:appG-constraint-commutators}
\end{equation}
where \(\approx0\) means equality on the constraint surface or modulo constraints.

Similarly, the projector must satisfy:
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm diff}
  \mathcal C_{\rm grav}
  \subseteq
  \mathcal C_{\rm grav}.
\end{equation}

More generally, one may require only that the full composed filter preserve equivalence
classes:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\Sigma}^{\rm grav}:
  \mathcal C_{\rm grav}/\mathrm{Diff}
  \to
  \mathcal C_{\rm grav}/\mathrm{Diff}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the gravitational admissibility condition is preservation of physical geometric
  equivalence classes.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-equivalence-class-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Linearized gravity}
\label{appsubsec:linearized-gravity-filter}

In weak-field gravity, one expands around a background:
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}
  =
  \bar g_{\mu\nu}+h_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}
For a flat background,
\begin{equation}
  \bar g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}

At linear order, infinitesimal diffeomorphisms act by
\begin{equation}
  h_{\mu\nu}
  \mapsto
  h_{\mu\nu}
  +
  \partial_\mu\xi_\nu
  +
  \partial_\nu\xi_\mu.
  \label{eq:appG-linearized-diff}
\end{equation}

The physical graviton modes are transverse-traceless. On a spatial slice, one represents them
by
\begin{equation}
  h_{ij}^{\rm TT},
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
  \partial^i h_{ij}^{\rm TT}=0,
  \qquad
  \delta^{ij}h_{ij}^{\rm TT}=0.
\end{equation}

A weak-field admissible gravitational filter is therefore:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm grav}^{\rm lin}
  =
  P_{\rm TT}
  \chi(A_{\rm TT})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm TT}}
  \chi(A_{\rm TT})
  P_{\rm TT},
  \label{eq:appG-linear-filter}
\end{equation}
where \(P_{\rm TT}\) projects to transverse-traceless data and \(A_{\rm TT}\ge0\) acts on that
sector.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{linearized gravitational filtering must act on physical spin-2 data.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-TT-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Transverse-traceless propagator shadow}
\label{appsubsec:TT-propagator-shadow}

In a positive spectral or Euclidean benchmark, a finite graviton propagator shadow may be
written schematically as
\begin{equation}
  D_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{\rm adm}(k)
  \sim
  \frac{F_{\rm adm}(k)}
  {k^2}
  P_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}^{\rm TT}(k).
  \label{eq:appG-TT-propagator}
\end{equation}

In the Euclidean benchmark,
\begin{equation}
  F_{\rm adm}(k)=\ee^{-\tau k^2}.
\end{equation}

In the FP/fiber realization, the safer physical analogue is:
\begin{equation}
  F_{\rm adm}
  \leadsto
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2},
\end{equation}
where \(\mu_j^2\) is an internal geometric or matter-coupled eigenvalue.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite gravitational propagator shadows are meaningful only after physical spin-2 or
  geometric projection.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-TT-propagator-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Lichnerowicz-type operators}
\label{appsubsec:Lichnerowicz-operators}

On a curved Riemannian or spatial background, natural geometric operators on symmetric
two-tensors include Lichnerowicz-type operators. A representative form is:
\begin{equation}
  (\Delta_L h)_{\mu\nu}
  =
  -\nabla^2 h_{\mu\nu}
  -
  2R_{\mu\rho\nu\sigma}h^{\rho\sigma}
  +
  R_\mu{}^\rho h_{\rho\nu}
  +
  R_\nu{}^\rho h_{\mu\rho}.
  \label{eq:appG-Lichnerowicz}
\end{equation}

The exact sign convention and curvature terms depend on the background, the gauge-fixing
choice, and the field space.

In MTT, such an operator is admissible only if it is:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item geometric;
  \item positive or controlled on the intended sector;
  \item compatible with constraints;
  \item compatible with diffeomorphism quotienting;
  \item compatible with Lorentzian causality when used in a physical Lorentzian sector.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A_{\rm grav}
  =
  \text{positive or constraint-compatible geometric stabilization operator}.
  }
  \label{eq:appG-Agrav-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Geometry-dependent heat kernels}
\label{appsubsec:geometry-dependent-heat-kernels}

For a positive geometric operator \(A_g\) on a fixed Riemannian geometry, the heat kernel is
\begin{equation}
  K_g(x,y;\tau)
  =
  \langle x|
  \ee^{-\tau A_g}
  |y\rangle.
\end{equation}
For small \(\tau\), one has a local asymptotic expansion of the schematic form:
\begin{equation}
  K_g(x,y;\tau)
  \sim
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{\sigma_g(x,y)}{2\tau}
  \right)
  \Delta_g^{1/2}(x,y)
  \left[
    1+\tau a_1(x,y)+\tau^2a_2(x,y)+\cdots
  \right].
  \label{eq:appG-heat-expansion}
\end{equation}

This shows that a gravitational finite kernel is shaped by:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item geodesic distance;
  \item curvature;
  \item parallel transport;
  \item volume measure;
  \item topology;
  \item boundary conditions.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravitational finite kernels are geometry-dependent coherent responses, not flat
  Gaussians pasted onto spacetime.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-geometry-dependent-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Stress-energy compatibility}
\label{appsubsec:stress-energy-compatibility-app}

Matter sources coupled to gravity must satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \nabla_\mu T^{\mu\nu}=0.
  \label{eq:appG-stress-conservation}
\end{equation}
A finite coherent matter source may replace a point idealization:
\begin{equation}
  T_{\mu\nu}(x)
  \sim
  m u_\mu u_\nu\delta(x-x_0)
\end{equation}
by
\begin{equation}
  T_{\mu\nu}^{\rm adm}(x)
  \sim
  m u_\mu u_\nu K_{\rm adm}(x,x_0).
\end{equation}

This replacement is admissible only if the resulting stress-energy tensor satisfies
conservation and the gravitational constraints.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite stress-energy profiles must be dynamically and geometrically consistent.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-stress-box}
\end{equation}

Finite smoothing of matter sources is not automatically gravitationally admissible.

\subsection{Horizons, boundaries, and Nil structure}
\label{appsubsec:horizons-boundaries-nil}

Gravity contains Nil structures in the form of:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item event horizons;
  \item apparent horizons;
  \item trapped surfaces;
  \item singularity boundaries;
  \item asymptotic boundaries;
  \item cosmological boundaries;
  \item black-hole endpoint sectors.
\end{enumerate}

A finite gravitational filter must respect boundary and horizon data. It may not arbitrarily
remove, smooth, or mix such structures unless the resulting geometry is an admissible survivor
geometry satisfying the constraints.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravitational Nil structures require admissible survivor-geometry rules.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-nil-boundary-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Curvature as Circle holonomy}
\label{appsubsec:curvature-holonomy-app}

Curvature is the infinitesimal holonomy of parallel transport. For a vector \(v^\rho\),
\begin{equation}
  [\nabla_\mu,\nabla_\nu]v^\rho
  =
  R^\rho{}_{\sigma\mu\nu}v^\sigma.
  \label{eq:appG-curvature-commutator}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  R^\rho{}_{\sigma\mu\nu}
  =
  \text{Circle holonomy of gravitational frame transport}.
  }
  \label{eq:appG-curvature-circle-box}
\end{equation}

The gravitational triplet is therefore:
\begin{align}
  \Lens_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{diffeomorphism quotient and geometric representation},\\
  \Circle_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{curvature and frame holonomy},\\
  \Nil_{\rm grav}
  &: \text{boundary, horizon, singularity, survivor geometry}.
\end{align}

\subsection{FP/fiber gravitational tower}
\label{appsubsec:FP-fiber-gravity-tower}

In the fixed-point realization, gravitational or geometry-coupled internal excitations may
contribute to effective four-dimensional gravity. If
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
\end{equation}
then internal admissibility gives weight
\begin{equation}
  W_j=\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

A schematic effective gravitational propagator or response may have the form:
\begin{equation}
  G_{\rm grav}^{\rm eff}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{
    Z_j^{\rm grav}
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}
  P_j^{\rm phys}(p),
  \label{eq:appG-grav-effective-tower}
\end{equation}
where \(P_j^{\rm phys}\) is the appropriate physical spin/geometric projector.

The zero mode is undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}

Nonzero internal modes are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0,
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{observed low-energy gravity is the diffeomorphism-compatible zero-mode shadow;
  nonzero internal gravitational excitations are admissibility-suppressed.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-gravity-zero-mode-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Weak-field phenomenological template}
\label{appsubsec:weak-field-gravity-template}

A controlled weak-field execution program is:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item choose a background \(\bar g_{\mu\nu}\);
  \item expand
  \begin{equation}
    g_{\mu\nu}=\bar g_{\mu\nu}+h_{\mu\nu};
  \end{equation}
  \item identify physical perturbations, e.g. transverse-traceless modes;
  \item choose a positive operator \(A_{\rm TT}\) on that sector;
  \item construct
  \begin{equation}
    B_{\rm grav}^{\rm lin}
    =
    P_{\rm TT}
    \chi(A_{\rm TT})
    \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm TT}}
    \chi(A_{\rm TT})
    P_{\rm TT};
  \end{equation}
  \item compute finite corrections to propagation or source coupling;
  \item compare with weak-field and gravitational-wave constraints.
\end{enumerate}

This program is perturbative. It does not solve nonperturbative quantum gravity.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{weak-field MTT gravity is an execution benchmark, not the full theory.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-weak-field-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Nonperturbative execution requirements}
\label{appsubsec:nonperturbative-gravity-requirements}

A full nonperturbative gravitational MTT sector must supply:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm grav},
  \qquad
  P_{\rm diff},
  \qquad
  \chi_{\rm grav},
  \qquad
  \tau_{\rm grav}.
\end{equation}

It must also satisfy:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item diffeomorphism compatibility;
  \item Hamiltonian and momentum constraint preservation;
  \item physical state-space construction;
  \item positive or controlled inner product;
  \item recovery of classical general relativity;
  \item Lorentzian causal admissibility;
  \item stress-energy compatibility;
  \item boundary and horizon consistency;
  \item no coordinate-dependent smoothing artifacts.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the schematic gravitational filter is an architecture; full quantum gravity requires
  sectoral construction.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-nonperturbative-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gravity failure modes}
\label{appsubsec:gravity-failure-modes-app}

A gravitational finite filter fails if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item \(A_{\rm grav}\) is taken to be naive \(\Box_g\) heat flow;
  \item metric components are damped as coordinate components;
  \item pure diffeomorphism modes are treated as physical;
  \item the Hamiltonian constraint is violated;
  \item the momentum constraints are violated;
  \item the constraint algebra is broken;
  \item stress-energy conservation fails;
  \item boundary or horizon data are erased without an admissible rule;
  \item a perturbative projector is used outside its regime;
  \item observable predictions depend on arbitrary coordinate or foliation choices.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravitational admissibility is diffeomorphism compatibility plus constraint
  preservation.}
  }
  \label{eq:appG-failure-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{appsubsec:gravitational-filters-summary}

The gravitational finite coherent admissibility operator is:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})
  P_{\rm diff}.
\end{equation}

It is admissible only when it acts on diffeomorphism-compatible geometric content.

In ADM form, it must preserve:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H=0,
  \qquad
  \mathcal H_i=0.
\end{equation}

In weak-field form, it should act on physical transverse-traceless data:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm grav}^{\rm lin}
  =
  P_{\rm TT}
  \chi(A_{\rm TT})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm TT}}
  \chi(A_{\rm TT})
  P_{\rm TT}.
\end{equation}

In the FP/fiber realization, zero-mode gravity is undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1,
\end{equation}
while nonzero internal geometric excitations are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravity is finite coherent admissibility constrained by diffeomorphism-compatible
  geometry.}
  }
\end{equation}
% ============================================================
\section{Fixed-point fiber realization and dimensional reduction}
\label{app:FP-fiber-reduction}
% ============================================================

This appendix records the fixed-point/fiber realization that gives the finite coherent
admissibility operator its geometric backbone. The purpose is to make explicit how the
abstract operator
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
\end{equation}
is realized in the compact internal/fiber setting and how its four-dimensional shadows arise
after dimensional reduction.

The central fixed-point realization is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6,
  }
  \label{eq:appH-M10}
\end{equation}
where \(Y^4\) is the observed Lorentzian base and \(X^6\) is a compact Riemannian internal
space or compact internal fiber geometry.

The finite coherent admissibility operator acts primarily on internal/fiber spectral data:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})
  P_{\rm coh}.
  }
  \label{eq:appH-FP-Badm}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Total space and sectoral Hilbert data}
\label{appsubsec:total-space-sectoral-data}

Let
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6.
\end{equation}
The base \(Y^4\) carries Lorentzian dynamics. The internal space \(X^6\) is compact and
Riemannian. A simple scalar Hilbert space model is
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{10}
  =
  L^2(Y^4)\otimes L^2(X^6),
\end{equation}
or, more carefully in Lorentzian settings, a Cauchy-space Hilbert structure of the form
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{\Sigma}
  =
  L^2(\Sigma)\otimes L^2(X^6),
\end{equation}
where \(\Sigma\subset Y^4\) is a Cauchy slice.

For fields with spin, gauge, or bundle content, one replaces \(L^2(X^6)\) by sections of the
appropriate internal bundle:
\begin{equation}
  L^2(X^6,E).
\end{equation}

Thus the generic sectoral Hilbert data have the form
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{\rm sector}
  =
  \mathcal H_{Y,\rm sector}
  \otimes
  \mathcal H_{X,\rm sector},
  \label{eq:appH-sector-Hilbert}
\end{equation}
subject to gauge, gravitational, and constraint reductions when relevant.

\subsection{Internal admissibility operator}
\label{appsubsec:internal-admissibility-operator}

The fixed-point internal admissibility operator is
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3
  \kappa_n\Delta_{B_n},
  \qquad
  \kappa_n>0.
  \label{eq:appH-Aint}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta_{B_n}\) are positive fiber Laplacians or sectoral positive elliptic operators on
compact internal directions.

Since each \(\Delta_{B_n}\) is nonnegative and \(\kappa_n>0\),
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}

This positivity is the key analytic reason the FP realization avoids the Lorentzian
\(\ee^{-\tau\Box}\) problem. The damping factor is:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}},
\end{equation}
not
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\Box_Y}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{FP damping acts on compact positive internal spectra, not on the indefinite
  Lorentzian d'Alembertian.}
  }
  \label{eq:appH-internal-not-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Internal spectrum}
\label{appsubsec:internal-spectrum}

Because \(X^6\) is compact and \(A_{\rm int}\) is elliptic in the internal directions, the
spectrum is discrete in the clean compact case:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
  \label{eq:appH-internal-spectrum}
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
  0=\mu_0^2
  \le
  \mu_1^2
  \le
  \mu_2^2
  \le
  \cdots.
\end{equation}

The finite damping factor acts as
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}\phi_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\phi_j.
  \label{eq:appH-internal-damping}
\end{equation}

The zero mode is undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
  \label{eq:appH-zero-mode-undamped}
\end{equation}

The nonzero modes are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
  \label{eq:appH-nonzero-suppressed}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{zero modes survive; nonzero internal modes are admissibility-damped.}
  }
  \label{eq:appH-zero-nonzero-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{The coherent projector}
\label{appsubsec:coherent-projector}

The fixed-point coherent projector is
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}\Pi_{B_2}\Pi_{B_3},
  \label{eq:appH-Pcoh}
\end{equation}
where \(\Pi_{B_n}\) projects to the harmonic sector of the \(n\)-th internal/fiber operator.

Its range is
\begin{equation}
  \Ran P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3
  \ker\Delta_{B_n}.
  \label{eq:appH-joint-kernel}
\end{equation}

Thus the coherent sector is not a vague retained sector. It is the joint fiber-harmonic sector:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \mathcal H_{\rm coh}
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3
  \ker\Delta_{B_n}.
  }
  \label{eq:appH-coherent-sector-box}
\end{equation}

In the clean spectral setting,
\begin{equation}
  [P_{\rm coh},A_{\rm int}]=0.
\end{equation}
Therefore the commuting modal formula applies:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}\phi_j
  =
  p_j
  \chi(\mu_j^2)^2
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  \phi_j,
  \label{eq:appH-FP-modal-action}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}\phi_j=p_j\phi_j,
  \qquad
  p_j\in\{0,1\}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{The gap-selected damping scale}
\label{appsubsec:gap-selected-scale}

Let \(\lambda_\ast>0\) be the relevant uniform spectral gap in the discarded internal sector.
Assume the discarded-sector flow satisfies
\begin{equation}
  \|Q\Phi_tQ\|
  \le
  C_Q\ee^{-\lambda_\ast t},
  \label{eq:appH-gap-flow}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
  Q=I-P_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}

Given an admissibility tolerance
\begin{equation}
  0<\epsadm<1,
\end{equation}
the damping-selected time is defined by
\begin{equation}
  C_Q\ee^{-\lambda_\ast\tau}=\epsadm.
\end{equation}
Solving gives
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}.
  }
  \label{eq:appH-tau-gap}
\end{equation}

If the characteristic internal radius is \(R\), then for Laplace-type operators,
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2}.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  \sim
  R^2
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
  \label{eq:appH-tau-R}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  }.
  \label{eq:appH-lcoh-R}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the finite coherent width is geometrically tied to the internal spectral gap.}
  }
  \label{eq:appH-geometric-width-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Dimensional reduction}
\label{appsubsec:dimensional-reduction}

A field on the total space may be expanded in internal eigenmodes:
\begin{equation}
  \Phi(x,z)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \varphi_j(x)\phi_j(z),
  \qquad
  x\in Y^4,
  \quad
  z\in X^6.
  \label{eq:appH-KK-expansion}
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
\end{equation}
then \(\mu_j^2\) appears in the four-dimensional effective theory as an internal mass
contribution. In the simplest scalar model:
\begin{equation}
  m_j^2=m_0^2+\mu_j^2.
  \label{eq:appH-effective-mass}
\end{equation}

The finite admissibility factor contributes:
\begin{equation}
  W_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
  \label{eq:appH-Wj}
\end{equation}

Thus the schematic four-dimensional effective propagator is:
\begin{equation}
  G_{4D}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{
    Z_j\chi(\mu_j^2)^2
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
  \label{eq:appH-effective-propagator}
\end{equation}

Here \(Z_j\) depends on normalization, internal wavefunction overlap, sectoral projection,
and physical-state positivity.

The zero-mode term is:
\begin{equation}
  \frac{Z_0\chi(0)^2}{p^2-m_0^2+\ii\epsilon}.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  \chi(0)=1,
\end{equation}
the zero-mode propagation is undamped.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{ordinary four-dimensional low-energy physics is recovered from undamped zero modes.}
  }
  \label{eq:appH-zero-mode-recovery-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Källén--Lehmann positivity}
\label{appsubsec:KL-positivity}

A four-dimensional spectral representation is physically acceptable only if the residues in
the physical sector are nonnegative:
\begin{equation}
  Z_j\ge0.
\end{equation}

The damping factor
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
\end{equation}
is positive, so it does not by itself introduce negative residues. However, it also does not
repair a sector that already contains ghosts, negative-norm states, or constraint violations.

Thus the effective propagator
\begin{equation}
  G_{4D}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{
    Z_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}
\end{equation}
is Källén--Lehmann positive only when
\begin{equation}
  Z_j\ge0
\end{equation}
in the physical sector.

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{internal damping preserves positive residues but does not cure nonphysical sectors.}
  }
  \label{eq:appH-KL-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gauge-sector dimensional reduction}
\label{appsubsec:gauge-dimensional-reduction}

For gauge fields, dimensional reduction must respect the gauge quotient. A schematic
gauge-sector tower has the form:
\begin{equation}
  D_{\mu\nu}^{\rm eff}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{
    Z_j^{\rm gauge}
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}
  P_{\mu\nu}^{(j)}(p).
  \label{eq:appH-gauge-tower}
\end{equation}

Here \(P_{\mu\nu}^{(j)}\) is the physical polarization or quotient-compatible projector for the
\(j\)-th mode.

The gauge-sector zero mode must reproduce the observed low-energy gauge field:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}

Nonzero internal gauge excitations are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gauge dimensional reduction must produce undamped physical zero modes and
  quotient-compatible damped excitations.}
  }
  \label{eq:appH-gauge-reduction-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gravity-sector dimensional reduction}
\label{appsubsec:gravity-dimensional-reduction}

For gravity, dimensional reduction must respect diffeomorphism compatibility and constraints.
A schematic gravitational tower has the form:
\begin{equation}
  G_{\rm grav}^{\rm eff}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{
    Z_j^{\rm grav}
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}
  P_j^{\rm phys}(p),
  \label{eq:appH-gravity-tower}
\end{equation}
where \(P_j^{\rm phys}\) is the appropriate spin/geometric physical projector.

The zero-mode gravitational sector must reproduce the observed low-energy gravitational
field:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}

Nonzero internal geometric excitations are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{gravity dimensional reduction must be diffeomorphism-compatible and constraint
  preserving.}
  }
  \label{eq:appH-gravity-reduction-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Internal overlaps and effective couplings}
\label{appsubsec:internal-overlaps}

Effective four-dimensional couplings are determined by internal overlap integrals. For example,
a schematic quartic scalar coupling may reduce as:
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_{ijkl}^{\rm eff}
  =
  \lambda_{10D}
  \int_{X^6}
  \phi_i(z)\phi_j(z)\phi_k(z)\phi_l(z)
  \,\dd\mu_X(z).
  \label{eq:appH-overlap-coupling}
\end{equation}

With finite admissibility, the corresponding contribution carries mode weights:
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_i^2},
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2},
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_k^2},
  \qquad
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_l^2},
\end{equation}
depending on how the finite filter enters the vertex.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{effective couplings are internal overlap integrals dressed by admissibility weights.}
  }
  \label{eq:appH-overlap-box}
\end{equation}

This is why numerical predictions require explicit internal geometry.

\subsection{Locality in total space and shadows on the base}
\label{appsubsec:locality-total-space-shadows}
