In the total space \(M_{10}\), dynamics or support may be local or controlled in the internal
directions. After projection to \(Y^4\), the same structure can appear as:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item finite source profiles;
  \item finite detector effects;
  \item non-pointlike form factors;
  \item suppressed internal-mode towers;
  \item apparent finite nonlocality;
  \item effective mixed states from unobserved internal variables.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{four-dimensional finite nonlocality can be a projection shadow of controlled
  total-space or fiber structure.}
  }
  \label{eq:appH-locality-shadow-box}
\end{equation}

This does not license arbitrary nonlocality. The Lorentzian base must still satisfy causal
admissibility.

\subsection{Base--fiber entanglement}
\label{appsubsec:base-fiber-entanglement}

A total state may fail to factor between base and internal variables:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi(x,z)
  \neq
  \psi(x)\phi(z).
\end{equation}
In an internal eigenbasis:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi(x,z)
  =
  \sum_j
  \psi_j(x)\phi_j(z).
  \label{eq:appH-base-fiber-expansion}
\end{equation}

If more than one internal mode contributes coherently, the base and fiber sectors may be
entangled.

After internal projection or tracing over inaccessible internal data, the four-dimensional
effective state may appear mixed:
\begin{equation}
  \rho_Y
  =
  \tr_X|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{some effective four-dimensional mixedness may be the shadow of base--fiber
  entanglement.}
  }
  \label{eq:appH-base-fiber-entanglement-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Internal coherence and Circle--Lens--Nil}
\label{appsubsec:internal-CLN}

The FP/fiber realization has a direct Circle--Lens--Nil reading.

Circle is internal spectral coherence:
\begin{equation}
  \Circle_{\rm FP}
  =
  \text{fiber phase, harmonicity, internal spectral closure}.
\end{equation}

Lens is projection to the coherent joint harmonic sector:
\begin{equation}
  \Lens_{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}

Nil is damping of nonzero internal modes:
\begin{equation}
  \Nil_{\rm FP}
  =
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  \text{Circle-coherent internal modes}
  \xrightarrow{\Lens}
  \text{joint harmonic sector}
  \xrightarrow{\Nil}
  \text{suppressed nonzero excitations}.
  }
  \label{eq:appH-CLN-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Relation to Kaluza--Klein theory}
\label{appsubsec:relation-KK-app}

The FP/fiber realization is explicitly Kaluza--Klein-compatible. The compact internal space
produces a tower of four-dimensional modes:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2
  \quad
  \longrightarrow
  \quad
  m_j^2=m_0^2+\mu_j^2.
\end{equation}

The MTT-specific addition is the finite coherent admissibility weight:
\begin{equation}
  W_j=\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2},
\end{equation}
together with the projection architecture:
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{ordinary KK gives internal towers; FP/MTT gives internal towers filtered by finite
  coherent admissibility.}
  }
  \label{eq:appH-KK-MTT-box}
\end{equation}

This is a strength, not a weakness. It gives the MTT finite scale a concrete geometric origin.

\subsection{Phenomenological scale translation}
\label{appsubsec:phenomenological-scale-translation}

If experiments constrain the first internal excitation by
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1\gtrsim M_{\rm exp},
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  R\lesssim M_{\rm exp}^{-1}.
\end{equation}

Using
\begin{equation}
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  },
\end{equation}
one obtains
\begin{equation}
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \lesssim
  M_{\rm exp}^{-1}
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  }.
  \label{eq:appH-scale-bound}
\end{equation}

Equivalently:
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}
  =
  \tau^{-1/2}
  \gtrsim
  M_{\rm exp}
  \left(
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  \right)^{-1/2}.
  \label{eq:appH-Lambda-bound}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{bounds on internal excitations translate into bounds on }R,\sqrt{\tau},\Lambda_{\rm eff}.
  }
  \label{eq:appH-bound-box}
\end{equation}

Precise numbers require explicit internal geometry, representation data, and couplings.

\subsection{Failure modes}
\label{appsubsec:FP-fiber-failure-modes}

The FP/fiber realization fails if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item \(A_{\rm int}\) is not positive;
  \item \(P_{\rm coh}\) does not project to the joint harmonic sector;
  \item \([P_{\rm coh},A_{\rm int}]\neq0\) but diagonal modal weights are assumed anyway;
  \item the zero mode is removed or damped;
  \item \(\chi(0)\neq1\) when low-energy recovery requires \(\chi(0)=1\);
  \item internal excitations violate experimental bounds;
  \item gauge quotienting is not preserved;
  \item gravitational constraints are not preserved;
  \item Källén--Lehmann positivity fails;
  \item the KK-style structure is hidden rather than stated honestly.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{FP/MTT is physically meaningful only when internal spectral damping, zero-mode
  recovery, quotient compatibility, and positivity all hold.}
  }
  \label{eq:appH-failure-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{appsubsec:FP-fiber-summary}

The fixed-point/fiber realization places MTT on:
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6,
\end{equation}
with Lorentzian base \(Y^4\) and compact Riemannian internal space \(X^6\).

The internal admissibility operator is:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3
  \kappa_n\Delta_{B_n},
  \qquad
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}

The coherent projector is:
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}\Pi_{B_2}\Pi_{B_3},
\end{equation}
with:
\begin{equation}
  \Ran P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3
  \ker\Delta_{B_n}.
\end{equation}

The fixed-point finite coherent admissibility operator is:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})
  P_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}

Internal eigenmodes satisfy:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j,
\end{equation}
with weights:
\begin{equation}
  W_j=\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Zero modes are undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  W_0=1.
\end{equation}

Nonzero modes are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  W_j<1.
\end{equation}

The damping scale is:
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the FP realization gives finite coherent admissibility a concrete compact-fiber,
  KK-compatible, positive-spectral foundation.}
  }
\end{equation}
% ============================================================
\section{Phenomenological scale relations and execution templates}
\label{app:phenomenological-scales}
% ============================================================

This appendix collects the scale relations used in the phenomenology section. The purpose is
to make explicit how the internal spectral gap, the damping time \(\tau\), the coherent length
\(\ell_{\rm coh}\), and the effective four-dimensional excitation scale are related.

The central chain is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \lambda_\ast
  \sim
  R^{-2}
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R\sqrt{\log(C_Q/\epsadm)}.
  }
  \label{eq:appI-main-chain}
\end{equation}

Equivalently:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}
  =
  \tau^{-1/2}
  \sim
  R^{-1}
  \left(
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  \right)^{-1/2}.
  }
  \label{eq:appI-Lambda-chain}
\end{equation}

These relations are structural. Precise numerical predictions require a completed sectoral
model.

\subsection{Dimensions}
\label{appsubsec:dimensions}

A Laplace-type internal operator has dimension
\begin{equation}
  [A_{\rm int}]
  =
  L^{-2}
  =
  E^2
\end{equation}
in units where \(\hbar=c=1\).

The damping factor
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
\end{equation}
is dimensionless, so
\begin{equation}
  [\tau]=L^2=E^{-2}.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \sqrt{\tau}
\end{equation}
has dimensions of length, and
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}
  =
  \tau^{-1/2}
\end{equation}
has dimensions of energy.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \tau
  \text{ is a length-squared or inverse-energy-squared scale.}
  }
  \label{eq:appI-tau-dimension-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Internal radius and spectral gap}
\label{appsubsec:radius-gap}

Let \(R\) denote a characteristic internal length scale. For a compact internal Laplace-type
operator, the first nonzero eigenvalue is typically of order
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2}.
  \label{eq:appI-gap-radius}
\end{equation}

More precisely, the coefficient depends on the internal geometry, boundary conditions,
bundle structure, curvature, and normalization of the operator. Thus one may write
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast
  =
  c_X R^{-2},
  \label{eq:appI-gap-cX}
\end{equation}
where \(c_X\) is a dimensionless geometry-dependent constant.

Then
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \frac{1}{c_X}R^2
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}.
  \label{eq:appI-tau-cX}
\end{equation}

Hence
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \sqrt{\tau}
  =
  \frac{R}{\sqrt{c_X}}
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  }.
  \label{eq:appI-lcoh-cX}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  \text{ is generically of order the internal radius, up to gap and tolerance factors.}
  }
  \label{eq:appI-lcoh-order-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Damping-selected time}
\label{appsubsec:damping-selected-time}

The damping-selected time is defined by the discarded-sector condition
\begin{equation}
  C_Q\ee^{-\lambda_\ast\tau}
  =
  \epsadm.
\end{equation}
Solving gives
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}.
  \label{eq:appI-tau-selected}
\end{equation}

This means that \(\tau\) is not an arbitrary ultraviolet cutoff. It is selected by:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the discarded-sector gap \(\lambda_\ast\);
  \item the discarded-sector constant \(C_Q\);
  \item the admissibility tolerance \(\epsadm\).
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \tau
  \text{ measures how long the positive internal damping must act to suppress the discarded
  sector below tolerance.}
  }
  \label{eq:appI-tau-meaning-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{First-mode admissibility weight}
\label{appsubsec:first-mode-weight}

Let the first nonzero internal eigenvalue be
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1^2.
\end{equation}
The corresponding amplitude-level admissibility weight is
\begin{equation}
  W_1
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_1^2}.
  \label{eq:appI-W1}
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1^2\simeq\lambda_\ast,
\end{equation}
then using \eqref{eq:appI-tau-selected} gives
\begin{equation}
  W_1
  \simeq
  \ee^{-\log(C_Q/\epsadm)}
  =
  \frac{\epsadm}{C_Q}.
  \label{eq:appI-W1-eps}
\end{equation}

More generally, define
\begin{equation}
  r_1
  =
  \frac{\mu_1^2}{\lambda_\ast}.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  W_1
  =
  \left(
    \frac{\epsadm}{C_Q}
  \right)^{r_1}.
  \label{eq:appI-W1-r}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  W_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
\end{equation}
is the universal amplitude-level internal-mode weight, while its numerical value depends on
the internal spectrum and tolerance choice.

\subsection{Illustrative tolerance benchmark}
\label{appsubsec:tolerance-benchmark}

As an illustrative benchmark, take
\begin{equation}
  C_Q=1,
  \qquad
  \epsadm=10^{-3}.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  =
  \log(10^3)
  \simeq
  6.9.
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  c_X=1,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  \simeq
  \sqrt{6.9}\,R
  \simeq
  2.6R.
  \label{eq:appI-illustrative-lcoh}
\end{equation}

If also
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1^2\simeq\lambda_\ast,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  W_1\simeq10^{-3}.
  \label{eq:appI-illustrative-W1}
\end{equation}

This benchmark is not a universal prediction. It is a scale example. A different \(C_Q\),
\(\epsadm\), geometry factor \(c_X\), or internal spectrum changes the coefficient.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \epsadm
  \text{ must ultimately be derived or fixed by sectoral admissibility, not chosen freely.}
  }
  \label{eq:appI-epsadm-warning-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Energy-length conversion}
\label{appsubsec:energy-length-conversion}

In natural units,
\begin{equation}
  \hbar c
  \simeq
  1.97\times10^{-16}\,{\rm GeV\,m}.
\end{equation}
Therefore an energy scale \(M\) corresponds to a length
\begin{equation}
  M^{-1}
  \simeq
  \frac{1.97\times10^{-16}\,{\rm m}}{M/{\rm GeV}}.
  \label{eq:appI-energy-length}
\end{equation}

For example, if
\begin{equation}
  M=1\,{\rm TeV}=10^3\,{\rm GeV},
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  M^{-1}
  \simeq
  2.0\times10^{-19}\,{\rm m}.
  \label{eq:appI-TeV-length}
\end{equation}

Thus an internal excitation scale
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1\sim M
\end{equation}
corresponds to
\begin{equation}
  R\sim M^{-1}.
\end{equation}

This conversion is useful for translating collider or scattering bounds into internal length
bounds.

\subsection{Bound translation}
\label{appsubsec:bound-translation}

Suppose an experiment constrains the first visible internal excitation to satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \mu_1\gtrsim M_{\rm exp}.
  \label{eq:appI-mu-bound}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
  R\lesssim M_{\rm exp}^{-1}.
  \label{eq:appI-R-bound}
\end{equation}

Using
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \frac{R}{\sqrt{c_X}}
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  },
\end{equation}
one obtains
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  \lesssim
  \frac{1}{M_{\rm exp}\sqrt{c_X}}
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  }.
  \label{eq:appI-lcoh-bound}
\end{equation}

Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}
  =
  \ell_{\rm coh}^{-1}
  \gtrsim
  M_{\rm exp}\sqrt{c_X}
  \left(
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  \right)^{-1/2}.
  \label{eq:appI-Lambda-bound}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \mu_1\gtrsim M_{\rm exp}
  \quad
  \Rightarrow
  \quad
  R\lesssim M_{\rm exp}^{-1},
  \quad
  \ell_{\rm coh}\lesssim M_{\rm exp}^{-1}\sqrt{\log(C_Q/\epsadm)/c_X}.
  }
  \label{eq:appI-bound-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Effective tower response}
\label{appsubsec:effective-tower-response}

After dimensional reduction, the generic four-dimensional response has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  G_{\rm eff}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{
    Z_j
    \chi(\mu_j^2)^2
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
  \label{eq:appI-effective-tower}
\end{equation}

The zero-mode term has
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
  \chi(0)=1,
\end{equation}
then the zero-mode weight is
\begin{equation}
  \chi(0)^2\ee^{-\tau\mu_0^2}=1.
\end{equation}

The nonzero-mode terms have
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}<1.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  G_{\rm eff}
  =
  G_{\rm zero}
  +
  G_{\rm damped\ tower}.
  }
  \label{eq:appI-zero-plus-tower}
\end{equation}

This is the basic phenomenological form of the FP/MTT reduction.

\subsection{Low-energy expansion}
\label{appsubsec:low-energy-expansion}

At energies much smaller than the first internal excitation,
\begin{equation}
  E\ll\mu_1,
\end{equation}
the nonzero internal modes can be integrated out. Their effects appear as higher-dimensional
operators suppressed by powers of
\begin{equation}
  \frac{E}{\mu_j},
\end{equation}
and by admissibility weights
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

A generic correction has the form
\begin{equation}
  \delta\mathcal O
  \sim
  \sum_{j\ge1}
  C_j
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  \left(
    \frac{E}{\mu_j}
  \right)^{d_j}.
  \label{eq:appI-low-energy-correction}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{low-energy corrections are suppressed both by heavy-mode decoupling and by finite
  admissibility.}
  }
  \label{eq:appI-low-energy-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Collider amplitude template}
\label{appsubsec:collider-template-app}

If an internal excitation \(j\) can be produced in a collider process, its schematic amplitude
contribution is
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal A_j(s)
  \sim
  g_j^{\rm in}g_j^{\rm out}
  \frac{
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {s-m_j^2+\ii m_j\Gamma_j}.
  \label{eq:appI-collider-amplitude}
\end{equation}

Here:
\begin{align}
  g_j^{\rm in}
  &: \text{effective production coupling},\\
  g_j^{\rm out}
  &: \text{effective decay coupling},\\
  \Gamma_j
  &: \text{width},\\
  m_j^2
  &: \text{effective four-dimensional mass}.
\end{align}

The observable cross section depends on
\begin{equation}
  |\mathcal A_{\rm SM}+\sum_j\mathcal A_j|^2.
\end{equation}

Therefore the amplitude weight
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
\end{equation}
does not by itself determine a universal cross-section suppression. Interference, width,
phase space, spin, gauge representation, internal overlap, and detector acceptance all matter.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  W_j=\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  \text{ is an amplitude-level admissibility weight, not a complete collider prediction.}
  }
  \label{eq:appI-cross-section-caution}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Yukawa correction template}
\label{appsubsec:Yukawa-template-app}

A massive internal mode exchanged between four-dimensional sources produces a Yukawa-type
correction. A generic static potential has the schematic form
\begin{equation}
  V(r)
  =
  V_0(r)
  \left[
    1+
    \sum_{j\ge1}
    a_j
    \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
    \ee^{-\mu_j r}
  \right],
  \label{eq:appI-Yukawa-template}
\end{equation}
where \(a_j\) depends on couplings, overlaps, and representations.

For electromagnetism:
\begin{equation}
  V_0(r)=\frac{q_1q_2}{4\pi r}.
\end{equation}

For Newtonian gravity:
\begin{equation}
  V_0(r)=-\frac{Gm_1m_2}{r}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{FP-realized short-distance corrections are typically Yukawa tower corrections dressed
  by admissibility weights.}
  }
  \label{eq:appI-Yukawa-box}
\end{equation}

Direct four-dimensional Gaussian smearing is a useful benchmark only when a four-dimensional
finite source profile has been derived.

\subsection{Finite source benchmark}
\label{appsubsec:finite-source-benchmark-appI}

A finite four-dimensional source benchmark replaces
\begin{equation}
  \delta^{(3)}(\mathbf r)
\end{equation}
by
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(3)}(\mathbf r)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-3/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{r^2}{4\tau}
  \right).
\end{equation}

For electromagnetism, this gives the finite Coulomb benchmark:
\begin{equation}
  \Phi_\tau(r)
  =
  \frac{q}{4\pi r}
  \operatorname{erf}
  \left(
    \frac{r}{2\sqrt{\tau}}
  \right).
  \label{eq:appI-finite-Coulomb}
\end{equation}

But this benchmark should not be automatically identified with the FP/fiber prediction. In
the FP realization, the finite width is primarily internal unless a physical four-dimensional
source kernel is derived.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite Coulomb smearing is a source-profile benchmark; internal-mode exchange is the
  default FP correction mechanism.}
  }
  \label{eq:appI-finite-source-caution}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Measurement visibility template}
\label{appsubsec:measurement-visibility-template}

For a two-branch interference experiment, branch damping gives
\begin{equation}
  V_{\rm obs}
  =
  |D_{12}|V_0.
  \label{eq:appI-visibility-template}
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  D_{12}
  =
  \ee^{-\tau_{\Meas}\Lambda_{12}^{(\Meas)}},
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \log\frac{V_0}{V_{\rm obs}}
  =
  \tau_{\Meas}\Lambda_{12}^{(\Meas)}.
  \label{eq:appI-visibility-log}
\end{equation}

This gives a direct way to fit measurement-context damping:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{visibility loss measures off-diagonal branch damping.}
  }
\end{equation}

It does not by itself determine outcome probabilities. Those require basin measures:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(i)
  =
  \frac{
    \mu(B_i^{(\Meas)})
  }{
    \sum_j\mu(B_j^{(\Meas)})
  }.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Entanglement and no-signaling template}
\label{appsubsec:entanglement-template-appI}

A joint measurement model supplies probabilities
\begin{equation}
  p(a,b|x,y),
\end{equation}
where \(x,y\) are local settings and \(a,b\) are outcomes.

Admissibility requires no-signaling:
\begin{equation}
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y')
  \label{eq:appI-no-signaling-A}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \sum_a p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \sum_a p(a,b|x',y).
  \label{eq:appI-no-signaling-B}
\end{equation}

In MTT terms, this constrains:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the joint coherent sector \(P_{AB}\);
  \item the joint admissibility operator \(A_{AB}\);
  \item the measurement effects \(E_{ab}^{(x,y)}\);
  \item the branch damping channel;
  \item the survivor-basin measure.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{joint coherent admissibility may be non-factorizing, but local record statistics must
  obey no-signaling.}
  }
  \label{eq:appI-no-signaling-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Execution table}
\label{appsubsec:execution-table-appI}

The following table summarizes the main quantities.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|c|c}
Quantity & Symbol & Meaning\\
\hline
Internal radius & \(R\) & characteristic compact-fiber size\\
Geometry factor & \(c_X\) & \(\lambda_\ast=c_XR^{-2}\)\\
Spectral gap & \(\lambda_\ast\) & first relevant nonzero internal gap\\
Tolerance & \(\epsadm\) & admissibility threshold\\
Discarded-sector constant & \(C_Q\) & gap estimate prefactor\\
Damping time & \(\tau\) & \(\lambda_\ast^{-1}\log(C_Q/\epsadm)\)\\
Coherent length & \(\ell_{\rm coh}\) & \(\sqrt{\tau}\)\\
Effective energy & \(\Lambda_{\rm eff}\) & \(\tau^{-1/2}\)\\
Internal eigenvalue & \(\mu_j^2\) & \(A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j\)\\
Mode weight & \(W_j\) & \(\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\)\\
Tower residue & \(Z_j\) & physical overlap/normalization\\
Visibility factor & \(D_{ab}\) & surviving branch coherence\\
Basin measure & \(\mu(B_i)\) & outcome-frequency weight
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The core relations are:
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast=c_XR^{-2},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}=\sqrt{\tau},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}=\tau^{-1/2},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  W_j=\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{What must be computed for numerical predictions}
\label{appsubsec:numerical-prediction-requirements}

To turn the structural templates into numerical predictions, one must compute:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the internal geometry \(X^6\);
  \item the internal spectrum \(\mu_j^2\);
  \item the coherent projector \(P_{\rm coh}\);
  \item the admissibility window \(\chi\);
  \item the gap \(\lambda_\ast\);
  \item the tolerance \(\epsadm\);
  \item wavefunction overlaps \(Z_j\);
  \item effective gauge and matter couplings;
  \item resonance widths \(\Gamma_j\);
  \item gauge and gravitational quotient projectors;
  \item anomaly and constraint consistency;
  \item detector effects and basin measures.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT becomes numerically predictive only after the sectoral data are explicitly
  computed.}
  }
  \label{eq:appI-predictive-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{appsubsec:phenomenological-scale-summary}

The FP/MTT scale relations are:
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \sqrt{\tau}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  },
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}
  =
  \tau^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}

Internal modes carry weights:
\begin{equation}
  W_j=\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
\end{equation}

Zero modes are undamped:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  W_0=1.
\end{equation}

Nonzero modes are suppressed:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad
  W_j<1.
\end{equation}

The generic four-dimensional effective response is:
\begin{equation}
  G_{\rm eff}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{
    Z_j\chi(\mu_j^2)^2\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}
  }
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
\end{equation}

Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the phenomenology of MTT is controlled by zero-mode recovery, admissibility-weighted
  internal excitations, finite measurement damping, and sectoral quotient constraints.}
  }
\end{equation}
% ============================================================
\section{Notation, glossary, and execution checklist}
\label{app:notation-checklist}
% ============================================================

This appendix collects the central notation used throughout the paper and gives a compact
execution checklist for applying the MTT finite coherent admissibility architecture to a
specific physical sector.

The central object is:
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
  \label{eq:appJ-Badm}
\end{equation}

The main interpretive rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent admissibility}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{projection shadows}
  \longrightarrow
  \text{effective physics}.
  }
  \label{eq:appJ-main-rule}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Core operator notation}
\label{appsubsec:core-operator-notation}

The basic analytic data are:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|l}
Symbol & Meaning\\
\hline
\(\mathcal H\) & sectoral Hilbert space or physical state space\\
\(A\) & nonnegative admissibility/stabilization operator\\
\(P\) & coherent, physical, or constraint-compatible projector\\
\(\chi(A)\) & bounded admissible spectral window\\
\(\tau\) & finite damping time or coherence scale parameter\\
\(\Badm\) & finite coherent admissibility operator\\
\(\Kadm(x,y)\) & integral kernel of \(\Badm\), when it exists
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The clean spectral assumptions are:
\begin{equation}
  A\ge0,
  \qquad
  P=P^\ast=P^2,
  \qquad
  [P,A]=0,
  \qquad
  0\le\chi(A)\le I.
\end{equation}

Under these assumptions:
\begin{equation}
  0\le\Badm\le I.
\end{equation}

If
\begin{equation}
  A\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n,
  \qquad
  P\phi_n=p_n\phi_n,
  \qquad
  p_n\in\{0,1\},
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
\end{equation}

The modal weight is:
\begin{equation}
  w_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}.
  \label{eq:appJ-modal-weight}
\end{equation}

The full operator \(\Badm\) is generally not a projector:
\begin{equation}
  \Badm^2\neq\Badm.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  P\text{ is the projector; }\Badm\text{ is the finite coherent admissibility filter.}
  }
  \label{eq:appJ-projector-distinction}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Circle--Lens--Nil notation}
\label{appsubsec:CLN-notation}

The Modal Triplet notation is:
\begin{align}
  \Circle &: \text{phase, coherence, return, holonomy, spectral closure},\\
  \Lens &: \text{representation, quotient, projection, contextual reading},\\
  \Nil &: \text{damping, boundary, record, survivor selection}.
\end{align}

The main structural reading is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{physical phenomena appear as }\Circle/\Lens/\Nil\text{ shadows of finite coherent
  admissibility.}
  }
\end{equation}

Examples:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|c|c|c}
Sector & \(\Circle\) & \(\Lens\) & \(\Nil\)\\
\hline
Wave/particle & phase coherence & local/spectral reading & delta limit/record\\
Measurement & branch phase & detector basis/context & survivor basin\\
Gauge & holonomy/flux & gauge quotient & charge/photon record\\
Gravity & curvature holonomy & diffeomorphism quotient & boundary/horizon geometry\\
Quantization & phase closure & representation consistency & stable label\\
Entanglement & joint coherence & subsystem split & correlated record
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{Geometry notation}
\label{appsubsec:geometry-notation}

The fixed-point/fiber realization uses:
\begin{equation}
  M_{10}=Y^4\times X^6.
\end{equation}

Here:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|l}
Symbol & Meaning\\
\hline
\(Y^4\) & four-dimensional Lorentzian base\\
\(X^6\) & compact Riemannian internal space or fiber\\
\(\Sigma_t\) & Cauchy slice in \(Y^4\)\\
\(z\in X^6\) & internal coordinate\\
\(x\in Y^4\) & base coordinate\\
\(R\) & characteristic internal radius\\
\(\lambda_\ast\) & relevant internal spectral gap\\
\(\mu_j^2\) & internal eigenvalue of \(A_{\rm int}\)
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The internal admissibility operator is:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}
  =
  \sum_{n=1}^3
  \kappa_n\Delta_{B_n},
  \qquad
  A_{\rm int}\ge0.
\end{equation}

The coherent projector is:
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \Pi_{B_1}\Pi_{B_2}\Pi_{B_3},
\end{equation}
with:
\begin{equation}
  \Ran P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3\ker\Delta_{B_n}.
\end{equation}

The FP finite coherent admissibility operator is:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})
  P_{\rm coh}.
  \label{eq:appJ-FP-Badm}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Scale notation}
\label{appsubsec:scale-notation}

The main scale relations are:
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm},
  \label{eq:appJ-tau}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_\ast\sim R^{-2},
  \label{eq:appJ-gap-radius}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  =
  \sqrt{\tau},
  \label{eq:appJ-lcoh}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_{\rm eff}
  =
  \tau^{-1/2}.
  \label{eq:appJ-Lambda}
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \ell_{\rm coh}
  \sim
  R
  \sqrt{
    \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}
  }.
\end{equation}

The internal-mode admissibility weight is:
\begin{equation}
  W_j
  =
  \ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}.
  \label{eq:appJ-Wj}
\end{equation}

Zero modes satisfy:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_0=0,
  \qquad
  W_0=1.
\end{equation}

Nonzero internal modes satisfy:
\begin{equation}
  \mu_j^2>0,
  \qquad
  W_j<1.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Kernel-shadow notation}
\label{appsubsec:kernel-shadow-notation}

When \(\Badm\) has a kernel,
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)=\langle x|\Badm|y\rangle.
\end{equation}

The two primary kernel shadows are:
\begin{align}
  \text{local reading}
  &: \quad
  x\mapsto \Kadm(x,x_0),
  \\
  \text{spectral reading}
  &: \quad
  \Kadm(x,y)
  =
  \sum_n
  w_n\phi_n(x)\phi_n^\ast(y).
\end{align}

The sharp limit is:
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)\to\delta(x-y).
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \delta(x-y)
  =
  \text{sharp local shadow of finite coherent admissibility}.
  }
\end{equation}

In the scalar Euclidean benchmark:
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right),
\end{equation}
with:
\begin{equation}
  \widehat K_\tau^{(d)}(k)=\ee^{-\tau k^2}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Measurement notation}
\label{appsubsec:measurement-notation}

A measurement context is:
\begin{equation}
  \Meas
  =
  (A_{\Meas},P_{\Meas},\chi_{\Meas},\tau_{\Meas},
  \{E_i^{(\Meas)}\},\frakB_{\Meas}).
  \label{eq:appJ-measurement-context}
\end{equation}

Here:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|l}
Symbol & Meaning\\
\hline
\(E_i^{(\Meas)}\) & finite detector effect\\
\(\frakB_{\Meas}\) & measurement survivor-basin partition\\
\(B_i^{(\Meas)}\) & survivor basin for record \(i\)\\
\(D_{ab}^{(\Meas)}\) & branch damping matrix\\
\(\mu(B_i^{(\Meas)})\) & basin measure of outcome \(i\)
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Branch damping is:
\begin{equation}
  \rho\mapsto D\circ\rho,
\end{equation}
with:
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}=1.
\end{equation}

Outcome probabilities are:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(i)
  =
  \frac{
    \mu(B_i^{(\Meas)})
  }{
    \sum_j\mu(B_j^{(\Meas)})
  }.
\end{equation}

The key distinction is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  D_{ab}
  \text{ controls visibility; }
  \mu(B_i)
  \text{ controls outcome frequencies.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gauge notation}
\label{appsubsec:gauge-notation}

Gauge equivalence is:
\begin{equation}
  A_\mu\sim A_\mu+\partial_\mu\alpha.
\end{equation}

The gauge-sector finite coherent admissibility operator is:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  P_{\rm phys}.
  \label{eq:appJ-gauge-Badm}
\end{equation}

Here:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|l}
Symbol & Meaning\\
\hline
\(P_{\rm phys}\) & projection to physical gauge content\\
\(A_{\rm gauge}\) & positive or gauge-compatible operator\\
\(Q_{\rm BRST}\) & BRST operator\\
\(P_T\) & transverse projector in simple Abelian settings\\
\(F_{\mu\nu}\) & gauge-invariant field strength\\
\(\oint_\gamma A\) & gauge holonomy around loop \(\gamma\)
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The safe rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{filter after quotienting, or filter covariantly before quotienting.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Gravity notation}
\label{appsubsec:gravity-notation}

Diffeomorphism equivalence is:
\begin{equation}
  g_{\mu\nu}\sim\varphi^\ast g_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}

The gravitational finite coherent admissibility operator is:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})
  P_{\rm diff}.
  \label{eq:appJ-gravity-Badm}
\end{equation}

In ADM notation, the Cauchy data are:
\begin{equation}
  (h_{ij},\pi^{ij}),
\end{equation}
subject to:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H=0,
  \qquad
  \mathcal H_i=0.
\end{equation}

Here:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|l}
Symbol & Meaning\\
\hline
\(P_{\rm diff}\) & diffeomorphism-compatible projection/reduction\\
\(A_{\rm grav}\) & positive or constraint-compatible geometric operator\\
\(P_{\rm TT}\) & transverse-traceless projector in weak-field gravity\\
\(\mathcal H\) & Hamiltonian constraint\\
\(\mathcal H_i\) & momentum constraints\\
\(R^\rho{}_{\sigma\mu\nu}\) & curvature tensor / infinitesimal holonomy
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The safe rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{filter geometric content, not coordinate artifacts.}
  }
\end{equation}

\subsection{Entanglement notation}
\label{appsubsec:entanglement-notation}

For a joint sector:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{AB}
  =
  \mathcal H_A\otimes\mathcal H_B
\end{equation}
when the tensor product factorization is valid.

The joint finite coherent admissibility operator is:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{AB}
  =
  P_{AB}
  \chi(A_{AB})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{AB}}
  \chi(A_{AB})
  P_{AB}.
  \label{eq:appJ-joint-Badm}
\end{equation}

Entanglement is signaled structurally by:
\begin{equation}
  P_{AB}\neq P_A\otimes P_B,
\end{equation}
or:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{AB}
  \neq
  B_{\rm adm}^{A}\otimes B_{\rm adm}^{B}.
\end{equation}

No-signaling requires:
\begin{equation}
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y')
\end{equation}
and:
\begin{equation}
  \sum_a p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \sum_a p(a,b|x',y).
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{joint coherence may be non-factorizing, but local record statistics must obey
  no-signaling.}
  }
\end{equation}

  +

  }

\subsection{Sectoral execution checklist}
\label{appsubsec:sectoral-execution-checklist}

To apply MTT to a concrete sector, one must provide the following data.

\paragraph{Step 1: Choose the state space.}
Specify:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H_{\rm sector}.
\end{equation}
For gauge and gravity sectors, specify the physical quotient or constraint-compatible state
space.

\paragraph{Step 2: Choose the admissibility operator.}
Specify:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm sector}.
\end{equation}
Check:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm sector}\ge0
\end{equation}
or positivity after projection/reduction.

\paragraph{Step 3: Choose the projector.}
Specify:
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm sector}.
\end{equation}
Check:
\begin{equation}
  P_{\rm sector}=P_{\rm sector}^\ast=P_{\rm sector}^2.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Step 4: Check commutation.}
Determine whether:
\begin{equation}
  [P_{\rm sector},A_{\rm sector}]=0.
\end{equation}
If yes, modal weights may be used. If no, operator-level estimates are required.

\paragraph{Step 5: Choose the window.}
Specify:
\begin{equation}
  \chi(A_{\rm sector}).
\end{equation}
Check:
\begin{equation}
  0\le\chi(A_{\rm sector})\le I
\end{equation}
in the clean bounded case, and verify that required zero modes are retained.

\paragraph{Step 6: Choose or derive \(\tau\).}
Specify:
\begin{equation}
  \tau.
\end{equation}
In the FP realization:
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Step 7: Construct the finite filter.}
Build:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm sector}
  =
  P_{\rm sector}
  \chi(A_{\rm sector})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm sector}}
  \chi(A_{\rm sector})
  P_{\rm sector}.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Step 8: Compute shadows.}
Compute the relevant physical shadows:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item local kernel;
  \item spectral weights;
  \item effective propagator;
  \item finite source profile;
  \item detector effects;
  \item branch damping;
  \item gauge quotient response;
  \item geometric projection;
  \item measurement basins;
  \item entanglement correlations.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Step 9: Check physical constraints.}
Verify:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item positivity;
  \item unitarity or channel complete positivity;
  \item Lorentzian causal admissibility;
  \item gauge identities;
  \item gravitational constraints;
  \item no-signaling;
  \item low-energy recovery;
  \item experimental bounds.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Step 10: Extract predictions.}
Only after Steps 1--9 are complete should one compute numerical predictions such as:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item masses;
  \item couplings;
  \item cross sections;
  \item precision corrections;
  \item visibility curves;
  \item basin probabilities;
  \item gravitational deviations;
  \item internal excitation bounds.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT prediction requires sectoral execution, not merely the formal presence of }
  \Badm.
  }
  \label{eq:appJ-execution-box}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Minimal admissibility checklist}
\label{appsubsec:minimal-admissibility-checklist}

A proposed sectoral realization must satisfy:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item \(A\ge0\) in the physical sector.
  \item \(P\) is a valid projector.
  \item \(\chi(A)\) is bounded and does not remove required zero modes.
  \item \(\tau\) has a sectoral origin.
  \item \(\Badm\) is not mislabeled as a projector.
  \item If \([P,A]\neq0\), diagonal modal weights are not assumed without proof.
  \item Lorentzian damping is not implemented as naive \(\ee^{-\tau\Box}\).
  \item Gauge quotienting is preserved in gauge sectors.
  \item Diffeomorphism constraints are preserved in gravity.
  \item Branch damping is CPTP in measurement sectors.
  \item No-signaling is preserved in joint sectors.
  \item Low-energy zero-mode recovery is maintained.
\end{enumerate}

Failure of any required item invalidates that sectoral implementation.

\subsection{Claim-status checklist}
\label{appsubsec:claim-status-checklist}

The paper makes claims at three levels.

\paragraph{Analytic theorem.}
These claims are mathematically proven under explicit assumptions:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item positivity and boundedness of \(\Badm\);
  \item scalar heat-kernel delta limit;
  \item Fourier damping in the scalar benchmark;
  \item Schur damping channel admissibility.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Structural corollary.}
These claims organize known physical structures through MTT:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item wave and particle as kernel shadows;
  \item measurement as finite effects plus survivor basins;
  \item gauge as Lens quotient structure;
  \item gravity as diffeomorphism-compatible projection;
  \item quantization as survivor-label structure;
  \item entanglement as non-factorizing coherent admissibility.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Execution task.}
These are not yet derived by the structural theorem alone:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item Standard Model representations;
  \item fermion generations;
  \item masses and couplings;
  \item full quantum gravity;
  \item exact collider signatures;
  \item complete Born-rule basin measure;
  \item numerical phenomenology.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{analytic theorem}
  \neq
  \text{structural unification}
  \neq
  \text{completed sectoral prediction}.
  }
  \label{eq:appJ-claim-status-box}
\end{equation}

  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}

% ============================================================
\section{Claim audit and reviewer-response map}
\label{app:claim-audit}
% ============================================================

This appendix states, in compact form, what the paper claims, what it does not claim, and how
the main possible objections are addressed. Its purpose is to prevent overreading of the
structural unification result.

The central distinction is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{analytic theorem}
  \neq
  \text{structural unification}
  \neq
  \text{completed sectoral prediction}.
  }
  \label{eq:appK-three-levels}
\end{equation}

The paper proves finite-filter, kernel, heat-kernel, and branch-channel statements under
explicit assumptions. It then uses these statements to organize quantum, gauge, gravitational,
measurement, and entanglement structures as projection shadows. It does not claim that every
Standard Model parameter, gravitational observable, or detector probability has already been
computed.

\subsection{Primary claim}
\label{appsubsec:primary-claim}

The primary claim of the paper is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite coherent admissibility is a common projection architecture behind particle,
  wave, measurement, gauge, gravity, quantization, and entanglement shadows.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-primary-claim}
\end{equation}

The operator expressing this architecture is:
\begin{equation}
  \Badm
  =
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}

In the fixed-point/fiber realization:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})
  P_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}

The coherent sector is:
\begin{equation}
  \Ran P_{\rm coh}
  =
  \bigcap_{n=1}^3\ker\Delta_{B_n}.
\end{equation}

Thus the coherent sector has a concrete meaning: it is the joint fiber-harmonic sector.

\subsection{What is mathematically proven}
\label{appsubsec:mathematically-proven}

The following claims are analytic claims proven under stated hypotheses.

\paragraph{Finite-filter theorem.}
If \(A\ge0\), \(P=P^\ast=P^2\), \([P,A]=0\), and \(0\le\chi(A)\le I\), then
\begin{equation}
  0\le
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P
  \le I.
\end{equation}

In a common eigenbasis:
\begin{equation}
  \Badm\phi_n
  =
  p_n\chi(\lambda_n)^2\ee^{-\tau\lambda_n}\phi_n.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Delta-limit theorem.}
For an approximate identity \(K_\tau(x,y)\),
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau(x,y)\to\delta(x-y)
\end{equation}
distributionally as \(\tau\downarrow0\).

\paragraph{Scalar benchmark theorem.}
For \(A=-\Delta\), \(P=I\), and \(\chi=1\) on \(\mathbb R^d\),
\begin{equation}
  K_\tau^{(d)}(x-y)
  =
  (4\pi\tau)^{-d/2}
  \exp\left(
    -\frac{|x-y|^2}{4\tau}
  \right),
\end{equation}
with Fourier transform
\begin{equation}
  \widehat K_\tau^{(d)}(k)=\ee^{-\tau k^2}.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Schur-channel theorem.}
If
\begin{equation}
  D\succeq0,
  \qquad
  D_{aa}=1,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
  \rho\mapsto D\circ\rho
\end{equation}
is a completely positive trace-preserving channel.

\paragraph{Lorentzian admissibility schema.}
A bounded equal-time Cauchy-slice kernel that enters as a lower-order term does not change
the hyperbolic principal symbol. Strict support-domain preservation, however, requires
additional support control or a preparation/readout interpretation.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the paper's hard analytic claims are conditional and explicitly scoped.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-analytic-scoped}
\end{equation}

\subsection{What is structurally argued}
\label{appsubsec:structurally-argued}

The following are structural interpretations supported by the analytic architecture.

\paragraph{Wave--particle duality.}
The same kernel has a local and spectral reading:
\begin{equation}
  \Kadm(x,y)
  \quad
  \begin{cases}
    \text{local reading}
    &\Rightarrow
    \text{particle/delta shadow},\\
    \text{spectral reading}
    &\Rightarrow
    \text{wave/interference shadow}.
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Measurement.}
Finite detector effects, branch damping, and survivor basins together produce measurement
records:
\begin{equation}
  \rho
  \to
  D\circ\rho
  \to
  B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Gauge theory.}
Gauge theory is Lens quotient structure. Therefore:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys}.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Gravity.}
Gravity is diffeomorphism-compatible geometric projection:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Quantization.}
Quantization is survivor-label discreteness generated by Circle closure, Lens consistency,
and Nil selection:
\begin{equation}
  \text{quantization}
  =
  \Circle\text{-closure}
  +
  \Lens\text{-quotient consistency}
  +
  \Nil\text{-survivor selection}.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Entanglement.}
Entanglement is non-factorizing joint coherent admissibility:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{AB}
  \neq
  B_{\rm adm}^{A}\otimes B_{\rm adm}^{B}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the structural claim is unification by projection architecture, not derivation of all
  numerical physics.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-structural-scoped}
\end{equation}

\subsection{What is not yet claimed}
\label{appsubsec:not-yet-claimed}

The paper does not claim to have already derived:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the full Standard Model gauge group from first principles;
  \item all Standard Model representations;
  \item three fermion generations;
  \item all masses and Yukawa couplings;
  \item CKM or PMNS mixing data;
  \item anomaly cancellation from the MTT operator alone;
  \item a full nonperturbative quantum-gravity Hilbert space;
  \item exact black-hole microphysics;
  \item all Born-rule basin measures;
  \item exact collider cross sections;
  \item exact numerical deviations in precision tests.
\end{enumerate}

These are execution tasks.

The correct claim is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT specifies the admissibility architecture within which such sectoral derivations
  must be carried out.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-execution-not-complete}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Response to the ``just a regulator'' objection}
\label{appsubsec:just-regulator-objection}

A possible objection is that
\begin{equation}
  \ee^{-\tau A}
\end{equation}
looks like an ordinary regulator.

The response is that MTT does not use the exponential merely as a computational cutoff.
The finite damping appears inside a larger architecture:
\begin{equation}
  P\chi(A)\ee^{-\tau A}\chi(A)P.
\end{equation}
The projector \(P\), the spectral window \(\chi\), the gap-selected \(\tau\), and the sectoral
quotient constraints are all part of the physical content.

In the FP realization, \(\tau\) is tied to the internal spectral gap:
\begin{equation}
  \tau
  =
  \lambda_\ast^{-1}
  \log\frac{C_Q}{\epsadm}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT is not merely ``put in a regulator''; it is a finite admissibility architecture with
  projector, window, gap scale, and physical quotient constraints.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-regulator-response}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Response to the ``just Kaluza--Klein'' objection}
\label{appsubsec:just-KK-objection}

A possible objection is that the FP realization is just Kaluza--Klein theory.

The answer is that the FP realization is intentionally KK-compatible. The compact internal
geometry supplies the positive spectrum:
\begin{equation}
  A_{\rm int}\phi_j=\mu_j^2\phi_j.
\end{equation}
Dimensional reduction gives a tower:
\begin{equation}
  G_{4D}(p)
  =
  \sum_{j\ge0}
  \frac{Z_j\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}}
  {p^2-m_j^2+\ii\epsilon}.
\end{equation}

The MTT addition is that the tower is embedded in a finite coherent projection architecture:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{ordinary KK gives compactification towers; FP/MTT gives compactification towers
  filtered through finite coherent admissibility and interpreted as projection shadows.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-KK-response}
\end{equation}

The KK backbone is not hidden. It is the geometric support of the framework.

\subsection{Response to the Lorentzian nonlocality objection}
\label{appsubsec:Lorentzian-objection}

A possible objection is that exponential finite operators in Lorentzian field theory are often
acausal or unstable.

MTT addresses this by rejecting the naive rule:
\begin{equation}
  A=\Box_g.
\end{equation}

The paper's Lorentzian rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  A\neq\Box_g
  \quad
  \text{as a naive fundamental damping operator.}
  }
\end{equation}

Instead, admissible \(A\)'s are:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item positive internal/fiber operators;
  \item positive spatial Cauchy-slice operators;
  \item Hamiltonian or constraint-compatible operators;
  \item gauge-quotiented physical operators;
  \item diffeomorphism-compatible geometric operators.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT is not a naive Lorentzian nonlocal theory based on }\ee^{-\tau\Box_g}.
  }
  \label{eq:appK-Lorentzian-response}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Response to the causality objection}
\label{appsubsec:causality-objection}

A possible objection is that finite kernels imply action at a distance.

The response is that finite support, finite detector resolution, or finite source width is not
the same as controllable acausal signaling. A finite detector samples a region. A finite source
has extent. A finite internal mode may project to a finite effective profile.

The strict causal requirements are:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item the Lorentzian principal symbol is not modified in an inadmissible way;
  \item no controllable signal can be transmitted outside the causal domain;
  \item globally supported dynamical kernels require additional support or no-signaling proof;
  \item preparation/readout kernels are distinguished from dynamical propagation kernels.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{finite overlap is not acausal signaling; controllable domain-of-dependence violation
  would be inadmissible.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-causality-response}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Response to the gauge objection}
\label{appsubsec:gauge-objection}

A possible objection is that finite filters can break gauge invariance.

MTT agrees and makes this a failure mode. The gauge-sector filter must be:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm gauge}
  =
  P_{\rm phys}\chi(A_{\rm gauge})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm gauge}}
  \chi(A_{\rm gauge})P_{\rm phys}.
\end{equation}

The safe rule is:
\begin{equation}
  \text{filter after quotienting, or filter covariantly before quotienting}.
\end{equation}

The filter must preserve:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item gauge quotienting;
  \item Ward identities;
  \item Slavnov--Taylor identities;
  \item BRST cohomology where relevant;
  \item current conservation;
  \item anomaly cancellation;
  \item global bundle and flux consistency.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{a gauge-breaking finite filter is not an admissible MTT gauge object.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-gauge-response}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Response to the gravity objection}
\label{appsubsec:gravity-objection}

A possible objection is that finite filtering of the metric is coordinate-dependent and violates
diffeomorphism invariance.

MTT agrees that coordinate-component smoothing is invalid. The gravitational filter must be:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm grav}
  =
  P_{\rm diff}\chi(A_{\rm grav})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm grav}}
  \chi(A_{\rm grav})P_{\rm diff}.
\end{equation}

It must act on diffeomorphism-compatible geometric content, not arbitrary metric components.

In ADM language, it must preserve:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal H=0,
  \qquad
  \mathcal H_i=0.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{coordinate smoothing of }g_{\mu\nu}\text{ is not MTT gravity; constraint-compatible
  geometric projection is.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-gravity-response}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Response to the measurement objection}
\label{appsubsec:measurement-objection}

A possible objection is that decoherence does not solve the measurement problem.

MTT agrees. The paper distinguishes:
\begin{equation}
  \text{branch damping}
\end{equation}
from
\begin{equation}
  \text{survivor-basin record selection}.
\end{equation}

Branch damping is:
\begin{equation}
  \rho\mapsto D\circ\rho.
\end{equation}

Record formation is:
\begin{equation}
  \Psi\to B_i^{(\Meas)}.
\end{equation}

Outcome frequencies require a basin measure:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(i)
  =
  \frac{\mu(B_i^{(\Meas)})}
  {\sum_j\mu(B_j^{(\Meas)})}.
\end{equation}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT does not identify decoherence alone with outcome selection.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-measurement-response}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Response to the Born-rule objection}
\label{appsubsec:Born-objection}

A possible objection is that the paper has not derived the Born rule.

The correct response is that the paper identifies where the Born rule must enter in the MTT
architecture: it must be a statement about basin measures:
\begin{equation}
  \mu(B_i^{(\Meas)}).
\end{equation}

The Born-form recovery condition is:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbb P(i)
  =
  \tr(\rho E_i).
\end{equation}

Thus the open task is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{derive conditions under which survivor-basin measures reproduce the quantum trace
  rule.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-Born-response}
\end{equation}

The paper does not overclaim that this has been completed in all detector contexts.

\subsection{Response to the entanglement objection}
\label{appsubsec:entanglement-objection}

A possible objection is that MTT might be a hidden-variable theory and conflict with Bell
experiments.

MTT is not a local hidden-variable theory. It does not assume pre-existing local survivor
labels for all possible measurement settings.

The MTT statement is:
\begin{equation}
  \text{joint coherent admissibility}
  \neq
  \text{pre-existing distribution over local records}.
\end{equation}

Entanglement is:
\begin{equation}
  B_{\rm adm}^{AB}
  \neq
  B_{\rm adm}^{A}\otimes B_{\rm adm}^{B}.
\end{equation}

Admissibility still requires no-signaling:
\begin{equation}
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y)
  =
  \sum_b p(a,b|x,y'),
\end{equation}
and similarly for Bob.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT permits non-factorizing joint coherence but requires no-signaling record
  statistics.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-entanglement-response}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Response to the predictivity objection}
\label{appsubsec:predictivity-objection}

A possible objection is that the framework is structural but not yet fully predictive.

The response is that the paper distinguishes three levels:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item structural predictions;
  \item model templates;
  \item numerical predictions.
\end{enumerate}

The structural predictions include:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item zero modes are undamped by internal admissibility;
  \item nonzero internal modes are suppressed by \(\ee^{-\tau\mu_j^2}\);
  \item \(\tau\) is tied to the internal spectral gap;
  \item gauge filters must preserve quotient identities;
  \item gravity filters must preserve diffeomorphism constraints;
  \item branch damping must be CPTP.
\end{enumerate}

Numerical predictions require:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item internal geometry;
  \item spectra;
  \item overlap integrals;
  \item couplings;
  \item widths;
  \item detector acceptances;
  \item basin measures.
\end{enumerate}

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT is predictive only after sectoral execution; this paper supplies the execution
  architecture and structural constraints.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-predictivity-response}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Response to the over-unification objection}
\label{appsubsec:over-unification-objection}

A possible objection is that claiming to unify wave, particle, gauge, gravity, quantization, and
entanglement sounds too broad.

The correct interpretation is not that all equations of physics have been derived from one
line. The claim is that these phenomena can be organized as different projection shadows of
one finite coherent admissibility architecture.

The unification is structural:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{different physical phenomena share the same projection grammar.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-structural-unification}
\end{equation}

It is not yet a complete numerical reduction of all phenomena to one computed model.

Thus:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT unifies the architecture of admissibility and projection, while sectoral physics
  still requires execution.}
  }
  \label{eq:appK-over-unification-response}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Reviewer-facing summary}
\label{appsubsec:reviewer-facing-summary}

A concise reviewer-facing summary is:

\begin{quote}
MTT does not postulate a naive Lorentzian nonlocal operator. Its finite coherent filter is
defined through positive or physically reduced sectoral operators. In the fixed-point
realization, the damping is internal/fiber elliptic, not Lorentzian \(\Box\)-damping. The
framework is KK-compatible: compact internal spectra generate zero modes and excitation
towers. The MTT contribution is the finite coherent projection architecture that interprets
delta localization, wave interference, measurement records, gauge quotienting, gravity,
quantization, and entanglement as projection shadows. The analytic theorems are conditional
and explicit; Standard Model closure, quantum gravity, Born-rule basin measures, and
numerical phenomenology remain sectoral execution tasks.
\end{quote}

In formula form:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  B_{\rm adm}^{\rm FP}
  =
  P_{\rm coh}\chi(A_{\rm int})
  \ee^{-\tau A_{\rm int}}
  \chi(A_{\rm int})P_{\rm coh},
  \qquad
  A_{\rm int}\ge0,
  \qquad
  A\neq\Box_g.
  }
  \label{eq:appK-reviewer-formula}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Final claim-audit table}
\label{appsubsec:claim-audit-table}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.32\linewidth}|p{0.30\linewidth}|p{0.30\linewidth}}
Topic & Claimed & Not claimed\\
\hline
Finite filter & Positive contraction under stated assumptions & Projector for arbitrary data\\
Dirac delta & Sharp limit of finite kernels & Fundamental physical point object\\
Wave behavior & Spectral shadow of same kernel & Separate wave substance\\
Lorentzian physics & Positive sectoral damping, not \(\Box\)-heat flow & Naive \(\ee^{-\tau\Box}\) theory\\
KK structure & Explicitly acknowledged FP backbone & Denial of compactification role\\
Gauge & Quotient-compatible filtering & Arbitrary damped gauge propagator\\
Gravity & Constraint-compatible geometric projection & Completed nonperturbative QG\\
Measurement & Damping plus survivor basins & Decoherence alone solves outcomes\\
Born rule & Basin-measure execution target & Fully derived in all contexts\\
Entanglement & Non-factorizing joint coherence with no-signaling & Local hidden-variable model\\
Phenomenology & Structural templates and scale relations & Complete numerical predictions
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{Summary}
\label{appsubsec:claim-audit-summary}

The paper's safe final claim is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{MTT provides a finite coherent admissibility architecture whose projection shadows
  organize particle, wave, measurement, gauge, gravity, quantization, and entanglement
  structures.}
  }
\end{equation}

The paper's safe limitation is:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{
  \text{the architecture becomes numerically predictive only after sectoral execution of }
  A,\;P,\;\chi,\;\tau,\text{ quotient data, and basin measures.}
  }
\end{equation}

This is the intended level of the synthesis paper.
